### Background Facts and Law

*(Note: all of the following is fictional.)*

South Carolina uses the First Restatement for horizontal choice of law. North Carolina uses the Second Restatement. Recall that, as stated several times in class, the choice of law analysis for a federal court in state A is the same as that for a state court in state A. Thus, a North Carolina court facing a horizontal choice of law problem would use the Second Restatement.

South Carolina state law requires a plaintiff who alleges that she was injured in a slip-and-fall tort accident[^1] on a business property to prove that the business proprietor was reckless in leaving the slippery conditions. This "recklessness" standard means that a plaintiff injured in a slip-and-fall accident would have to show that the business knew about the slippery conditions, but despite that knowledge, failed to clean up properly. Under North Carolina law, however, a plaintiff need only prove negligence in a slip-and-fall case. Negligence means a failure to exercise due care under the circumstances. A plaintiff can prove negligence without proving actual knowledge of the slippery conditions (by, for example, proving that the business proprietor failed to monitor a premises adequately for slippery conditions).

Both North Carolina and South Carolina state law include "quiet title" causes of action identical to the Michigan cause of action that the plaintiff asserted in *Grable v. Darue*.

[^1]: In a "slip and fall" case, the plaintiff walks on the business' premises and is injured due to slipping on unsafe, slippery floor conditions.

<!-- page: 3 -->

In late January of 2014, Congress passed (and President Obama signed) the Emergency Home Mortgage Act (the "EHMA"), Section 1 of which reads as follows:

*Section 1: Importance of this Act and the Programs It Creates*

*The Congress hereby finds that homeowners in the United States are just emerging from a crisis in paying their mortgages that was unprecedented in the history of our nation or even the world. The financial consequences of this crisis were catastrophic to the United States economy and, thus, to our national security. Accordingly, the provisions of this statute are perhaps the most important to have been passed by Congress in its history. We intend this statute to assure that no such crisis ever occurs again.*

Under the EHMA, each state was granted the power, as a matter of federal law, to change the terms of any contract or mortgage relating to a home so as to make those terms more favorable to a homeowner. The EHMA also empowered the state to issue an order transferring title of a piece of property from one person/corporation to another. The EHMA requested that each state appoint a state "mortgage czar" to exercise this modification power. Most states, including South Carolina and North Carolina, duly appointed mortgage czars, and by law empowered their mortgage czars to exercise all of the powers of the EHMA. Mortgage czars had these powers with respect to property that lay within their respective states.

Helga Hufflepuff was born in Charlotte, North Carolina, and lived just outside (on the western portion) of the city with her parents as she began high school. Her parents died in her junior year of high school, and lacking their financial support, Helga began to work her way through high school. Ultimately, Helga's familial home was lost to foreclosure, and she began living out of her car.

Hoping to pursue her dream of a career in international business in New York, she decided to try to go to college. She gained admission to the University of South Carolina — Spartanburg, located in the northern part of South Carolina. During her first year of college, she slept in and generally lived out of her car, worked in Gastonia, NC (west of Charlotte), and attended school full time, getting very little sleep. The experience pushed her to the edge of a nervous breakdown. In the Spring of 2016, she decided to try to establish residency in South Carolina so that her tuition payments would be lower, and also to try to get control of her life. She had enough saved for the down payment, and an aunt who had just received a generous annuity from her prior work agreed to help Helga make her monthly payments. So Helga began searching for a house to buy. She began parking her car to sleep every night in Spartanburg, SC, so as to give her more time to search for a house.

She found one on the market in Spartanburg, near her college, for the bargain price of $65,000. The home was located at #4 Privet Drive, Spartanburg, SC. It was owned by Gringotts Bank, Inc., which was incorporated in Delaware. Gringotts had foreclosed on

<!-- page: 4 -->

the previous owners and had clear title to the property. Gringotts did most of its business in South Carolina, where its loan portfolio included everything from business and car loans to mortgages. Its headquarters was in Durham, North Carolina, and it held its board and shareholders' meetings there. Its CEO was Lord Voldemort, who lived in the Durham area. Lord Voldemort, a multi-millionaire, had vehemently opposed the EHMA, arguing that it was beyond Congress' powers under the Constitution to pass (and therefore unconstitutional). He had donated substantial amounts of money through various front entities to members of Congress who opposed the EHMA, and had purchased campaign ads on TV stations around the nation arguing that the EHMA was a big government effort designed to kill American economic freedom. The constitutionality of the EHMA continued to be a major issue after its passage. Scholar after scholar wrote articles on the subject, some arguing that the statute was constitutional, others saying the opposite.

In July of 2016, in Gringotts' Spartanburg office, Helga and Gringotts signed a Purchase and Sale Contract whereby Helga and Gringotts agreed that on the closing date of September 12, 2016 Helga would buy and Gringotts would sell the property at #4 Privet Drive. Helga would obtain her mortgage from Gringotts, and she agreed that she would make a down payment of $5000 and would pay $1500/month for several years to Gringotts for the mortgage until the loan was paid off. The market value of the house was $65,000.

On August 2, 2016, Helga's aunt died. Helga realized that without her aunt's help, she would not be able to pay $1500 a month. She immediately sought help from the South Carolina EHMA mortgage/housing czar. On August 15, the South Carolina mortgage czar issued an order modifying the agreement between Helga and Gringotts such that Helga would have to pay $750/month for fifteen years with no down payment. Helga immediately sent a copy of the order to Gringotts with a cover letter stating that she intended to close the deal in September of 2016. Gringotts immediately asked Helga for a meeting in its Spartanburg, SC office to discuss the issue.

At the meeting, which took place in Spartanburg, SC on August 22 during an enormous rainstorm, Lord Voldemort personally told Helga that Gringotts considered the South Carolina mortgage czar's order, and the entire EHMA, unconstitutional. Lord Voldemort further told Helga that Gringotts expected her to close the deal in September of 2016, make the $5000 down payment, and immediately begin paying $1500/month. Helga told Lord Voldemort that she would not do so, and that if Gringotts refused to go forward, she would ask the South Carolina mortgage czar to issue a second order awarding her title to the house, given that she could not find another house in that price range and would be living out of her car again. Lord Voldemort then handed her a letter he had apparently written before the meeting stating that Gringotts was cancelling the Purchase and Sale Contract. Helga reiterated that she would sue.

<!-- page: 5 -->

As Helga was on her way out of Gringotts' office that day, she slipped in a puddle and tore several ligaments in her knee and ankle. As an ambulance arrived to take her to the hospital, she said to Lord Voldemort, "I'm going to sue Gringotts for this, too." Three days later, Helga sent a letter to Gringotts reiterating her threat to sue for both the canceled house contract and the damage to her knee. In the letter, she stated that she had no medical insurance. She attached estimates from her doctors saying that she would need about $20,000 in medical care.

On September 15, 2016, the South Carolina Mortgage Czar issued an order declaring that Helga held title to #4 Privet Drive, and directing the relevant county clerk to record on county records that Helga owned the property. Gringotts immediately issued a press release stating that it considered the order unconstitutional, null, and void, and that it was the owner of #4 Privet Drive.

You are an associate in the firm representing Gringotts. Lord Voldemort has been an excellent client of yours for some time, bringing millions of dollars in business to your 2400-attorney law firm over the past three years. You are anxious to please him. Lord Voldemort says that he investigated the puddle that Helga slipped on and found that it had been caused by another customer's umbrella a few hours before Helga slipped. Although no one at Gringotts actually knew about the puddle, Lord Voldemort acknowledged that the manager of the store was supposed to watch out for puddles during rainstorms and had not done so that day. Lord Voldemort is currently facing lawsuits for fraud filed by various business partners in South Carolina state court, so he is anxious to avoid having the Hufflepuff litigation occur there. He wants a South Carolina federal court to say that Gringotts owes no money to Helga and that Gringotts is the true and rightful owner of #4 Privet Drive. Never a patient man, Voldemort doesn't want Gringotts to wait for Hufflepuff to sue Gringotts, and wants to file suit immediately.

The partner you work for, on her way out the door for a weekend of sunbathing in Miami Beach, asks for a memo from you immediately. She asks you to address the following questions:

### Assignment Questions

1. Can Gringotts sue Helga in a South Carolina federal court to get the federal court to say that
   a. Gringotts owes nothing to Helga on the slip-and-fall (in a lawsuit concerning only the slip and fall)?
   b. That Gringotts is the true and proper owner of #4 Privet Drive (in a lawsuit concerning only the property)?
   c. Both of these two together in a single lawsuit?

   *Note: in discussion question 1, consider only the jurisdictional issues, not the merits of any of these claims.*

2. Is Gringotts likely to prevail in any litigation over the slip-and-fall? Would the answer depend on whether the lawsuit was filed in a court in South Carolina or a court in North Carolina? Why or why not?

<!-- page: 6 -->

NOTE: You may assume that the South Carolina federal court has personal jurisdiction over Helga in the contemplated lawsuit. Do not discuss personal jurisdiction.

## Question 1

SC federal court must have FSMJ over Gringotts' lawsuit for it to bring it there. 28 U.S.C. § 2201(a) allows a declaratory judgment to proceed in federal court if the imaginary lawsuit (the lawsuit that the DJ pre-empts) meets FSMJ requirements. So, for Gringotts to sue in SC federal court there must be FSMJ over the slip-and-fall tort action Helga plans to bring against Gringotts.

No AU jurisdiction for the slip-and-fall because no federal cause of action, nor *Smith-Grable* exception because no embedded federal question. No diversity jurisdiction either because the AIC is $20,000, less than the $75,000 AIC necessary to get into federal court under diversity. Maybe no complete diversity of citizenship (*Strawbridge*) either: Gringotts is a citizen of Delaware and NC because its PPoB is likely NC (headquarters + board/shareholder meetings in Durham). Ambiguous where Helga's domiciled. The last place she had official residence was NC, so that's her assumptive domicile, but she may have become an SC domiciliary since she started sleeping in SC in addition to going to school there. Further, she evinced intent to stay in SC by trying to buy a house there. Could go either way, but most likely SC. If she's a SC domiciliary, then there's diversity; if she's still an NC domiciliary, then no diversity.

There may be AU SMJ for the lawsuit over #4 Privet Drive. Because Gringotts is suing under a quiet title action which is a state law claim, no federal cause of action so the Well-Pleaded-Complaint (*Mottley*) rule isn't met. However, there may be a *Smith-Grable* exception to the WPC rule if there's a federal question embedded in the state claim, the federal question is "contested and substantial," and granting SMJ would not jeopardize the state-federal division. Embedded within Gringotts' action to quiet title is a question of federal law — the constitutionality of EHMA. This affects whether Gringotts properly refused the new terms the mortgage czar imposed and therefore whether Gringotts owns #4 Privet Drive. This embedded federal question is contested and substantial — scholars disagree as to its constitutionality, and section 1 of EHMA says its provisions might be "the most important" to have ever been passed. Granting SMJ may jeopardize the state-federal division however because this case involves land and contract law, traditionally thought to be in the purview of the states. But EHMA's already seeking to regulate this, so granting SMJ is overall probably OK. In which case, there is AU SMJ for Gringotts' suit.

There cannot be SMJ under diversity jurisdiction because the AIC is $65,000 < $75,000. Further, there may not be complete diversity (see above analysis).

Gringotts cannot sue on both claims in a single lawsuit because the two claims are not part of the same case or controversy nor do they share the same operative facts. This is a requirement for supplemental jurisdiction under § 1367 both where the anchoring claim has AU SMJ and where there's diversity SMJ. The issues/facts involving slip-and-fall center around whether Gringotts was negligent/reckless to not clean the puddle, whereas

<!-- page: 2 -->

the issues/facts involving quiet title center around the purchase-and-sale agreement and EHMA's legitimacy.

Suppose the two claims did involve the same case and controversy. Gringotts could not use the slip and fall as the anchoring claim because it is not a federal claim (no AU) and is less than $75,000 (no diversity). Further, the court might exercise discretion under § 1367(c) and decline to hear the supplemental tort claim if it thinks the quiet title claim "substantially dominates" the anchoring tort claim. So the action to quiet title must be the anchoring claim under AU (diversity would not be available because the anchoring claim is $65,000 < $75,000). So if the two claims involved the same case and controversy, they could be in a single lawsuit under supplemental jurisdiction.

Alternatively, if Helga is domiciled in SC and if the lawsuits are part of the same case or controversy, Gringotts would not need to use supplemental jurisdiction at all. It could bring both claims simultaneously under diversity jurisdiction, there being complete diversity (SC v. NC & DE) and $65,000 + $20,000 = $85,000 > $75,000 under the diversity jurisdiction "stacking" rule.

## Question 2

Gringotts is bringing a DJ in SC federal court, which uses the First Restatement. A federal court uses the COL analysis of the state it sits in (*Klaxon*), so First Restatement COL rules apply. Whether negligence or recklessness standard applies is a substantive COL problem, so the federal court will ask "where's the blood" and therefore use SC law because Helga slipped and fell in SC. SC uses recklessness as a standard in slip-and-falls, so Gringotts will likely prevail because no one at Gringotts actually knew of the puddle. Actual knowledge is a necessary element of recklessness.

If Gringotts sued in NC fed court, then the Second Restatement applies and the NC court would cut the case into issues and evaluate which state's law should apply to each issue according to where the "center of gravity" of the transaction was and what state interests were involved (*Babcock*). The tort occurred in SC, so that's the "center of gravity"; further SC has interests in citizens' safety in its territory. The defendant is an SC citizen and perhaps Helga is too, which also speaks to SC state interests. But if the NC court recognized Helga as an NC citizen, then NC has state interests to apply NC law. On balance NC will probably apply SC law, so Gringotts would prevail (see above). If NC applied NC law, then Helga would likely prevail because Gringotts failed to monitor the puddles when it should have.

## Assignment 1
Civil Procedure, Fall 2016
Professor Jim Greiner

### Question 1(a)

Owing to lack of SMJ, Gringotts cannot sue Helga Hufflepuff (HH) in SC federal court for a federal declaratory judgment (FDJ) stating only that Gringotts owes nothing to HH on the slip-and-fall suit. This suit is purely a state-law claim, and it doesn't present an embedded federal question that is substantial, disputed, and legal (as necessary for AUJ under *Smith/Grable* and *Empire*). Thus, there's no AUJ. Moreover, this suit fails to satisfy the requirements of diversity jurisdiction because the AIC is $20,000. Although this defect is sufficient to bar diversity jurisdiction, the parties' lack of complete diversity (*Strawbridge*) would also probably keep the suit out of federal court. HH is likely a domiciliary of NC, her home state, since college doesn't alter domicile, residence is distinct from domicile (*Mas v. Perry*), and HH hasn't expressed the intention to remain in SC following graduation. (However, it's possible that HH's domicile has changed to SC because she has apparently severed her ties to NC and begun living in SC, where she intends to own a house.) As a corporation, Gringotts is domiciled in its state of incorporation (DE) and — per *Hertz* — its "nerve center," or where decisionmaking and overall control are based (NC).

### Question 1(b)

Gringotts almost certainly can sue HH in SC federal court for a FDJ on the quiet title action alone. This particular action falls under the *Smith/Grable* exception for AUJ. There is a federal question embedded in this state-law claim (whether EHMA violates the Constitution, thus rendering the mortgage czar's orders invalid). The issue is substantial (per Section 1's assertion that EHMA's "provisions . . . are perhaps the most important . . . [ever] passed by Congress") and disputed (per scholarly debate over EHMA's constitutionality). In accordance with *Empire*, this dispute is largely legal, not factual, since the statute's constitutionality is a question of law.

Exercising AUJ here wouldn't impermissibly infringe on state sovereignty because cases involving EHMA — a congressional statute — are of particular interest to federal courts (which are well suited to adjudicate them), and ordinary quiet title actions remain the province of state courts. (NB: There is no diversity jurisdiction for this claim since the AIC is $65,000.)

### Question 1(c)

Per § 1367(c) and *Gibbs*, Gringotts can probably sue HH for FDJs on both claims in one lawsuit. We're dealing with claim joinder, and the anchoring claim (quiet title) has AUJ as its SMJ basis, which simplifies the assessment of supplemental jurisdiction for the slip-and-fall claim. Broadly speaking, the tort claim forms part of the "same case or controversy" as the quiet title action because it occurred in the midst of the parties' disagreement over ownership of #4 Privet Drive. But there isn't much fact-relatedness between these claims, since the quiet title action is largely legal and thus doesn't really share a "common nucleus of operative fact" with the tort claim (*Gibbs*). However, the federal issue predominates (§ 1367(c)(2)), and the tort claim doesn't appear to raise complex or novel issues of state law (§ 1367(c)(1)); furthermore, judicial economy would militate in favor of exercising supplemental jurisdiction because the parties to the dispute are the same (*Gibbs*). Because supplemental jurisdiction isn't a plaintiff's right but rather a doctrine of discretion (*Gibbs*), it's difficult to predict with certainty whether SC federal court would exercise supplemental jurisdiction. Nonetheless, because there don't appear to be "compelling reasons for declining jurisdiction" (§ 1367(c)(4)), SC federal court would probably exercise supplemental jurisdiction. (Alternatively, if HH is a SC domiciliary, then the claims can

<!-- page: 2 -->

proceed together in federal court with diversity jurisdiction as the basis of SMJ. In that scenario, the parties are diverse, and — after stacking the two state-law claims' AICs — the total AIC is $85,000.)

### Question 2

Gringotts might well prevail in litigation over the tort claim, although this probability hinges on where the suit is filed. As a threshold matter, the slip-and-fall standard is a substantive issue, meaning that we look to the Restatements for the HCOL analysis. SC uses the 1st Restatement, and its inquiry thus turns on where the wrong blossomed into a tort. NC uses the 2nd Restatement, and therefore its inquiry rests on fuzzier "interest" and "center of gravity" (COG) analyses. SC state law requires recklessness, while NC state law is satisfied by negligence. The facts establish that Gringotts was negligent, but not reckless, regarding the puddle (no one at Gringotts knew about it, but the manager *should* have known).

If the suit were filed in SC, Gringotts would prevail because the "blood" is in SC, meaning that the recklessness standard applies. Since HH cannot show that Gringotts was reckless, Gringotts avoids liability under SC law. However, if HH were to file in NC, then Gringotts might not prevail, since the interest/COG analyses under the 2nd Restatement could result in the application of NC law, which only requires negligence (and which HH can establish). In representing Gringotts, we would argue that SC law should apply because the tort occurred at an office within SC (interest) and evidence/witnesses are likely located in SC (COG). HH would likely argue for an extension of the doctrine in *Babcock* and *Neumeier*, contending that because HH and Gringotts are (arguably) both domiciliaries of NC, state interest and convenience tend toward the application of NC law. It's hard to say how a NC court would rule on this issue, and thus the HCOL outcome is less predictable if the claim is filed in NC.

## Question 1

We need to determine whether the SC federal court has SMJ over the claims.

Because this is a declaratory judgment action, we analyze SMJ for the Imaginary Lawsuit that would likely have been brought by Helga but for Gringotts' declaratory judgment action.

#### Does the SC fed court have diversity SMJ?

If Gringotts and Helga are diverse parties and the AIC (amount in controversy) is met, then the SC fed court has diversity SMJ over Gringotts' declaratory judgment motion. The AIC is met because, though neither of Helga's claims exceeds $75,000 (and thus neither could be asserted by itself as a diversity claim), they may be aggregated for the purposes of a SMJ AIC calculation. Aggregated, the claims equal $85,000, which is greater than the required $75,000.

Gringotts is a citizen of NC by the *Hertz* nerve center test (because board meetings are held there). Gringotts is also a citizen of Delaware because that is the state in which it is incorporated. Therefore, the SC federal court has diversity jurisdiction if Helga is not a citizen of NC or Delaware.

Helga's domiciliary status is ambiguous. Helga was born in NC, where her family lived, so is a citizen of NC for the purposes of diversity SMJ unless her citizenship has since changed. Helga lives in a mobile home now and seems to have few personal and sentimental connections to NC remaining. Further, she attends school in SC, and though university attendance typically does not alter one's citizenship, perhaps here it evinces a decision or intention to live in SC permanently. Against this is the fact that, for the majority of her studies, she lived in NC.

Eventually, however, Helga did explicitly intend to change residence to SC, and was searching for a house to buy. She was living out of her car, which she began parking in SC. Is this enough to change her place of residence? It is unclear — she intends to stay if she successfully closes on the SC house, but if the house doesn't close she might go back to parking the car in NC, indicating that she was only temporarily residing in SC.

#### Does the SC fed court have AUJ?

Assuming the SC federal court does not have diversity jurisdiction over the claims, can it still hear them on the basis of AUJ?

The slip-and-fall claim is a classic state law tort and thus it can only be heard in federal court if it is heard supplemental to an anchoring claim. The title-passage claim is ostensibly another classic state law property claim. However, just like in *Grable*, the title passes

<!-- page: 2 -->

pursuant to a federal statute, and so it is likely the *Smith-Grable* exception kicks in because the state claim has an embedded federal question, resulting in AUJ.

Note that *Smith-Grable* requires that granting AUJ won't upset the balance of powers between state and federal courts, and it's hard to tell how this housing statute will affect that balance. Further, this is a very situation-specific issue that will likely draw on the circumstances surrounding Helga's finances and her aunt, though ruling on the constitutionality of the czar's order is a more general issue of law. Finally, this is likely a circumstance where federal courts would worry about quality of state adjudication — the federal courts likely value interstate uniformity in EHMA interpretation. Therefore, the *Smith-Grable* factors, on balance, point to AUJ.

Finally, for § 1367 supplemental jurisdiction, the slip-and-fall claim must arise out of the same case or controversy as the title-passage claim. These claims are causally linked, and the same parties are involved, however the connection between the two claims is tenuous. If they are part of the same case or controversy, and none of the discretionary 1367(c) factors are met, the fed court has supplemental jurisdiction over the tort claim.

### Question 2

We must perform a choice-of-law analysis in both forums to determine likelihood of success on the merits. Because a slip-and-fall claim is a substantive issue, we must look to the restatement each forum abides by.

*If the litigation is brought in NC:* NC is a 2nd restatement state, so we must look to state interests and the "center of gravity" of the case. Because the tort occurred in SC, the house Helga was looking at was in SC, and Helga might even be domiciled in SC, SC likely has the most significant relationship with the case. Further, SC has a strong interest in protecting its citizens from dangerous puddles and spills within its borders. Therefore, SC law likely applies. Therefore, "recklessness" would be the legal standard and the declaratory judgment action would likely succeed. If for some reason, perhaps because the court finds Helga an NC resident, the court chooses to apply NC law, "negligence" would be the legal standard. In that case the action is more likely to fail.

*If the litigation is brought in SC:* SC is a 1st restatement state and so because the harm occurred in SC, SC law will apply. This means "recklessness" is the legal standard, which is certainly not met. Hence the declaratory judgment action is likely to succeed.

## 2016-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 4

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

#### Part a

Gringotts would need to seek a declaratory judgment on Helga's slip and fall (S&F) claim (28 U.S.C. § 2201). Courts have SMJ over a declaratory judgment lawsuit if the court would have SMJ over a suit filed by Helga (Imaginary Lawsuit Rule). Because an S&F claim is a state law claim, Gringotts lacks FQJ and can only sue Helga in SC federal court if there is complete diversity (*Strawbridge*). Gringotts is domiciled in NC because its nerve center is in Durham (*Hertz*) and DE because it's incorporated there. Helga's current living situation is in flux between NC and SC, but her domicile doesn't seem to have changed since she was born in NC. Although Helga intends to stay in SC, she hasn't yet taken up permanent residence there (*Mas*). Because living in a car is likely too transitory to establish residency, Helga is likely domiciled in NC. Even if Gringotts successfully argued that parking her car every night in SC established Helga's residency and intent to stay in SC, the suit is AIC deficient since Helga's medical bills only amount to $20,000. Therefore Gringotts likely can't sue Helga in SC federal court in a lawsuit concerning only the S&F claim.

#### Part b

Because SC federal court likely doesn't have diversity jurisdiction (Part a) and the suit would be AIC deficient regardless (the house is only worth $65,000), Gringotts can only proceed in its quiet title action over #4 Privet Drive (4PD) in SC federal court if the court has FQJ. A quiet title action is a state law claim, but there may be FQJ under the *Smith-Grable* exception because the EHMA is a federal issue that's embedded in the quiet title action. First, the quiet title action depends on the court's interpretation of the EHMA, meaning the dispute is primarily an issue of law — the constitutionality of the EHMA — not fact. Second, the EHMA is a substantial federal interest — its provisions are "perhaps the most important to have been passed by Congress" — and warrants a federal forum because it's in the government's interest for its legislation to be resolved in a federal court. Treating the claim as "arising under" might upset the division of labor between state and federal courts because it could sweep many quiet title actions that contest the constitutionality of the EHMA into federal court. However, a court will likely hold that the *Smith-Grable* exception applies given the centrality of the EHMA in the claim and importance of the statute. Therefore Gringotts can likely sue Helga in SC federal court in a lawsuit concerning 4PD.

#### Part c

<!-- page: 2 -->

If the court found that Helga is domiciled in SC, then SC federal court would have diversity jurisdiction over a lawsuit contesting both claims because compounding the value of 4PD and the medical bills surpasses the required AIC. However, since diversity jurisdiction is unlikely and SC federal court likely doesn't have SMJ over the S&F claim, Gringotts could only bring both claims together in a single lawsuit if the court had supplemental jurisdiction. A federal court can accept a state claim tacked onto a federal anchoring claim if both claims arise out of the same transaction or occurrence (t/o) with a common nucleus of operative fact (*Gibbs*). The S&F occurred when Helga met with Voldemort to discuss the deal, but the only common facts are the location, plaintiff, and defendant. The court is unlikely to find that the S&F, which centers on the maintenance of Gringotts' facility and only tangentially coincided with the controversy over the title of 4PD, stems from the same case or controversy. Therefore, Gringotts likely cannot bring both of the claims together in a single lawsuit.

### Question 2

Gringotts can only prevail in any litigation over the S&F if SC law applies since the puddle resulted from the manager's negligence. Because S&F tort law is substantive, determining the appropriate standard of care requires a horizontal choice of law analysis.

If the case is in SC court, the court will use the 1st restatement for HCOL. Because Helga slipped in Gringotts' SC office, the blood is in SC (*Alabama Great Southern RR*). Therefore the recklessness standard for S&F tort accidents would apply and Gringotts would likely prevail because the manager didn't act recklessly.

If the case is in NC court, the court will use the 2nd restatement for HCOL. Therefore NC will consider state interests that apply to the issue and look for the "center of gravity" of the transaction (*Babcock*). Helga would likely argue that NC law should apply because NC has an interest in protecting its residents. Gringotts will likely argue that SC law should apply because the S&F took place in SC and SC has an interest in regulating the maintenance of business facilities. However, since NC law will allow for more adequate protection of its citizens, a NC court is more likely to apply a negligence standard for S&F tort accidents. Therefore, Gringotts would likely lose in NC court because the manager acted negligently.

- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time.

<!-- page: 2 -->

---

### Background Facts and Law

*(Note: all of the following is fictional.)*

Virginia uses the First Restatement for horizontal choice of law. West Virginia uses the Second Restatement. Recall that, as stated several times in class, the choice of law analysis for a federal court in state A is the same as that for a state court in state A. Thus, an Eastern District of Virginia court facing a horizontal choice of law problem would use the First Restatement.

Under Virginia law, a plaintiff can recover for personal injuries sustained in a drone crash only if the drone operator was reckless. Virginia defines recklessness as an operator intentionally taking an action that was highly likely to cause injury. Under West Virginia law, a plaintiff can recover for personal injuries in a drone crash if the drone operator was negligent. West Virginia defines negligence as a failure to exercise the due care that a reasonable drone operator would exercise under the circumstances.

The Chudley Cannons are a drone racing team. Their manager is Ludo Bagman. The team owns about forty drones and maintains them in workshops located in Wheeling, West Virginia, and in Roanoke, Virginia. The team is incorporated in West Virginia and has its principal place of business in Wheeling, WV (that is where all its employees work, and where it holds its races). The team's board meetings are held in Charleston, WV.

Oliver Wood, a citizen of West Virginia, is a drone racer. He races for the Chudley Cannons and does so as an independent contractor. Oliver was performing a test run in Roanoke, Virginia with a Chudley Cannons drone called the Tornado 360. The drone crashed into Kingsley Shacklebolt, a citizen of Virginia, injuring him seriously. Kingsley was a spectator at the test run. The Tornado 360 turned out to be defective; it had a steering malfunction that caused many crashes. The malfunction had been reported to the Chudley Cannons by three previous operators, including Oliver himself. The Chudley Cannons had done nothing to fix the malfunction.

Kingsley retained an attorney and filed suit in the Eastern District of Virginia (EDVA) against Oliver Wood and the Chudley Cannons. Kingsley's complaint alleged (1) negligent operation of a drone against Oliver Wood, (2) negligent maintenance of a drone against the Chudley Cannons, and (3) reckless maintenance of a drone against the Chudley Cannons. Kingsley alleged damages of $300,000 in each count.

<!-- page: 3 -->

Both Oliver Wood and the Chudley Cannons have moved to dismiss the complaint in its entirety for failure to state a claim upon which relief can be granted. In the alternative, both defendants move to transfer the case from the EDVA to the Southern District of West Virginia (SDWV).

### Assignment Questions

You are an associate in the firm representing Kingsley Shacklebolt. The partner in charge of the case asks you for a memo addressing the following:

1. How should the EDVA judge rule on the defendants' motion to dismiss? In particular:
   a. How should the EDVA judge rule on the motion to dismiss the negligent operation count against Oliver Wood?
   b. How should the EDVA judge rule on the motions to dismiss the two counts (negligent and reckless maintenance) against the Chudley Cannons?

2. How should the EDVA judge rule on the defendants' motion to transfer? In particular:
   a. Should the EDVA judge transfer the case, and if so, why?
   b. If the EDVA judge does transfer the case, what law will the SDWV apply to Kingsley's claims?

**Note:** You may assume that EDVA has proper personal jurisdiction over both defendants and that venue in EDVA is proper.

## 2016-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2016-assignment-02-model-answer-1.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 2 — Civil Procedure 2, 2016

### Question 1: Motion to Dismiss

#### Count 1 — Negligent Operation Against Oliver Wood

The EDVA should deny the motion to dismiss Count 1. Because EDVA sits in Virginia and this is a diversity case, EDVA applies Virginia's First Restatement for horizontal choice of law. Under the First Restatement, the court asks "where is the blood?" — i.e., where did the tort occur? The drone crashed in Roanoke, Virginia, so Virginia law governs. Virginia requires recklessness for drone crash claims, not negligence. Count 1 pleads only negligence against Oliver Wood. Since Virginia law applies and negligence is insufficient under Virginia law, Count 1 fails to state a claim.

However, dismissal under Rule 12(b)(6) requires the court to accept all well-pleaded facts as true (*Twiqbal*). The facts as alleged — that the Tornado 360 had a known steering malfunction reported by three prior operators including Wood himself — could plausibly support an inference of recklessness even if the count is labeled "negligent operation." The court should consider whether the pleaded facts are sufficient to sustain any viable claim. If the court reads the complaint as pleading facts that support recklessness, the motion to dismiss Count 1 should be denied. If the court reads the complaint strictly as pleading only negligence, it should grant the motion to dismiss with leave to amend.

#### Counts 2 and 3 — Negligent and Reckless Maintenance Against Chudley Cannons

Virginia law applies to both maintenance counts (tort occurred in Virginia). Count 2 alleges negligent maintenance, which is insufficient under Virginia's recklessness standard for drone crashes. Count 3 alleges reckless maintenance. The facts support Count 3: the Chudley Cannons had received reports of the steering malfunction from three prior operators and did nothing to fix it. Knowingly failing to repair a defect that had already caused crashes is intentional inaction in the face of a known high probability of injury — this meets Virginia's recklessness standard. Count 3 survives the motion to dismiss.

Count 2 should be dismissed because Virginia requires recklessness; negligence alone is insufficient.

### Question 2: Motion to Transfer

#### 2(a) — Should EDVA Transfer?

The court should transfer to the Southern District of West Virginia (SDWV). Under 28 U.S.C. § 1404(a), a court may transfer to any district where the case could have been brought if transfer serves the convenience of parties and witnesses and the interests of justice.

<!-- page: 2 -->

The case could have been brought in SDWV: the Chudley Cannons have their principal place of business in Wheeling, WV, which is in the Northern District; however, the SDWV may also be proper if there is a connection to that district. More precisely, transfer is proper to any district with proper venue. The Cannons' principal place of business in WV and Oliver Wood's West Virginia citizenship mean that the SDWV likely has proper venue.

Factors favoring transfer: (1) the Chudley Cannons' witnesses and records are predominantly in West Virginia; (2) the Cannons' maintenance was performed in the Wheeling, WV workshop; (3) two of the three drone workshops are located in WV. Factors against transfer: the tort occurred in Roanoke, Virginia; Kingsley Shacklebolt is a Virginia citizen; Virginia witnesses such as the other spectators and medical providers are in Virginia. On balance, the convenience factors may favor transfer given that the Cannons' maintenance records and employees are in WV, but this is a close question.

#### 2(b) — What Law Applies After Transfer?

If the transferring court (EDVA) had proper venue, the receiving court (SDWV) applies the transferor court's choice of law rules under *Van Dusen v. Barrack* (defendant-initiated transfer). EDVA is in Virginia, which uses the First Restatement. Under the First Restatement, the blood is in Virginia (the crash occurred in Roanoke, VA), so Virginia law applies. Therefore, SDWV would apply Virginia law — including Virginia's recklessness standard — to Kingsley's claims, even though SDWV itself sits in a Second Restatement state.

## 2016-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2016-assignment-02-model-answer-2.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 2 — Civil Procedure 2, 2016

### Question 1: Motion to Dismiss

The EDVA is a federal court sitting in Virginia, a diversity case. Under *Klaxon Co. v. Stentor*, federal courts in diversity apply the choice of law rules of the state in which they sit. Virginia applies the First Restatement. Under the First Restatement, tort claims are governed by the law of the place where the wrong occurred — i.e., "where the blood is" (*Alabama Great Southern RR v. Carroll*). The drone crashed in Roanoke, Virginia, so Virginia law governs all three counts.

Virginia requires recklessness — intentionally taking an action highly likely to cause injury — for drone crash personal injury claims. West Virginia requires only negligence.

#### Count 1 — Negligent Operation Against Oliver Wood

Count 1 should be dismissed. Virginia law applies, and Virginia requires recklessness for drone crash claims. Negligent operation is legally insufficient under Virginia law regardless of how plausibly the facts are alleged. The court should dismiss Count 1 for failure to state a claim under Rule 12(b)(6). However, the court should grant leave to amend so Kingsley can plead reckless operation if the facts support it. Whether Wood's continuing to operate a drone he had personally reported as defective constitutes recklessness is a question worth exploring in a recklessness-based amendment.

#### Count 2 — Negligent Maintenance Against Chudley Cannons

Count 2 should also be dismissed for the same reason. Virginia requires recklessness; the negligent maintenance count is insufficient as a matter of law under Virginia's standard.

#### Count 3 — Reckless Maintenance Against Chudley Cannons

Count 3 should survive the motion to dismiss. Applying Rule 12(b)(6) under *Twombly/Iqbal*, the court accepts all well-pleaded facts as true and asks whether the claim is plausible. The facts alleged — that the Tornado 360 had a known steering malfunction reported by three previous operators, including Wood himself, and that the Cannons did nothing to fix it — plausibly support recklessness. The Cannons had actual knowledge of a defect likely to cause injury and deliberately failed to address it. This satisfies Virginia's definition of recklessness (intentional action/inaction highly likely to cause injury). Count 3 states a plausible claim.

### Question 2: Motion to Transfer

#### 2(a) — Transfer Analysis

<!-- page: 2 -->

Under 28 U.S.C. § 1404(a), transfer is available to any district where the case could have been brought, for the convenience of parties and witnesses, in the interest of justice. The case could have been brought in SDWV: the Chudley Cannons are incorporated in West Virginia with their principal place of business there, establishing proper venue and personal jurisdiction.

Private interest factors: The Cannons' maintenance records and most witnesses are in West Virginia. The maintenance workshop relevant to the Tornado 360's defect is in Wheeling, WV. Oliver Wood is a West Virginia citizen. These factors favor transfer to SDWV.

Public interest factors: Virginia has an interest in compensating its injured citizen (Kingsley is a Virginia citizen) and in regulating drone activity within its borders. West Virginia has an interest in the conduct of its businesses and citizens. These factors are roughly neutral.

The defendant has the burden of showing that transfer is warranted. Given the location of the maintenance records, the defect history, and most of the witnesses relevant to the maintenance claims, transfer to SDWV is appropriate.

#### 2(b) — Choice of Law After Transfer

Under *Van Dusen v. Barrack*, a § 1404(a) transfer initiated by the defendant does not change the applicable law. The receiving court (SDWV) applies the choice of law rules of the transferring court (EDVA, sitting in Virginia). EDVA applies Virginia's First Restatement. Under the First Restatement, the blood is in Virginia (the crash occurred there), so Virginia substantive law applies. The SDWV would therefore apply Virginia's recklessness standard despite being located in a Second Restatement state.

This result is consistent with the purposes of *Van Dusen*: defendants should not be permitted to use § 1404(a) transfer as a mechanism to obtain more favorable substantive law.

## 2016-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2016-assignment-02-model-answer-3.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 2 — Civil Procedure 2, 2016

### Question 1: Motions to Dismiss

As a threshold matter, the EDVA must apply Virginia's choice of law rules (*Klaxon*). Virginia uses the First Restatement, so the applicable substantive law is determined by "where the blood is" — i.e., the location of the tort. The drone crash occurred in Roanoke, Virginia, so Virginia substantive law governs all three counts.

#### Count 1 — Negligent Operation Against Oliver Wood

Virginia requires recklessness, not negligence, for drone crash personal injury claims. Count 1 pleads negligent operation, which is legally insufficient under Virginia law. The EDVA should grant the motion to dismiss Count 1. The facts as pleaded — that Wood operated a drone he himself had previously reported as defective — may support a recklessness theory, so Kingsley should be given leave to amend to reframe the claim as reckless operation.

Under *Iqbal/Twombly*, a complaint must plead factual content that makes a claim plausible, not merely possible. Even assuming the court were to construe Count 1 liberally, "negligent operation" does not state a cognizable claim under Virginia law regardless of how well-pleaded the facts are. Dismissal with leave to amend is appropriate.

#### Count 2 — Negligent Maintenance Against Chudley Cannons

Same analysis as Count 1. Virginia's recklessness requirement means negligent maintenance is insufficient. Count 2 should be dismissed. The underlying facts may support a recklessness claim, but negligence alone does not state a claim under Virginia law.

#### Count 3 — Reckless Maintenance Against Chudley Cannons

Count 3 survives. Recklessness under Virginia law means intentionally taking an action highly likely to cause injury. The Cannons received reports of the steering malfunction from three operators, including Wood, and took no corrective action. Continuing to deploy a drone with a known, repeatedly-reported defect that had caused crashes constitutes intentional inaction in the face of near-certain injury. Under *Twombly/Iqbal*, Count 3 is plausible on its face and states a viable claim. Motion to dismiss Count 3 should be denied.

### Question 2: Motion to Transfer

#### 2(a) — Whether to Transfer

<!-- page: 2 -->

Under § 1404(a), the court weighs (1) whether the case could have been filed in the transferee district, (2) the convenience of parties and witnesses, and (3) the interest of justice.

The Chudley Cannons are a West Virginia corporation headquartered in Wheeling, WV. SDWV has personal jurisdiction over the Cannons and venue is proper there. The case could have been filed in SDWV. Oliver Wood is a West Virginia citizen; SDWV would also have personal jurisdiction over him.

Convenience factors favoring transfer: the Cannons' main operations, employees, and maintenance records are in West Virginia; most documentary evidence of the defective Tornado 360 and the reports about the steering malfunction would be located there. Convenience factors against transfer: Kingsley is a Virginia citizen; the crash occurred in Virginia; local Virginia witnesses (medical providers, other spectators) are in Virginia.

On balance, because the core issue is the Cannons' knowledge of the defect and their failure to act — a matter centered on their West Virginia operations — transfer to SDWV is likely appropriate.

#### 2(b) — Law Applied After Transfer

Under *Van Dusen v. Barrack*, when a defendant moves for transfer under § 1404(a), the transferee court applies the choice of law rules of the transferor court. The transferor is EDVA (Virginia), which uses the First Restatement. Under the First Restatement, Virginia law applies because the crash occurred there. The SDWV therefore applies Virginia substantive law — the recklessness standard — to Kingsley's claims.

The rationale for *Van Dusen* is that transfer should not change the law applicable to the merits. A defendant should not be able to forum-shop for favorable substantive law by moving for a convenience transfer.

## 2016-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2016-assignment-02-model-answer-4.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 2 — Civil Procedure 2, 2016

### Question 1: Motions to Dismiss

The EDVA applies Virginia's choice of law rules (*Klaxon*). Virginia uses the First Restatement, meaning the substantive law of the place of the wrong governs. The tort occurred in Roanoke, Virginia — that is "where the blood is" — so Virginia law governs all three counts.

Virginia requires recklessness (intentionally taking an action highly likely to cause injury) for drone crash personal injury claims. West Virginia requires only negligence.

#### Count 1 — Negligent Operation Against Oliver Wood

The EDVA should grant the motion to dismiss Count 1. Virginia law governs, and Virginia requires recklessness, not negligence. Regardless of how well-pleaded the negligence facts are, they fail to state a claim cognizable under Virginia law. Wood should be aware that amendment to allege recklessness is possible given the underlying facts (Wood had himself reported the Tornado 360's malfunction), but Count 1 as pleaded must be dismissed.

#### Count 2 — Negligent Maintenance Against Chudley Cannons

The EDVA should grant the motion to dismiss Count 2 for the same reason. Virginia's recklessness requirement precludes a standalone negligence-based drone crash claim. Count 2 fails as a matter of law.

#### Count 3 — Reckless Maintenance Against Chudley Cannons

The EDVA should deny the motion to dismiss Count 3. Under *Twombly/Iqbal*, the court accepts well-pleaded facts as true and determines whether the claim is plausible. The Cannons had received reports of the Tornado 360's steering malfunction from three operators; they did nothing to repair it. This amounts to intentionally ignoring a known, serious defect — squarely within Virginia's definition of recklessness. Count 3 is plausibly pleaded and states a claim.

### Question 2: Motion to Transfer

#### 2(a) — Transfer

<!-- page: 2 -->

Under 28 U.S.C. § 1404(a), the EDVA may transfer to any district where the action could have been brought, for convenience of parties and witnesses and in the interest of justice.

The Chudley Cannons are a West Virginia corporation with their principal place of business in Wheeling, WV. Venue would be proper in SDWV and the court would have jurisdiction over the Cannons there. Oliver Wood is a West Virginia citizen and would be subject to personal jurisdiction in SDWV. The case could have been brought there.

Convenience: The key evidence on the maintenance claims — records of the steering malfunction reports, the Tornado 360's maintenance history, the Cannons' internal communications — is in West Virginia. The Cannons' employees most knowledgeable about the defect are in West Virginia. These witnesses and documents would be more conveniently accessed in SDWV.

Against transfer: The crash site is in Virginia; Virginia witnesses (Kingsley's treating physicians, other spectators) are in Virginia; and Kingsley, the plaintiff, chose Virginia as his forum. Courts generally give some weight to a plaintiff's forum choice.

On balance, the maintenance-focused nature of the surviving claims (Count 3) and the location of relevant evidence in West Virginia support transfer.

#### 2(b) — Applicable Law After Transfer

Under *Van Dusen v. Barrack*, transfer under § 1404(a) at the defendant's behest does not alter the applicable substantive law. The SDWV applies the choice of law rules of the transferor court — EDVA, applying Virginia's First Restatement. Under the First Restatement, the wrong occurred in Virginia, so Virginia law governs. Accordingly, SDWV would apply Virginia's recklessness standard even though West Virginia itself uses the Second Restatement and applies a negligence standard.

This outcome reflects the Erie/choice-of-law principle that defendants cannot gain a substantive advantage through § 1404(a) transfer. As the Supreme Court recognized in *Black & White Taxicab*, allowing parties to manipulate applicable law through forum selection — whether by reincorporating in another state or by seeking transfer — would undermine the uniformity that *Erie* and *Van Dusen* seek to preserve.

### Background Facts and Law

*(Note: all of the following is fictional.)*

Manufactured Interests, Inc. ("MIP") is a corporation incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in New York. MIP manufactures and sells a product called the BAM. The BAM is a small handheld device that releases a burst of air from a canister that MIP claims reduces pain and distress. The BAM is marketed as effective for treating anxiety and post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), among other things. In 2009, MIP began selling the BAM nationally.

Outrage, Inc. ("Outrage") is a corporation incorporated and with its principal place of business in Delaware. Outrage's sole business is investigating and publicizing what it considers fraudulent corporate conduct. Outrage investigates companies through a combination of gathering publicly available information and surreptitiously installing recording and transmission devices in corporate offices.

Outrage began investigating MIP in 2012. Over the course of its investigation, Outrage gathered publicly available materials suggesting that the BAM was medically ineffective and that its release had been preceded by a corrupt approval process involving bribed FDA officials. Outrage also installed a recording device in MIP's board room.

In 2013, Outrage published its findings in a lengthy pamphlet entitled "The BAM Scam." The pamphlet relied on publicly available materials, the recording device evidence, and an expert analysis. The expert stated that, in her opinion, the BAM was medically ineffective, by which she meant that the BAM would not work for more than 50% of those who tried it (the "expert statement"). Outrage sent the pamphlet to several news organizations and relevant government officials. None of the news organizations or government officials reported on or responded to the pamphlet.

<!-- page: 3 -->

MIP claims that the BAM is effective in about 70% of users. You may assume that the true effectiveness rate for the BAM is an open question. MIP's own internal documents suggest that the BAM was effective in about 70% of users in clinical trials, but those clinical trials have never been peer reviewed. The expert's statement that the BAM is effective in fewer than 50% of users was based on her reading of MIP's clinical trial documents, which she obtained via FOIA request.

In late 2014, MIP filed suit in the United States District Court for the Southern District of New York (SDNY) against Outrage. MIP pleaded causes of action for (1) misappropriation of trade secrets (based on the recording device evidence), (2) trespass (also based on the recording device), and (3) defamation (based on the expert statement in the pamphlet regarding the effectiveness of the BAM). The parties agreed that New York law governs all claims in the suit. Discovery ensued for eighteen months.

After discovery, MIP and Outrage cross-moved for summary judgment on all three claims. The trial judge, citing lengthy submitted declarations, entered summary judgment for Outrage on the trade secrets and trespass claims. In so doing, the judge stated that, while she had seen "lots of evidence" suggesting Outrage had installed recording devices, she "just didn't find it convincing." With respect to the defamation claim, the judge ruled that there were genuine issues of material fact, denying both parties' motions for summary judgment.

The defamation claim went to trial before a jury. After hearing the evidence, the jury returned a verdict for Outrage on the defamation claim. After the jury announced its verdict, MIP made a motion for judgment as a matter of law (JAMOL). The judge denied the JAMOL. MIP has now moved for a new trial on the defamation claim.

### Assignment Questions

1. After the jury returned its verdict for Outrage, was the judge correct to deny MIP's JAMOL?
2. Should the judge grant MIP's new trial motion on the defamation claim?
3. If the judge grants MIP's new trial motion, can Outrage immediately appeal the grant of a new trial?
4. Does the use of a jury in adjudicating MIP's claims raise any concerns? Any benefits?

## Assignment 3 — Civil Procedure 2, 2016

### Question 1: JAMOL

The judge was correct to deny MIP's JAMOL. A JAMOL is appropriate only when no reasonable jury could have found for the non-moving party — i.e., when the evidence, viewed in the light most favorable to Outrage (*Reeves v. Sanderson Plumbing*), is insufficient to support the verdict. The court may not make credibility determinations or weigh evidence; it must draw all reasonable inferences in favor of the verdict-winner.

The defamation claim centered on the expert statement that the BAM was effective in fewer than 50% of users. MIP claimed 70% effectiveness; the expert, reviewing MIP's own clinical trial documents, concluded the rate was below 50%. The true effectiveness rate is an open question. A reasonable jury could have found the expert's analysis credible and concluded that the statement — that the BAM worked in fewer than 50% of users — was a defensible, non-defamatory opinion based on disclosed underlying data. Alternatively, a reasonable jury could have found that Outrage's publication lacked the requisite fault for defamation. The existence of competing expert interpretations of MIP's own unreviewed clinical data is precisely the kind of factual dispute that juries are designed to resolve. The JAMOL was correctly denied.

### Question 2: New Trial

The judge should evaluate MIP's new trial motion under the "great weight of the evidence" standard. Unlike JAMOL, a new trial may be granted when the verdict is against the great weight of the evidence, even if a reasonable jury could have reached it. The trial judge, who observed the witnesses and evidence firsthand, has broad discretion.

Here, there are grounds to consider granting a new trial on the defamation claim. The expert's opinion was based on clinical trial documents obtained via FOIA request. MIP's internal documents suggested 70% effectiveness. The expert's methodology — her interpretation of those documents — was the central dispute. If the trial judge believed the jury gave insufficient weight to MIP's evidence about effectiveness, or that the expert's testimony was not credible, she could conclude the verdict was against the great weight of the evidence and grant a new trial.

<!-- page: 2 -->

However, the judge must be cautious about using new trial motions to simply override jury verdicts she disagrees with. The "great weight" standard is higher than mere disagreement. Unless the verdict is clearly against the weight of the credible evidence — a conclusion that is difficult to sustain when the underlying issue (BAM effectiveness) is genuinely contested and subject to expert disagreement — the judge should deny the new trial motion.

On balance, given the genuine factual dispute over effectiveness and the availability of reasonable interpretations supporting Outrage's position, the judge should deny the new trial motion.

### Question 3: Immediate Appeal of New Trial Grant

If the judge grants MIP's new trial motion, Outrage cannot immediately appeal. Under 28 U.S.C. § 1291, courts of appeals have jurisdiction only over "final decisions" of district courts. A grant of a new trial is not a final decision — it sends the case back for further proceedings. Outrage must wait until after the new trial results in a final judgment before appealing the grant of the new trial.

This rule prevents piecemeal appeals and promotes judicial efficiency. The only exception for interlocutory appeal in this context would be if the district court certified the issue under § 1292(b), which requires the district court to determine that the order involves a controlling question of law and that immediate appeal would materially advance the litigation — unlikely in this circumstance.

### Question 4: Concerns and Benefits of Jury

**Concerns:** Juries may lack the technical expertise to evaluate complex scientific or medical evidence, such as conflicting expert analyses of clinical trial data about the BAM's effectiveness rate. Jurors may be swayed by sympathy or by presentation style rather than the underlying merits. Jury verdicts are also opaque — unlike bench decisions, they provide no reasoned explanation, making them harder to review on appeal and possibly inconsistent across cases.

Additionally, juries may be susceptible to anchoring on dramatic or emotionally resonant aspects of Outrage's investigation (the surreptitious recording devices, the "fraud" allegations), even on a narrow defamation question.

**Benefits:** Juries introduce community-norm assessment — particularly relevant for defamation claims, where the standard of what counts as a defamatory statement of fact versus protected opinion may properly reflect community standards. Jury decisions distribute the adjudicatory function across a cross-section of the community, enhancing legitimacy and reducing the appearance of judicial bias. In cases involving accusations of corporate fraud and media investigations, a jury verdict may carry more public credibility than a bench decision.

## 2016-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2016-assignment-03-model-answer-2.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 3 — Civil Procedure 2, 2016

### Question 1: JAMOL

The judge should have granted MIP's JAMOL. On a motion for JAMOL, the court views the evidence in the light most favorable to the non-movant (Outrage) and determines whether a reasonable jury could have found for Outrage (*Reeves v. Sanderson Plumbing Products*; *Scott v. Harris*). The court does not assess credibility and draws all reasonable inferences in favor of the verdict-winner.

Nevertheless, JAMOL is appropriate when the evidence is so one-sided that reasonable people could not disagree. Here, the defamation claim centered on the expert's statement that the BAM is effective in fewer than 50% of users. MIP's own internal documents — which the expert used as her source — show 70% effectiveness in clinical trials. While those trials are unreviewed, the expert's contrary interpretation of the same underlying data, without a peer-reviewed basis of her own, may be insufficient to defeat MIP's claim as a matter of law. Under *Celotex*, once MIP established a prima facie case through its own documents, Outrage needed to produce evidence sufficient to allow a reasonable jury to disbelieve MIP's evidence. The expert's reading of MIP's own data — essentially speculating that MIP's internal results were inflated — may not meet that threshold.

That said, the effectiveness of the BAM is acknowledged to be an "open question," and the expert had a basis for her opinion. A court could reasonably conclude that this was a close enough factual question that JAMOL was correctly denied. The argument that JAMOL should have been granted is the stronger position, but the denial was defensible.

### Question 2: New Trial

The judge should grant the new trial. The new trial standard — "great weight of the evidence" — is broader than the JAMOL standard. The judge may weigh the evidence and assess credibility to some degree. Here, the judge observed the trial and is well-positioned to conclude that the jury's verdict was against the great weight of the evidence.

<!-- page: 2 -->

MIP's own internal clinical trial documents suggest 70% effectiveness. The expert's disagreement was based solely on her reading of those same documents, without independent clinical testing, peer review, or other expert corroboration. The balance of the evidence arguably weighs in MIP's favor on the effectiveness question, supporting a finding that the expert statement was defamatory. The new trial should be granted.

### Question 3: Immediate Appeal

Outrage cannot immediately appeal a grant of new trial. The order is not a final judgment under 28 U.S.C. § 1291 — it simply directs the case to be retried. Outrage must wait for a final judgment following the retrial before appealing. The new trial grant is not immediately appealable as a collateral order (it does not meet the *Cohen* test because it is effectively reviewable on appeal from a final judgment). § 1292(b) certification is theoretically possible but unlikely to be granted here.

### Question 4: Jury — Concerns and Benefits

**Concerns:** The core dispute here — whether the BAM is effective in fewer than 50% of users — is a technical and scientific question. Jurors without relevant medical or statistical expertise may struggle to evaluate competing interpretations of clinical trial data. The danger is that jurors will decide based on narrative (a corporation vs. a public-interest watchdog) rather than the evidence.

The judge's summary judgment rulings highlight another concern: the judge found "lots of evidence" that Outrage installed recording devices but did not find it "convincing." On JAMOL, a court cannot make credibility determinations — but a judge would be able to do so in a bench trial, potentially leading to a more accurate result. The jury's inability to explain its verdict compounds this concern.

**Benefits:** The defamation claim involves questions about community standards for what constitutes actionable false statements versus protected opinion about a product's medical effectiveness. Juries are better suited than judges to determine what a community of reasonable people would understand as defamatory. The jury also provides a check on judicial power and brings lay common sense to bear on questions of reputational harm. In high-stakes commercial disputes involving potential fraud and public health, the jury's community-legitimacy function is valuable.

## 2016-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2016-assignment-03-model-answer-3.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 3 — Civil Procedure 2, 2016

### Question 1: JAMOL

The judge was correct to deny MIP's JAMOL. JAMOL is appropriate only when, viewing all evidence in the light most favorable to the verdict-winner (Outrage) and drawing all reasonable inferences in Outrage's favor, no reasonable jury could have found for Outrage (*Reeves v. Sanderson Plumbing*). The court may not weigh evidence or make credibility determinations on JAMOL.

The defamation claim turned on whether the expert's statement that the BAM is effective in fewer than 50% of users was false and defamatory. The true effectiveness rate is an open question. A reasonable jury could have credited the expert's interpretation of MIP's FOIA-obtained clinical trial documents over MIP's unreviewed internal figures. The jury also could have found that the expert statement was a defensible opinion rather than a false statement of fact, which would defeat defamation liability. Either conclusion would support Outrage's verdict. Because a reasonable jury could have found for Outrage on these grounds, the JAMOL was correctly denied.

### Question 2: New Trial

The judge should grant MIP's new trial motion. The new trial standard permits the court to weigh evidence and grant a new trial when the verdict is against the great weight of the evidence, even if a reasonable jury could technically have reached it. The trial judge has broad discretion and, having observed the witnesses and evidence, is in the best position to assess credibility.

On the record here, the expert's opinion rested entirely on her interpretation of MIP's own unreviewed clinical trial documents showing 70% effectiveness. Without independent clinical evidence or peer review supporting the sub-50% conclusion, the weight of the evidence favors MIP's position. If the judge concluded that the expert's methodology was unreliable or that the jury gave undue weight to Outrage's narrative of corporate fraud at the expense of MIP's actual effectiveness evidence, a new trial is warranted. The conduct-related claims (recording devices) resulted in summary judgment for Outrage; the remaining defamation question is narrow and technical. The great weight of the available evidence on that question supports MIP.

<!-- page: 2 -->

### Question 3: Immediate Appeal

Outrage cannot immediately appeal the grant of a new trial. An order granting a new trial is not a final judgment under 28 U.S.C. § 1291 — the case continues. Outrage must await a final judgment from the new trial before bringing an appeal. The order does not qualify as an immediately appealable collateral order because it does not finally resolve a separable issue, and review after final judgment is adequate. Interlocutory appeal under 28 U.S.C. § 1292(b) requires certification by the district court, which is unlikely to be granted for a routine new trial grant.

### Question 4: Jury — Concerns and Benefits

**Concerns:** A central concern is juror competence on technical scientific and medical evidence. The core question — whether the BAM works in fewer than 50% vs. more than 70% of users — requires understanding clinical trial methodology, statistical analysis, and expert credibility. Jurors without such background may substitute narrative sympathy for analysis.

The opacity of jury verdicts is also a concern. Juries do not explain their reasoning. Courts reviewing JAMOL or new trial motions must infer what the jury found, making error correction difficult. A judge could have been asked to make specific findings of fact, providing a cleaner record on appeal.

The trade secrets and trespass claims were resolved on summary judgment, meaning the jury only decided the defamation question. But the salience of Outrage's surreptitious recording activities — even though those claims were decided in Outrage's favor before trial — may have influenced the jury's assessment of MIP's motives and credibility unfairly.

**Benefits:** The defamation context involves community-norm questions: what reasonable people would understand as a defamatory statement about a product's medical effectiveness, and whether a public-interest organization's publication was made with the requisite fault. Juries are traditionally the appropriate body to apply community standards. The jury's verdict also carries democratic legitimacy that a bench verdict lacks. In a case pitting a corporation against a public-interest watchdog over product-safety speech, a community verdict may be more socially credible than a judicial one.
