## Assignment 1 (ungraded), Civil Procedure 2, 2017, Professor Jim Greiner

### Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** September 19, 2017
**Due date/time:** 9 am on Monday, September 25, 2017
**Word limit:** 900 words
**Time limit:** 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will choose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is ungraded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

<!-- page: 2 -->

**Suggestions for your answer:**
- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time.

---

### Background Facts and Law

*(Note: all of the following is fictional.)*

New Jersey uses the First Restatement for horizontal choice of law. Oregon uses the Second Restatement.

New Jersey law provides that forum selection clauses are void as against public policy and courts must refuse to enforce them. Oregon law provides that forum selection clauses are presumptively valid and courts must enforce them absent compelling countervailing circumstances. For purposes of this assignment, whether a forum selection clause is enforceable is a substantive issue.

Malfoy Industries, Inc. ("Malfoy") is incorporated in Delaware, with its principal place of business in Portland, Oregon. Malfoy manufactures automobiles. Its most popular product is the Comet 260 sedan.

Hermione Granger is a citizen of New Jersey. She purchased a new Comet 260 in 2015 in New Jersey. The purchase contract contained a forum selection clause (Paragraph 9.75) specifying that all disputes arising from the purchase must be filed in courts located in Portland, Oregon. The contract also contained a warranty provision (Paragraph 4.5) under which Malfoy warranted that the Comet 260 would be free from defects in materials or workmanship for the life of the car.

Farley Granger is Hermione's husband. He is also a citizen of New Jersey.

In June of 2017, Hermione was driving the Comet 260 on Interstate 5 near Eugene, Oregon. The steering mechanism failed, causing the car to veer off the road. The car crashed, injuring Hermione seriously. Farley was a passenger in the car and was also injured.

<!-- page: 3 -->

Hermione hired an attorney in Portland, Oregon. Her attorney advised her that Malfoy had breached Paragraph 4.5's warranty, and that this breach was actionable under a New Jersey consumer protection statute and Oregon product liability law. Her attorney further advised that Paragraph 9.75 (the forum selection clause) was valid. Malfoy, by contrast, believed that Paragraph 9.75 was unenforceable.

Seeking to litigate in a court it found favorable, Malfoy filed a declaratory judgment action in New Jersey state court in September of 2017. The complaint sought a declaration that Paragraph 9.75 was unenforceable under New Jersey law and that the venue and forum for any disputes should be in New Jersey.

Hermione's attorney, who also represents Farley, removed the case to the United States District Court for the District of New Jersey (USDC-DNJ), invoking diversity jurisdiction.

Hermione then filed a counterclaim in the USDC-DNJ asserting her warranty and products liability claims. Farley filed a claim in the same action asserting his own personal injury claims arising out of the same accident. Hermione and Farley each claim damages well in excess of $75,000.

### Assignment Questions

1. Malfoy moves to remand the case to New Jersey state court, arguing that there is no SMJ. Should the USDC-DNJ remand the case?

2. Assume Malfoy's remand motion fails. Malfoy then moves to transfer the case from the USDC-DNJ to the United States District Court for the District of Oregon (USDC-DOR). Should the USDC-DNJ grant the transfer? If so, what law will the USDC-DOR apply to Hermione's claims?

3. Assume instead that the case remains in the USDC-DNJ. Does the USDC-DNJ have SMJ over Farley's claims?

## 2017-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2017-assignment-01-model-answer-1.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 1 — Civil Procedure 2, 2017

### Question 1: Remand

The USDC-DNJ should deny Malfoy's motion to remand. Removal was proper if the USDC-DNJ has SMJ over the case. The case was removed on diversity grounds. For diversity jurisdiction: (1) complete diversity between the parties, and (2) AIC exceeding $75,000.

**Complete diversity (*Strawbridge*):** Hermione and Farley are New Jersey citizens. Malfoy is a citizen of Delaware (state of incorporation) and Oregon (principal place of business, under *Hertz* nerve center test). No party is a citizen of the same state as an opposing party. Complete diversity is satisfied.

**AIC:** Hermione claims damages well in excess of $75,000. Malfoy's declaratory judgment action concerns the enforceability of Paragraph 9.75 — the forum selection clause. The value of the declaratory judgment is the cost of litigating in an unfavorable forum, which is likely to exceed $75,000 given the underlying warranty and products liability claims. Alternatively, the court may look to Hermione's counterclaim to measure AIC. Because Hermione claims well in excess of $75,000, AIC is met.

The USDC-DNJ has diversity jurisdiction. Remand should be denied.

> *Professor's note: Good analysis of DJ value and AIC.*

### Question 2: Transfer

#### Whether to Transfer

The USDC-DNJ should transfer to the USDC-DOR under 28 U.S.C. § 1404(a). Transfer is proper if the case could have been brought in the transferee district and transfer serves the convenience of parties and witnesses and the interests of justice.

**Could the case have been brought in USDC-DOR?** Malfoy's principal place of business is Portland, Oregon. The USDC-DOR would have personal jurisdiction over Malfoy (it is at home there). The crash occurred on Interstate 5 near Eugene, Oregon — a substantial part of the events giving rise to the claims occurred in Oregon, so venue is proper under § 1391(b)(2).

<!-- page: 2 -->

**Convenience and interest of justice:** The accident occurred in Oregon. Physical evidence of the crash site is in Oregon. Hermione's attorney is in Portland. Malfoy's operations and most knowledgeable employees about the Comet 260 design are in Oregon. Witnesses to the accident are likely in Oregon. These factors strongly favor transfer.

Against transfer: Hermione and Farley are New Jersey citizens. However, their own attorney is in Oregon, suggesting they are already oriented toward Oregon litigation.

Transfer to USDC-DOR is appropriate.

#### Law Applied After Transfer

Under *Van Dusen v. Barrack*, when a defendant moves for § 1404(a) transfer, the transferee court applies the choice of law rules of the transferor court (USDC-DNJ, sitting in New Jersey). New Jersey uses the First Restatement.

**Enforceability of Paragraph 9.75 (forum selection clause):** Under the First Restatement, contract formation and validity issues are governed by the law of the place of contracting (*Albanese*). Hermione purchased the Comet 260 in New Jersey, so New Jersey law governs the contract's validity. New Jersey law holds forum selection clauses void. Therefore, Paragraph 9.75 is unenforceable.

**Products liability and warranty claims:** Under the First Restatement, tort claims are governed by the law of the place of the wrong. The accident occurred in Oregon, so Oregon law governs the products liability claim. The warranty is a contract claim; New Jersey law governs (place of contracting). The USDC-DOR, applying New Jersey First Restatement rules, applies Oregon law to the tort claim and New Jersey law to the warranty claim.

### Question 3: Supplemental Jurisdiction over Farley's Claims

The USDC-DNJ has supplemental jurisdiction over Farley's claims under 28 U.S.C. § 1367(a). The anchor claim — Hermione's warranty and products liability claims — is properly before the court on diversity grounds. Farley's claims arise from the same car crash, the same steering mechanism failure, on the same trip. They share a common nucleus of operative fact with Hermione's claims (*United Mine Workers v. Gibbs*). Supplemental jurisdiction is appropriate.

The § 1367(b) limitation: because the anchor is diversity, § 1367(b) bars claims by plaintiffs against persons made parties under Rules 14, 19, 20, or 24 where doing so would be inconsistent with § 1332's requirements. Farley is a co-plaintiff joining under Rule 20. § 1367(b) expressly limits "claims by plaintiffs" against persons joined under Rule 20. Because Farley is a New Jersey citizen joining against Malfoy (a DE/OR citizen), diversity as to Farley is independently satisfied anyway. But even if Farley's damages did not independently meet the AIC requirement, *Exxon Mobil Corp. v. Allapattah Services* held that § 1367 grants supplemental jurisdiction over the claims of co-plaintiffs who do not satisfy the AIC requirement, as long as at least one plaintiff meets AIC. Here Hermione's AIC is met. Farley's claim is properly before the court.

## 2017-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2017-assignment-01-model-answer-2.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 1 — Civil Procedure 2, 2017

### Question 1: Remand

The federal judge should not grant the motion to remand.

While Hermione filed for removal more than 30 days after the removable event, Malfoy filed for remand more than 30 days after Hermione's removal motion. As a result of Malfoy's late remand filing, the judge can only remand for lack of SMJ, and SMJ is most likely satisfied because the parties are completely diverse (*Strawbridge*, § 1332). Hermione has moved around a lot, and while she does not intend to return to New Jersey, she has not lived anywhere else with an intent to stay (*Mas*). She does not intend to stay in Oregon. So Hermione is a New Jersey domiciliary, Malfoy is a citizen of Delaware and Oregon, and the AIC ($135k) is sufficient. If Malfoy had filed for remand on time (within 30 days of removal), then the judge should grant the motion because Hermione breached the 30-day window and is a New Jersey resident.

### Question 2: Transfer

The judge should grant the transfer motion.

Hermione's transfer motion can only be granted if the lawsuit could have been brought in the District of Oregon at the time of the original filing (*Hoffman*). Suit could have been filed in Oregon: SMJ via diversity (see Question 1); venue is proper per § 1391(b)(2) because the car was purchased and the accident occurred in Oregon; and Oregon can exercise specific IPJ (FRCP 4(k)(1)(A)) because of purposeful availment and minimum contacts (*International Shoe*).

Public and private interest factors support the transfer. The NJ federal court would use NJ state choice-of-law rules (*Klaxon*). The accident happened in Oregon and the contract was signed in Oregon (the blood is there), so the HCOL analysis would require NJ to use Oregon substantive laws. It is better to have an Oregon court apply Oregon state laws. There is also a public interest in allowing Oregon jurors to assess the reasonableness of the warranty promise in the contract, to enforce Oregon state laws, and to evaluate the testimony of Oregon witnesses. Oregon has a greater interest in the dispute (Malfoy's customers are there). Private interest factors support the transfer as well. The transfer is not inconvenient to Malfoy (it chose NJ but has its PPOB in OR) and Oregon is Hermione's home state. Evidence is in Oregon, witnesses are in Oregon, and both parties own property in Oregon so the judgment can be enforced.

### Question 3: Supplemental Jurisdiction over Farley

Based on the cases and statutes we have read, a federal district court should not have subject matter jurisdiction over Farley's claim, joined with Hermione's, against Malfoy.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Farley brought state law claims, so diversity is the only source of SMJ. Farley and Malfoy are diverse parties (Farley = NJ, Malfoy = DE & OR, see Question 1), but Farley's claim does not have a sufficient AIC (only $50k). While the Constitution allows for minimal diversity and imposes no AIC requirement, § 1332 requires complete diversity (*Strawbridge*). It would make little sense to strictly require complete diversity — based on state citizenship — but allow one party to have insufficient AIC. A judicial system that allowed a federal district court to have SMJ over Farley's claim could incentivize gamesmanship. For example, a group of plaintiffs with small claims would be incentivized to find one plaintiff with one claim over $75k, just to get into federal court. The text of § 1332 supports that the federal court should not have SMJ: § 1332 requires that the "matter in controversy" exceed $75k and Farley's claim does not.

The text of § 1367 and judicial efficiency, however, do support that the court could hear claims brought by Farley and Hermione together. For efficiency, hearing the two claims together would require just one trial, one judge, one time that witnesses are in court, one jury, etc. And § 1367 states that the district courts have jurisdiction over related claims — here the claims brought by Farley and Hermione derive from the same contract and the same car accident.

However, efficiency is not an end in itself. Given that diversity jurisdiction is less relevant today than it was pre-Civil War (when we really worried about judicial bias against out-of-state parties), the FRCP should be read to limit — rather than expand — diversity jurisdiction.

## 2017-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2017-assignment-01-model-answer-3.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 1 — Civil Procedure 2, 2017

### Question 1: Remand

No. The 30-and-30 rule was violated in both instances. Hermione filed to remove outside the 30-day allotted window, so normally the motion to remand would be granted. But Malfoy moved to remand more than 30 days after Hermione's removal motion, so no remand. The federal court could still remand for lack of SMJ. No AUJ exists — this is a state law cause of action. Looking to diversity jurisdiction: AIC is over $75k. There is complete diversity — Malfoy's PPOB is in Oregon and Hermione is domiciled in NJ — despite not having lived there since high school, she has never moved to another location with the intent to stay there. SMJ is proper under § 1332.

### Question 2: Transfer

Federal district court transfers are governed by the transfer statutes.

**Step 1 — Could the case have been brought in Oregon?** Per *Hoffman*, the statutes are limited by whether the plaintiff could have brought the case in the transferee court in the first place, so we must determine whether the court would have IPJ and whether venue could be properly laid.

For IPJ, a federal court piggy-backs on the long-arm statute of the state in which it sits. Oregon's long-arm statute goes to the limits of the Constitution. There is no general IPJ over the defendant because Hermione is not domiciled in Oregon. For specific IPJ, there must be minimum contacts between the defendant, the forum, and the subject matter of the litigation. Here, the defendant purposefully availed herself of the resources of Oregon by purchasing the car there and the accident occurred there, so minimum contacts are likely sufficient. The plaintiff could have brought the case against Malfoy in Oregon.

Turning to venue — venue is properly laid under § 1391(b)(2) since Oregon is the district in which a substantial part of events giving rise to the claim occurred: the car was bought there and the accident occurred there.

The lawsuit *could* have been brought in Oregon.

**Step 2 — Should the case be transferred?** Using a § 1404(a) analysis, going through public and private factors. The presumption of plaintiff's choice of forum can only be overcome by a clear outweighing of factors.

Public factors: hearing the case in NJ would add a jury duty burden for NJ residents for an accident that occurred in Oregon due to a sale in Oregon — weighs in favor of transfer; Oregon has a local interest in ensuring that cars being sold in the state are not defective and dangerous to consumers — weighs in favor of transfer; possible docket congestion for either court, but the prompt does not indicate whether either court is particularly congested, so no bearing on this decision.

Private factors: more access to witnesses in Oregon since that is where the accident and sale occurred — weighs in favor of transfer; less cost to defendants for travel and out-of-state attorneys, more convenient for Malfoy since its PPOB is in Oregon — weighs in favor of transfer; possibility to view the accident scene/wreckage located there — weighs in favor of transfer.

**Answer:** The case could have been brought in Oregon and should be transferred there since local interests, convenience of evidence, witnesses, and cost all weigh against the presumption of plaintiff's forum.

<!-- page: 2 -->

### Question 3: Supplemental Jurisdiction over Farley

The district court should not have SMJ to hear Farley's claim against Malfoy alone because, although there would be complete diversity, the AIC requirement for diversity jurisdiction is not met. The court must therefore consider whether it can exercise supplemental jurisdiction under § 1367 to hear Farley's claim together with Hermione's.

Section 1367(a) says that all claims that form the same case or controversy are permissible, but § 1367(b) narrows that to disallow claims that would frustrate the jurisdictional requirements of § 1332. Section 1332 requires both complete diversity and AIC. It is clear that diversity is maintained if Farley's claim is allowed, but AIC is less clear. On its own, Farley's claim is below the $75k AIC amount at $50k. The question is therefore whether a supplemental claim under a diversity action must meet both the complete diversity and AIC requirements of § 1332, or whether it is sufficient that both claims together meet the requirements.

The purpose of diversity jurisdiction is to prevent home cooking, and the AIC requirement is merely a way of weeding out complaints that are relatively trivial, preserving the state/federal division of labor and preventing federal courts from being overcrowded. Since the claim would be supplemental to a claim that already meets diversity jurisdiction requirements, it would not greatly increase the work of the court for a merely trivial amount alone. The total of both Farley's and Hermione's claims exceeds the AIC requirement, preserving the § 1332 requirements if both plaintiffs are considered together. Furthermore, it would frustrate efficiency to have a state court hear the entire case again to decide Farley's claim, and inconsistent verdicts in state and federal court risk judicial inequity. Therefore, the claim should be allowed.

---

### Background Facts and Law

*(Note: all of the following is fictional.)*

Alabama law prohibits the enforcement of forum selection clauses. Under Alabama law, a clause in a contract that purports to limit the amount of damages that a plaintiff can recover is per se invalid. Under New York law, Ontario law, and federal common law, forum selection clauses are valid, and there is no prohibition in either jurisdiction against contract clauses limiting damages.

**Note:** You may assume in answering this question that the validity of damages-limiting clauses in contracts is a substantive issue for vertical choice of law (*Erie*-analysis) purposes but is procedural for horizontal choice of law (state-state) purposes. Thus, the issue of the validity of a damages-limiting clause is just like the applicability of state statutes of limitation periods in federal court diversity cases.

Quality Quidditch Supplies, Inc. ("QQS") is an Alabama corporation with its principal place of business in Tuscaloosa, Alabama. (Note: the federal court whose geographic jurisdiction includes Tuscaloosa is the United States District Court for the Northern District of Alabama.) QQS manufactures high-quality racing broomsticks for Quidditch players around the world. QQS's motto is, "QQS: When jet speed won't cut it."

In September of 2016, QQS entered into two contracts, both negotiated and signed at QQS's Tuscaloosa offices.

The first contract was with Slytherin Straw Suppliers, Inc. ("SSS"), a Delaware corporation with its principal place of business in Manhattan (the federal district court having jurisdiction over Manhattan is the Southern District of New York). In this first contract, SSS agreed to supply QQS with top-notch, intensely magical, thick straw for QQS's racing brooms. Prices and supply dates were set in the contract, with the baseline price of $500,000.00 per ton of magical straw. The QQS-SSS contract included the following boilerplate clauses:

> Paragraph 9.75: The parties agree that any litigation relating to the subject matter of this contract shall take place in the United States District Court for the Southern District of New York.
>
> . . .
>
> Paragraph 713: The parties agree that under no circumstances will any recovery for breach of this agreement exceed the sum of $100,000.00 per shipment.

The second contract was with Hufflepuff Handles, Inc. ("HH"), an Ontario corporation with its principal place of business in Ontario, Canada. In this second contract, HH agreed to supply QQS with intensely magical broomstick-quality wood. The baseline price was $200,000.00 per ton. The QQS-HH contract included the following boilerplate clause:

> Paragraph 12: The parties agree that any litigation relating to the subject matter of this contract shall take place in a court located in Ontario, Canada.

<!-- page: 3 -->

In May of 2017, QQS took delivery of one ton of magical straw from SSS and one ton of magical wood from HH. Upon attempting to incorporate the straw and the wood into a new line of Firebolt 8s, QQS concluded that both the straw and the wood were defective. In fact, neither the straw nor the wood had any magical qualities, rendering both useless for QQS's purposes. Further investigation revealed that the evil Lord Voldemort had purchased both SSS and HH in December of 2016, and had attempted to sabotage QQS's operation by causing each company to produce inferior goods.

To maintain its production schedule for its Firebolt 8s, QQS turned to alternate suppliers. QQS obtained a replacement ton of magical straw and a replacement ton of magical wood from Lovely Longbottom, Inc. The straw cost $700,000.00, and the wood cost $450,000.00.

In August of 2017, QQS filed two lawsuits, both in Alabama state court located in Tuscaloosa. In the first lawsuit, the only defendant was SSS, and the only cause of action asserted was breach of contract. QQS sought $200,000.00 ($700,000.00 - $500,000.00) for the alleged breach. In the second lawsuit, the only defendant was HH, the only cause of action asserted was breach of contract, and the amount sought was $250,000.00 ($450,000.00 - $200,000.00).

Within two weeks, both defendants had removed their respective lawsuits to the United States District Court for the Northern District of Alabama.

In mid-September 2017, both defendants filed motions in their respective lawsuits. SSS moved to transfer the case against it to the United States District Court for the Southern District of New York, pointing to the forum selection clause in the QQS-SSS contract. HH moved to dismiss the case against it on the grounds of forum non conveniens, citing *Atlantic Marine* and pointing to the forum selection clause in the QQS-HH contract.

You are a law clerk for the Northern District of Alabama federal judge handling both lawsuits.

### Assignment Questions

1. With respect to the QQS-SSS lawsuit:
   a. Should the transfer motion be granted?
   b. If it is granted, is it likely that QQS's recovery will be limited to $100,000.00?

2. With respect to the QQS-HH lawsuit, should HH's forum non conveniens motion be granted?

## 2017-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2017-assignment-02-model-answer-1.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 2 — Civil Procedure 2, 2017

### Question 1(a): Transfer Motion — QQS v. SSS

The transfer motion should be granted. This case is properly in federal court because there is complete diversity between the parties and there is only one claim, with damages requested above the $75,000 amount-in-controversy requirement.

Pursuant to the Supreme Court's opinion in *Stewart*, a federal court looks to the transfer statutes to decide the transfer motion despite a state prohibition on forum selection clauses — i.e., under an *Erie* analysis, the transfer statutes are valid and applicable. In doing a transfer statute analysis under *Atlantic Marine*, no weight is given to private party interests when there is a forum selection clause, since the plaintiff already expressed a private interest in the transferee forum by signing the contract. No strong public policy considerations weigh against the transfer; it is a dispute between two private parties based on a contract negotiated only with one another. The court should approve the transfer.

### Question 1(b): Damages Cap After Transfer

QQS's recovery will probably be limited to $100,000.00. Under *Atlantic Marine*, after transferring to a location selected by a forum selection clause, we conduct the HCOL analysis from the perspective of the transferee court (here, SDNY in New York). The HCOL rules of New York apply (*Ferens*). According to the prompt, the validity of recovery-limiting clauses is procedural for HCOL purposes, so it does not matter which restatement New York uses or which state's substantive laws will ultimately govern the merits; New York's procedural law governs this issue. New York law and federal common law both enforce contract clauses limiting damages. Therefore, the court will likely apply New York law and enforce the clause limiting damages. QQS's recovery will probably be limited to $100,000.00.

### Question 2: Forum Non Conveniens — QQS v. HH

There is no Supreme Court case directly on point, so we do an *Erie* analysis to decide whether to follow state law or federal law. Forum non conveniens is a federal common law doctrine, so under *Hanna* (any version), we consider the twin aims of *Erie*.

Enforcing state law might encourage plaintiffs who have signed forum selection clauses to file in states that do not enforce them, but applying federal law would encourage parties to choose a federal forum simply because it will override state law. This suggests using state law. Moreover, since contracts signed in Alabama would be subject to different laws depending on whether the case is in state or federal court, there would be inequitable administration of laws if federal law is applied — which again suggests using state law under the *Hanna* majority test.

Under *Harlan in Hanna*, the court asks whether the rule affects parties' primary conduct — i.e., their pre-litigation behavior. Prior to litigation, parties might choose in which state to negotiate and sign their contracts hoping that certain clauses are or are not enforced. This suggests the rule is substantive, pointing toward state law.

Under the *York* test, this affects the outcome of whether the motion would be approved, and since granting a dismissal might mean the case is filed in Ontario — which might have different procedures and laws — it could affect the ultimate outcome. Under *York*'s permissive test, this is probably substantive, suggesting state law.

The court should apply Alabama law and find the forum selection clause invalid for forum non conveniens purposes. With the forum selection clause invalid, there is little reason to dismiss the case. The other FNC factors do not weigh in favor of dismissal; QQS filed in Alabama, has witnesses in Alabama, and the contract was negotiated in Alabama. This suit should remain in the Alabama federal court. If the court determines that the issue of forum selection clauses in a forum non conveniens motion is procedural for VCOL purposes, then under federal common law it should enforce the forum selection clause and dismiss the case on forum non conveniens grounds.

## 2017-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2017-assignment-02-model-answer-2.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 2 — Civil Procedure 2, 2017

### Question 1(a): Transfer Motion — QQS v. SSS

The transfer should be granted. The court will find no reason to sua sponte remand. SMJ is established by diversity (the AIC is easily sufficient).

The federal transfer statutes govern the district court's disposition of forum selection clauses, not state law (*Stewart*). Despite the apparent vertical conflict of laws, the court should apply the transfer statutes. Further, federal courts will enforce valid forum selection clauses except in exceptional cases (*Atlantic Marine*). Courts will consider the public interest factors, but these are rarely enough to invalidate a clause. The understanding is that the plaintiff has already waived private interest factors by entering into a contract with the clause. There is nothing exceptional about the public interest factors in this case.

As venue and personal jurisdiction exist in SDNY, the original lawsuit could have been brought in the transferee court and therefore the transfer is granted.

### Question 1(b): Damages Cap After Transfer

Yes, the recovery will likely be limited. Following the transfer, the NY federal court will apply NY law (*Klaxon*). Instead of having to use the happy court rule, the forum selection clause results as if the lawsuit was originally brought in the NY court (*Atlantic Marine*). The prompt states that damages-limiting clauses are substantive for VCOL purposes, so the SDNY uses the law of some state. Within a HCOL analysis, procedural issues fall under the law of the forum. NY law considers damage-limiting clauses as procedural for HCOL and does not invalidate contract clauses. Applying NY law will likely result in limited recovery.

### Question 2: Forum Non Conveniens — QQS v. HH

No. The motion to dismiss should be denied.

While the federal transfer statutes do not apply to non-federal court venues, forum non conveniens still exists within federal courts (*Atlantic Marine*). Through federal common law, *Piper Aircraft Co. v. Reyno* — analyzing private interests of litigants, enforceability of judgment, fair trial, public interest — a court could decide to grant the FNC motion to dismiss. However, under state law a court does not necessarily need to grant the motion. State law prohibits forum selection clauses, the Alabama court has proper jurisdiction to hear the case, and can decide what to do with the motion. Since there is a conflict of state and federal law, an *Erie* analysis is necessary.

Following the *York* test: if the outcome of a suit would differ under federal versus state law, use state law. Here, the outcome would be different under federal law (grant FNC motion to dismiss) as opposed to state law (deny FNC motion to dismiss). Therefore, apply state law and deny the motion to dismiss.

*(Note: York did not specify whether the test pertains to deciding an issue or the case. The previous analysis was for deciding an issue — to grant the FNC motion. You could argue that the case might ultimately get dismissed under the application of state law for a different reason, and therefore the final outcome would be the same. If the outcome is the same, then federal law would apply.)*

<!-- page: 2 -->

Under the *Hanna* majority: since there is no valid and applicable on-point federal statute (*Atlantic Marine*), the court looks to federal common law (FNC and *Piper*) and considers the twin aims of *Erie*. If the federal law is against the twin aims (prevent forum shopping and prevent inequitable administration of laws), apply state law. Here, the difference between federal and Alabama law could easily cause an out-of-state plaintiff to choose state or federal court, depending on whether the plaintiff wants the forum selection clause to apply. This leads to forum shopping. Therefore, apply state law and deny the motion to dismiss.

Under *Harlan in Hanna*: Does federal law change the parties' pre-litigation conduct? Here, HH might not have entered into the contract if it knew that Alabama law would apply and invalidate the forum selection clause. HH might have been motivated to act because of federal common law, thinking it could move for a FNC motion to dismiss if litigation began in Alabama. Since it appears that federal law influenced the party's pre-litigation conduct, apply state law and deny the motion to dismiss.

## 2017-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2017-assignment-02-model-answer-3.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 2 — Civil Procedure 2, 2017

### Question 1(a): Transfer Motion — QQS v. SSS

The motion to transfer presents a conflict between Alabama law, which would prohibit the NDAL from enforcing the forum selection clause and transferring the lawsuit to the SDNY, and the federal transfer statutes, which would compel the transfer unless "extraordinary circumstances unrelated to convenience of the parties" clearly disfavor granting the motion (*Atlantic Marine*). Under *Stewart*, Alabama law will not govern, so the forum selection clause should be enforced and the transfer motion granted unless extraordinary circumstances involving public interest factors defeat the transfer motion. There are no extraordinary circumstances from the facts as given, and, regardless, public interest factors rarely defeat a transfer motion. The transfer motion should be granted.

### Question 1(b): Damages Cap After Transfer

Absent a forum selection clause, state law applicable in NDAL would also apply in SDNY under the happy court rule, because NDAL is a happy court (personal jurisdiction and venue exist due to contract negotiation there), per *Klaxon*. But because the parties are bound by a forum selection clause, the SDNY will not use the transferor court's law. *Atlantic Marine*.

Damage-limiting clauses are procedural for HCOL purposes, so the SDNY district court should use the forum's law. Since New York is the forum and New York law allows damage-limiting clauses, SDNY will enforce the clause and limit recovery to $100,000.

There are no vertical choice of law (VCOL) issues in this case, as federal common law and New York state law do not differ with respect to the validity and enforcement of damage-limiting clauses. Regardless, since damage-limiting clauses are a substantive issue for VCOL purposes, SDNY will use state law.

### Question 2: Forum Non Conveniens — QQS v. HH

A vertical choice of law analysis is required.

The federal transfer statutes only govern transfers within the federal court system. In cases in which a forum selection clause points to a foreign forum outside of the federal court system, the doctrine of forum non conveniens applies. *Atlantic Marine*. The QQS-HH contract's forum selection clause points to Ontario, Canada, so forum non conveniens applies.

However, the NDAL must decide whether Alabama state law or federal common law applies, since Alabama state law would prohibit the enforcement of the forum selection clause while federal common law would allow it. Looking to the *Erie* doctrine, under *York*: if the outcome of the suit would be determined differently based on the use of federal or state law, the court must use state law. As it pertains to the lawsuit itself, there would be different outcomes if state or federal law is used (i.e., whether the suit is dismissed or not), so the court should use state law. However, arguably, the outcome of the dispute itself (i.e., the breach of contract claim) would not be determined at this stage since QQS is still able to file the suit in a court in Ontario, Canada, which may be a reason for the court to use federal common law.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Under the *Hanna* majority (any interpretation of it): for conflicts between state law and federal common law, the court must look to the twin aims of *Erie* (preventing forum shopping and the inequitable administration of law), and if the use of federal common law would go against these aims, apply state law. The difference between federal and Alabama law would incentivize choosing state court over federal court and vice versa at the time of filing depending on the goals of the parties, leading to forum shopping. Additionally, inequitable administration of laws is likely to occur given the difference between the use of federal or state law. Accordingly, the court should use state law.

Under the *Harlan* concurrence in *Hanna*: the court asks if the use of state or federal law will cause parties to change their "primary conduct," i.e., their general behavior regarding their affairs. Arguably, parties may draft their contracts to include forum selection clauses requiring litigation in foreign forums if the court applies state law, or may forgo such clauses entirely if the court applies federal law. This test also suggests using state law.

---

### Background Facts and Law

*(Note: all of the following is fictional.)*

An issue that sometimes arises in the context of limitations periods is when the limitations clock begins to "accrue" or tick. In other words, suppose that a statute of limitations for a certain kind of tort is __ years. At what point in time does the __-year period begin to run? Broadly speaking, there are two general approaches to this question: the "misconduct rule" and the "discovery rule."

- If the **"misconduct" rule** applies, the applicable statute of limitations begins to run on the date that the act or omission giving rise to the claim occurs, assuming of course that some harm eventually results (or else there would be no tort). In other words, under the "misconduct rule," the limitations period begins to run on the date that the defendant commits the relevant misconduct — the date upon which the act or omission occurs — not when the plaintiff discovers the fact that an injury/harm has occurred.

- If the **"discovery rule"** applies, in contrast, the limitations period begins to run instead on the date that the claimant first knows about, or first should have known about, the act or omission giving rise to the claim. In other words, under the "discovery rule," the limitations period begins to run on the date that the plaintiff first discovers, or should have discovered, that the defendant committed some kind of misconduct. This might be when some hidden injury or disease first manifests itself.

To illustrate the difference between the "misconduct rule" and the "discovery rule": Suppose a patient visits the doctor on January 2, 1996. The doctor tells the patient that she is healthy. In fact, a non-negligent doctor would have diagnosed the patient with a serious illness. The patient does not find out that she in fact has been suffering all along from a serious illness until she visits a second doctor on May 4, 1999. Under the "misconduct rule," the applicable limitations period began to run on January 2, 1996. Under the "discovery rule," the applicable limitations period might not begin to run until May 4, 1999.

Federal courts, as a matter of federal common law, have long used the discovery rule. The states of Maine and Nebraska use the misconduct rule.

<!-- page: 3 -->

The Nebraska limitations period for suits alleging negligent driving of a motor vehicle is two years. The Maine limitations period for suits alleging negligent driving of a motor vehicle is three years. Both states have long-arm statutes that go to the limits of the U.S. Constitution.

Both Nebraska and Maine have rules of civil procedure identical to the federal rules, and their state high courts have handed down opinions interpreting their state rules as requiring plaintiffs' complaints to meet the plausibility pleading test identified in recent United States Supreme Court case law. Under both states' laws, the elements of the tort of negligence are that the defendant owed a duty of due care to the plaintiff, that the defendant breached that duty by failing to act with due care, and that the breach caused injury suffered by the plaintiff.

In 2011, Congress passed the "Federal Limitations Period Act of 2011" (the "FLPA"). In relevant part, the FLPA provides as follows:

> In any action in a federal court in which the plaintiff alleges damages resulting from the negligent operation of a motor vehicle, the applicable limitations period shall be one year.

On March 1, 2016, Ginny Weasley and Harry Potter, both residents and domiciliaries of Nebraska, left their home in Omaha on a driving trip to Portland, Maine. Both were beer fanatics, and they hoped to sample some of the state of Maine's local brews. When they arrived in Portland, they did in fact sample, and sample somewhat liberally. On March 15, 2016, Ginny and Harry attended a local beer festival. On the way back to their hotel, outside 12 Grimmauld Place, in Portland, their car hit Aberforth Dumbledore, a Maine domiciliary, who was crossing the street with his herd of goats. Ginny and Harry stumbled out of their car to see if Aberforth was hurt. At the time, Aberforth reported that he was not hurt, but Ginny and Harry gave Aberforth their contact information in case things turned out badly. They told Aberforth that Ginny was driving. When they got back to their hotel, however, Ginny and Harry resolved to have nothing to do with Aberforth if he contacted them.

In the following weeks, Aberforth felt no discomfort. In late April of 2017, however, Aberforth visited his personal physician. The physician noticed an unusual swelling on Aberforth's left hip. Subsequent examination revealed that Aberforth had a life-threatening degeneration of his hip vasculature. The physician said that the degeneration was most likely caused by trauma to the area, such as might occur in a car accident. Surgery was immediately performed, and Aberforth racked up $150,000 in medical, physical therapy, and medication bills.

After unsuccessful attempts to contact Ginny, on August 12, 2017, Aberforth sued her in the United States District Court for the District of Nebraska. Aberforth's complaint was as follows:

<!-- page: 4 -->

[Case caption omitted]

**Complaint for Negligence**

1. Defendant Ginevra Weasley is a citizen of the State of Nebraska. Plaintiff Aberforth Dumbledore is a citizen of the state of Maine. The amount in controversy, without interest and costs, exceeds the sum or value specified by 28 U.S.C. § 1332.
2. On March 15, 2016, on the street outside 12 Grimmauld Place, Portland, ME, the defendant negligently drove a car while intoxicated, and as a result of this intoxication, hit the plaintiff with the car.
3. As a result of the collision, the plaintiff suffered damage to his hip, lost wages and income, suffered physical and mental pain, and incurred medical expenses of $150,000.

Therefore, the plaintiff demands judgment against the defendant for $150,000, plus costs.

August 12, 2017 [Signature]

---

Aberforth arranged service on Ginny by hand in Omaha. Immediately after service, before Ginny had time to respond to the complaint, Aberforth moved to transfer the case to the United States District Court for the District of Maine. The Nebraska federal court granted the motion immediately, and the case was transferred to Maine Federal District Court.

Ginny filed a motion to dismiss the complaint in Maine District Court on the grounds that it was filed outside the applicable limitations period and that it failed to state a claim.

### Assignment Questions

**Question 1:** You are the law clerk for the Maine District Court judge, who asks for your recommendation regarding whether the complaint should be dismissed. What recommendation do you make? Explain.

Suppose the Maine District Court denied the motion to dismiss. The case proceeded to a jury trial on an accelerated schedule. At the trial, Ginny testified that although Harry and she had told Aberforth that she had been driving the car on the night of the accident, in fact, Harry had been driving. Ginny testified that, immediately after the accident, Harry and she had agreed that they would lie to Aberforth because Harry had been drinking, and should not have been driving. On October 3, the jury rendered a verdict for Ginny. In response to special interrogatory questions, the jury found that Harry, not Ginny, had been driving the car on the relevant day. The court entered judgment in accordance with the jury's verdict.

**Question 2:** If Aberforth now sues Harry in Maine Federal Court for negligence in driving the car while drunk, can Aberforth invoke issue preclusion so as to estop Harry from denying that he was the driver of the car? Does it matter whether federal law, Nebraska law, or Maine law is used to answer this question?

## 2017-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2017-assignment-03-model-answer-1.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 3 — Civil Procedure 2, 2017

### Question 1: Motion to Dismiss

The complaint should not be dismissed.

**Preliminaries**

The Nebraska district court (DCNE) has SMJ via diversity: Ginny = NE, Aberforth = ME, AIC = $150,000 > $75,000. There is no HCOL issue presented by this fact pattern: NE and ME both use (i) the *Twiqbal* pleading standard, (ii) the misconduct rule for SOL initiation, and (iii) limitations periods of two years or longer. IPJ and venue were proper in DCNE (general IPJ, FRCP 4(k)(1)(A), § 1391(b)(1)). But had there been an IPJ or venue problem, the Maine district court (DCME) is a happy court. ME's long-arm statute goes to the limits of the Constitution. Ginny has minimum contacts with ME. She purposely availed herself of Maine by driving there and enjoying the state's protections. She also sampled local brews and attended a beer festival in ME, both connected to the accident leading to Aberforth's claim. It is also fair and reasonable to allow IPJ: it is convenient for Aberforth; ME has an interest in promoting road safety; the accident happened in ME so evidence and witnesses are there. Venue is also proper because the accident happened in ME (§ 1391(b)(2)).

**Failure to State a Claim**

The complaint probably meets the plausibility pleading standard. First, conclusory statements should be disregarded. Here, "negligently drove a car" is conclusory. Nonetheless, the complaint presents enough factual content to plausibly show all elements required to state a tort claim: Ginny drove the car and hit Aberforth while she was drunk and shouldn't have been driving (duty and breach); Aberforth was injured (harm); and plausibly the injury was caused by the car accident (causation). Thus, DCME should not dismiss the complaint on Rule 12(b)(6) grounds.

**Statute of Limitations**

DCME must choose between the misconduct/discovery rule and the applicable SOL. Under the discovery rule, the filing is within both federal and state SOLs. Under the misconduct rule, the timing is beyond the federal 1-year SOL but within both state SOLs (NE = 2 years, ME = 3 years). Because DCME is sitting in diversity and federal law differs on both choices, there are two *Erie* issues: the misconduct/discovery rule and the applicable SOL length.

**State Misconduct Rule vs. Federal Discovery Rule**

*York*: If DCME uses the federal SOL (1 year from filing), the choice between misconduct and discovery rules is outcome-determinative — if the misconduct rule is used, the case would be dismissed; if the discovery rule is used, the case would survive. So DCME should use the misconduct rule under *York*. If DCME uses the NE state SOL (2 years from misconduct), the choice is not outcome-determinative, and DCME can apply the discovery rule.

<!-- page: 2 -->

*Hanna*: The discovery rule is federal common law. The court consults the twin aims of *Erie*. Because the discovery rule is generally more lenient to victims, the twin aims likely suggest using state law (the misconduct rule) to discourage forum shopping and avoid inequitable administration of laws.

*Harlan* probably suggests using the misconduct rule, because the state misconduct rule may affect people's primary conduct: a misconduct rule may reduce a tort victim's incentive to discover harms and file lawsuits early. It would likely also affect the business strategies of car insurance companies.

**If DCME applies the federal discovery rule**, SOL is not an issue, and DCME should not dismiss the complaint.

**If DCME applies the misconduct rule**, an *Erie* analysis is required to determine the applicable SOL length. The time between misconduct (3/15/2016) and filing (8/12/2017) is approximately 17 months — beyond the federal 1-year SOL but within NE's 2-year SOL.

*York*: Using the state SOL (either NE or ME) is outcome-determinative when compared to the federal 1-year SOL, so state law should be used.

*Hanna* (statutory track): The FLPA is likely valid — Article III and the Necessary and Proper Clause likely give Congress authority to enact it. The FLPA is also applicable: its plain text states that when the plaintiff alleges "negligent operation" of a car, the SOL "shall be" 1 year. The FLPA is directly on point and should be applied. *Harlan* probably would apply the federal SOL, as a choice of SOL length alone rarely affects people's primary conduct.

**Overall conclusion**: Only by mixing the state misconduct rule (for start date) and the federal 1-year SOL would the complaint be dismissed — a combination that no rational court should adopt. The misconduct/discovery rules are intimately connected to SOL length, so it makes little sense to create a hybrid by combining state and federal laws. The court will likely choose to apply either federal law or state law to both issues. Under either coherent combination, Aberforth filed the lawsuit within the applicable limitations period. The complaint should not be dismissed.

### Question 2: Issue Preclusion Against Harry

Aberforth cannot invoke issue preclusion against Harry.

Harry and Ginny are different parties. Because Harry did not have an opportunity to appear in court to defend himself in Lawsuit 1 (the Ginny case), Due Process forbids invoking issue preclusion to deprive him of his day in court. Additionally, Ginny is not in privity with Harry. There is no suggestion that Harry controlled Ginny or was otherwise identified with her. Although Ginny voluntarily agreed to lie, thus "assuming" Harry's potential liability, this is not the same as stepping into the "legal shoes" of Harry as required to establish privity.

The answer remains the same regardless of what law is applied. The focal point is not about preclusion doctrine per se — it is that Due Process requires a defendant to have a meaningful opportunity to be heard.

## 2017-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2017-assignment-03-model-answer-2.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 3 — Civil Procedure 2, 2017

### Question 1: Motion to Dismiss

This case should not be dismissed.

Since the parties are domiciled in different states and the amount in controversy is over $75,000, the Maine District Court can exercise diversity jurisdiction.

**Failure to State a Claim**

Dumbledore's pleading satisfies the standards of FRCP 8, *Twombly*, and *Iqbal*. The conclusory statement that Weasley was acting "negligently" should be struck pursuant to *Twombly*. However, Dumbledore's remaining complaint still pleads the elements of negligence. He pleads that Weasley owed a duty of due care because she was driving on a public road. He further pleads that Weasley breached this duty by driving drunk. Lastly, Dumbledore pleads that defendant's breach led Weasley to strike him, causing damages. If accepted as true, these facts would nudge Dumbledore's claim across the line to plausibility. Thus, this case should not be dismissed for failure to state a claim.

**Statute of Limitations**

We must also ask if this case should be dismissed because it was filed outside the statute of limitations. Under federal law, the statute of limitations is one year and begins to run upon discovery of the misconduct. Under both Nebraska and Maine law, the statute of limitations is longer (2 and 3 years respectively), but begins to run when the misconduct occurs. Because under either state law the filing would be timely and the misconduct rule applies, a horizontal choice of law analysis is unnecessary. However, if both the federal SOL and the state misconduct rule were applied, this case would not be timely and should be dismissed. If any other combination of law applies, it would be timely and may proceed. An *Erie* analysis is therefore necessary.

Under the *York* test, the state SOL would be applied. This case is almost the inverse of *York* — under the federal SOL (for which there was no statute in *York*) the case could proceed, while under the state SOL it could not. Because the SOL would be outcome-determinative, it would be treated as substantive law and the state SOL would apply. Under Justice *Harlan*'s concurrence in *Hanna*, SOL would most likely be procedural because it is unlikely to affect the pre-litigation conduct of the parties. While it is possible that it would change the pre-litigation conduct of large actors who regularly take part in litigation (e.g., insurance companies), that is not sufficient to make SOL substantive. Therefore, the federal SOL would apply. Under the *Hanna* majority, the Federal Limitations Period Act of 2011 is a valid exercise of Congress's Article III powers over the judiciary. And it is applicable because there is no way for both the federal statute and state SOL to apply at the same time. So the federal statute would apply, the twin-aims of *Erie* notwithstanding. Based on the precedent from *York* and the *Hanna* majority, the federal SOL should be applied.

<!-- page: 2 -->

We must also apply the *Erie* tests to determine whether the misconduct or discovery rule should govern.

Under *York*, state law would be applied. Under the state-adopted misconduct rule this case would be untimely and would be dismissed. Under the federally-adopted discovery rule this case would be timely and would not be dismissed. Because this difference is outcome-determinative, the rule is substantive and state law would apply. However, under *Harlan in Hanna*, the federal discovery rule would apply. These rules would impact the pre-litigation conduct of some large parties, such as insurance or investment companies, because they would face greater liability under the discovery rule (more timely litigation) than the misconduct rule. However, because the discovery and misconduct rules are generally irrelevant to parties' actions before they engage in misconduct that could give rise to litigation, it is unlikely that they would affect pre-litigation conduct. So the rule would be considered procedural and federal law would apply. Under the *Hanna* majority test, state law would apply. Because the federal rule is based on common law, we look to the twin aims of *Erie*. Allowing Dumbledore to apply federal law would encourage forum shopping, given the outcome-determinative difference between these rules. Thus, under *Hanna*, state law would apply.

Overall, the federal law should apply. But under either law, this case should not be dismissed. We cannot blend elements of state and federal SOL, which is the only way to render this case untimely. Therefore, Dumbledore should be able to maintain his suit.

### Question 2: Issue Preclusion Against Harry

No, this issue is not precluded. While the question of Harry's negligence was at issue and was decided in the prior suit, that decision cannot bind him in the subsequent case for two reasons. First, it is not clear whether that issue was necessary to the decision. While the jury did not have to find Harry negligent to rule in Ginny's favor, Harry's negligence was the only defense Ginny presented. Therefore, arguably, it was necessary to the decision of no damages. Even so, Harry was not a party in the prior suit, and he and Ginny are not in privity. Therefore, regardless of whether federal law or some state law applies, the issue of Harry's negligence is not precluded. To hold otherwise would deny him his right to Due Process.

## 2017-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 3 — Civil Procedure 2, 2017

### Question 1: Motion to Dismiss

**Preliminary: SMJ, IPJ, Venue**

Courts should dismiss for lack of SMJ. Here, § 1332 provides SMJ: Aberforth ("AD") is a Maine domiciliary and Ginny ("GW") is a Nebraska resident/domiciliary, and the AIC of $150k exceeds $75k. Ginny was personally served in her home state, establishing personal jurisdiction. Venue of the transferor court was properly laid as Nebraska is GW's state of residence (§ 1391).

**Limitations Period: Transfer Analysis and VCOL**

HCOL is moot because both Maine and Nebraska have adopted the misconduct rule and their differences in SOL length cannot matter as to which state's rule applies on that specific issue.

| | SOL Start Date | SOL Length |
|---|---|---|
| Federal | Discovery Rule (FCL): 4/2017 | 1 year (Statute) |
| Maine | Misconduct Rule: 3/15/2016 | 3 years |
| Nebraska | Misconduct Rule: 3/15/2016 | 2 years |

Because the suit was filed 8/12/2017, the only circumstance where the limitations period could result in dismissal is if we mix and match: using either state's SOL start date (misconduct rule) with the federal 1-year SOL. Under all other combinations, AD filed in time.

**Transfer/Happy Court:** There is no happy court/transfer/HCOL issue. Under either Nebraska or Maine law (limitations period length and/or start date), the result is the same: the lawsuit is timely if we use either state's law, and untimely only if we mix the federal limitations period with either state's start-date rule. Nebraska was a happy court (Ginny domiciled and personally served there, FRCP 4(k)(1)(A), § 1391(b)(1)), so assume Nebraska law applies.

**VCOL:** There are two *Erie* issues: the applicability of the federal common law discovery rule for start dates, and the FLPA 1-year SOL.

**SOL Length:**

| | If Nebraska's Misconduct Rule Applies | If Fed Discovery Rule Applies |
|---|---|---|
| *York*: apply state law if outcomes vary? | Outcomes vary → apply Nebraska's 2-year SOL | Outcomes don't vary → can apply Fed 1-year SOL |
| *Hanna* Majority (statutory): apply federal statutes where valid and applicable | Apply Federal 1-year SOL — valid (Article III ambit) and applicable (FLPA specifically addresses this fact pattern) | Apply Federal 1-year SOL — valid and applicable |
| *Harlan*: apply state law if primary conduct would be affected | Apply Federal 1-year SOL — the SOL length wouldn't drive pre-litigation behavior, unless it affects car insurance companies | Apply Federal 1-year SOL — same reasoning |

Given the above analysis, it seems likely that the Federal 1-year SOL would apply.

<!-- page: 2 -->

**SOL Start Date:**

| | If Nebraska's 2-Year SOL Applies | If Fed 1-Year SOL Applies (likely) |
|---|---|---|
| *York*: apply state law if outcomes vary? | Outcomes don't vary → can apply Federal Discovery Rule | Outcomes vary → apply Nebraska's Misconduct Rule |
| *Hanna* Majority (FCL): apply state law if doing so serves twin aims of *Erie* | Apply Nebraska's Misconduct Rule — discourages forum shopping and inequitable administration by aligning outcomes across fora | Apply Nebraska's Misconduct Rule — same rationale |
| *Harlan*: apply state law if primary conduct would be affected | Apply Federal Discovery Rule — when SOL begins wouldn't drive pre-litigation behavior (unless insurance companies) | Apply Federal Discovery Rule — same reasoning |

Given the above, the *Erie* analysis suggests adopting the state's misconduct rule — but this is "Franken-law." Mixing the state's SOL start date with the federal 1-year SOL would result in a hybrid limitations period that conforms to neither state nor federal law. We should not recommend dismissal here.

**Failure to State a Claim**

Both states have adopted *Twiqbal* plausibility pleading. The analysis requires (1) striking conclusory statements from the pleading, and (2) assessing the remaining preliminary facts to ask whether they state a plausible case.

After striking the conclusory "negligence" language, the remaining preliminary facts are that GW was driving while intoxicated and collided with AD, causing him injury. Under both states' negligence tort theories, these facts if true satisfy the three elements and state a plausible case. We should not dismiss for failure to state a claim.

### Question 2: Issue Preclusion Against Harry

Issue preclusion requires: same issue; actually litigated; necessary to the decision; and mutuality/party identity.

- **Same issue:** Yes — who was the negligent party in the accident: who was driving while intoxicated.
- **Actually litigated:** Yes — specific jury findings via special interrogatories.
- **Necessary to decision:** Yes — GW's verdict depended on the jury finding it was Harry, not Ginny, who was driving.
- **Mutuality:** Depends on forum. Federal courts allow non-mutual offensive collateral estoppel (NMOCE) in limited situations where the defendant, who lost in L1, is precluded from re-litigating an issue (the posture is that the plaintiff changes from L1 to L2; the defendant is constant). Here, Harry was not a party in L1, so NMOCE would not be allowed.

But none of the above really matters: Harry (the potentially estopped party) was not a defendant in L1 and did not previously litigate the issue. It does not matter whether Nebraska, Maine, or federal law applies — estopping a non-party who was not in privity with a party would violate Due Process.

### Suggestions for your answer
