- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Applicable Law

In all jurisdictions relevant to this assignment, a tort exists called "fraud in the inducement to contract" (usually shortened to "fraud in the inducement"). The elements of a claim for fraud in the inducement are as follows:

1. a contracting party intentionally makes a statement (oral or written), or fails to make a statement, during contract negotiation with an opposite party;
2. such statement, or omission, is false or misleading;
3. such statement or omission is material;
4. the opposing party reasonably relies on the false or misleading statement or omissions by entering into a contract; and
5. the opposing party suffers damages as a result of entering into the contract.

Under Texas Law, the last event needed to make the tort of fraud in the inducement complete is that the plaintiff/opposite party suffers damages (which may be earlier than when she discovers the harm). Texas does not allow punitive damages for fraud in the inducement.

Under Louisiana Law, the last event needed to make the tort of fraud in the inducement complete is that the plaintiff/opposite party discovers that she suffered the loss or harm. Punitive damages are available for the tort of fraud in the inducement.

TX uses the Second Restatement for HCOL purposes. LA uses the First Restatement. Both jurisdictions' long-arm statutes go to the limits of the Constitution.

Case law in both Texas and Louisiana has established that, if a state or federal statute specifies that a contracting party must make a particular disclosure to the opposite party during contract negotiations/formation, then an omission of that disclosure, or a false statement made during that disclosure, is conclusively deemed to be "material" for the purposes of a fraud in the inducement tort.

Texas has four United States District Courts (Northern, Southern, Eastern, and Western). The Northern District of Texas is located in the northern portion of the state, and the primary courthouse is in Dallas. The Southern District of Texas is located in the southern portion of the state; the primary courthouse is in Houston.

Delaware has only one District, the United States District Court for the District of Delaware.

Louisiana has three United States District Courts (Western, Middle, and Eastern). The Eastern District of Louisiana is located in the eastern portion of the state, and the primary courthouse is in New Orleans. The Western District of Louisiana is located in the western portion of the state, and the primary courthouse is in Shreveport.

New York has four United States District Courts (Western, Southern, Northern, and Eastern). The Northern District is located in the Northern portion of the state, with its primary courthouse in Albany.

<!-- page: 3 -->

### The Truth in Bank Consumer Investment Accounts Act of 2012

In 2012, reacting to the Great Recession, Congress passed and the President signed the Truth in Bank Consumer Investment Accounts Act ("TBCIAA") of 2012. Section 9.75 of the Act provides as follows:

> **Section 9.75**
>
> Any investment account provided by a bank to a consumer shall be memorialized by a written contract signed by both the consumer and an appropriately empowered officer of the bank. If such investment account empowers the bank to invest the consumer's funds in bonds issued by business or corporations, such written contract shall specify the ratings level of bonds that constitute permissible investments, according to a large ratings agency.

No other portion of the Truth in Bank Consumer Investment Accounts Act of 2012 is relevant to this assignment.

### Facts

Gringotts Bank is incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Albany, New York. It has for some time offered investment accounts in which consumers could deposit money with Gringotts, which would then pool it with other such accounts for investment purposes. Gringotts' typical investments were corporate bonds. Gringotts has a single branch in Texas, located in Houston.

You are a lawyer in the office of general counsel of Gringotts Bank; your office is in Albany, NY. On August 17, 2018, you receive a complaint that was filed and served in the United States District Court for the Northern District of Texas, in the Dallas Courthouse. Service of process was completed on that same day. In the complaint, Alicia Spinnet alleged that the court had jurisdiction "pursuant to sections 1331 and/or 1332 of Title 28 of the United States Code," and that venue "was proper under section 1391." She alleged that she was a citizen of Texas.

The complaint further alleged as follows: On or about May 2, 2018, Ms. Spinnet visited the Houston branch of the defendant, Gringotts Bank, seeking an appropriate place to invest her savings. During her visit, Ms. Spinnet spoke to Gringotts Customer Service Agent ("CSA") Bellatrix Lestrange. CSA Lestrange suggested that Ms. Spinnet consider a Gringotts Grade AAA Investment Account. In the conversation, CSA Lestrange explained that the name "Grade AAA" referred to ratings applicable to the kind of bonds that an investment account of this type would buy. More specifically, CSA Lestrange explained, Gringotts Grade AAA Investment Account funds would be used to buy only bonds rated AAA by the Standard & Poor's ratings agency; AAA is Standard & Poor's highest rating, i.e., in the company's opinion, AAA bonds are the least risky available on the market. Ms. Lestrange further explained that Gringotts would take a share of the return on the investment as its fee, "So we will keep your money safe for you because that's what is best for us." Ms. Spinnet told Ms. Lestrange (using the words that follow, or words to that effect) that she was interested only in "extremely safe investments," and that "AAA bonds sound OK, but if y'all were going to use anything more risky, I'd rather stick with the savings account that I have. It may be boring, but at least it's safe." Ms. Lestrange responded, "The Grade AAA Investment Account is perfect for you, then. We're experts. You can rely on us."

After hearing these statements, Ms. Spinnet explained that she wanted to open a Grade AAA Investment Account with Gringotts, but that she did not have a transfer slip with her that would allow her to deposit the funds (which were currently on deposit from another bank). Ms. Spinnet stated that she was to travel the next day to New Orleans to visit a friend, and asked if she could deposit $150,000.00 at the Gringotts branch in New Orleans. Ms. Lestrange said that would be fine, but she urged Ms. Spinnet to execute a contract opening the Grade AAA Investment Account immediately.

<!-- page: 4 -->

So, on May 2, 2018, in the Houston Gringotts branch, Ms. Spinnet signed a contract opening a Gringotts Grade AAA Investment Account. The contract specified, "Funds deposited in this account shall be invested only in corporate bonds graded AAA by Standard & Poor's." Ms. Lestrange signed for Gringotts, as she was authorized by Gringotts policy to do.

On May 3, 2018, Ms. Spinnet traveled to New Orleans. It was her first time visiting Louisiana; she was there for a friend's wedding, and she stayed two days before leaving. While there, she visited the New Orleans branch of Gringotts and deposited $150,000.00 into her Grade AAA Investment Account, using a cashier's check from the other (non-Gringotts) bank in which she'd previously had a savings account.

In late May of 2018, Mr. Fenrir Greyback, a senior investment manager for Gringotts, using his authority as the investment manager for Gringotts, invested all of Ms. Spinnet's $150,000.00 in high-risk bonds issued by the Voldemort Corporation. The Voldemort Corporation runs oil rigs in the Gulf of Mexico near New Orleans. Standard and Poor's rated these bonds CCC, signifying that they were high risk and extremely speculative. In late June of 2018, several of the Voldemort Corporation's oil rigs malfunctioned, resulting in oil spills in the Gulf of Mexico. The Voldemort Corporation immediately declared bankruptcy, with liabilities (including those from environmental lawsuits) far exceeding its assets. The Voldemort Corporation's bonds became worthless. By email dated July 11, 2018, Greyback informed Ms. Spinnet as follows: "Due to circumstances beyond our control, your Grade AAA Investment Account balance now stands at $0. Because there are no funds on deposit, we are closing the account. We hope you have a nice day." Ms. Spinnet received the email in New Orleans on the same day it was sent; she had such a good time at her friend's wedding that she decided to spend a three-day vacation there.

At your boss's request, you hire a private investigator to do a little fact finding. A couple of days later, the investigator provides her report, which informs you as follows: Alicia Spinnet was born in Delaware, where she lived with her family until she entered college at the University of Houston. While there, she began playing online poker, and discovered that she was good at it. By April of 2018, in her junior year at the University of Houston, Spinnet had amassed a fortune of around $150,000.00 in poker winnings. On April 15, 2018, Spinnet wrote an email to Seamus Finnegan, her boyfriend since high school who still lived in Delaware, saying, "I love it here! I've thrown away my coat and all of my cold weather clothes. I have $150,000.00 in the bank from playing the online poker game that I learned how to play in one of my classes. The professor who taught me how to play poker says as soon as I graduate, she'll hire me as a permanent TA for the class. I'm shopping right now for a condo near my university dorm to buy after graduation. Texas has no state income tax! It's the best! Come live here with me, or we'll probably have to break up."

<!-- page: 5 -->

## Questions

### Question 1

Your boss asks you for a memo discussing any grounds Gringotts might use to try to get Spinnet's lawsuit dismissed or, in the alternative, transferred to a different federal district court.

### Question 2

Suppose the court transfers the case to the United States District Court for the Eastern District of Louisiana, New Orleans courthouse. If Ms. Spinnet is successful in proving her claim of fraud in the inducement, will she be able to recover punitive damages?

## 2018-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2018-assignment-01-bova-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

Probably can't dismiss on SMJ. There may be diversity: AIC is $150K (>$75K) and parties are citizens of different states (§ 1332); Gringotts is a Delaware and NY citizen, and Alicia could be domiciled in Texas based on going to college, substantial assets, job offer, shopping for condo, asking boyfriend to move there, and saying she'll never leave. However Alicia may still be domiciled in Delaware if she's not considered permanently moved (doesn't yet own the condo, hasn't started the job), in which case complete diversity does not exist. But there's still FQ-SMJ under *Smith*-*Grable*: it's a state cause of action, but there's a federal issue regarding the written contract and specification of bond rating levels under TBCIAA. This is a substantial issue invoking serious federal interests — Congress wants to have the power to address investment problems contributing to the Great Recession and better regulate investments. It's disputed because Gringotts will fight Alicia's version of the story to avoid paying damages. It won't upset the federal-state balance of work — many such cases could end up in federal court anyway under diversity jurisdictions due to corporations' citizenship, and fraudulent investments of this type aren't so common they'd overwhelm federal court system (compared e.g. to divorce). Though the federal issue isn't purely legal, the interest is sufficiently substantial to override this.

Can't dismiss on IPJ. Texas can't exercise general IPJ over Gringotts — Gringotts isn't a Texas citizen (it's incorporated in Delaware and PPOB is in NY). There's no evidence a large proportion of Gringotts' business occurs in Texas (only one branch there), so no general IPJ under *Daimler*'s "at home" principle either. However there's specific IPJ because Texas' long-arm goes to limits of Constitution, and there are minimum contacts linking Alicia, Gringotts, and Texas (Gringotts has a Texas branch, Alicia signed contract and opened account there). *International Shoe*. There are no problems of fairness/reasonableness because Gringotts opted to do business in Texas and thus "purposefully availed" itself of Texas law. *Hanson v. Deckla*.

Gringotts CAN move to transfer based on improper venue in NDTX. There's no venue under § 1391(b)(1) because NDTX courthouse lacks IPJ over Gringotts (no branch there, no contacts at all); nor under § 1391(b)(2) because no events happened in NDTX (no minimum contacts); nor § 1391(b)(3) because it could've been brought in another court that has both IPJ and venue (see next paragraphs). NDNY would fit easily because it's Gringotts' PPOB.

Gringotts could move to transfer to SDTX because IPJ is okay anywhere in Texas (see analysis above) and venue is proper under § 1391(b)(1) or § 1391(b)(2) — there are minimum contacts linking Gringotts to that district, and most of the events happened there. This transfer would likely be granted because witnesses like Bellatrix are there, and it's where Alicia lives so it's convenient for her.

Gringotts could move to transfer to EDLA because IPJ is okay (minimum contacts linking Gringotts, suit, and state since Gringotts has a branch there and thus "purposefully availed" itself of LA law, Alicia deposited money there, and Alicia received Gringotts' email there), and venue is okay under § 1391(b)(1) (again minimum contacts). This transfer would likely be granted because some of the events happened there, some witnesses are there (e.g. person who helped Alicia deposit the $150,000), and it's not too inconvenient for Alicia (it borders Texas, she's already traveled there).

Gringotts could move to transfer to Delaware or NDNY because they have general IPJ over Gringotts, and venue is proper under § 1391(b)(1) (Gringotts has residency in both districts). However transfer to either of these courts is NOT likely to be granted — they're inconvenient for Alicia (she has no links to either state), no events happened there, no witnesses there, and those courts would have to apply a different state's law (TX or LA).

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Question 2

Yes punitive damages if Louisiana law applies, no if Texas law applies. HCOL analysis is required. Under *Klaxon* federal courts normally apply COL rules of state where they're sitting, which leads to Louisiana COL rules. Under *Van Dusen* a transfer should be nothing more than a change of courts, assuming venue was proper in the transferor court (happy court rule). In this case, the transferor court (NDTX) was NOT happy because venue was improper (see above). Therefore the *Van Dusen* rule is not applicable here, and the EDLA court should apply the COL rules of state where it's sitting — Louisiana. Louisiana uses First Restatement, so we need to find "where the blood is" — where the injury actually occurred. Alicia learned of the fraud in Louisiana (that's where she received the email saying she had $0), so Louisiana law applies. Since Alicia did in fact discover that she suffered the loss/harm (that's what the email told her), the last element of fraud in the inducement in Louisiana could be proven. Thus Alicia can get punitive damages assuming she wins the suit.

## 2018-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2018-assignment-01-davis-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

**MTD FRCP 12(b):**

**SMJ AU:** No arising under the *Mottley* rule (must meet § 1331 and Article III) since no federal COA created by TBCIAA. If embedded in state action (fraud in inducement = FI) is substantial issue of serious federal interest (TBCIAA is embedded since its requirements impact statements being "material" for FI), court considers whether exercising federal jurisdiction over the state COA sweeps lots of cases into federal court, if clear issue of law or fact-bound/specific and if trust state to adjudicate. TBCIAA important (Great Recession) but would sweep too many cases into federal court if each time defendant violates federal statute, FI could be heard in federal court (*Merrell Dow*), so it is unlikely there is AU.

**SMJ DJ (§ 1332):** GB = DE and NY (PPBO = nerve center, *Hertz*), AS = TX (likely changed from DE to TX since in residence in TX and has intention to stay in TX — though attending school in state doesn't normally trigger change, fact that shopping for condo and has job for after school in TX indicates intention to stay in TX) so complete diversity likely satisfied. AIC met ($150,000 lost is greater than $75,000.00).

**IPJ:** 4(k)(1)(a) piggyback on TX long-arm goes to limits of Constitution. Minimal contacts between GB, forum and subject (*International Shoe*): minimal contacts = GB purposefully reached into TX by operating TX branch (*McGee*), which is where key events occurred (the solicitation of the business that gave rise to this lawsuit). TX exercising IPJ would not offend fair play/substantial justice (witnesses with knowledge include Bellatrix, the contract was signed there, plaintiff in TX who has interest in a convenient place to litigate, TX has a strong state interest in regulating conduct in its borders).

**Venue:** substantial part of events giving rise to claim occurred in SDTX whereas court in NDTX. GB not deemed to reside in ND since not sufficient contacts to subject GB to PJ if ND were separate state (§ 1391(d)) so venue not proper = grounds for MTD or to transfer. § 1391(b)(3) is not available because there is a district where the action may be brought that is a proper venue (ex: SDTX).

Based on the analysis that the venue in the ND of TX is improper, a motion to transfer under the Transfer Statutes would be approved if the motion was to transfer to a federal district court where the lawsuit could have been brought (*Hoffman* — where venue and IPJ — federal courts as a whole have SMJ). As venue is proper in SD of TX (all contacts discussed in IPJ section occurred in SDTX) and TX has IPJ over GB, motion to transfer to this court would be granted.

## Question 2

Since transferor court was not happy (*Van Dusen*, *Ferens*) (venue not proper), use COL rules of EDLA. Since issue of availability of punitive damages is substantive for HCOL, use COL rules of state in which court is sitting, LA, and since it is a first restatement state, look to where blood was spilt (know last event needed to make tort of FI in LA is that AS discovers she suffered damages — AS was in LA when received email from Greyback). LA allows punitive damages for FI. Assuming no MTD is successful and AS succeeds in proving FI claim, AS can recover PD.

## 2018-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2018-assignment-01-kulp-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

**SMJ:** DJ is possibly not satisfied. AIC = $150,000 (>$75,000) is sufficient. Gringotts is a corporate citizen of both DE (place of incorporation) and NY (PPoB — *Hertz*). However, Spinnet's domicile is unclear. Although entering college normally doesn't trigger a domicile change (*Mas*), in which case Spinnet = DE, Spinnet clearly intended to stay in Texas, even going so far as to throw away her cold-weather clothes. That being said, she hasn't yet acquired the permanent TA job and hasn't purchased the intended condo so her change is not yet definitively permanent. The court could reasonably go either way (Spinnet = DE or TX; if DE, no diversity).

There's no federal COA so likely no AUJ (*Mottley*). Is there a *Smith*-*Grable* exception? TBCIAA embeds a federal question into a state COA. Given the Great Recession, the effective promulgation of TBCIAA is likely of great federal interest. However, fraud in the inducement cases are fact-bound, and accepting *Smith*-*Grable* could sweep many state claims into federal courts and upset the federalism balance for state COA's. Nor does this have anything to do with TBCIAA's constitutionality (*Smith*). It seems that states can handle this effectively so no *Smith*-*Grable* exception.

**IPJ:** There's no general IPJ of Gringotts since only a small portion (one branch) of business in Texas (*Daimler*), but specific IPJ? Federal court "piggy-backs" on Texas long-arm statute that goes to the limits of the constitution (FRCP 4(k)(1)(A)), so we must perform a minimum contacts test (*International Shoe*). The fraudulent Lestrange conversation and contract signing in Texas should be enough to satisfy the minimal contacts portion of the constitutional test for specific IPJ. With regards to fairness and reasonableness, although Spinnet deposited the funds in Louisiana and Greyback's email came from NY, Texas has a strong interest in redressing conduct against its citizens (*McGee*). Besides, the email encouraged fraudulent

<!-- page: 2 -->

conduct and was directed to Lestrange in Texas, and funds entered the account which was opened there. Specific IPJ and thus IPJ is not a problem.

**Venue:** Houston branch (the only Texas branch) is in SDTX but the case was filed in NDTX. There's only "district-by-district" IPJ over Gringotts in SDTX (§ 1391(d)) because there's no residency in NDTX and no substantial part of events in NDTX. Because venue is proper elsewhere (*e.g.*, EDLA, see below), § 1391(b)(3) is unavailable. Therefore, venue is improper (§ 1391(b)) and the case should be transferred.

**Transfer:** A case can only be transferred to a court where it could have been brought (*Hoffman*). The case should be transferable to EDLA. Specific IPJ is likely, for: (i) Voldemort runs oil rigs near New Orleans which Gringotts knowingly invested in, resulting in damages, (ii) the money was deposited at a New Orleans branch, and (iii) Lestrange knew it'd be deposited there. It's reasonable that conducting business as such means that Gringotts purposefully availed itself of Louisiana's laws. Venue's probably proper in EDLA, as the aforementioned "substantial part of the events" occurred there (§ 1391(b)(2)).

Transfer will also be proper to NDNY and DDE, as there exists general IPJ (§ 1332(c)(1)) for Gringotts due to its incorporation in DE and its PPoB being in NY (*Hertz*). Furthermore, because the PPoB is in Albany (which is in NDNY), venue under § 1391(b)(1)/§ 1391(d) is proper in NDNY. Delaware has only one judicial district and since IPJ exists in DE, then so too is venue proper.

## Question 2

<!-- page: 3 -->

If transferred to EDLA, Texas COL rules won't follow since Texas court wasn't a "happy court" (lacked venue, see Q1). Use Louisiana COL. Louisiana is a 1st Restatement state, and so the actual realization by Spinnet of the sufferance of damages would determine which law to apply because it completes the tort under Louisiana law. Thus, if damages (loss of funds) were realized by Spinnet while in Louisiana, punitive damages are allowed. If while in Texas, punitive damages are not allowed.

Spinnet received the email in New Orleans on the same day it was sent and thus realized the damages in Louisiana. Therefore, if Spinnet's successful in proving her claim of fraud in the inducement, she will indeed be able to recover punitive damages.

## 2018-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2 (Graded)

**Civil Procedure 2, 2018**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** Oct 12, 2018
**Due date/time:** 9 am on Monday, Oct 16, 2018
**Word limit:** 900 words
**Time limit:** 2.5 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is ungraded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

### Suggestions for your answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Applicable Law

Please review Fed. R. Civ. P. 11(a).

Texas state rules of civil procedure include the following rule. TXRCP 11:

> **(a) Signature.** Every complaint or answer must be signed on each and every page by at least one attorney of record — or by a party personally if the party is unrepresented. Each such page must state the signer's address, e-mail address, and telephone number below the signature. The court shall immediately grant a motion to dismiss responding to a complaint that fails to comply with this rule, and must immediately grant a default judgment against a defendant whose answer fails to comply with this rule. The court may issue an order of dismissal or default sua sponte without notice to the party affected.

In the 2010 case of *Weasley v. Malfoy*, the Texas Supreme Court (the highest court in Texas) held that the purpose of TXRCP 11(a) was to force the attorney of record to review each page of the complaint for accuracy, something it doubted attorneys would do without the signature requirement.

Supposed that in January of 2013, Congress passed, and President Trump signed, the Diversity Limitations Period Act of 2013. It provided as follows: "In all civil actions in which a United States federal district court sits in diversity, the applicable limitations period shall be four years."

Texas has a 2-year limitations period for all negligence tort actions.

### Facts

On May 16, 2018, Padma Patil, a domiciliary of Oklahoma, sued Vernon Dursley, a domiciliary of Texas of Dallas, TX, in the United States District Court for the Northern District of Texas (which includes the city of Dallas). The complaint alleged that on May 12 of 2015, Dursley had played a drinking game at a bar for three hours before trying to drive his car home. On the way home, in the Deep Ellum neighborhood of Dallas, he had driven into oncoming traffic and hit Patil, who was driving her own car, causing her $200,000 in damages to her car and to her person. The complaint alleged that Dursley's drunk driving caused the crash and thus her injuries. Patil's lawyer, Aberforth Dumbledore, signed only the last page of the five-page complaint.

On May 30, 2018, Dursley's attorney moved to dismiss the complaint for failure to comply with Texas' 2-year limitations period and with TXRCP 11.

## Questions

### Question 1

You are a law clerk to the judge deciding the case. The judge asks you for a discussion of how she should rule on the motion to dismiss. What is your response?

### Question 2

Stepping out of your role as a law clerk: what do you think the answer to Question 1 should be, if you could arrange Erie law to your satisfaction? As you discuss, use some of the possible justifications of the Erie doctrine (theoretical, constitutional, statutory, and prudence) that we discussed in class. Are the two Erie issues in Question 1 different vis-à-vis these justifications? As you answer this question, use the cases we read and the principles we discussed in class.

## 2018-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2018-assignment-02-elshafie-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

I should advise that the case be not dismissed.

### 1. SOL

The Texas Limitation period has been exceeded, but not that of the Act. *Erie* is required to decide which applies:

**A. *York*:** Outcome determinative, so we would expect to use state law. No Act of Congress was at issue in *York*, however, so may not be applicable here. Or, because Congress is not subject to the "Rules Enabling Act," it may be that even if outcome-determinative, the Act should govern.

**B. *Hanna* Majority/Scalia:** The Act covers the point in dispute. It "represents a valid exercise of Congress' authority under the Constitution" (*Stewart*), because it falls under the power to create the lower federal courts and the "necessary and proper clause." The Act governs.

**C. *Hanna* Concurrence:** May affect pre-litigation conduct, because parties might adjust their life and investment strategies based on when lawsuits expire. On the other hand, limitations periods are also about when lawsuits must be filed, which might not affect how clients (as opposed to their lawyers) behave. The answer is unclear.

**D. *Hanna* Majority/Ginsburg:** The TXSOL seems to serve a state policy, and create a right "not to expect suits." But, the Act and TXSOL are not reconcilable, and Congress is not subject to the Rules-Enabling Act ("REA"), and does not seem to have exceeded constitutional limits. So, Act applies.

### 2. Signature

If TXR, then must dismiss. If FR, case may continue. *Erie* is required to decide which applies:

**A. *York*:** Outcome determinative, so, TXR probably applies. No FR was at issue in *York*, so a little unsure.

**B. *Hanna* Majority/Scalia:** Rule applies, because within REA and Constitutional Bounds. There is a conflict, because TXR presents an "additional hurdle." FR applies because the rule "really regulates procedure" (*Sibbach*) and does not, on its own, abridge or enlarge a right. It speaks to the issue at hand because it says that a signature is required, not a signature required on every page.

**C. *Hanna* Concurrence:** Would not have affected pre-litigation conduct (except, probably, for choosing a meticulous lawyer). FR applies.

**D. Ginsburg:** TXR serves an important state policy. FR and TXR may be reconciled, if we take the signature requirement of the FR to be the minimum required, which is supplemented by the TXR (*Cohen*). TXR would apply.

Thus, the case should not be dismissed, because plaintiff complied with the Act and Federal Rule.

## Question 2

I think the case should be dismissed for exceeding the TXSOL, but not for noncompliance with the TXR.

### 1. Limitation

There may be something to be said for bringing unity into the Federal system. Yet, such an Act presents a terrible alternative, both because it confounds *Erie*, and allows Congress unprecedented and dangerous liberty.

<!-- page: 2 -->

*Erie*, for all its "murky water," was justified as a means to prevent forum-shopping and the inequitable administration of the Law. Such a sweeping rule would close the Federal courts before plaintiffs, howsoever legitimate their claim or excusable their delay, compelling them to stand before a state court. Where a huge corporation is a defendant, for instance, we are left with something worse than the pre-*Erie*, *Swift* regime, because defendants may choose the state with the most favourable SOL, and plaintiff may not even appeal to the old "LAW" of *Swift*.

Furthermore, Congress is not bound "not to abridge or enlarge" substantive rights; and, should it be allowed to change procedure in such a fashion, is to engender a harm that nothing can remedy but a judgement of Unconstitutionality, applicable only to individual Acts. Any rule may be characterised as Procedural, and the Federal Courts are Congress's "creatures," and Congress may set their "house-keeping rules." Is it so inconceivable that, moved by no higher interest in justice but that of corporate lobbyists, Congress would make it much harder for consumers, say, to sue manufacturers by such apparently procedural "House-keeping" Acts? I think not.

### 2. The Signature

This rule presents the other extreme, and, I think, should not be allowed to govern in Federal Court.

While there is some plausibility to the reasons given by the Texas Supreme Court, applying such clearly procedural rules in Federal Courts deserves a special scrutiny. Could not imposing an "Anti-misspelling Rule" be justified on the same grounds? What about proper formatting? Paper size? This would bring us back to the time when Federal Courts had to use the procedures of State Courts, without the old advantage of knowing that this would always be the case.

By extending *Erie* so far, it seems that we also disserve its aims.

Between the Act and the Rule lies the virtuous mean; where the states may realise their substantive aims without wantonly claiming Substantive Purposes for clearly procedural rules, and where Congress may provide "house-keeping" rules for its courts without closing them before those for whom they were intended with "procedure" as its pretext.

## 2018-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2018-assignment-02-gallon-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

The motion to dismiss shouldn't be granted. SMJ is ok — no AUJ because there's no federal cause of action, but DJ is satisfied because there's complete diversity (PP = Oklahoma and VD = Texas) and AIC satisfied ($200,000 > $75,000).

Because court sitting in diversity, need to see 1) which limitations period applies and 2) if TXRCP 11 applies under *Erie*.

### SOL

The federal 4-year limitations period probably applies. Under *York*, the TX 2-year limitations period would apply because the limitations period determines the outcome of the litigation (if it applies → case dismissed; if it doesn't apply → not dismissed) and is therefore substantive so state law applies. Under Harlan's concurrence in *Hanna*, the 4-year period under federal law should apply because different limitations periods don't really affect pre-litigation primary conduct and therefore aren't substantive so fed law would apply. It's arguable that a limitations period might affect primary conduct like insurance coverage but it's more likely just time keeping. The *Hanna* majority and *Stewart v. Ricoh* point strongly towards applying the federal 4-year limitation because it is imposed by statute so 1) it's presumed valid and 2) it's applicable to the case because this is a civil action in a diversity court. Supremacy Clause states it trumps state law and therefore the 4-year period will apply. Both Scalia's and Ginsburg's opinions in *Shady Grove* would align with this.

### TXRCP 11

FRCP 11 probably applies. Under *York*, TXRCP 11 applies because what signature requirements are in place determines the outcome of the litigation (if TXRCP 11 applies → case dismissed; if FRCP 11 applies → case not dismissed). Under Harlan's concurrence in *Hanna*, FRCP 11 applies because signature requirements don't impact pre-suit primary conduct, so the rule is procedural and fed procedure rules apply. Under *Hanna* majority, because FRCP 11 conflicts with TXRCP 11 (TXRCP requires signatures on each page, FRCP doesn't), FRCP will be used if it's valid and applicable. FRCPs are presumed valid. Scalia would find FRCP 11 applicable because it "really regulates" procedure; we don't even need to look at TXRCP in this case. Signature requirements on pleadings are about as procedural as you can get and also don't abridge or modify any substantive state rights. Ginsberg would read FRCP 11 narrowly to accommodate the state law policy — here, to encourage attorneys to review pleadings before filing. Since FRCP 11 doesn't ban or even speak to additional signature requirements, she would probably find TXRCP 11 compatible with FRCP 11 and apply it.

Because both the federal 4-year limitations period and FRCP 11 probably apply, the motion to dismiss shouldn't be granted.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Question 2

Scalia's construction of the *Hanna* majority test leads to equitable outcomes in both federal-state law conflicts present in Question 1.

With respect to FRCPs: they should control, as long as they are valid and "really regulate procedure." FRCP 11 should apply in this case and all other cases. It "really regulates procedure"; it does not abridge or modify any state substantive rights and therefore also doesn't encourage forum shopping. It does not bar any potential plaintiff from vindicating their rights in court. It may entail marginal federalism and separation-of-powers concerns in that the federal rules override state rules in district courts sitting in diversity, but this intrusion on state interests is slight, and a bright line, uniform rule that procedural rules apply across the board is workable and efficient. In contrast, Ginsberg's approach to accommodate state law policy in whether or not federal rules should apply is unworkable. Different federal courts would apply their federal procedural rules differently depending on the states they sit in; whether federal or state procedures applies would depend on how well states articulate the state policy behind their procedural rules rather; and judicial economy would be diminished by potentially requiring the litigation of every federal rule with respect to every state policy. Similarly, *York*'s "outcome determinative" test should not apply, as basically *any* procedural issue — from signature requirements to the paper size of pleadings — could affect the outcome of the case and therefore be substantive. Therefore, as long as FRCPs just regulate procedure and don't alter substantive rights, they should apply.

Similarly, ultimately federal statutes, as long as they are valid and applicable, should trump state laws under the Supremacy Clause. In the present case, it's unclear if Texas's limitations period is set by statute, common law, or even state constitution, making this a slightly harder case. The preemption of state common law is more palatable than preemption of a state constitutional provision. But what is clear is that the federal statute implicates the justifications for why *Erie* was created to begin with. The federal statute undermines federalism by altering substantive rights set by the state, like when a plaintiff can and can't sue. Further, the statute undermines equal protection under the law and implicates forum-shopping concerns, affording some plaintiffs a much longer time to vindicate their rights than others. However, there are also much stronger constitutional concerns regarding the federal government's authority to promulgate laws that are necessary and proper to carry out its functions. Sometimes this will require state laws to yield to federal law and even lead to inconsistent results between plaintiffs depending on where they are suing. Yet this inconsistency and intrusion on state substantive law cannot and should not surpass the constitutional grant of authority to Congress nor the Supremacy Clause.

## 2018-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2018-assignment-02-li-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

There are two distinct vertical choice-of-law issues; applying state law in either results in granting the motion to dismiss.

### Limitations Period (SoL): DLPA 2013 (4 years) vs. TX (2 years)

The *York* test favors state law because the SoL would be outcome-determinative: TX law would bar but DLPA would permit the 3-year-old suit.

Harlan's *Hanna* concurrence test favors federal law because private individuals like Patil and Dursley are not changing pre-litigation conduct in any way based on SoL, though sophisticated business entities like insurers might act differently based on perceiving reduced litigation risk.

Scalia's *Shady Grove* plurality test favors federal law. The federal law is applicable: Congress, in plain language, intends to establish SoL covering all diversity actions, in direct conflict with TX law. The federal law is valid: Art. I § 8 of the Constitution expressly grants Congress the power to create lower courts and legislate as necessary and proper to do so.

Ginsburg's *Shady Grove* dissent test favors federal law. SoL might serve important state interests like providing finality in Texan lives and incentivizing quicker dispute resolution. Note, the DLPA is a federal statute, not an FRCP, so Ginsburg might think twice before trying to narrowly construe it. (But see *Gasperini*). However, it seems impossible to interpret out of a conflict in this case: TX SoL is 2 years, and DLPA says the SoL "shall be" 4 years.

### Signature Requirement: FRCP 11(a) (1 page) vs. TXRCP 11(a) (all pages)

The *York* test favors state law because the signature requirement would be outcome-determinative. The suit, with a complaint signed only on the last page, would be non-compliant with TXRCP 11(a) but fine under FRCP 11(a).

Harlan's *Hanna* concurrence test favors federal law because the rules deal exclusively with litigation conduct: the signing of litigation documents.

Scalia's *Shady Grove* plurality test favors federal law. The FRCP is applicable because it would be read to broadly control signature requirements. The FRCP is valid because Congress delegated authority to promulgate FRCP in the REA, and the rule doesn't abridge, enlarge, or modify any obvious substantive right — TX Supreme Court says the TXRCP is for improving complaint accuracy.

Ginsburg's *Shady Grove* dissent test favors state law. Ginsburg would credit the important state interest identified by the TX Supreme Court: more accurate complaints means better litigation and representation for Texans. To avoid finding the rule applicable, she would narrowly construe the FRCP as a minimum requirement — the "must be signed" language requires one signature — that doesn't preclude requiring more. She would thus allow the TXRCP to co-exist, layering it on

<!-- page: 2 -->

as an additional requirement by Texas, like what NJ was allowed to do with bond requirements in *Cohen*.

## Question 2

### Limitations Period

*York* is distinguishable because there was no federal statute. With the DLPA, Congress has spoken clearly on the precise issue, reducing concerns about separation of powers between branches of the federal government (i.e. the judiciary creating common law without congressional approval). Prudential concerns around judge-created common law overriding state law (e.g. with *Erie*) are absent. SoLs do not raise a serious federalism concern. The Constitution expressly grants to Congress the power to "constitute Tribunals inferior to the supreme Court" (Art. I, § 8, cl. 9) and the ability to "make all Laws which shall be necessary and proper" (Art. I, § 8, cl. 18) to execute its enumerated powers. By the Supremacy Clause (Art. VI), these laws trump individual state laws. The strongest constitutional challenge comes from equal protection concerns: a 4-year SoL for all diversity suits, if applied in Texas, favors out-of-state plaintiffs (ones with diversity of citizenship from their defendants) compared to in-state plaintiffs who have otherwise identical negligence tort claims. This encourages forum-shopping and bringing suit in federal court instead of state court. Ultimately, this seems insufficient to outweigh the underlying constitutional authority supporting Congressional authority for DLPA.

### Signature Requirement

With the REA, Congress delegated authority to promulgate the FRCP to the judiciary. The FRCP are a strange blend of statutorily-authorized common law. However, Congress's later refusal to do the same for the Federal Rules of Evidence (passed as legislation with significant changes) suggests an intention to curb the rule-making authority of the judiciary. Accordingly, when faced with conflicting state law, we should treat the FRCP as less authoritative than an actual statute and be more cautious in overriding state law. However, there is a reasonably strong federal interest in a uniform set of procedures in its courts. Uniformity of procedures allows for a common market of professional lawyers (government needs legal staff at DoJ / agencies to handle cases across the country) and makes it easier for repeat players (not only the government, but entities with a national presence e.g. big businesses, labor unions) to participate in the federal court system.

How does this compare to other conflicts between FRCP and state rules / laws? It is possible to follow what the Court did in *Cohen*, narrowly construing the FRCP as a minimum, non-exhaustive requirement and allowing TXRCP 11(a) to apply. However, it's difficult to take the TX Supreme Court's explanation of the reasoning behind TXRCP 11(a) seriously: introducing this additional procedural burden on attorneys is more likely to incentivize them to spend less time on substantively improving the document, or to pass on increased costs to their clients. Ultimately, I

<!-- page: 3 -->

weigh the value of having a uniform set of procedures in federal courts more highly and would uphold the FRCP.

**Civil Procedure 2, 2018**

This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

### Suggestions for your answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over a non-resident defendant only if the latter has 'minimum contacts' with the forum, and the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Applicable Law and Facts

New Hampshire and Maine each have only one federal district, D.N.H. (for NH) or D. Me. (for ME). Both states have long-arm statutes that go to the limits of the Constitution. Both have state rules of civil procedure identical in text to the federal rules of civil procedure. In the 2015 case of *Malfoy v. Patil*, the Supreme Court of Maine held that Maine, under the Maine rules of civil procedure, followed the federal plausibility pleading doctrine articulated in *Bell Atlantic* and *Iqbal* in interpreting the Maine Rs. C. P. 8, 12. In the 2016 case of *Longbottom v. Parkinson*, the Supreme Court of New Hampshire held that New Hampshire did not follow the federal doctrine articulated in *Bell Atlantic* and *Iqbal* in interpreting New Hampshire Rs. C. P. 8, 12, and instead continued to follow the notice pleading doctrine as exemplified by *Dioguardi v. Durning*.

You are a solo practitioner in Portland Maine. In September of 2018, Aberforth Dumbledore, a domiciliary of Maine, walks into your office. He tells you that on March 15, 2018, while wearing a bright yellow orange reflector shirt as he walked his dog, he was crossing the street in front of 12 Grimmauld Place, Portland at 10:30 at night. He heard a screech of tires and was then hit by a car hard enough to fall down. Two individuals got out of the car, later identified as Ginny Weasley and Harry Potter, with Ms. Weasley exiting from the driver's side of the car. Ms. Weasley stayed near the car, while Mr. Potter approached Aberforth. Aberforth detected a mild smell of alcohol on Potter's breath. At the time, Aberforth reported that he was not hurt, but Potter gave Aberforth contact information for both Potter and Weasley in case things turned out badly. The contact information demonstrated that Potter and Weasley were residents of New Hampshire. In the following weeks, Aberforth felt no discomfort. In late April of 2018, however, Aberforth visited his personal physician. The physician noticed an unusual swelling on Aberforth's left hip. Subsequent examination revealed that Aberforth had a life-threatening degeneration of his hip vasculature. The physician said that the degeneration was most likely caused by trauma to the area, such as might occur in a car accident. Surgery was immediately performed, and Aberforth racked up $150,000 in medical, physical therapy, and medication bills.

You do some investigation. You find from official records that Potter and Weasley were both born and raised in New Hampshire and that they attended a local high school. After graduation, they lived at the same address in Manchester, New Hampshire and continue to work there.

You decide to file a lawsuit against Ginny Weasley for negligently hitting Aberforth with a car. The elements of the cause of action for negligence are: that the defendant (i) acted in a way inconsistent with ordinary care under the circumstances, (ii) that the failure to act with ordinary care caused some accident or event, and (iii) that the plaintiff suffered injuries as a result.

## Questions

### Question 1

In what court systems/locations could you file the complaint? Which of them would provide your best chance of success? Why so?

<!-- page: 3 -->

### Question 2

Now assume that Aberforth sues Ginny in the United States District Court for the District of Maine.

Draft the operative paragraphs of a short complaint based on the facts as articulated above, in Aberforth's suit against Weasley. You need not include the case caption, a prayer for relief, or a signature block.

### Question 3

Continue to assume that Aberforth sues Ginny in the United States District Court for the District of Maine. He demands damages in the amount of $150,000, and no other form of relief. After discovery, Aberforth moves for summary judgment on the issues of whether Ginny was drunk and when she drove into Aberforth, and that her driving negligently caused her to hit Aberforth with her car. The district court grants the motion, reserving the issue of the amount of damages to be awarded for a later trial. Ginny immediately appeals the summary judgment grant the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit. Aberforth moves to dismiss the appeal.

Should the First Circuit grant Aberforth's motion to dismiss Ginny's appeal? Why or why not?

## 2018-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2018-assignment-03-ascher-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

SMJ, PJ, and venue are all proper in both D. Me. and D.N.H.

IPJ is OK in a Me. state court because it has a longarm statute going to the limits of the constitution, and because Ginny's purposeful availment action of driving in Me. and getting into an accident there provides minimal contacts and makes the exercise of jurisdiction fair and reasonable. IPJ is OK in N.H. state court because Ginny is domiciled there. IPJ is thus OK in N.H. or Me. federal court under FRCP 4(k)(1)(A).

SMJ is OK in any federal court because Ginny is domiciled in N.H., Aberforth in Me., and damages > $150K. Venue lies in D.N.H. because the defendant is a domiciliary of N.H. § 1391(b)(1). Venue lies in D. Me. because all of the events giving rise to this claim took place in Me. § 1391(b)(2).

This means I can file in federal court in either D.N.H. or D. Me. N.H. state court uses notice pleading (requiring only "a short, plain statement showing plaintiff's entitlement to relief," *Dioguardi*). Me. state and federal court will each use *Twiqbal*'s plausibility pleading standard. N.H. federal court will probably use plausibility pleading too: Rule 8(a) is a valid (see Rules Enabling Act) and applicable procedural law, so a court sitting in diversity will apply it (*Hanna*). Rule 8(a) = plausibility pleading (*Twiqbal*).

N.H. state court is the only forum where I can get notice pleading, which is good for me since it's a lower burden. And if I file in N.H. state court, Ginny cannot remove to federal court, so I definitely get the notice pleading standard. That said, I probably have enough underlying facts to satisfy plausibility pleading, and as a result I may wish to sue in Me. to avoid the possibility of home cooking in N.H. It's a close call, but I think the benefit of notice pleading outweighs the potential harm of home cooking — N.H. state court it is!

## Question 2

**Jurisdiction & Venue**

The plaintiff is a domiciliary of Maine. The defendant is a domiciliary of New Hampshire. The amount in controversy, without interest and costs, exceeds the amount specified by 28 U.S.C. § 1332. Venue is proper under 28 U.S.C. § 1391(b)(2) as all of the events giving rise to this claim took place there.

<!-- page: 2 -->

**Allegations**

On March 15, 2018, defendant Ginny Weasley negligently hit plaintiff Aberforth Dumbledore with her car. At 10:30 pm, plaintiff was walking his dog, wearing a bright orange reflector jacket. He heard screeching tires and was then struck by car. Harry Potter and Ginny Weasley got out of the car, with Weasley exiting the driver's side. Plaintiff smelled alcohol on Potter's breath.

In April 2018, plaintiff visited his doctor, who diagnosed him with life-threatening hip vasculature degeneration. The doctor stated this condition was caused by physical trauma, of the type experienced in a car crash. Plaintiff underwent surgery, and the sum of his medical costs is $150,000.

Defendant failed to act consistent with ordinary care when she drove drunk. Defendant caused the accident when she drove into Plaintiff. Harm has resulted from the accident in the form of vascular degeneration.

## Question 3

The appeal should be dismissed. First, the district court has not yet entered a final order in the case under § 1291 — though it has reached a finding on liability, because Ginny's appeal was immediate, it has not yet disposed of Aberforth's prayer for relief (*Liberty Mutual*). Because there is only one issue, Rule 54(b) does not apply. Because this is a damages action, § 1292(a) does not apply. It is extremely unlikely that the trial judge would certify the issue for appeal under § 1292(b) since there is no disagreement over a controlling question of law necessary to the disposition of a case. If the trial judge did certify it, there's no way the court of appeals would hear it. There is no collateral order, so *Cohen* doesn't authorize an immediate appeal. And certainly the appellate court would not have to think twice about denying a petition for a writ of mandamus: here, there's no reason for hurry, it's not clear the trial court erred, and neither the balance of the equities nor the public interest demand the writ.

## 2018-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2018-assignment-03-hollister-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

Weasley lives in New Hampshire, and it looks like she intends to stay there. She is a NH domiciliary. Aberforth is a Maine domiciliary. Assuming Aberforth sues for the full amount of his medical and related bills, AIC > $75k and diversity is met. NH has general IPJ over Weasley because she is a NH domiciliary, and Maine has specific IPJ because the suit arises out of conduct in Maine (*Kane*): Maine has a long-arm statute that goes to the limits of the constitution. Weasley established minimum contacts when she purposely availed herself of Maine when she drove through Portland, hitting Aberforth. She has minimum contacts and specific IPJ in Maine is fair and reasonable. That makes state and federal court IPJ OK under FRCP 4(k)(1)(A). Venue is proper in NH through § 1391(b)(1), and in Maine through § 1391(b)(2). So, there are four possible systems in which Aberforth could file suit: NH state court, Maine state court, and federal court in NH or Maine. Maine federal and state courts, and NH federal court, require plausibility pleading (*Twiqbal*, *Malfoy v. Patil*), which creates a higher burden for plaintiffs to meet on pleading. NH state court uses notice pleading (*Longbottom v. Parkinson*), which requires only that Aberforth show "discernable grounds for relief" sufficient to lead to discovery (*Dioguardi*). As there is a valid and applicable federal law governing pleadings (FRCP 8(a), via the Rules Enabling Act), NH fed court will apply federal procedural law (*Hanna*), which in this case is plausibility pleading (*Twiqbal*). Or, at least, that is likely enough to be a serious cause of concern. NH state court is therefore the least burdensome system for Aberforth. As an out-of-state plaintiff, Aberforth might be worried about home cooking, and prefer federal court. But the heightened burden of plausibility pleading is probably more limiting than home cooking. I recommend filing in NH state court.

## Question 2

1. Plaintiff Aberforth Dumbledore is a domiciliary of Maine. Defendant Ginny Weasley is a domiciliary of New Hampshire. The amount in controversy, $150,000, exceeds the value specified by 28 U.S.C. § 1332. Jurisdiction is proper under 28 U.S.C. § 1332(a)(1).
2. Dumbledore is entitled to relief for Weasley's negligence related to a car accident that occurred on March 15, 2018.
3. At 10:30 pm on March 15, 2018, plaintiff Dumbledore was knocked to the ground by a car while crossing the street in front of 12 Grimmauld Place, Portland, Maine.
4. At the time, Dumbledore was wearing a brightly-colored reflective shirt.
5. Immediately before being struck, Dumbledore heard a screeching of tires.
6. Immediately after he was struck, Dumbledore observed defendant Weasley emerging from the driver's side of the vehicle that hit him.
7. When Weasley's companion, Harry Potter, approached Dumbledore, Dumbledore detected a smell of alcohol.
8. In April of 2018, Dumbledore was diagnosed with degeneration of his hip vasculature.
9. Dumbledore's physician has advised that this degeneration was most likely caused by trauma to the area.

<!-- page: 2 -->

10. Trauma from a car accident is the type of trauma that causes degeneration.
11. Dumbledore's physician identified the degeneration as life-threatening.
12. Following immediate surgery and related treatment of the injury, Dumbledore incurred $150,000 in medical, physical therapy, and medication bills.

## Question 3

The First Circuit should grant Aberforth's motion to dismiss. § 1291 grants Courts of Appeals jurisdiction over appeals from final decisions of district courts, but decisions aren't final until relief has been granted (*Liberty Mutual*). As Ginny filed immediately, there has been no relief and the District Court's judgment isn't final. This is a suit for damages only, so § 1292(a)(1) doesn't apply, and there is no indication of any difficult issue that calls for double discretion, so § 1292(b) most likely doesn't apply. The litigation was over a single claim so no Rule 54(b) jurisdiction. Likewise, there are no collateral orders so no *Cohen* exception. This is not a class action so Rule 23(f) doesn't apply. Finally, there is no reason to believe that the District Court judge has exhibited a pattern of bad behavior meriting a § 1651 writ of mandamus. There is no appellate jurisdiction, and the First Circuit should grant the motion.

## 2018-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2018-assignment-03-ravinsky-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

You could file the complaint in either state or federal court in both Maine and NH. For SMJ, state courts are courts of general jurisdiction and there is DJ for FedCts: There's diversity — AD is domiciled in Maine, therefore he is a citizen of that state, and GW is domiciled in NH since she was born there and never lived anywhere else (*Mas*), therefore she is a citizen of NH; and AIC is good, since we have $150,000 in damages. Venue is properly lain in either district: For D.N.H., § 1391(b)(1) because GW resides in district; for D. Me., § 1391(b)(2) because substantial events occurred there. GW is a citizen of NH, so NH has general IPJ, so a NH state (general jurisdiction) or a NH federal court (FRCP 4(k)(1)(A)) can exercise general IPJ. Maine doesn't have general IPJ but certainly has specific IPJ under minimum contacts test (*International Shoe*): Maine has a long-arm statute that goes to the constitution; for minimal contacts, accident happened in Maine, AD is a citizen of Maine, and the state has an interest in regulating drivers on its road; for traditional notions of fairness, more convenient for AD to litigate, all the evidence is there, NH is close to Maine so not too inconvenient for HP and GW. So a Me. state or federal (FRCP 4(k)(1)(A) court) could exercise IPJ.

AD should sue GW in NH state court because it uses notice pleading, so it's easier to withstand MTD. Even if GW removes to FedCT under § 1446, AD can have it remanded, since GW is a citizen of NH and it is a DJ case. There is at least a strong probability that any federal court would use a plausibility pleading standard under a *Hanna* majority *Erie* analysis, so AD should avoid federal court. And a Me. state court would definitely use plausibility pleading.

## Question 2

**Jurisdiction And Venue**

1. Court has SMJ pursuant to § 1332 since there is diversity of citizenship and AIC is greater than $75,000.
2. Venue is proper under § 1391(b)(2) because substantial portion of events transpired in this district.

**Parties**

3. Plaintiff AD is a citizen of the State of Maine.
4. Defendant GW is a citizen of the State of NH.

**Facts**

5. On March 15, 2018, Plaintiff was crossing the street with his dog while wearing a bright orange reflector shirt so that he would be visible to cars on the road.

<!-- page: 2 -->

6. While crossing, Plaintiff was hit by a car.
7. After colliding with Plaintiff, Defendant got out of the car on the driver's side.
8. Defendant's companion, HP, got out of the passenger side and went to check on Plaintiff, who smelled alcohol on HP's breath, leading Plaintiff to believe they had been drinking.
9. Because she was under the influence, Defendant was unable to detect Plaintiff crossing the street, despite his bright orange reflector vest.
10. Defendant's alcohol-inhibited attention and driving skills led her to hit Plaintiff with the car.
11. In late April 2018, Plaintiff visited his doctor, who detected a life-threatening degeneration of Plaintiff's hip vasculature.
12. According to Plaintiff's doctor, the degeneration was caused by trauma to the area, such as that caused by a car accident.
13. Plaintiff needed surgery to correct the degeneration, incurring $150,000 in expenses.

**Count I: Negligence**

14. Plaintiff incorporates by reference paragraphs 1 through 11.
15. Under the circumstances, Defendant's drinking and driving was inconsistent with ordinary care.
16. Defendant's failure to act with ordinary care led to them hitting Plaintiff, thus causing the accident.
17. The injury that Plaintiff suffered was a result of the accident.

## Question 3

For appellate jurisdiction, go through *Liberty Mutual*. There's no final judgment, so no jurisdiction under § 1291. There's no injunction, so no jurisdiction under § 1292(a)(1). There's no order about which there's substantial ground for difference of opinion on the question, so no double-discretion jurisdiction under § 1292(b). There's only one claim, so no jurisdiction under Rule 54(b). Also, the SJ order is not collateral to the case, so no jurisdiction under *Cohen*. Finally, nothing extraordinary happened, so no mandamus.

The First Circuit should grant AD's motion to dismiss GW's appeal.
