<!-- page: 2 -->

## Substantive Assignment

### Applicable Law

Under Washington and Oregon state law, a plaintiff may sue for a tort called negligent interference with contractual relations. To prevail in this cause of action, a plaintiff must prove that the defendant:

(i) committed an act or omission that is a departure from ordinary care (i.e., negligent),
(ii) that the plaintiff knew, or should have known, would cause the plaintiff to suffer damages in the form of an inability to receive the benefit of a valid contract not involving the defendant, and
(iii) that the plaintiff did in fact suffer such damages.

Under California, Delaware, and Idaho state law, there is no tort for negligent interference with contractual relations. There is, however, a cause of action called intentional interference with contractual relations. To prevail in this cause of action, a plaintiff must prove that the defendant:

(i) committed an act or omission *with the intention* of causing the plaintiff to suffer damages in the form of an inability to receive the benefit of a contract not involving the defendant, and
(ii) that the plaintiff did in fact suffer such damages.

For both the negligent and the intentional version of the interference with contractual relations tort, the last event necessary to complete the tort is the breach of the contract with which the defendant interfered.

All states relevant to this assignment have a standard cause of action for breach of contract. To prevail on a common law breach of contract claim the plaintiff must show: (1) the plaintiff and defendant made a valid enforceable contract, (2) the plaintiff performed his/her part of the contract or offered a justification for nonperformance, (3) the defendant failed to perform his/her part of the contract, and (4) damage resulted to the plaintiff. The plaintiff must prevail on all elements to win.

Oregon and Washington follow the First Restatement for horizontal choice of law. Delaware, California, and Idaho follow the Second Restatement.

Oregon, Idaho, and Delaware have only a single federal district. Washington has two federal districts, the Eastern and Western. The city of Potomac is located in the Eastern District of Washington. California has four federal districts. The city of Sacramento is in the Eastern District of California.

Oregon, Idaho, Delaware, Washington, and California all have long-arm statutes that go to the limits of the Constitution.

### Facts

Florean Fortescue was a farmer. He leased federal land in Oregon, where he was born and lived his entire life, to grow crops. His primary farmland was located in an isolated area of northwestern Oregon. Due to recent fires, washouts, and other natural disasters, Florean's farmland was accessible only by a logging road that, in the sleepy town of Hogsmeade, Oregon, passed over the Hogsmeade Bridge. The town of Hogsmeade raised revenue by charging any vehicle larger than a passenger car a toll to cross the bridge.

<!-- page: 3 -->

In early 2017, the Commissioners of the town of Hogsmeade decided that the Hogsmeade Bridge, which was also used by the town's few residents, was in need of repairs, although the repairs were not urgent. They contracted with Cho Chang Contracting Corp., incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Potomac, Washington, to do the repairs. Chang Corp. does business exclusively in the states of Washington and Oregon. Chang Corp. advised the Commissioners that the repairs would require that the Bridge be shut down for approximately two weeks. The contract for the repairs stated that Chang Corp. would take care to schedule the shutdown "keeping in mind the needs of farmers in northwestern Oregon to access the bridge for the purpose of delivering their harvests to markets in the central and eastern portions of the state, and to give due notice to such farmers of the dates of any shutdown." Otherwise, the contract allowed Chang Corp. to choose the time of the shutdown, so long as all repair work was complete by January of 2021.

In January of 2018, Florean signed a contract with Bathilda Bagshot Industries, Inc., incorporated in Delaware. The contract was negotiated via email exchange, after in-person negotiations occurring at Florean's farm and Bathilda's headquarters, with Florean writing from FloreanFortescueFarmer(at)gmail.com and Bathilda writing from BathildaBagshotBuyer(at)gmail.com. It was signed by both parties using an online signature verification service. In the contract, Bagshot agreed to buy 300,000 bushels of Florean's corn at $2.00 per bushel. The contract specified that Florean was to deliver the corn to a facility in Sacramento, California, adjacent to Bagshot's headquarters. Delivery was to be made at any time during the month of September, 2018. According to the contract, Bagshot had seven days to inspect the corn. If within those seven days, Bagshot believed that the corn's condition was not in accordance with industry standards, it could refuse delivery, in which case either party could sue the other for breach.

In February of 2018, Bathilda sold the rights to 150,000 bushels of Florean's corn to Ted Tonks Industries, Inc., incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Idaho. The sales contract was negotiated over the telephone, with calls made between Bathilda's offices in Sacramento and Tonks' offices in Boise, Idaho. The contract was signed on the Internet via an online signature verification service.

On Friday, August 31, 2018, a fleet of approximately 300 semis gathered at a distribution facility in one of Florean's farms, loaded up 1,000 bushels of corn each, and set off for Sacramento. They drove through part of the night, but when they arrived at the Hogsmeade Bridge, they found that Chang Corp. had closed it for repairs. The fleet of semis was unable to get through. Upon hearing of these events, Florean immediately drove to Potomac to meet with Chang Corp.'s Chief Executive Officer, Ms. Marietta Edgecombe. Edgecombe told Florean that she, from her office in Potomac, had scheduled the Hogsmeade job herself. She agreed that she could have scheduled the Hogsmeade Bridge repairs at any time, from August of 2018 to February of 2019 (note that there is no farm harvest in Oregon in January or February), but stated that she thought it best to "get those repairs out of the way." Florean demanded that Chang Corp. reopen the bridge immediately to allow his corn harvest through, but Edgecombe said, "No, now that we've started, trying to stop will cause the bridge to be closed even longer."

The Hogsmeade Bridge remained closed for about two weeks, during which time Oregon experienced unseasonably hot weather. The fleet of semis finally arrived in Sacramento on September 17, 2018. Bathilda and Tonks immediately sent in their inspectors, and one of Florean's farm hands who had accompanied the fleet, a Mr. Dobby Kreacher, also examined the corn's condition.

<!-- page: 4 -->

Initially, the Bathilda and Tonks inspectors believed that the corn was up to industry standard. On September 18, Bathilda and Tonks each hand-delivered a form letter to Dobby in Sacramento telling him that they accepted the corn. Two days later, however, they each hand-delivered a second letter to Dobby, who was still in Sacramento, stating that further inspection of the corn had revealed it to have been damaged from exposure to the heat, and that they would pay only $1.25 per bushel for the corn. Subsequently, each wired $187,500.00 into Florean's bank account.

**Note:** if the actions of Bathilda and Tonks constituted a breach of contract, the last event necessary to complete the breach was the hand delivery of the second letter to Dobby in Sacramento.

### The Client's Situation

You were a lawyer Florean has consulted in the past. Florean called you in a rage. He told you that Bathilda's and Tonks' second letter constituted a breach of contract. The corn was fine, Florean tells you. Each of Bathilda and Tonks, separately, owes him 150,000 × ($2.00 − $1.25) = $112,500.00. He wants to sue them in federal court.

Florean was also in a rage about Chang Corp.'s closure of the Hogsmeade Bridge. He said that if the corn was defective, then Chang Corp. should have to pay him $112,500 × 2 = $225,000.00 for the money he lost. (Assume, which is true, that applicable law will allow him to sue "in the alternative," meaning that Florean can try to recover $112,500.00 from each of Bathilda and Tonks on the theory that the corn was fine, and in the same lawsuit try to recover "in the alternative" $225,000.00 from Chang if it turned out that the court ruled that the corn was defective.)

### Questions

Florean wanted to know the following:

1. Under the civil procedure doctrines we've studied thus far in class, could Florean sue Bathilda Corp., Tonks Corp., and Chang Corp. in a single lawsuit in some federal court? Can he do so? In what court? Why? (Do not concern yourself with whether, as a matter of substantive contract or tort law, any of these theories of relief would succeed.)

2. Assuming (which might not be true) that Florean can sue all three in the same lawsuit in some federal court, where (geographically) would he be most likely to succeed? Why?

## 2019-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-01-fortney-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1 Model Answer

## Question 1

For one suit, Florean needs SMJ and IPJ and Venue over all three in one location. Florean can sue in DE and maybe OR.

### SMJ

Since all of Florean's claims are state tort/contract claims, there is no AUJ. *Smith-Grable* does not apply because these claims do not involve federal questions. However, there is SMJ under 1332 because there is complete diversity of citizenship (which is based on domicile) as required by *Strawbridge* and AIC > 75k for all claims. AIC for claim against Chang is $225,000, and $112,500 for claim against each Bathilda/Tonks. Florean = OR (*Mas v. Perry*), Chang Corp. = DE (place of inc.) and WA (PPOB) (*Hertz*). Similarly, Bagshot = DE and CA (PPOB), and Tonks = DE and ID. It is okay if defendants share citizenship (DE) because there is complete diversity between parties on opposite sides of the lawsuit.

### IPJ

**Chang** — There is general IPJ in DE and WA. There is specific IPJ in OR. OR has a long arm statute that goes to limit of constitution, and 4(k)(1)(a) allows federal courts to piggy back. By serving as the contractor on a bridge repair in OR, there are more than sufficient minimal contacts among Chang, OR, and the dispute. OR's exercise of jurisdiction would not offend "traditional notions of fair play and substantial justice" (*International Shoe*); OR has an interest in Chang's conduct, which occurred in its borders, and key evidence (regarding the bridge closure) is in OR. However, because Chang does no business in CA and has no contacts there, there is no IPJ there.

**Bagshot** — There is general IPJ in DE and CA. There is likely specific IPJ in OR because of Long arm + 4(k)(1)(a), and Bagshot likely has minimum contacts with OR. While the contract specified grain delivery in CA, the contract was partly negotiated in OR and Bagshot knew Florean would produce the grain in OR. The exercise of jurisdiction would be fair and reasonable because of the business relationship with an OR farmer under *McGee*.

**Tonks** — There is general IPJ in DE and ID. There is likely specific IPJ in CA. CA has a long arm + 4(k)(1)(a). Tonks agreed to receive product in CA by contracting with Bagshot in CA and agreeing to receive corn in CA, and thus purposefully availed itself to the benefits and protections of CA law (*WW Volkswagen*). IPJ in OR is uncertain. It is unclear that by stepping into Bagshot's shoes in a contract Tonks has availed himself to OR law. Tonks might not know that corn is even coming from OR. (This could be more like *Hanson* than *McGee*). A contrary argument might be that Tonks likely does know that the corn came from OR (perhaps from conversations with Dobby, or with Bagshot), and that he purposefully availed itself of the economic benefit of commerce with an OR farmer. But absent any indication that the corn was uniquely "Oregonian," this seems weak.

### Venue

Under 1391(b)(1) Venue could be laid in DE because all defendant corporations are DE residents and all three are subject to PJ in DE. Under 1391(2) venue could be laid against Chang in WA or OR. Florean could argue the events arose in OR (at the bridge) or in WA at the corporate headquarters where Chang made the decision when to repair the bridge. Under 1391(2) Venue could be laid in EDCA against Bagshot/Tonks because corn inspection/rejection occurred there. Florean could try to claim that event that *led* to corn rejection occurred in OR, so it should count for venue, but this could be challenging.

---

## Question 2

Florean would want WA or OR substantive law for his claims against Chang because there is negligent interference, which is easier to satisfy than the intentional interference required by CA, DE, and ID. The substantive law doesn't matter for Florean's breach of contract claims against Bathilda/Tonks. Florean could sue the parties in DE or maybe OR (if OR has IPJ for Florean's claim against Tonks and Venue for Florian's claims against Bagshot/Tonks) but Florean would be more likely to succeed in DE. A Federal Court sitting in diversity will use HCOL rule of state in which it sits (*Klaxon*).

**OR Analysis**

Florean should NOT sue defendants in OR. OR is a 1st Restatement state for HCOL, which means that courts in OR will apply the substantive law of the state where the last act required to complete the tort occurred to the Chang claim. The final element of the cause of action is "that the plaintiff did in fact suffer such damages," which occurred when Bathilda/Tonks delivered the letter in CA! If Florean sues defendants in OR, Florean will get CA substantive law in the Chang claim, which does not have *negligent* interference with contractual relations but only *intentional* interference.

**DE Analysis**

Florean should sue defendants in DE. DE is a 2nd restatement state and thus would take into account the interests of the state and the parties and use the substantive law of the area most affected. The DE court likely will use OR substantive law, since most of the events happened there (or WA, but WA has negligent interference as well), to determine the claim against Chang. The Chang-Florean relationship is centered on OR; OR has an interest in protecting its resident (Florean) and in regulating conduct that occurred in its borders. Thus, there would be a negligent interference with contract cause of action available, which is better for Florean.

## 2019-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-01-leiva-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1 Model Answer

**Parties:** Florean Fortescue (FF) domiciled in OR; Bathilda Bagshot (BB) a citizen of CA (PPoB) & DE (inc); Cho Chang (CC) a citizen of WA (PPoB) & DE (inc); Ted Tonks (TT) a citizen in ID (PPoB) & DE (inc).

---

## Question 1: Federal Lawsuit

### SMJ

No AUJ because state law contract claims; no federal claims. No *Smith*/*Grable* exception analysis because no federal issue embedded in state law claims. FF's diversity jurisdiction claims against BB lie (AIC = $112,500 > $75K), against TT lie (AIC = $112,500 > $75K), and against CC lie (AIC = $225,000 > $75K). There is also complete diversity with FF (plaintiff) domiciled in OR and the three defendants domiciled in DE, WA, ID, and CA (*Strawbridge*).

### IPJ & Venue

Can have general IPJ over all three defendants in DE since that is where they are truly at home. Similarly, have venue under §1391(b)(1).

General IPJ over BB in CA. CA has long-arm statute that goes to limits of the Constitution so can use 4(k)(1)(a) to establish specific IPJ over other defendants. Unlikely to have specific IPJ over CC in CA since CC only does business in OR and WA and no evidence of any Chang contacts in CA. CA does not have minimal contacts with CC (*Int'l Shoe*).

General IPJ over TT in ID but other defendants have no contacts with ID.

Only other state with Specific IPJ over defendants would be OR. OR has long arm statute that goes to limits of Constitution so federal court piggy-backs on that using 4(k)(1)(a). CC purposely availed itself of OR law by contracting with the OR Commissioners of Hogsmeade and exclusively doing business in OR (*Int'l Shoe*). BB also purposely availed itself of OR law by contracting with an OR farmer to get produce grown, harvested, and transported from OR (*McGee*). TT's minimum contacts with OR are a bit trickier because BB was an intermediary between FF and TT. Furthermore, it is unclear if TT knew where the corn was coming from since the contracting between BB and TT was done over the phone and internet, with TT never stepping foot in OR throughout this entire transaction. It is fair and reasonable to sue in OR because OR court has interest in protecting its residents, efficient resolution with all evidence of bridge closure in OR, convenient forum for at least three of the four parties who have been in OR recently to negotiate contracts for this transaction. But unclear if it is fair and reasonable to sue TT in OR since TT might not be on notice that jurisdiction would be available to OR court (*Denckla*). Yet, TT had inspectors ready to assess the corn bushels, signed a contract with BB about the corn, and had an interest in knowing where the corn was coming from. The state and plaintiff's interests, along with the likelihood of an efficient resolution in OR where most of the evidence is located, weighs in favor of IPJ over TT. Bridge closure evidence also allows proper venue to be laid under §1391(b)(2).

---

## Question 2: Strategic Considerations

**HCOL:** Federal court sitting in diversity will use the horizontal choice of law rules of the state in which it sits to determine what substantive law to apply (*Klaxon*). Want the federal court to apply negligent interference elements because those are easier to meet; FF more likely to succeed against CC with this lower bar.

Under 1st Restatement analysis, would use law of state where the harm occurred. In this case the harm occurred in CA because the last event necessary to complete the breach of contract was the second letter delivery in Sacramento. Under 2nd Restatement analysis, would use law of state that was the nexus of controversy and account for state interests. The spoilage of corn resulted from the Hogsmeade bridge closure, most of the parties contracted in OR, and an OR court has an interest in protecting its residents and regulating contracting behavior within its borders — so apply OR law. Prefer to apply OR negligence standard over CA intentional threshold. So use a 2nd Restatement court, either CA, DE, or ID depending on IPJ and Venue.

DE is the best court to file in because of 4(k)(1)(a) and long arm statute that goes to limits of Constitution, uses 2nd Restatement, and would likely apply OR negligence threshold. While CC's closure of the bridge did not account for "the needs of farmers in northwestern Oregon," it is unlikely a court will find the closure rose to the level of intentional interference with FF's contract. It was merely negligent because the bridge could have been repaired during the offseason in January. So we want a court that will apply OR law of negligent interference, leading us to file in DE rather than OR.

## 2019-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-01-londono-pardo-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1 Model Answer

## Question 1

Yes, Florean can sue Bathilda, Tonks, and Chang Corp in a single lawsuit.

### SMJ

Florean will sue Bathilda and Tonks for breach of contract. No AUJ, as breach is state law. No federal question is embedded under *Smith-Grable*. Diversity jurisdiction applies. Parties are diverse: Florean domiciled in Oregon; Bathilda is a citizen of Delaware (place of incorporation) and California (PPoB); Tonks is a citizen of Delaware (inc) and Idaho (PPoB). Plaintiff completely diverse from the defendants. (Defendants do not need to be.) AIC is above $75K at $225,000 > $75,000, the difference between the agreed price and amount paid (and thus Florean's damages).

Florean will sue Chang for interference with contractual relations. No AUJ, as state law cause of action. No federal question embedded. Diversity jurisdiction applies; parties diverse: Florean domiciled in Oregon; Tonks a citizen of Delaware (inc) and Idaho (PPoB). AIC above $75K at $225,000 > $75,000 (the amount of damages if the tortious interference suit succeeds).

### PJ

Florean will likely be able to establish PJ over the three parties in Delaware and Oregon. California is not likely an option. Idaho is not an option, as Tonks is the only one with exposure in that state.

**Delaware:** Delaware has PJ over Chang, Bathilda, and Tonks under General IPJ. All three are actually at home in Delaware because they are all incorporated there.

**Oregon:** Oregon likely has PJ over Chang, Bathilda, and Tonks.

Can establish Specific IPJ over Bathilda and Chang given that they do business in that jurisdiction. Under minimum contacts test (*Int'l Shoe*), there is: 1) a state long arm statute and 4(k)(1)(a) allows the federal court to piggy-back, and 2) minimal contacts are met. Both Bathilda and Chang availed themselves to Oregon; they do business with that state, Bathilda had in-person negotiations at Florean's farm. Fair and just for them to get hailed to Oregon; Chang's conduct occurred there (OR has an interest in regulating that), and Bathilda negotiated a contract with an OR farmer (*McGee*).

It is less clear if Oregon has Specific PJ over Tonks, but it likely does. Minimal contacts are more likely met because Tonks knowingly purchased the rights to 150K of Bathilda's bushels from Florean, which included a contract signing. Tonks' inspectors participated in checking the corn that had come from Oregon; knew it was buying bushels coming from Florean from Oregon, or, at the very least, should have taken the time to figure out where the corn was coming from. Unlike *Hanson*, where one of the parties unilaterally moved after contract was made, here Tonks contracted to receive products from a party, and onus was to know location at time of contract. Likely Specific IPJ over Tonks in Oregon.

**California:** Likely no Specific IPJ over Chang because does business exclusively in Washington & Oregon, incorporated in Delaware, and PPB in Washington. Any contacts are too attenuated — it strains credulity to imagine that a decision to close the Oregon bridge would affect specific parties in CA; did not purposely avail itself of OR. (*WW Volkswagen*). CA has general IPJ over Bathilda; at home. Specific IPJ over Tonks: 1) long arm & 4(k)(1)(a), and 2) minimum contacts met by doing business with Bathilda.

**Idaho:** Tonks is the only one with any contacts there. No PJ.

### Venue

Within both Delaware and Oregon, only one district. Venue proper in D. Delaware because all defendants "reside" there (incorporation → IPJ). Venue proper in D. Oregon because substantial part of events occurred there (corn harvest, bridge closure, transport beginning), or because subject to the court's PJ. If California were an option, Eastern District because substantial part of the events happened there.

---

## Question 2

The two possibilities are Delaware or Oregon. Florean would be most likely to succeed if he sued them in Delaware. Federal court sitting in diversity will use the HCOL rules of the state in which it sits. (*Klaxon*)

### HCOL

**If Florean sues in Oregon:** Oregon is a 1st Restatement state, so it will use the law of California with regard to interference with contractual relations against Chang. This is because the last event necessary to complete the breach was the hand delivery of the second letter to Dobby in Sacramento, CA. California has the "intentional" standard for interference with contractual relations, which means that Florean will have to show that Chang interfered with the corn contract by shutting down the bridge "with the intention of causing [Florean] to suffer damages." This is a higher standard to meet than a negligence standard.

**If Florean sues in Delaware:** Delaware is a better place to sue. It is a 2nd Restatement state, and will take into account a more holistic consideration with respect to HCOL. Because Florean is based in Oregon, Chang Corp does business in Oregon, and the interference (shutting down the bridge) happened in Oregon, Delaware will see that Oregon was the "center of gravity" for the transaction. (*Babcock*) Delaware will thus determine that it should use Oregon's law, which follows the "negligent" standard for interference with contractual relations. Florean is more likely to succeed if Oregon's law is used given the lack of clarity regarding Chang Corp's intention in shutting down the bridge. He should sue in Delaware.

## 2019-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-01-taylor-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

# Assignment 1 Model Answer

Florean can probably sue Bathilda, Tonks, and Chang in the same lawsuit in federal court. Florean will make a standard breach of contract claim against Bathilda and Tonks, and a negligent (depending on HCOL) interference claim against Chang.

---

## SMJ

Since these claims all fall under state law, there is no arising-under jurisdiction. Jurisdiction is based on complete diversity (*Strawbridge*). Florean is domiciled in OR (plaintiff); Bathilda a citizen of DE (inc) and CA (PPoB) (defendant); Tonks in DE (inc) and CA (PPoB) (defendant); and Chang in DE (inc) and WA (PPoB) (defendant). Each individual claim would meet the AIC requirement because Tonks/Bathilda's claims are $112.5k each (> $75k), and the claim against Chang for damages would be $225k (> $75k).

---

## PJ and Venue

4(k)(1)(a) long-arm allows federal court to exercise jurisdiction over state-law claims, and all states here have long-arm to limits of constitution.

**DE** — Florean can sue all three in DE because all domiciled there = general jurisdiction. Venue would be proper because all incorporated there — § 1391(b)(1).

**OR** — Could possibly get specific jurisdiction over everyone in OR. Bathilda passes the minimum contacts test because Bathilda contracted directly with Florean to purchase OR corn and negotiated purchase at Florean's farm in OR, thus purposefully availing itself of business in OR. Jurisdiction would be fair/reasonable because of this business relationship.

- Specific jurisdiction over Chang because Chang contracted to build the OR bridge, also availing itself of OR business and establishing minimal contacts to exercise jurisdiction over Chang in OR. OR has an interest in regulating the conduct that occurred in state (bridge repair/closure), which impacted an OR farmer, so it is fair to hail Chang to OR.
- Specific jurisdiction over Tonks in OR may be a roadblock to suit here. Contracts were signed over the internet, which is difficult to locate (*Griffis*), and Tonks's contract isn't even with Florean — Tonks bought corn from Bathilda, a CA/DE company. A court could say Tonks knew it bought corn coming from OR, thus purposefully availing itself of corn business in OR, but this isn't a surefire win. It is not clear that Tonks's business to purchase Florean's corn from Bathilda establishes minimal contacts with OR or that it would be fair to hail Tonks into court in a state in which it has not done business directly.
- If IPJ established over all, bridge closure = § 1391(b)(2) venue in OR (only one district).

**ID** — Likely out — only have general jurisdiction there over Tonks and others don't have contacts there (except perhaps Bathilda has contacts through the phone call doing business, which is easier to locate than internet activity). Definitely not over Chang.

**CA** — Chang likely isn't subject to jurisdiction there, as there are no contacts relating CA to Chang; have general jurisdiction over Bathilda and likely specific jurisdiction over Tonks for their business with Bathilda. If could sue in CA, venue would be proper in Eastern District where Bathilda's HQ located.

**WA** — No. Chang at home, but Bathilda and Tonks have no relevant contacts.

---

## Choice of Law (COL)

Under *Klaxon*, a federal court sitting in diversity will apply the HCOL analysis of the state in which it sits.

For 1st Restatement states, *lex loci delicti* will mean applying CA law to the breach claim where final act of breach was made, which also equals the final act for the negligent contract interference claim against Chang.

For 2nd Restatement states, CA law will probably also apply to the breach of contract claim (place where corn delivered); unless we say that the center of the parties' relationship is OR where the business began and where the bridge blockage occurred that caused the damage — when including Chang in the claim, this center makes more sense than CA. 2nd Restatement will also allow using different law for the Chang claim besides the last act of the breach, so alternatively, court might apply CA law to the breach claim and OR law to the negligence claim against Chang.

**Choice:** OR/WA negligent interference is likely the only successful claim against Chang. No evidence of specific intent to hurt farmers — just didn't follow orders and take them adequately into account. So Florean will want to choose a jurisdiction that will apply OR/WA law to that claim in case his claim against Bathilda/Tonks is not successful. DE seems like the best choice because it definitely has jurisdiction over all defendants and is a 2nd Restatement state. It will apply the same substantive law to the breach claim because all relevant states have this same law. Regarding the tort claim against Chang, likely the relationship of the two parties for the interference claim is centered in OR, OR substantive law will be applied, and Florean will only have to prove negligence, rather than specific intent. OR has interest in regulating the legality of conduct that occurred in OR, and OR has interest in protecting OR resident (*Babcock*).

CA requires intentionality for a tortious interference claim, and the facts probably do not support the theory that Chang intentionally interfered with the Florean-Bathilda/Tonks contracts. Edgecombe showed negligent indifference to the damage she could cause farmers by not scheduling the bridge repairs in the winter at a time with no harvest, but did not specifically intend to cause the harm. If CA law applies, Florean almost certainly loses. Best chance is DE court applying OR law.
