# Assignment 2 (Graded)
## Civil Procedure 2 & 6, 2019
### Professor Jim Greiner

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** October 25, 2019
**Due date/time:** 9 am on Tuesday, Oct 29, 2019
**Word limit:** 850 words
**Time limit:** 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is ungraded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

**Suggestions for your answer:**

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Substantive Assignment

You are a legal aid attorney in St. Louis, MO. One morning, Hermione Granger walks into your office to ask for your help. Hermione had just received seizure orders from two different state courts, one in Illinois, the other in North Carolina. She asks for your help with both of them.

### The Illinois Bank Account Seizure Order

Hermione was born in St. Louis, MO (note: "MO" is the postal code for the state of Missouri) and has lived in St. Louis for all of her life. She lived most of her life in poverty. A deflected curse early in her career as an Auror for the Ministry of Magic left her disabled, and she survived largely on disability benefits, supplemented by payouts from a program run by the state of Missouri. Sometimes, especially long and cold winters drove up her heating bill, she struggled to keep up financially. She had to pay her heating bill, put food on the table for herself and her children, take care of rent, and maintain her life and the lives of her children. Sometimes, she had to charge basic life necessities to an American Express card, which she struggled to pay off. To keep her financial life simple, she had only ever used the single AmEx card, and had only opened a single checking account. All charges on the AmEx card were with local vendors in St. Louis. The checking account was with Hogwarts Bank, Inc., an Illinois corporation with its principal place of business in Fairmont City, IL. Hogwarts was a small community bank with three outlets, all located in or around Fairmont City, IL. Hermione's parents had used the bank, so Hermione continued to patronize it even though she had to cross the state line between St. Louis, MO, where she lived, and Fairmont City, IL, where the bank was located, to visit the bank. She used a bus to make the trip.

During 2018, Hermione got behind on the payments to her AmEx card, and the heating company threatened to cut off her heat. She ended up charging a lot to her AmEx card, but then struggled to make the monthly payments. Hermione eventually stopped making payments to AmEx in October of 2018. She now thinks that she had about $400 left to pay on the card. Around that time, Hermione closed her mailbox at the local St. Louis post office. She had been getting all her mail delivered to the post office box for almost two decades, but she decided that she wanted to save $5 per month it cost her to keep it to help to pay for heat, and she hated getting mail, anyway. She told Hogwarts, her utility companies, and her friends to start using her apartment address for mail (she was able to receive mail there), but no one else.

In April of 2019, things went from bad to worse for Hermione. She woke to find a letter from Hogwarts waiting for her. The letter informed her that, pursuant to a writ of seizure issued by an Illinois state court, her Hogwarts checking account had been seized/frozen. She could no longer write checks on it, and any checks she had written that hadn't cleared yet would not be honored. The letter included a copy of the writ, reciting that the petitioner on the writ was WeBuyDebts.com, Inc. The letter, which Hermione showed you, recited that as of the time of the seizure, the bank account had $975 in it.

With Hermione sitting in your office, you do a little research on the Internet about WeBuyDebts.com. You find out as follows. WeBuyDebts.com buys delinquent debts from credit card companies and sues the alleged debtors to try to collect. WeBuyDebts.com is known for its aggressive investigative staff. The company's website includes a description of its "beyond outstanding" investigative team, reciting that the team regularly peruses social media, government records, the pages of local newspapers, and any other source of information to find assets belonging to alleged debtors that it can seize.

<!-- page: 3 -->

You are able to look up the Illinois state court lawsuit online, and you quickly review the filings in the case. From the complaint, you find out that it is a quasi-in-rem suit. WeBuyDebts.com asked the court to seize the bank account as part of the quasi-in-rem suit. The underlying basis of the lawsuit was the alleged non-payment of $400 owed on the AmEx card. The complaint alleged that WeBuyDebts.com had purchased the rights to collect on the debt from AmEx. The complaint alleged that "attempts to contact Ms. Granger via mailing to a P.O. Box in St. Louis to resolve this issue were not successful." The complaint asked for recovery in the amount of $400, plus fees and costs and interests totaling $300, for a total of $700. Also included in the case file was the motion for seizure of the Hogwarts, IL bank account. The motion for seizure restated the statements in the complaint, and sought seizure of the bank account as assurance of payment, as per a historically standard quasi-in-rem action, for $700 allegedly owed. The motion was signed by an attorney. A judge signed the order the same day the motion was filed, and the writ of seizure was served on the bank the next day. There were no other papers in the file.

### The North Carolina Racing Broom Seizure Order

Going back to January of 2019, Hermione thought her luck had changed. She'd been able to save about $900 in her checking account. More importantly, her friend Fenrir Greyback reached her through one of Hermione's cousins who also lived in St. Louis (Hermione herself did not own or rent a phone). Fenrir said that he had a great investment deal for her. All she had to do was to mail him a money order for $500. Fenrir would make her half owner of a rare, extremely valuable racing broom. The broom was on sale from a store outlet of a company called "WeBuyDebts.com" (the same company as in the Illinois case, above). According to Fenrir, WeBuyDebts.com bought debts, and sometimes accepted valuable items in return for forgiving debts. It frequently sold those valuable items in its outlet stores. One such store was in Columbia, SC, where Fenrir lived, and that store had the racing broom in question. The broom was priced at only $25,000.00. Fenrir assured Hermione that the broom was really worth at least $50,000.00. Fenrir proposed that Fenrir himself would buy the broom for both of them on an installment contract. He would make a first payment of $1,000, using Hermione's $500 plus $500 of his own money. They would both have to sign an agreement to pay $1,000.00/month until the $25,000.00 was paid off, or they could repay the entire purchase price at any time. Fenrir assured Hermione that he could resell the broom very quickly, so quickly that they would never have to make the payment due one month after the purchase of the broom.

Hermione agreed. Fenrir mailed Hermione a copy of the WeBuyDebts.com purchase agreement, which Fenrir himself had already signed. Hermione signed it in January, made a copy for herself at her local library, and mailed it back to Fenrir, along with a money order for $500.00. Her signature on the WeBuyDebts.com purchase agreement included her address, as follows, "Hermione Granger, #4 Privet Drive, St. Louis, MO." Fenrir wrote to Hermione in mid-February to say that he had purchased the broom the day before, giving WeBuyDebts.com the $1,000 ($500 from Fenrir and $500 from Hermione), and was looking for a buyer.

<!-- page: 4 -->

In late February, Fenrir wrote Hermione with the following news: From his home in South Carolina, Fenrir bought advertisements in print newspapers (Fenrir was old-fashioned) in South Carolina (where he had lived for all his life) and Virginia (where he had some friends). He took no other action to publicize the sale. The ads all said the following words, or words to this effect: "Hermione Granger of St. Louis, MO and Fenrir Greyback of Columbia, SC seek to sell rare racing broom they own. Call Fenrir Greyback, XXX-XXX-XXXX, to hear a description of valuable broom and to offer bid." Fenrir's ads drew the attention of a reputable racing broom dealer in Roanoke, VA, and Fenrir negotiated a purchase price of $65,000.00. Fenrir wrote to Hermione to tell her this news, and Hermione had high hopes, as half of the proceeds of the broom sale was more money than she had ever had in her life.

In early April of 2019, Fenrir arranged to drive the broom himself to Roanoke on a particular day. On his way from South Carolina to Virginia, however, just after he drove into North Carolina, a sheriff from a North Carolina state court pulled him over. The sheriff showed Fenrir a writ of seizure issued the previous week by a North Carolina state court authorizing seizure of the broom. The writ showed the petitioner to be WeBuyDebts.com, Inc., a Delaware Corporation with its principal place of business in New York. Fenrir had to give the broom to the Sheriff. He drove back home and called Hermione's friend to get in touch with her to give Hermione the bad news. He emailed a copy of the writ to the mutual friend, who gave Hermione a printout. In the email, Fenrir admitted that he never made the first month's payment on the broom, which was due in the middle of March, 2019.

You recognize WeBuyDebts.com as the same company that had requested the seizure order of the Illinois bank account. You are able, on the Internet, to download the papers from the North Carolina case.

- The complaint alleges that Hermione and Fenrir failed to make the payment on the broom due in the middle of March, 2019. The complaint further shows that the case is an in rem proceeding; WeBuyDebts.com asked the court for an order declaring that it owned the broom and returning the broom to its possession. The complaint alleges that WeBuyDebts.com retained an ownership/property interest in the broom until all of the installments were paid off.
- Attached to the complaint is the purchase/sale agreement for the broom. You see in that agreement that, indeed, it provides that WeBuyDebts.com retains an ownership/property interest in the broom until all payments were made. The agreement also provides that in the event of any missed payment, the entire amount of the loan becomes immediately due, and that the seller (WeBuyDebts.com) may petition any court of competent jurisdiction for an order to seize the sold item (here, the broom).
- Also attached to the complaint is an affidavit from an employee at WeBuyDebts.com swearing under penalty of perjury that the copy of the purchase/sale agreement is true and correct, and that the March payment was never paid.
- Also attached to the complaint is a copy of the receipt given to Fenrir for the initial $1,000 payment made at the time when he first bought the broom in Columbia, SC, in early February.
- The last documents in the file are a motion for seizure, incorporating all of the documents just described by reference, and the North Carolina Court's seizure order signed by a judge, which was issued the same day the motion was filed.

<!-- page: 5 -->

### Questions

You do a little investigating. You find out that both the North Carolina and Illinois legislatures had some years ago adopted the Uniform Pretrial Seizure Code, a model statute governing predisposition civil seizures of property.

- Based on what you know thus far, what (i) ***procedural due process***, and (ii) ***quasi-in-rem/in rem jurisdiction over the property arguments*** could you make against the seizures in either North Carolina or Illinois or both?
- The seizures were implemented in North Carolina and Illinois using the same statutory scheme, the Uniform Pretrial Seizure Code. You decide to review this statute to try to form (i) ***procedural due process***, and (ii) ***quasi-in-rem/in rem jurisdiction over the property*** arguments you could use to attack its constitutionality. What aspects of this statute will you look for to see if they might support arguments against seizures like those your client suffered? What corresponding arguments might you make?

## 2019-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-02-belo-osagie-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## 1. Assessing the Seizures

### a. Assessing Constitutionality

**i. The Illinois Seizure**

The IL seizure is likely unconstitutional. Notice must be reasonably certain to reach the relevant party (*Mullane*) and supplementary notice is required if the initial attempt fails (*Flowers*). Here, WeBuyDebts is aware that Hermione did not receive notice and did not follow up. Additionally, it definitely could have. Its crack investigative team could have found another method of contacting her through its research. So they failed to use the best method practicable (*Schroeder*). Further, there was no pre-seizure hearing and the property seized is hugely significant to Hermione, as in *Connecticut v. Doehr*.

**ii. The North Carolina Seizure**

The NC seizure is constitutionally stronger: (a) decision maker is a judge, (b) competent documentary evidence has been submitted to support seizure, (c) WeBuyDebts has a clear pre-seizure interest in the property, (d) there is some notice + consent within the purchase agreement of the possibility of this kind of seizure.

### b. Assessing Jurisdiction

**i. Quasi-in-rem (Illinois)**

Per *Shaffer*, it's difficult to entirely separate a party's interest in their property (Hermione's bank account in IL) from the party itself. And, the case for jurisdiction over Hermione herself in Illinois is very questionable — Hermione is a Missouri citizen, who accumulated debts in Missouri. It's hard to make a specific jurisdiction case that related the forum, the subject matter of the litigation, and the defendant. While Hermione purposefully availed herself of IL as a forum, her bank account is not closely related to the subject matter of the litigation (the credit card debt).

**ii. In rem (North Carolina)**

This is less problematic because the broom was located in NC at the time of the seizure and NC presumably has an interest in enforcing the law within its territory. But, Fenrir and Hermione did not agree to buy the broom in NC, did not purchase the broom from WeBuyDebts in NC, and did not advertise the broom for sale in NC. Further, Hermione did not purposefully avail herself of NC or have any knowledge that the broom was brought to NC (nothing suggests she even knew of Fenrir's driving route).

<!-- page: 2 -->

## 2. Attacking the Constitutionality of the Statutory Scheme

### a. Procedural Due Process

To attack the constitutionality of the statutory scheme, I would assess:

**i. Who is the decision maker?** If the decision-maker is a judge, as in the NC seizure, the confiscation is more likely to survive a due process challenge. However, if the decision-maker is a clerk, its constitutionality is more suspect.

**ii. Does the party seeking relief have a pre-existing interest in the property being confiscated?** This factor weighs heavily against the scheme in the quasi-in-rem context of the IL seizure, since there's no interest in the property. In the NC context, WeBuyDebts retained a property interest in the broom until the full price was paid. So, it had a preexisting interest in the property being confiscated.

**iii. Is the seizure purely to establish jurisdiction or is it for security purposes?** Here, the IL bank account seizure is purely to establish jurisdiction so it is relatively less compelling. However, WeBuyDebts has a valid argument that the NC broom seizure is required to prevent debtors from absconding with the property. On the other hand, there seems to be a limited risk of destroying the property, since that would place the debtor in an even more financially imperiled position. This cuts against the IL seizure's constitutionality and in favor of the NC seizure's constitutionality.

**iv. Does the applicant for the seizure have to show probable cause or meet a lesser burden of proof?** In the NC context, WeBuyDebts has at least shown something more than conclusory allegations. However in IL, there is little information. The former is constitutionally stronger than the latter.

**v. Can the dispute be resolved by uncontested documentary evidence?** If not, there should be a hearing. This weighs in favor of the constitutionality of the NC seizure because it's a case that can be resolved on the papers. They've provided documentation of the purchase agreement and the missed payment. The other seizure is more difficult to determine. In any event, there was not a lot of supportive documentation provided.

**vi. Is a bond and counterbond procedure available?** If not, this would weaken the scheme's constitutionality. No information is provided on this.

**vii. Is a hearing available either pre- or post-seizure?** A pre-seizure hearing would be best. But, an immediately post-seizure hearing would also strengthen the scheme. If no hearing is available, I would attack on this basis.

### b. To Attack Jurisdiction

**i. Quasi-in-rem:** The fact that the statute allows for seizure in quasi-in-rem context may be constitutionally suspect per *Shaffer*.

**ii. In rem:** Less problematic. Might argue that seizing mobile items (like racing brooms or cars) could lead to litigating in a forum that has no connection to the suit. The NC case is a good example of that.

## 2019-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-02-campbell-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Procedural Due Process

In both suits, there was probably inadequate notice. WeBuyDebts had to make reasonable efforts to notify Hermione under *Mullane*. In the IL suit, mailing to the PO box could be inadequate. WeBuyDebts says it has good investigative abilities, so maybe they should have figured out the PO box was closed. If they were told by the post office that Hermione no longer received mail there, they would likely have to take further action (*Flowers*). They also had her address from the broom contract, so mailing only to the PO box seems unreasonable. In the NC suit, they did not even allege making any effort to provide notice, and they had her address, so they most likely don't meet the *Mullane* standard.

The IL suit probably required a pre-deprivation hearing. *Eldridge* factors likely favor Hermione. WeBuyDebts' interest was just based on their $700 debt, which seems small for a large debt-buying company. No pre-existing interest in the account. They could likely afford to wait for Hermione to pay. No evidence that Hermione would attempt to evade collection. Hermione has pressing interest in her bank account. She's poor and needs her money now. Failing to have a hearing pre-deprivation increases risk of wrongly preventing Hermione from accessing her whole account (which has $200 more than WeBuyDebts is even asking for). *Cf. Doehr*. Hearing would allow judge to conclude that seizure was unnecessary, or to issue a narrowly tailored seizure order (e.g., set aside $700 in the account).

Maybe the NC suit required a pre-deprivation hearing, too, but the argument isn't as strong. *Eldridge* factors don't clearly favor Hermione. WeBuyDebts retained interest in broom under the contract, and Fenrir was about to go and sell it in a way that would impair WeBuyDebts' ability to reclaim it. Hermione and Fenrir likewise have interest in broom, but not in a way that outweighs WeBuyDebts'. Relatively minimal risk of erroneous deprivation without hearing beforehand because the contract and the non-payment is really all the evidence necessary to establish that WeBuyDebts is entitled to the broom.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Personal Jurisdiction

NC probably didn't have jurisdiction over the broom. The item needs to be in the territory of the state, and the broom wasn't in NC when the order was issued. But it was in NC when the order was executed, and perhaps that's enough. But under *Shaffer*, Hermione and Fenrir must also have an interest in having the broom in NC. Hermione can say they didn't have such an interest. Fenrir was only taking the broom through NC to get to VA to sell it. The broom's presence in NC was incidental. But WeBuyDebts will say that Fenrir nevertheless intentionally brought the broom into NC, so Hermione and Fenrir did have an interest in having it there. On balance, it seems unfair for NC to exercise jurisdiction over the broom when it was only going to be there for a couple hours in Fenrir's car en route to VA.

IL may not have had jurisdiction over Hermione's account. Account was in IL, but did Hermione have interest in having it there? She will say no. It's just where she happened to bank. But she did physically go to IL to bank at Hogwarts, so it's clearer that she's intentionally maintaining an IL bank account. Not just online banking with a bank that happens to be in IL. Since the bank is close enough for her to go regularly, it seems fair for her to be haled into IL court over the account. Contacts are definitely stronger than in *Shaffer*.

## Statute

Compare statutory requirements against those in *Vitek*. *Vitek* was about deprivation of liberty, not property, though, so the statute can perhaps be more flexible. But the seizures here are pre-judgment, whereas *Vitek* is for the whole process, so statute should perhaps be stricter.

What the statute probably needs, in light of the caveats above: reasonable attempt at notice, as well as a hearing either before or shortly after the seizure. At that hearing, parties can present and cross-examine witnesses. There is a neutral decision maker. The decision maker sets forth the basis of its decision in a written statement, or at least has to make a finding that the evidentiary standard is met (judge cannot just rubber stamp requests for seizure). And the party receives notice of these rights. If the statute doesn't provide for any of these things, Hermione can argue it doesn't meet the constitutional minimum for due process.

<!-- page: 3 -->

I would also look for the evidentiary standard for a pre-judgment seizure (lower standard = less likely to be constitutional). A bond requirement would make the statute stronger, and a counter-bond option (or other way for Hermione to retain access to her property) even more so. A right to interlocutory appeal could also strengthen the scheme. The statute should also give adequate time to get an attorney and prepare a defense (*Roller*). Absence of those things strengthens the argument against constitutionality.

Also, the statute most likely would violate the due process clause to the extent it purported to confer jurisdiction over property where the *Shaffer* requirements aren't met.

## 2019-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-02-lowens-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

I advise a limited appearance to contest jurisdiction over the person/property in both cases. *Shaffer* says that quasi-in-rem and in rem suits must be assessed using the *International Shoe* framework, so we look to whether the property seized is physically in the jurisdiction, and to the extent of the contacts among the "defendant's" interest in the property, the subject matter of the litigation, and the forum (although after *Burnham*, we don't know whether how much of *Shaffer* is good law).

**A), Illinois:** Arguably in 2019 bank accounts should be considered to "be" where the account holder is, not where the bank is — some people access their accounts online. But Hermione regularly traveled to the Illinois branch, so that argument doesn't work here; the account is in Illinois.

**A), NC:** The property was in NC.

**B), Illinois:** There is a good case Hermione did not intend or want her account to be in Illinois — it's a coincidence and even an *inconvenience* that it is there. As her attorney, I would point to *Shaffer* and argue that it is unfair and unreasonable to hold that the Illinois court has personal jurisdiction over my largely sedentary Missouri client's property when she has no interest in its being there. Unfortunately, this case might distinguish from *Shaffer* because Hermione regularly traveled to Illinois to access the account, so it's harder to say she did not associate the asset with Illinois. Moreover, the bank only existed in Illinois (perhaps deliberately to be subject to fewer regulations, which allows them to offer better interest rates), so there is, arguably, something inherently Illinois-specific about it. All that said, the bank account has no relation to the underlying dispute between Hermione and WeBuyDebts.

**B), NC:** The defendants had no interest in the property being in NC; it's pure coincidence. If *Shaffer* is taken seriously, this case should probably be dismissed (though the case would be even stronger if Fenrir accidentally went to NC by a wrong turn or somehow didn't know he was in NC).

We have even stronger procedural due process claims. Before a court seizes property, there must be reasonable attempts at notice.

**Illinois:** Though Plaintiff did make an effort to contact Hermione using an address on file (a baseline set by *Mullane*), a second attempt after unsuccessful delivery may have been necessary (*Flowers*). The equities favor Hermione: (1) WeBuyDebts.com has investigative capabilities, so looking further would be easy. (2) Some people/entities, including Hogwarts Bank *and another division of WeBuyDebts*, knew her address. (3) There is reason to believe that had she been contacted, Hermione would have acted differently — she could've paid off the $400 debt with the money in her account.

**NC:** In rem for real property does not require notice because it is assumed that the owner would keep tabs on her property and realize it were seized (*Pennoyer*). But the broom is not real property. It seems like the baseline of using the address you have on file (*Mullane*) should apply, and WeBuyDebts did not mail anything to Hermione.

## Question 2

We can attack UPSC's constitutionality on due process grounds because it provides insufficient opportunity to be heard before harming the defendant. *Sniadach* and *Doehr* are informative.

<!-- page: 2 -->

First, how does UPSC weigh Plaintiff's interests in seeking a remedy? Does UPSC require showing that the property is likely to soon become unreachable? Hermione never moves and her bank account was stable for months — we have a strong case seizing it was overkill (*Sniadach*). Does it consider the parties' pre-litigation interests in the property (*Doehr*)? That factor would also cut against seizing the bank account, though not the broom.

Second, what safeguards does UPSC include to prevent erroneously harming the Defendant? Since this case concerns property, not liberty, *Vitek* factors like opportunity to present witnesses or be represented is too much to ask. But aspects of "opportunity to be heard" like written notice (see discussion of *Mullane*/*Flowers*, supra), sharing documentary evidence, and some sort of hearing, especially in 2019 when communication is virtually costless, seems like a reasonable baseline. In Hermione's cases, inadequate notice and communication impacted the bank account seizure because Hermione could (and probably would) have paid what she owed if notified (thereby also advancing a government interest in facilitating out of court settlement). The broom case was a "documents case," resolved by the contract and the defendants' failure to pay. There, a pre-seizure hearing might not have affected much. Next, under UPSC, do judges or clerks authorize seizure? Judges mitigate risk to defendants because they're less likely to make major errors in the first instance, especially if a complicated contract matter with lots of documents like the broom case. UPSC should also consider the effects that seizure will have on the defendant (see, e.g., *Eldridge*). In the case of a checking account, it could be very damaging: automatic payments or outstanding checks will be bounced, and in Hermione's case, almost all of her assets became unavailable. Additionally or alternatively, Plaintiff could have to post a bond or defendants have a chance to post a counterbond (*Sniadach*) in order to mitigate the effects of seizure.

## 2019-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-02-wang-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Q1

**i.** Applying the three-part *Matthews v. Eldridge* test, procedural due process is triggered in the IL seizure. Hermione's interest here is important: in the bank account is all her money to support the life of herself and her children, including rent, food, and heating. WeBuyDebts.com also has a property interest ($700), but the risk of erroneous deprivation and the value of additional procedural safeguards are great. The same-day one-judge judgment, the one-sided, conclusory allegation in the complaint, and the lack of bonds posted create great risks [*Doehr*]. No extraordinary situation justifies the deprivation of due process before seizure [*Fuentes*], and no post-seizure hearing alleviates the harm [*Mitchell*]. According to procedural due process, then, Hermione should have reasonable notice [*Mullane*] (particularly, more than one attempt [*Jones v. Flowers*], especially given WeBuyDebts.com's "beyond outstanding" investigative team), and the opportunity to be heard [*Sniadach*].

For the NC seizure, Hermione's interest is still great ($20,000 in profit to improve her livelihood). Although risk of erroneous deprivation is smaller (more specific, sworn affidavit-based complaint and purchase/sale agreement), the judgment is still same-day, unnoticed, and unbonded, without the opportunity to be heard or receive relief. Further, signing the purchase/sale agreement doesn't waive basic procedural due process rights [*Fuentes*].

**ii.** Quasi-in-rem suits are also subject to in personam jurisdiction analysis, requiring minimum contacts [*Shaffer*], and attaching the IL property is only ok if the cause of action arises out of it. However, the cause of action arises out of failure to pay AmEx in MO, so the IL court had no jurisdiction to seize the bank account. One can argue that Hermione purposefully availed herself in and benefited from IL by continuing to use the IL account and physically traveling there (by bus) to access the bank account, but it might not be fair or reasonable for Hermione to be expected to be sued in IL simply because of that [*International Shoe*]. Also, holding stocks in the forum state is not sufficient by itself in *Shaffer*, so neither should having a bank account.

For the in rem suit, NC is the domicile of neither Hermione (MO) nor Fenrir (SC), but this might not be relevant as it's the broom that's being sued, and the broom is seized while in NC. However, it is unclear whether in-state seizure is alone sufficient [*Burnham*], and further, the case is distinguishable in that the *Burnham* husband "availed himself of the benefits provided by CA," while Fenrir/the broom was just passing by NC (the contract was with dealer in VA; Fenrir purposefully availed himself in VA, not NC).

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Q2

**i. Procedural Due Process:**

1. **Notice:** (1) UPSC needs to require notice reasonably calculated, under all circumstances, to apprise interested parties of the pendency of the action and afford them an opportunity to present their objections [*Mullane*]. If UPSC now permits WeBuyDebts.com's attempt at notice, it probably should not. Hermione's apartment address is readily available; mailing to her address involves small expense; and receiving notice of lawsuits at home address is traditional and expected. (2) UPSC needs to require notice to include detailed explanation of reasons for actions being taken, and the defendant's right (e.g., to appear by a written pleading) [*Aguchak*, *Finberg*].

2. **Opportunity to be heard:** We might argue that UPSC needs to provide opportunity to be heard, and further, that such opportunity should be before the seizure [*Fuentes*].
   - a) The default period should be reasonable to minimize risk of erroneous deprivation, allowing time for defendant to answer. The current UPSC allowed writ of seizure to be served to the bank the day after the court order, giving the defendant no time to find a lawyer or prepare for the case.
   - b) The UPSC should provide opportunity for defendant to get relief to minimize risk of erroneous deprivation.

3. **Level of specificity:** UPSC needs to require plaintiff to provide specific information before the seizure [*Doehr*]. Bottom line, conclusory allegations shouldn't be sufficient. This is more of an issue in IL than in NC. For example, it could require statements written under the penalty of perjury, establishment of likelihood of success on merits, and documentary evidence (much of which was present in NC). Constitutionality of the current UPSC is dubious as it allowed the IL seizure.

4. **Bond:** Constitutionality would've been stronger had UPSC required bond to be posted, and allowed counterbond from defendant to get the property back.

5. **Relationship of the lawsuit and the property seized:** UPSC needs to require a strong relationship: plaintiff cannot attach a property just in case she wins [*Doehr*]. Unclear whether the IL seizure satisfies this.

**ii. Jurisdiction:**

1. The UPSC should require either in personam jurisdiction to be established for quasi-in-rem suits, or that the cause of action arises out of the property [*Shaffer*], as *Pennoyer* ruled that the Due Process Clause poses limits on in personam jurisdiction. Thus, minimum contacts should be required [*International Shoe*]. Since minimum contacts isn't satisfied even when shareholders had stocks in DE [*Shaffer*], Hermione having a bank account in IL shouldn't satisfy it either.

2. The in rem lawsuit poses serious notice and opportunity to be heard issues. In this case, it is a broom moving with the possessor, so the possessor can be notified. However, if it were a property elsewhere, the possessor might not receive notice.

# ASSIGNMENT 2019-assignment-03 — Assignment 3 (Graded) — Civil Procedure 2 & 6, 2019

Source: content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-03.md

## 2019-assignment-03 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3 (Graded)
## Civil Procedure 2 & 6, 2019
### Professor Jim Greiner

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** Thursday, Nov 14, 2019
**Due date/time:** 9 am on Monday, Nov 18, 2019
**Word limit:** 850 words
**Time limit:** 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is ungraded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

**Suggestions for your answer:**

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Substantive Assignment

Consider the following three short hypotheticals.

**Hypothetical one:** South Carolina Rule of Civil Procedure 11 requires that a lawyer (for a represented plaintiff) or the plaintiff herself (if unrepresented) must sign every page of the complaint. Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 11, however, requires only that a "pleading" (a term that includes a complaint) "must be signed by [the] attorney of record . . . or by a party personally if the party is unrepresented," a requirement that means only a single signature on a multi-page complaint. A plaintiff files a multi-page diversity complaint in South Carolina federal court bearing an attorney signature only on the last page, and the defendant moves to dismiss for failure to comply with S.C.R.C.P. 11. By the time the South Carolina federal court adjudicates the motion, the limitations period has run, so a new lawsuit would be time-barred.

**Hypothetical two:** Texas Rule of Civil Procedure 65 addresses preliminary injunctions. It provides that to obtain a preliminary injunction, a movant must convince a court that the balance of the following four factors favors the issuance of the injunction: likelihood of success on the merits, irreparable harm, balance of the equities, and the public interest. From our discussion of *Thane-Coat*, you know in the Fifth Circuit (Texas is in the Fifth Circuit), FRCP 65 makes the four items just listed prerequisites (meaning a movant must prevail on each one), as opposed to factors to be balanced. Assume for the purposes of this question that the rule from the Fifth Circuit (*i.e.*, that the four listed items are prerequisites, not factors) is the rule in all federal courts across the United States. A plaintiff files a diversity action in a Texas federal court and moves for a preliminary injunction. After a hearing, the judge hearing the motion informs the parties that she finds the plaintiff's evidence in support of the motion extremely strong with respect to irreparable harm, balance of the equities, and the public interest. But she thinks that the plaintiff's showing on likelihood of success on the merits, while more than tenuous, was not enough to tip this consideration to the plaintiff's favor, although she acknowledges that the plaintiff might well be able to develop persuasive evidence in the next several weeks. The movant plaintiff argues that the injunction should nevertheless issue under the standards of Texas Rule 65.

**Hypothetical three:** On October 1, 2019, Congress passes, and the President signs, the following statute: "In any civil action commenced after the passage of this statute in which the federal district court sits in diversity, the applicable period of limitation shall be three years." On October 21, a plaintiff files a diversity action in a federal district court. The otherwise applicable limitations period of the state in which the district court sits is two years. The plaintiff filed the action approximately 30 months after the events that triggered the lawsuit.

<!-- page: 3 -->

## Questions

**Question 1:** Analyze these three hypotheticals under the *Erie* tests we discussed in class. You may group some of them together if doing so would facilitate your answer, or analyze them separately, as you please.

**Question 2:** Take a step back. Briefly identify what is relevantly similar and different about these hypotheticals. Your analysis will need to include some speculation as to the nature of the policies and values the relevant state laws/rules and federal laws/rules are designed to further, as well as the justifications for the *Erie* doctrine we discussed in class. In your view, are the *Erie* tests you applied in Question 1 sufficiently flexible to address the variation in the importance of the states and federal interests implicated? Why or why not?

## 2019-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-03-baker-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Q1

**H1:** If state law applies, the case should be dismissed. If federal law applies, the motion to dismiss should fail.
**H2:** If state law applies, a preliminary injunction should issue. If federal law applies, it shouldn't.
**H3:** If state law applies, the case is dismissible for being filed outside the statute of limitations. If federal law applies, the case isn't time-barred.

Under *York*, state law should govern in H1 and H3 because the choice of law is outcome-determinative. H2's preliminary injunction, formally, isn't outcome-determinative — instead, affecting the litigants' rights before final adjudication on the merits. However, because the court found significant risk of irreparable harm to the movant and *York* sought to avoid "substantially different result[s]" in state and federal court, the preliminary injunction decision does seem outcome-determinative of harm to the movant. Thus, state law should arguably apply.

Under the *Hanna* majority test, federal law applies if valid and applicable. Federal courts have never found the FRCP invalid under the REA or Constitution. And because FRCP 11 and 65 could be considered to "really regulate procedure" under *Sibbach*, they are both valid. (This includes H2's judicial interpretation of FRCP 65.) The federal statute in H3 is also valid because it was passed via bicameralism and presentment and is constitutional under Congress' power to create the lower federal courts and the Necessary and Proper Clause. Here, Scalia and Ginsburg's interpretations of the test diverge. If Scalia finds federal law applicable to the situation at hand (which is likely), he believes federal law should govern. Ginsburg would identify a substantive policy being advanced by state law and, if possible, construe the federal law to avoid a conflict.

Harlan's *Hanna* concurrence considers whether the choice of law would affect parties' primary (pre-litigation) conduct. If so, state law is substantive and should apply. If not, the law is procedural and federal law applies.

**H1**

*Scalia:* FRCP 11 is applicable because it addresses complaint signature requirements, which this dispute hinges on. Federal law controls.

*Harlan:* SC's signature requirement governs a litigation document so doesn't affect parties' primary conduct (except possibly in choosing a meticulous lawyer). Federal law applies.

*Ginsburg:* Ginsburg may have difficulty finding a substantive policy goal in SCRCP 11. Two possibilities could be an inducement to prepare complaints with care or a desire to prevent fraudulent legal filings. Both are fairly weak. That said, with such a finding, Ginsburg could easily construe the FRCP 11 mandate as a floor (not a ceiling), thus avoiding a conflict with SCRCP 11 and allowing SC's stiffer requirement to govern.

**H2**

*Scalia:* FRCP 65 (as judicially interpreted) directly applies to the issuance of preliminary injunctions. Federal law controls.

*Harlan:* Nothing about preliminary injunctions affects parties' pre-litigation behavior. Federal law applies.

<!-- page: 2 -->

*Ginsburg:* A clear state policy goal is being permissive/flexible in granting preliminary injunctions. However, it would be hard for Ginsburg to narrowly interpret FRCP 65 as inapplicable. FRCP 65 will probably apply.

**H3**

*Scalia:* The statute is applicable because it speaks to all diversity civil actions. The federal statute of limitations will govern.

*Harlan:* The applicable statute of limitations generally won't affect primary conduct except for sophisticated parties like insurers. If that's enough, the state statute of limitations controls. Otherwise, the federal statute of limitations controls.

*Ginsburg:* There are clear state policies implicated by a statute of limitations: desire to put conflict to rest, limit the specter of liability, and compel timely discovery. Though a stretch, Ginsburg — prompted by federalism concerns — may read the federal statute of limitations as applicable only absent a pre-established limitations period for a given cause of action, interpreting away conflict with federal law. Accordingly, the state statute of limitations would govern.

## Q2

The four *Erie* tests are insufficiently flexible to address variations in the magnitude of state/federal interests implicated. In H1, a court might find (contrary, apparently, to the judgment of state lawmakers) that requiring multiple signatures provides minimal benefit compared to one signature. However, H2's differing standards for the issuance of preliminary injunctions could lead to stark differences in substantive outcomes for litigants. Despite this, H1 and H2 are subject to the same valid-applicable analysis for FRCP in Scalia and Ginsburg's versions of the *Hanna* majority test. Ginsburg's framework is more flexible because it wrestles with state policy interests and federalism, yet in H2, it seems especially difficult for her to interpret away conflict between state and federal law, despite a far more state-interest-encumbering result when federal law applies in H2 compared with H1. Given that *Erie* aimed to avoid forum-shopping and "inequitable administration of the laws," it seems problematic that choice of federal or state forum of adjudication would so drastically affect the rights of litigants.

The *York* outcome-determinative test is inflexible in its own way, almost always favoring state law. It thus needlessly prevents uniform procedure in diversity cases, even when the ultimate results are negligible.

Harlan's test comes closest to taking into account the gravity of state concerns, worrying about effects on primary conduct, an area generally left for the states to regulate. However, it still precludes federal overreach outside the realm of primary conduct. In H3, for example, when the federal government broadly preempts all state statutes of limitations in diversity cases, it gives rise to serious federalism concerns.

## 2019-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-03-fife-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

### Hypothetical one: Federal law.

*York* outcome-determinative test favors state law because only the state signature requirement, and not the federal, warrants dismissal.

Under *Hanna* majority, both Scalia and Ginsburg would apply FRCP as valid and applicable. FRCPs are valid under the REA if they "really regulate procedure," *Sibbach*, and do not abridge or modify a substantive right. A signature requirement doesn't affect a substantive right and clearly regulates procedures of the court.

Here, both Scalia and Ginsburg agree FRCP is applicable. Scalia looks at the text of the FRCP and it's explicit on signature requirement. Ginsburg wants to look at the state's interest and interpret the FRCP narrowly. Here, state may have interest in ensuring parties carefully review court filings. She could say SCRCP is compatible as FRCP sets a floor, not a ceiling. Without more facts or legislative record, the state interest doesn't seem strong enough to warrant this strained interpretation.

Harlan will look at how the rule affects pre-litigation conduct. A signature requirement wouldn't affect a party's behavior unless they planned on sloppy lawyering. Apply federal law.

### Hypothetical two: State law.

*York* likely permits the FRCP — a preliminary injunction is a preliminary ruling before the court fully adjudicates the claim, so it's likely not outcome determinative under *York* where *York* involved a statute of limitations dismissal preventing the entire claim from going forward.

Under both Scalia and Ginsburg interpretations the law is valid because it "really regulates procedure" and does not modify, abridge, or enlarge a substantive right.

Ginsburg will apply TRCP by interpreting the FRCP to accommodate Texas' strong desire to preserve judicial discretion without overly formalistic constraints. She will apply *Walker*-esque analysis and say FRCP is just a default and bend the FRCP beyond recognition to accommodate state concerns. TRCP applies.

Scalia will just look at federal law and not worry about the state law at all. FRCP speaks directly to injunctions, so therefore apply FRCP.

Harlan will apply state law. FRCP discourages preliminary injunctions, which makes corporations and policymakers bolder because it diminishes the effectiveness of a valuable litigation tool from parties trying to stop a corporate merger or new government policy. Similar to *Cohen*, the FRCP affects pre-litigation behavior.

### Hypothetical three: Federal law.

<!-- page: 2 -->

If faced with a motion to dismiss, *York* applies state law because the suit is time-barred under state law, but not under federal statute.

*Hanna* majority both apply federal law. They'll agree it's valid as necessary and proper to Congress' creation of the federal courts, passed through bicameralism and presentment. Congress intended to reach the issue — the statute specifically mentions diversity jurisdiction and there's no language permitting exceptions.

Scalia applies statute — Congress validly spoke directly to the matter at hand. For Ginsburg, the state interest is hard to see — perhaps efficient dispute resolution or ensuring availability of evidence, but a statute validly enacted by Congress provides less flexibility to accommodate state interests, especially when they're minimal. Statute, therefore, applies.

Harlan will apply statute — statute of limitations mostly affects behavior when considering litigation, not pre-litigation primary conduct.

## Question 2

None of the tests capture the diversity of interests and the magnitude to which they are implicated across the hypos. The first hypo minimally implicates federalism and equal protection. Federal signature rule hardly infringes on a meaningful state interest and forum shopping would only occur if a plaintiff intended to do bad lawyering.

The second hypo more greatly affects equal protection/forum shopping because a litigant will have different remedies depending on where they file, which gives preference to out of state litigants seeking injunctions. Moreover, the hypo strongly implicates federalism because the standards of preliminary injunctions involve serious policy decisions about whether we want to provide judges discretion or if we want to be really certain before issuing an injunction.

The final hypo speaks to the nature of the law — when Congress legislates, the ball is out of the judiciary's court and unless Congress surpassed its authority, the federal bench must listen. So, when Congress acts in accordance with an enumerated power, the federal courts can feel comfortable applying federal law because acting according to statute diminishes separation of power concerns and the supremacy clause justifies deference to federal law.

Each of the tests are overly focused on a single *Erie* interest and therefore too inflexible to capture all of these interests and their varying degrees of magnitude. Scalia and *York* are far too formalistic. *York* focuses too myopically on forum shopping, while Scalia's deference to text only accounts for separation of powers. Ginsburg, by contrast, focuses predominantly on federalism without much regard for litigation gamesmanship and likely contravenes separation of powers by morphing FRCP to accommodate state law. Moreover, Harlan's test thinks only about preventing litigation gamesmanship, not separation of powers or the nature of the law.

## 2019-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-03-hine-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

### Hypothetical one:

Under *York*, state law applies if the difference in rules is outcome-determinative. Here, the state rule would be applied because the lawsuit is dismissed under SCRCP but not under FRCP (attorney signed only the last page of the complaint, which is allowed under FRCP 11, but requires dismissal under SRCP 11).

Under the *Hanna* majority (Scalia) valid and applicable test, the FRCP would be applied. FRCP 11 is valid because REA § 2072 empowers the Supreme Court to promulgate the rule and it does not abridge, enlarge, or modify substantive rights — it "really regulates procedure" under *Sibbach*. Under a broad reading of FRCP 11, the Rule is applicable because it controls the application of signatures on a complaint.

The narrow *Hanna* test (Ginsburg in *Shady Grove*) favors the SCRCP. It's not clear how important the state interest is in its signature rule, but a narrow reading of its applicability is possible — because FRCP 11 requires a signature but does not address how many signatures are required, it can be construed to not apply to the issue at hand.

Under Harlan's *Hanna* concurrence, we examine whether the rule would affect the parties' pre-litigation conduct. The number of signatures required on the complaint would not affect the pre-litigation conduct — it would only affect the actions of the lawyers in the litigation. Thus, the rule is procedural and FRCP 11 would apply.

### Hypothetical two:

Unlike in hypothetical one, this is a rare case where the *York* test is going to favor the federal rule. Since any ruling on the injunction will merely be preliminary, the suit will go forward either way and the choice of rule is not outcome-determinative.

Under the *Hanna* majority (Scalia) valid and applicable test, the result is the same as in hypo one: the FRCP would be applied. However, it's a bit of a closer call on validity. The application of factors in a preliminary injunction comes closer to modifying substantive rights than the signature rule. However, the FRCP is always going to be found to be procedural per *Sibbach*'s "really regulates procedure" test. Because the issue is a preliminary injunction, FRCP 65 is clearly applicable.

The result is the same under the narrow *Hanna* (Ginsburg) test. Though the state presumably has an interest in providing preliminary injunctions to plaintiffs who satisfy the Rule 65 factors and can develop persuasive merits arguments (though who might not have the requisite evidence right away), it is difficult to construe the federal law narrowly given that FRCP 65 directly applies to preliminary injunctions.

Under Harlan's *Hanna* concurrence, the choice of rule is not going to affect the parties' pre-litigation behavior: FRCP 65 applies.

<!-- page: 2 -->

### Hypothetical three:

As above, the *York* test is outcome-determinative, so the state rule would apply in this situation.

Under the *Hanna* majority (Scalia) test, we go with the federal statute if it is valid and applicable. The federal statute is valid because it was passed through bicameralism and presentment pursuant to Congress's power under the Necessary and Proper Clause. It applies because the issue is whether the suit was filed within the limitations period.

Under the narrow *Hanna* (Ginsburg) test, the result is the same. The federal statute is valid and applicable. Because it's a federal statute there is probably less of an inclination to construe it narrowly (but see *Gasperini*); regardless, it is difficult to say that the federal limitations period does not apply to a question about whether the suit was filed within the limitations period.

Harlan in *Hanna* probably favors the federal statute because statutes of limitation tend to not affect a party's pre-litigation conduct. However, if the parties are sophisticated corporate entities, the applicable limitations period could affect pre-litigation conduct (such as insurance) based on the perceived litigation risk.

## Question 2

The *Erie* tests applied in Question 1 are not sufficiently flexible to address the variation in the importance of state and federal interests implicated. The *York* test is totally inflexible because it will nearly always choose the state law no matter how compelling the federal interest is (and if it chooses the federal law it will be because there's no difference in outcome of the case, not because of the strength of the interests involved).

The two *Hanna* tests are also inflexible because the inquiry is based on the source of the law — statutory, regulatory, or common law — rather than the policy rationales underlying the laws. Thus, in hypotheticals one and two, the *Hanna* majority test chooses the federal rule, even though the state interest is stronger in regulating whether plaintiffs can receive preliminary injunctions than regulating the number of signatures on a pleading. And the narrow *Hanna* Ginsburg test actually reaches the counterintuitive result: it chooses the state signature rule in hypothetical one due to narrow construction of applicability to accommodate state interests — but the interest is stronger in hypothetical two.

Harlan's *Hanna* concurrence seems to provide the most coherent test to distinguish between substantive and procedural law and thus is probably best able to address the variation in importance of state and federal interests. Of course, Harlan's test made no difference vis-à-vis the varied interests in hypotheticals one and two, but in general it will be more likely to accommodate state interests as the primary regulators of behavior in cases where the rules affect pre-litigation conduct.

## 2019-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2019-assignment-03-phillips-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Q1

**Hypo1:** *York* test results in state law because outcome-determinative: suit is possible under federal — but not state — law. *Hanna* concurrence favors federal law because the pleadings signature requirement would not affect people's pre-litigation conduct, so it is therefore procedural. Under "Scalia" test, FRCP 11 is valid because Congress validly passed the REA and delegated authority to promulgate the Rules. This Rule probably qualifies as "arguably procedural" under *Sibbach* test because it doesn't appear to enlarge/abridge/modify any substantive right — so therefore valid. FRCP 11 also clearly applicable here since it articulates different standards for the exact same thing. Since the federal rule is valid and applicable, it controls because of supremacy. Under "Ginsburg" test, the Rule is similarly valid, but probably not applicable. Ginsburg would read narrowly and say the Rule doesn't conflict with state law because both require the same thing — one signature — and are therefore not in conflict. The state law just puts an extra signature requirement on top of the federal minimum, so state law controls because the Rule isn't applicable.

**Hypo2:** The injunction would go opposite ways under state/federal law, but unclear whether this is an "outcome" for the *York* test. In theory, whether the preliminary injunction is issued has no bearing on the later judgment, so this wouldn't be outcome-determinative. But functionally, the grant/refusal of a preliminary injunction can greatly affect the status quo and parties' rights — and often results in settlement — so this probably qualifies as outcome-determinative and state law would apply. *Hanna* concurrence: few specific situations where differing standards re the availability of injunctive relief would create opportunistic pre-litigation behavior, so federal law probably applies. Like Hypo1, the Rule would be valid for both Ginsburg/Scalia tests. Further, both Ginsburg/Scalia would probably find the Rule applicable. There's little grounds not to find a conflict; TXRCP and FRCP diverge on how the same 4 factors apply to the exact same analysis. Since FRCP is valid and applicable, both the Ginsburg and Scalia tests would yield federal law.

**Hypo3:** *York* = same as Hypo1. *Hanna* concurrence = similar to Hypo2, but statute of limitations difference would likely affect peoples' pre-litigation conduct (ex: SoL length changes how companies value litigation risks and affects the price of insurance). However, probably only changes the behavior of the relatively few parties who strategically think specifically about litigation *ex ante*; few regular people are concerned about how long they have to bring suit until *after* they are considering suing. For both Scalia/Ginsburg, federal statute is valid because passed through bicameralism/presentment and appears constitutional (Article I, § 8). Statute seems even more clearly applicable than Hypo2: applying federal SoL would entirely contravene any state SoL and vice-versa. Since federal statute is both valid and applicable, federal law applies.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Q2

All three hypos concern the same basic problem: whether or not federal law overrides state law when the two prescribe different things on the same issue. The primary difference between hypos is the importance of the interests that the state/federal provisions attempt to govern. In Hypo1, the interest at issue is how closely the attorney/plaintiff needs to read the complaint, ostensibly for the purpose of promoting effective assistance of counsel and judicial economy. In Hypo2, the interest is the availability of pre-trial injunctive relief, which involves determinations balancing fairness and due process. In Hypo3, the interest is whether the plaintiff can sue at all, weighing fairness and access to the judicial system against economy and finality.

The *Erie* tests — as currently articulated — are incapable of distinguishing the differences in how important/serious these interests are. Promoting careful lawyering via signature stipulations for pleadings might well be a worthy state policy goal, but it bears on a right that's clearly less important than provisions controlling whether suit can be brought *at all*: it's irrelevant if 0/1/all pages of the complaint are signed if you're time-barred from suing. Ironically, under current *Erie* doctrine, Hypo3 appears to be more conclusively "procedural" than Hypo1, even though the statute of limitations requirement regulates a far more significant right.

Current *Erie* doctrine tends to promote supremacy and uniformity. The FRCP, for example, usually control over state law, helping to establish uniform rules and expectations across federal courts in different states. Few statutes/rules ever qualify as invalid, and the determination of whether state/federal law controls hinges on (contorted) decisions regarding "applicability". This isn't necessarily *bad*, as uniformity and equivalency/predictability of forum across different states is one of the primary goals of the federal courts in diversity cases.

<!-- page: 3 -->

But this current scheme isn't really true to the philosophical/constitutional *point* of *Erie* doctrine: that state law will control re important stuff, and federal law will control ticky-tacky mechanics of litigation. Bizarrely — as Q1 illustrates — our current system would likely prescribe state law for the *least important issue* (whether every page of the complaint must be signed) and federal law for the *most important* (whether you can sue at all). This appears totally opposite to the philosophical justification for *Erie*, and pays little concern to principles of federalism like deference to state lawmakers (in Hypos 2/3, for example, federal law would essentially be invalidating state laws controlling important rights).

## 2020-assignment-01 · Assignment prompt
