2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is graded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

### Suggestions for your answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of a lengthy quotation of the standard.

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Substantive Assignment

Assume the following (some of which is legally inaccurate, and all of which is factually fictional), for the purposes of this assignment.

Historically, an action for negligence encompassed four elements: (i) that the defendant owed a duty to the plaintiff, (ii) that the defendant breached that duty by failing to exercise due care, (iii) that the breach of due care caused the plaintiff's injury, and (iv) that the plaintiff suffered damages (including pain and suffering) as a result. Negligence was historically an action at law.

Historically, an action for breach of a duty owed to a third-party beneficiary to a contract, also known as a "third-party breach" lawsuit, encompassed the following elements: (i) that the defendant engaged in a contract, (ii) that the primary purpose of the contract was to benefit a third party named in that contract, or a class of individuals named in that contract, (iii) that the defendant breached the contract, (iv) that the plaintiff suffered damages as a result (including pain and suffering). The remedy for a third-party breach action was traditionally an order from the chancellor to the defendant to perform the contract or, if that were impossible, for disgorgement to the plaintiff of the amount of money the defendant had received in payments for the contract. Third-party breach was historically an action in equity.

Assume the following background: Invoking its powers under U.S. Const. amends. XIV–XV, in January of 2018, Congress passed the Election Improvement Act of 2018 (the "EIA"), Section 1 of which created the United States Election Agency (the "USEA"). Congress designed the USEA to administer certain issues relating to voting rights. Relevant portions of the EIA read as follows:

### Section 2: Remedies

Any person or entity who alleges that a US government agency, a state agency, or a private individual acted in violation of applicable law relating to the administration of an election may bring a petition before the USEA. If an administrative law judge within the USEA finds a violation of law, the agency shall order anyone responsible, including a federal or state or local governmental entity, to take such action as the USEA deems necessary to prevent violations of law. Moreover, the USEA may direct the person or entity who acted in violation of applicable law to provide such monetary relief as the USEA shall, in its discretion, deem appropriate.

### Section 3: Review

Any party aggrieved by a decision, order, or other action of the USEA may seek a declaration of its invalidity by suing the agency in the federal district court having jurisdiction over the situs of the events giving rise to the controversy. The district court shall review all

<!-- page: 3 -->

matters of law de novo, but it shall hear no new evidence to decide the dispute, and shall upset agency findings of fact only if such are clearly erroneous.

### Section 4: Procedures

The USEA shall adjudicate all disputes with the utmost expedition. In keeping with the extraordinary requirement of speedy resolution of election disputes, the USEA shall make its findings of fact and conclusions of law based solely on a written record submitted by the parties. The record may include affidavits from willing witnesses, any documents the parties might submit, and any other exhibits. In no event, however, shall the parties to a USEA dispute be entitled to take discovery, nor shall the USEA hear live testimony, nor shall the USEA allow any action filed within it to expand beyond the parties and claims in the original complaint. In all other matters, the USEA may use such procedures as it deems appropriate, including any principles or rules used in federal courts, such as the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure.

### Section 5: No Option To Bypass the Agency

No federal or state court, nor any state administrative agency, nor any federal agency other than the USEA, shall have jurisdiction over any lawsuit described in Section 2, other than as specified in Section 3.

---

In the November 3, 2020 election, voters in some municipalities around the State of Massachusetts complained of long waits at polling booths. In a November 4, 2020 interview with *The Boston Globe*, Secretary of State William Galvin claimed that his office was not responsible for any delays that might have occurred. According to Secretary Galvin, any problems that might have existed (and the Secretary did not admit anything had gone amiss) were the fault of the Walter White Printing Services, Inc. ("WW"), a Massachusetts corporation with its principal place of business in Boston, MA. WW was the printing company the Secretary's office had hired to print the Massachusetts ballots. Secretary Galvin stated that he had originally ordered 3,000,000 paper ballots to be printed, but that one month before the election, he had orally increased the order to 5,500,000. In fact, according to Secretary Galvin, WW had provided only 2,000,000 ballots.

On November 14, Jane Margolis, a domiciliary of Springfield, MA, filed a handwritten petition before the USEA against WW. In her petition, Margolis stated that she had arrived at Boston Polling Place 157 at 9 am, but had had to wait until 5 pm to cast her vote. Margolis also stated that she was disabled due to a muscular and skeletal problem with her back and legs, and the long wait (most of which she had to spend standing, due to a lack of seats and pandemic-related social distancing requirements) had caused her excruciating pain. Her petition stated, "I wasn't going to quit. Too many people died to give me the right to vote." She asked the USEA to order WW to pay her $10,000 for her pain and suffering. The last three sentences of her petition were as follows: "I know very little about the law, and I want the government to give me a lawyer to help me with this case, as it is too complicated for me to handle on my own. I don't know how or what I'm supposed to tell you. I swear that everything I wrote here is true, under penalty of perjury." Margolis signed her petition. She also attached to it a copy of the *Boston Globe* article cited above. The USEA gave WW notice of the action and invited a response.

<!-- page: 4 -->

On December 6, 2020, WW filed an answer denying Margolis' allegations. It submitted an affidavit of its CEO, Mr. Walter White, stating (i) that its written contract with the Commonwealth of Massachusetts required it to print and deliver only 3,000,000 ballots, something it had done in a timely manner, (ii) that it had received no communication, oral or written, from Secretary Galvin or from any other Massachusetts official asking it to provide more ballots. White's affidavit attached a copy of the WW contract with Massachusetts, which did require WW to print 3,000,000 ballots. The contract also stated that it was intended to provide ballots in a timely manner for the benefit of each and every Massachusetts voter. Finally, the contract also stated that Secretary Galvin "could, via written or oral communication, increase the number of ballots requested by so communicating to WW, the charges for which will be" as specified in a later clause in the contract. White signed his affidavit under penalty of perjury. WW's lawyers also filed a short legal brief which, among other things, demanded a jury trial before concluding, "WW stands ready to provide such further evidence in support of its contentions as the USEA might require, if the USEA will only notify us of what might be persuasive or useful."

On December 20, 2020, the USEA issued a Decision And Order, which read in its entirety as follows: "We find that Petitioner Margolis has demonstrated that WW acted wrongfully in not providing the required number of ballots in a timely fashion, but we find that she has not proven her pain and suffering damages with sufficient specificity. Therefore, we award her $100."

## Assignment Question

You are a lawyer for the law firm representing WW. Walter White asks you if WW has good grounds for a lawsuit challenging the procedures the USEA used to reach its decision. He also asks whether Margolis might be able to challenge the decision on grounds. Upon what grounds might either WW or Margolis challenge the USEA decision? (Note: you need not discuss in what court such a lawsuit would take place. In addition, you need not address whether the USEA had subject matter jurisdiction, personal jurisdiction, or venue.)

## 2020-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2020-assignment-03-canneri-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## WW's Challenges

WW should challenge the USEA's decisions on two procedural grounds. First, WW should challenge the decision as a violation of its Seventh Amendment right to a jury trial. Under *Chauffeurs v. Terry*, a court evaluates the right to a jury trial by (1) comparing the statutory action to 18th century actions brought in courts of England, determining whether the suit would have been brought in a court of equity or in a court of law, and (2) examining the remedy sought is legal or equitable in nature.

Here, the first factor could go both ways. WW will want to argue that Margolis' suit is most analogous to a historical action for negligence. WW can argue that Margolis is arguing that WW breached its duty to Margolis (and other electors) to provide enough election ballots by failing to exercise due care, and that such a breach led to Margolis' pain and suffering. USEA will reply that this is more analogous to a third-party breach, because of the contract between WW and Massachusetts. Either way, the historical analogy is muddled and does not clearly point to one over another. The second factor, however, weighs in WW's favor, as Margolis' suit was for damages, a type of relief that is clearly legal. Since *Chauffeurs* made clear that the second factor is the most important, this test seems to weigh in favor of WW.

Following *Atlas Roofing*, the USEA will likely argue that this situation falls under the public rights exception to the right to a jury trial. Such an exception applies when (1) the government is involved on one side of the dispute and (2) the government is acting as a regulator/sovereign, not an economic actor. Here, WW has a strong argument that this exception does not apply, as the government is clearly not a party — it is an action between two private parties, Margolis and WW, adjudicating private rights. Under *Granfinanciera*, such factors clearly point to a right to a jury trial.

Second, WW should challenge the decision as a violation of its 14th Amendment due process rights. Here, a court might look to *Vitek* for guidance on what is required of a new adjudicatory scheme for it to comply with procedural due process. Here, many *Vitek* factors are absent. The USEA categorically does not provide for live testimony, making its decision based solely on a written record submitted by the parties, with no in-person hearing. The written statement issued by the USEA did not elaborate on the evidence relied on and the reasons for its decision. Though the USEA provided notice, there is no further description of notice provisions, and no set procedures for a defendant's opportunity to respond. Moreover, though *Vitek* explicitly states that the lack of discovery or appeal does not *per se* render a system unconstitutional, their absence removes significant safeguards which might otherwise mitigate the unavailability of the other protections listed above.

## Margolis' Challenges

Margolis should challenge USEA procedures on right to counsel grounds, especially because she explicitly requested counsel in her petition before the USEA. This argument is likely an uphill battle. Per *Lassiter*, there is no guarantee to counsel in civil proceedings; even further, the case illustrates the presumption that there is no right to counsel unless personal liberty is at stake. In any case, the controlling test here is that in *Mathews v. Eldridge*, which weighs (1) the affected private interest(s), (2) the risk of erroneous deprivation, and (3) the government's interest as counterbalanced against the private interests implicated.

Here, the private interest at stake — whether or not Margolis will receive damages for her pain and suffering — seems relatively minor, especially in light of *Lassiter*, which denied

<!-- page: 2 -->

counsel in a case involving parental rights. If the termination of parental rights is not a private interest of sufficient importance to warrant counsel, it is unlikely that Margolis' request for damages — though arguably required to make her whole — is enough to overcome *Lassiter*'s presumption, especially since no personal liberty is at stake.

Though the risk of erroneous deprivation may seem high, since Margolis herself states that she knew very little about the law when she initiated the proceeding, USEA will likely argue that there is no risk of "deprivation" since Margolis is simply asking for damages. However, if measuring two counterfactual states of the world — one in which Margolis has access to counsel and one in which she does not — it seems likely that Margolis would secure a better outcome in the former.

Finally, the government has a significant interest in adjudicating election disputes quickly, because of the extraordinary importance of electoral integrity. Moreover, the cost of providing counsel to all those aggrieved would be noteworthy, especially in relation to the relatively minor interest involved. Thus, *Mathews* would likely point to no right to counsel for Margolis.

## 2020-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2020-assignment-03-hackney-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Successful PDP Claims

Either party could challenge the overall procedural safeguards provided by the USEA as deficient when compared to the minimum requirements laid out in *Vitek*. Some requirements are met. USEA is an independent decision-maker. Witnesses are represented through sworn affidavits. The lack of discovery is not antithetical to *Vitek*: it's not mandated. That an appeal can be made is more than *Vitek* requires. And *Vitek* doesn't require *per se* legal counsel at state expense. Some issues are concerning. Although notice was given, we are not made privy to the details of notice, meaning it could be insufficient. Relatedly, although the ALJ made a written explanation, it's unclear whether this is required. Additionally, while WW was given an opportunity to respond, *Vitek* likely would have required both parties the opportunity to contest each other's evidence had it been between two private parties (as opposed to the government and a private party). And *Vitek* does require a hearing. Especially because the dispute concerned a testimonial dispute, it seems difficult to judge Margolis's claim on the papers. Overall, without more information, there seems to be a global due process concern.

WW is right to request a jury trial. To decide if there is a right to a jury trial, *Terry* counsels a 2-prong test. The first prong considers the nature of the issue, while the second looks at the nature of the remedy. When evaluating, we consider the historical context of the cause of action, analogizing if necessary. Admittedly, the analysis is muddled. Though we know that historically an action for a third-party breach was an action in equity, this claim is ultimately about compensatory damages to Margolis — a remedy at law. By contrast, an equitable remedy could have been for an injunction or WW's specific performance (brought prior to Nov. 3). So, prongs one and two are directly contrasting. But *Terry* says prong two dominates. Layered on this is that at the heart of the dispute is a factual inquiry: whether an oral increase in the number of ballots was actually made. Rather than a conclusion of law, which a judge is better suited to decide, a jury is the more appropriate factfinder.

The strong claim WW has for a jury trial could be defeated if there is a public rights exception. This comes into play when Congress establishes a new adjudicator. Notwithstanding the *Terry* analysis above, if a public right is at issue, then the 7th Amendment does not prevent Congress from assigning factfinding to an administrative forum. This requires the government to (1) be a party to the litigation and (2) be acting as a sovereign. In the case at bar, the government is not a party, so the exception does not apply.

## Unsuccessful PDP Claims

Margolis will have a tough time requesting legal counsel at state expense. Begin with *Lassiter*, which provides a presumption *against* provision of counsel in the absence of an incarceration risk. Noting that, conduct an *MvE* analysis, augmented for private parties as described in *Doehr*. The private interest of Margolis for pain and suffering damages appears comparatively minimal. In context, this is relatively small in comparison to the private interests recognized as significant (often when seizure is involved), like parental rights or involuntary treatment in a mental hospital. WW's private interest is paying damages. Because a seizure has not occurred pre-judgment, this

<!-- page: 2 -->

is similarly minor. The risk of erroneous determination is muddled. Mitigating concerns are that claims must be substantiated, rather than conclusory, and statements are made under penalty of perjury, as opposed to mere allegation.

WW could not bring a successful procedural due process challenge to the USEA's prohibition on expanding the case beyond parties and claims to the original complaint. Even though it seems like the injury Margolis complains of could be due to the negligence of the polling place (it seems more likely that lack of seating, rather than insufficient quantities of ballots, caused her pain and suffering), WW nevertheless may not avail itself of joinder, cross-suit, nor other procedural actions available in an Article III court. Note that none of these are part of the minimum requirements in *Vitek*. Though ability to respond is part of *Vitek*, it would be a stretch to characterize these deficiencies under that umbrella. Thus, this claim would likely fail. And an *MvE* test (modified by *Doehr*) counsels against allowing these safeguards. The private interests, discussed above, are both relatively minor. And the risk of erroneous determination is minor: the inability to draw in the polling place as a party and allege their negligence proximately caused Margolis's pain and suffering likely won't increase the odds an ALJ erroneously determining a lack of ballots was the true source of her harm.

## 2020-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2020-assignment-03-livingston-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

Both WW and Margolis have grounds to challenge the procedures used by the USEA. WW will likely prevail on their jury trial claim. Either party may succeed in a general PDP challenge. Margolis will likely lose her claim for an attorney.

## 1. WW's Right to a Jury Trial

WW was probably entitled to a jury trial, after one was timely requested. Jury trials are only available at law, not equity. Since 7A "preserves" the right to a jury trial, we need historical analysis. *Terry*.

Our first prong of the *Terry* test — the historical analysis — is unclear (especially since neither complaint nor decision specified a COA), but suggests equity. The COA that fits best with the complaint and order is 3rd party breach, because the only reason WW would have a "duty" to Margolis, as required by negligence, would be as a 3rd party beneficiary. (Remember, the ballot contract was "for the benefit of MA voters.")

BUT we know from *Terry* that prong 2 — type of remedy requested — is more important. Though the remedies EIA envisions *sound* equitable, this is still probably a suit at law — Margolis is asking for damages, NOT disgorgement ("give me the money you made from the ballot contract") or specific performance ("print extra ballots").

Even if this suit is "mixed law/equity," we have chucked the clean-up rule, *Terry*, and federal courts favor jury trials. WW likely prevails.

### a) Public Rights Exception Doesn't Apply

This case was heard in an agency, not a federal court. Because there is no historical corollary to the administrative state, we need to test whether the public rights exception allows the agency to avoid sitting a jury. We need an *Atlas Roofing* test!

But because the USEA is not acting as a regulator here (it's not even a party), there's no public rights exception. *Granfinanciera*. Notably, it's not even really adjudicating statutorily created public rights, since it's enforcing garden-variety election law.

## 2. Margolis' Right to an Attorney

A *Mathews* analysis suggests Margolis has no right to an attorney at government expense. To begin, we presume that because Margolis' physical liberty is not at stake, she has no right to an attorney. The law will view her private interests as minimal-to-moderate. This is a deprivation of "property" (her damages), not liberty, nor her means to live, *Goldberg*. *Matthews* suggests property rights carry less weight.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Though WW ostensibly has an interest in fair adversarial processes, they also have an interest in efficient case resolution, weighing against a state-funded attorney for Margolis.

The court will likewise view the risk of erroneous deprivation as acceptable, given the government's interests in conserving already-scarce attorney resources. Margolis did say she couldn't understand the proceedings, and her opponent did have an attorney, but while an attorney would probably have made a positive difference, the actual question of her damages isn't terribly complex, and state resources are already taxed to the max by providing counsel in liberty cases.

## 3. General Insufficiency of Procedural Scheme

When evaluating the general sufficiency of a procedure, *Vitek* provides a guide. While there was an independent decision-maker (not a clerk like in *Sniadach*), several other elements mentioned in *Vitek* are missing, particularly the opportunity to present evidence and question witnesses.

### A) Written Notice

We have little information about the notice WW was given. While it's clear they had actual notice, the agency may not have provided the constitutional *Mullane* minimum.

### B) Opportunity to Present Evidence / Question Witnesses

Here, the parties were "heard" by way of written documents — so this isn't a case with *no* pre-deprivation hearing (like *Doehr*), just one with an imperfect pre-deprivation hearing.

Because this is a fact-intensive case turning on conflicting accounts (Galvin said he requested more ballots, WW said he didn't), rather than a cut-and-dry case of, say, medical evidence, live testimony seems necessary. *Mathews*. Plus, written submissions are likely inadequate, as indicated by both parties' uncertainty about what to include. *Kelly*.

The parties' ability to question the witnesses is also lacking. The ALJ clearly relied on the *Boston Globe* article, and therefore unsworn "testimony" by Galvin, but there was no procedure by which WW could question him. While adjudicating cases "on the papers" increases efficiency and decreases cost, it decreases accuracy, which should outweigh the benefits here.

### C) Written Decision

While the written decision was given, it was vague, and didn't cleanly state the grounds for the decision — making appeal difficult. The written decision here also highlights how badly we need a more robust ability to give evidence — since it said Margolis had not proven her damages and that Margolis had proven the breach of duty by WW, without providing the parties a robust opportunity to contest or supplement either factual question or indicating what evidence it relied on.

## 2020-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2020-assignment-03-sanders-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## WW's Challenges

Under *Chauffers*' two-prong test, WW likely should have been granted a jury trial. Under *Chauffers*' first prong, we look to an analogous 18th-century suit to determine whether the suit would have been brought in a court of law or equity. In this case, WW would likely argue that this is a suit for negligence which historically was an action at law. On the other hand, this suit also looks like a third-party breach since Margolis is a third-party beneficiary to WW's contract with the Commonwealth of MA, which was historically an action in equity. Taking these into account, the first prong does not provide a definitive answer. Under *Chauffers*' second prong, the remedy sought is damages, which is legal in nature. Although the first prong doesn't provide a definitive answer, the second prong, which is the more important factor under *Chauffers*, supports a finding of a right to a jury trial.

Further, the public rights exception is likely not applicable in this case. The government is not a party to the dispute, and even though the government is acting as a regulator, the case centers around private rights (liability for third-party breach of contract or negligence) between two private parties (an individual and a corporation). (*Atlas Roofing* / *Granfinanciera*) Additionally, although federal election law likely represents a public regulatory scheme/program, the suit is to recover damages which is also a private right. Due to the mix of both law and equity, the legal remedy sought, and the fact that the public rights exception likely does not apply here, a jury trial should have been granted.

WW could also argue that the statutory scheme violates procedural due process under the DPC. *Vitek* provides a series of factors to consider when evaluating if a new adjudicatory scheme satisfies irreducible minimums of due process. First, WW was given notice of the action and invited to respond, but depending on the exact notice procedures utilized by the USEA, WW may have grounds for a due process challenge. We would need further information to evaluate this. Second, WW may have a due process challenge based on the opportunity to be heard. Although the opportunity to respond/be heard was given before a resolution was issued by USEA, WW may be able to challenge the type of opportunity that was given. For example, maybe a hearing should have been granted with live testimony, rather than just an opportunity for a written response, given that the case likely turned in part on a factual dispute (whether the MA Secretary of State ordered more ballots). Further, WW was not granted the opportunity to present, confront, or cross-examine any witnesses for Margolis; indeed, Margolis didn't offer any other than herself, and her testimony concerned more her damages than WW's wrongdoing. Due to this, they were not given the opportunity to confront any of the claims made against them in the evidence provided by Margolis. Lastly, in addition to their decision and order, the USEA did not provide a written statement or any information regarding the evidence they relied on in reaching their decision. While *Vitek* may not require all of these factors, an absence of them supports a challenge for a violation of WW's due process rights.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Margolis' Challenges

Margolis might challenge the USEA decision on the grounds that her right to counsel was violated. However, there is not a concern of incarceration or a significant risk to Margolis' liberties, and therefore, under *Lassiter*, Margolis likely does not have a strong case for a right to counsel in this proceeding. Nevertheless, according to *Lassiter* and *Turner*, the court conducts a *Mathews/Eldridge* test analysis, considering the private interest, risk of erroneous deprivation, and government interests, to evaluate a party's right to counsel.

First, Margolis' private interest is whether she will have access to damages for her pain and suffering. While legal counsel would likely help Margolis make a more detailed and succinct claim to the USEA, with the procedures USEA currently has in place regarding submissions of evidence for claims, Margolis' private interests are likely not as significant as the rights of a parent.

Second, it is unlikely that legal counsel would significantly reduce risk of erroneous deprivation. Neither party is required to have counsel to file the requisite documentation to form the written record in which the USEA bases its dispute decisions. Additionally, neither party is entitled to discovery or live testimony. Therefore, there is no substantial risk of erroneous deprivation without access to counsel.

Lastly, the government's interest in expeditious disputes regarding violations of federal election law likely outweighs Margolis' private interests. Lawyers extend proceedings, and increase the level of formality within disputes (*Powell*, in *Vitek*). Margolis' interests and risks probably do not supersede the government's. Therefore, Margolis likely does not have a strong claim for a right to counsel.
