## 2021-assignment-01 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 1 (ungraded)
Civil Procedure 2 & 3, 2021
Professor Jim Greiner

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** Friday, September 24, 2021, 4pm
**Due date/time:** 9 am on Tuesday, September 28, 2021
**Word limit:** 900 words
**Time limit:** 3.0 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is ungraded.
11. **PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.**

### Suggestions for your answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Law regarding self-driving trucks and front proximity detectors

### Federal Law

In 2016, Congress passed the Willow Rosenberg Self-Driving Trucks Protection Act (the "Willow Act"). Named after a woman hit by a self-driving truck as she was standing on a sidewalk casting a spell, the Willow Act included the following provisions:

> **Section 201: Findings And Purpose**
> The Congress finds that self-driving trucks pose hazards to pedestrians almost as great as human-driven trucks. In particular, the Congress finds that front proximity detectors[^1] have proven to be a particular technological challenge for manufacturers of self-driving trucks. One purpose of this Act is therefore to empower the Secretary of Transportation (the "Secretary") to issue regulations promoting the safety and well-being of pedestrians through appropriate regulation, with particular attention to front proximity detectors.

> **Section 202: Department of Transportation ("DoT") Duties**
> (a) To promote the safety and well-being of pedestrians, after appropriate notice and comment as required by the Administrative Procedure Act, the Secretary shall issue regulations concerning the design, manufacture, sale, purchase, and maintenance of self-driving trucks, with special attention to the issue of front proximity detection.
> (b) . . .

In 2017, acting pursuant to the Willow Act, DoT Secretary Riley Finn promulgated the following regulations.

> **49 C.F.R. § 5X5: Front Proximity Detectors**
> (a) Any corporation or other business that manufactures or sells self-driving trucks shall, before completing the sale of any self-driving truck to any other person or entity, conduct an appropriate test of the front proximity detector and corresponding braking mechanism (such as causing the truck to drive slowly toward an obstacle or barrier to see if it stops on its own) in the presence of the buyer to discover whether the front proximity detector on the vehicle is in good working order.

<!-- page: 3 -->

> (b) No corporation or other business shall purchase a self-driving truck without witnessing the test of the front proximity detector described in subsection (a).
> (c) . . .

No other provision of the Willow Act nor of Secretary Finn's regulations has any language relevant to this exam.

### Law relating to personal jurisdiction, choice of law, respondeat superior, and civil procedure

All states relevant to this exam have long-arm statutes allowing the exercise of personal jurisdiction whenever the Constitution so allows.

Virginia and Delaware use the Second Restatement for horizontal choice of law. Pennsylvania, North Carolina, and South Carolina use the First Restatement.

### States and districts

Pennsylvania has three federal district courts; Philadelphia is located in the Eastern District of Pennsylvania. Virginia has two federal district courts; Alexandria, VA is located in the Eastern District of Virginia. South Carolina, Maryland, and Delaware each have a single federal district court. North Carolina has three districts; if a lawsuit relevant to this exam were to occur in North Carolina, it would occur in the Eastern District of North Carolina.

### Facts

Xander Harris, Inc. ("XH") is a Delaware corporation with its principal place of business in Baltimore, Maryland. It manufactures self-driving trucks of all sizes, from light pickups all the way up to 18 wheelers. XH conducts no sales activity to consumers or business, nor does it advertise. Instead, it sells its products exclusively in an enormous purchase and sale clearinghouse market facility located in Alexandria, VA called "The Spike." At The Spike, sellers and buyers of self-driving vehicles meet, conduct inspections, negotiate sales terms, transfer money, and take delivery of vehicles. XH sells to various distributors at The Spike. XH insists that any company to whom it sells one of its vehicles must subsequently sell the vehicle directly to a consumer. The XH employee in charge of many sales transactions was named Tara Maclay.

Faith, Inc. is a North Carolina corporation with its principal place of business in Columbia, South Carolina. Faith is a large distributor (to consumers) of self-driving vehicles. It advertises and sells self-driving vehicles in the following states only: Pennsylvania, Virginia, North Carolina, and South Carolina. Faith purchases self-driving trucks from, among other sources, manufacturers who sell at the clearinghouse market at The Spike in Alexandria, VA. The Faith employee in charge of purchasing self-driving vehicles was named Cordelia Chase.

In mid-December of 2019, XH completed manufacture of a set of 50 self-driving light pickup trucks. In the middle of the night of December 16–17, 2019, Maclay, driving an XH car, accompanied the fleet of 50 light pickups in an overnight trip to The Spike. Maclay's instructions were to sell the trucks for a price of $25,000 each, more if she could get it. On the morning of December 18, 2019, Maclay met with Chase at The Spike. Chase was excited about the new XH trucks, so excited that she immediately began negotiations. Maclay and Chase quickly concluded negotiations for the purchase and sale of the 50 light

<!-- page: 4 -->

pickups, and by lunchtime had agreed on a price of $28,000 per truck. Neither Maclay nor Chase, nor anyone else, requested a test of or actually tested the front proximity detectors of the trucks while they were at the Spike.

On December 20, 2019, Chase had her assistant and fellow Faith employee, Anya Jenkins, transport the fifty pickups to Faith's facility near Columbia, SC. Later, Chase had 10 of the trucks from this set of 50 transported up to a Faith Philadelphia parking site in anticipation of a sale.

In early 2020, Faith began advertising a set of XH self-driving trucks throughout its multi-state service area, with television ads running on local TV stations in different cities in Pennsylvania as well as all of the states in which Faith sold trucks, as identified above. Faith also ran ads in local newspapers in Pennsylvania cities, including Philadelphia; similar ads ran in local newspapers of cities in the other above-mentioned states. The ads all directed those interested in self-driving pickups to Faith's website, where potential buyers could see complete information, including photographs, of the trucks for sale. People interested were instructed to contact a Faith employee named Cordelia Chase. The ads that ran in Philadelphia specified that Faith had 10 XH self-driving trucks waiting in a rented parking facility in the area, ready for immediate inspection and delivery.

In early February of 2020, Chase received an Internet message from Buffy Summers. Buffy was born and raised in Charlotte, NC. In 2015, she moved to Philadelphia to attend Temple University. After graduating in 2019, she decided that she really liked Philadelphia. She decided to take a "gap year" between college and law school, intending to spend the time slaying vampires. Because she liked Philadelphia so much, she had applied early admission to the University of Pennsylvania Law School, but had not yet heard about admission.

Buffy inquired to Chase how soon she could inspect one of the XH light vehicles she'd seen advertised on her local TV station, and how much it would cost. A series of email exchanges followed, in which Buffy indicated an interest in purchasing one of the trucks. Buffy was delighted to learn that she could buy the truck as soon as she liked after inspecting it, and that she could inspect it right outside her house, because Chase could instruct the truck to drive itself to her house and park there. On February 12, 2020, Chase used the Internet to deploy the codes for one of the trucks to instruct it to drive itself to park in front of Buffy's Philadelphia house at 11 am that morning.

At 10:45am, Buffy bundled up and went to the sidewalk in front of Buffy's house, located at 1630 Sunnydale Drive, in Philadelphia. As she waited on the sidewalk next to Sunnydale Drive and occupied herself by practicing her singing, a pursuit at which she was as enthusiastic as she was unskillful. At 11:00am, the XH pickup approached the house, but Buffy did not notice it. Instead of stopping a couple of feet short of the curb, the pickup pulled its tires right up against the curb. In doing so, the pickup's front right fender and front right side headlight struck Buffy, throwing her to the ground.

Buffy suffered a concussion, a broken leg, and several cuts; she required care in a nearby Philadelphia hospital for 25 days and underwent physical therapy before eventually making her recovery. She suffered at least $200,000 in injuries, pain, suffering, and medical bills. You are a friend of Buffy's, and a lawyer. On May 4, 2020, Buffy emailed you, saying that she wanted to sue both XH and Faith. Buffy's theory is that both XH and Faith employees had failed to inspect the front proximity detectors of the lot of trucks sold at the Spike, of which the truck that hit her was one. According to Buffy, that failure

<!-- page: 5 -->

constituted negligence.[^2] You do a little research and find out that this "failure to inspect" theory will likely constitute negligence only under the substantive laws of North Carolina and Virginia, but not of any other state relevant to this exam. Under the laws of only these two states, a plaintiff may allege or show that the defendant violated a state or federal statute established to protect a set of persons that includes the plaintiff, such as, for example, Secretary Finn's regulation appearing at 49 C.F.R. § 5X5. This is called a "per se" theory of negligence. This is the theory of negligence that Buffy wants to use.

**Question 1:** If Buffy wants to sue both XH and Faith in a federal district court lawsuit somewhere, can she do so? Explain.

**Question 2:** Assuming that Buffy can sue in federal court, in what federal districts could she sue, and in which federal district should she sue to give herself the best chance to prevail? (Note: you need not discuss venue in your answer.)

[^1]: Front proximity detectors are devices that detect objects or obstacles in front of a vehicle. Should they detect an object or obstacle of sufficient size too close to the front of the vehicle while it is moving forward, the vehicle's control system should engage the brakes to stop the vehicle from hitting the object or obstacle.

[^2]: You may assume for the purposes of this exam that Buffy may sue XH and Faith for the negligence of its employees (if she can sue anyone at all). In other words: All states relevant to this exam use the doctrine of respondeat superior, meaning that a company is legally responsible (and thus can be sued) for any action taken, or any failure to take an action, within the scope of an employee's employment duties. For example, if a restaurant delivery-person drives too fast on their way to drop off a pizza at a customer's house and, as a result, hits and injuries a pedestrian, then the pedestrian may sue the restaurant for damages as vicariously liable under the doctrine of respondeat superior.

## 2021-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-01-baskin-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

**AUJ:** Asserts 1 count of negligence, so state law cause of action and no AUJ (*Mottley*, § 1331) but there may be an embedded substantial and contested federal question so potential *Smith-Grable* issue. Likely both substantial and contested here. However, S-G exception unlikely as would sweep any cases involving any federal law violation into federal court, which would place undue burden on federal court system and blur state/federal courts division, the issue is primarily one of fact rather than one of law, and, not very important to the federal government (likely not an issue of such importance that the court wouldn't trust state court to handle).

**DJ:** There is still SMJ if S-G does not apply if complete diversity (§ 1332); AIC well above $75k. (*Strawbridge*) Buffy may be PA or NC domiciliary. On one hand, Buffy born and raised in NC and went to PA only for college (specific end time; schooling not usually = domicile change), suggesting NC domiciliary. However, Buffy decided she really likes PA after graduation and decided to spend her "gap year" there before law school, so possibly intends to reside there indefinitely. That said, these are still specific end times (one year "gap year"; three years law school), so close call whether Buffy has intent to remain indefinitely in PA (*Mas*). XH is a DE (incorporation) and MD (PPoB) citizen. Faith is an NC (incorporation) and SC (PPoB) citizen. If Buffy is an NC domiciliary, complete diversity with XH, no complete diversity with Faith. If Buffy is a PA domiciliary, complete diversity with XH and Faith.

## Question 2

**SC and NC:** XH: no general IPJ — XH not a citizen and does not do any business in those states so not "essentially at home" (*Daimler*); no specific IPJ — minimal contacts not met. Faith: general IPJ (NC incorporation; SC PPoB).

**MD and DE:** XH: General IPJ — DE (incorporation); MD (PPoB). Faith: no general IPJ — not a citizen of DE or MD; no specific IPJ — minimal contacts not met.

**PA:**

Faith: No clear basis for general IPJ (could be *Daimler* "at home" if PA = >50% of business but don't know % of business across states where operating) and no consent IPJ. For specific IPJ, PA has a long-arm (go to limits of Constitution) and FRCP 4(k)(1)(a) will piggy-back on state IPJ, so look for minimal contacts connecting defendants, subject matter of litigation, and PA, and fairness and reasonableness of PA exercising IPJ (*International Shoe*). Faith shows purposeful availment of PA customers, selling directly without an intermediary (unlike *J. McIntyre*), including storing goods in rented PA facility and targeting forum with significant advertisement, which is directly related to litigation (how Buffy became customer). Also fair and reasonable as a convenient place for Faith to litigate given noted connections and harm occurred there, PA has interest in protecting its citizens and regulating potentially negligent conduct of businesses targeting it.

XH: No general IPJ — not a citizen and not "essentially at home" (*Daimler*). XH potential PA IPJ. Minimal contacts issue as did not target PA, does not directly do business there, does not advertise anywhere. Reaching state through intermediary (*J. McIntyre*) or through "stream of commerce" foreseeability could end up there (*WWV*) not enough for IPJ. Possible that XH insisting companies it sells to sell directly to consumer is enough to establish greater connection through intermediary, but unlikely.

<!-- page: 2 -->

**VA:**

XH and Faith not citizens or essentially at home in VA (no general IPJ). But both XH and Faith have minimum contacts in VA (specific IPJ): XH's sole market is the Spike in VA and Faith advertises/sells/purchases vehicles there, both purposefully availing themselves through state and directly linked to litigation — failure to inspect that is basis of lawsuit occurred in VA. (*International Shoe*)

### Likelihood

Whether Buffy's negligence claim is likely to succeed will depend on which state's law will apply — she is likely to prevail only under NC/VA law. The issue of whether the § 5X5 violation can form the basis of her negligence claim is substantive (could affect pre-litigation conduct); HCOL analysis is necessary. The harm occurred in PA, which under RS1 (PA, NC, SC) would be the applicable law as *lex loci delicti* (where "blood" was spilled — i.e., Buffy got hit), so Buffy would prefer RS2 state (DE, VA) which will consider factors beyond this because claim unlikely to succeed under PA law. Since DE only has PJ over XH and not over Faith (as described above), VA filing preferable.

Under RS2, begin with presumption for *lex loci delicti* (PA) and then consider center of gravity: faulty truck manufactured in MD, sold/purchased in VA where alleged negligence (XH and Faith's failure to conduct front proximity detector test) occurred, Buffy hit in PA. PA and VA both have relevant state interests — both have interest in regulating business in the state (ads targeted PA/sale was to occur there; negligent sale occurred in VA and XH business concentrated there), central relationships in creating harm were formed both in VA (XH/Faith) and PA (Buffy/Faith), and the injured party (Buffy) likely PA domiciliary. Given mixed analysis, court may be more likely to remain with presumption and apply PA law, but a key fact in favor of VA law is that VA has the strongest relationship to the particular issue here, which is whether regulation § 5X5 can form the basis for the negligence claim, because that was where the failure to test occurred.

## 2021-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-01-herndon-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

Yes.

**AUJ:** No federal law cause of action (negligence = state law claim), so no § 1331 (*Mottley*), but there might be *Smith-Grable*: Embedded federal issue (Willow Act violation) in state law negligence claim — Willow Act expressed significant federal interest, DoT regs at issue. Depending on frequency of violations, could sweep a lot of claims into federal court, but strong federal interest in promoting safety/holding companies accountable, competing with state's incentives to attract business at expense of safety, could justify *Smith-Grable* exception.

**DJ:**

Diversity: Complete diversity likely met. XH = DE (incorporation), MD (PPOB); Faith = NC (incorporation), SC (PPOB). Buffy is trickier: She's from NC and was domiciled there, but arguably has become a PA domiciliary. Even though Penn hasn't gotten back to her yet, she really likes PA and may intend to stay there regardless, but it's unclear if she'll continue to reside there (*Mas*). If Buffy is still a NC citizen, there's no DJ.

AIC: Her claim ($200,000) is well above $75,000 AIC requirement.

## Question 2

Buffy should file in EDVA.

### PJ

No state has general jurisdiction over both XH and Faith.

Faith's truly at home in NC, SC, but not essentially at home in any of the others, since no evidence suggests any one of the states they sell in has the majority or a large fraction of their business (*Daimler*).

XH's truly at home in DE, MD, and maybe essentially at home in VA (*Daimler*) since they do all their sales there — substantial contacts — and there's a long arm.

**Specific Jurisdiction:**
SC/NC/DE/MD/VA/PA all have long-arm statutes to the limits of the Constitution, so any of the federal district courts in those states could piggyback with 4(k)(1)(a).

SC/NC/DE/MD non-starters for SJ: Faith has no contacts with DE/MD, XH has no contacts with NC/SC.

Probably no SJ in PA for XH, but OK for Faith.

For XH, the exercise of jurisdiction would be inconsistent with due process because insufficient contacts between XH, forum, and subject matter of the litigation (*Int'l Shoe*). XH doesn't target any state, uses a third-party distributor, and while maybe foreseeable that products end up in PA, east coast, *Nicastro* probably means XH's contacts with PA are insufficient. The actions of a third party aren't enough to establish IPJ (*Hanson*). If minimum contacts were okay, then it'd probably be fair/reasonable to exercise jurisdiction in PA: XH nearby, most witnesses in PA, PA has interest in regulating, HCOL probably favors PA at a minimum on some issues (see below). But because minimal contacts are insufficient, PA likely doesn't have IPJ over XH.

<!-- page: 2 -->

For Faith: Min contacts sufficient between Faith, PA, subject matter of litigation (*Int'l Shoe*). Faith purposefully availed themselves of privileges of conducting activities in PA by advertising in PA, derived economic benefit through sales there (*McGee*), employees there (*Int'l Shoe*), and induced the car causing injury to enter PA. Exercise of jurisdiction would be fair/reasonable in PA: witnesses there, Faith does business there so fair to ask them to return, PA has interest in protecting its citizens and regulating businesses operating there, HCOL probably favors PA on at least some issues.

VA probably has SJ for XH/Faith.

For XH, there's minimal contacts between XH, VA, and subject matter of the litigation: XH's employee's negligence happened in VA when Maclay saw trucks crash and still sold them the next day. XH availed themselves of privileges of doing business in VA by selling all their goods there (*Int'l Shoe*, *McGee*).

For Faith, minimal contacts sufficient between Faith, VA, subject matter of litigation — Chase bought faulty vehicles there without testing them, saw trucks crash while leaving the Spike, and sold them anyway; Faith buys products in VA to sell them elsewhere and so benefitted from doing business in VA (*Int'l Shoe*/*McGee*).

Exercising jurisdiction fair/reasonable for both: sales in VA, XH and Faith can clearly get there, modern transportation makes it accessible, VA has interest in preventing sales of faulty goods.

### HCOL

The key difference in state law is whether the violation of regulation 5X5 can serve as the basis of Buffy's negligence claim. Because the issue's substantive (question about the nature of the claim, rather than procedural issue like time limits on filings), we need HCOL analysis.

In first restatement states (PA, NC, SC), the place where the harm happened governs. Harm happened in PA, so PA law would govern in any of these states, which likely precludes theory's per se/failure to inspect theory of negligence.

A second restatement state (VA/DE) would be better because they begin with the first restatement, but then consider the section six factors to see if strong considerations lead to a different result. Center of gravity could favor PA or VA (harm in PA, but most of the failures to inspect in VA plus sales there). VA has a strong state interest in regulating faulty sales within their borders, but PA has competing strong interest in protecting litigants who are their own citizens. The second restatement favors cutting up the case into issues, however, so perhaps the relevance of a failure to inspect could be governed by VA law, where the failure happened. If Buffy sues in VA and the § 6 factors end up somewhat balanced, a VA court may weigh in favor of selecting VA law because of the needs of the interstate judicial system — VA more familiar with their own law — and Buffy could use the per se/failure to inspect theory.

## 2021-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-01-jaffer-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## (1)

**§ 1331** — No federally created cause of action — negligence = state law claim & not created by federal law (*Mottley*). *Smith-Grable* exception might apply since key issue — compliance with federal statute (Willow Act) — is embedded within the negligence claim, and it is actually disputed and substantial. The federal government has a strong interest in adjudicating claims with important federal issues, here, preserving pedestrian safety. But it might bring too many cases to FCs (disturbing balance of federal and state responsibilities) — every time a defendant breaks this federal law, plaintiffs can bring federal suit despite a state COA. Also, question is likely very fact heavy. Arguably not very embedded/necessary to federal law — could prove negligence by not using "per se" theory. Maybe *Smith-Grable*, maybe not.

**§ 1332** — AIC sufficient against both parties (>$75k). So, there is complete diversity if Buffy is a Pennsylvania domiciliary (*Buffy [Penn] v. XH [DE & Maryland] and Faith [NC & SC]*), but no DJ if she's not. (*Strawbridge*). Place of domicile is the last place you lived w/o intention to leave at a particular time. Maybe Buffy's domiciled in Charlotte — moving to a different place for school doesn't change domicile and she might have an intention to return back there. More likely, she is domiciled in Pennsylvania. Buffy "decided she really liked Philadelphia," took a gap year, and applied to school here. She hasn't expressed an intention to return anywhere.

## (2)

### Where she CAN sue

**SC/NC** — Though these states have general IPJ (GIPJ) over Faith (PPoB and place of incorporation), neither has IPJ over XH, so can't sue here. No GIPJ over XH because XH is not domiciled in either state (not really at home — *Daimler*). No minimal contacts (no sales activity nor advertisements here) so no SIPJ.

**MD/DE** — Though both have GIPJ over XH (PPoB and incorporation), neither has IPJ over Faith, so can't sue here. No GIPJ over Faith because not domiciled in either state (not really at home — *Daimler*). No SIPJ because no minimal contacts (no business conducted in either state).

**PA:**

- **Faith** — No GIPJ (not domiciled/essentially at home). But likely SIPJ — PA has a long-arm statute that goes to constitutional limits, federal courts piggy back off state courts (4k1a). Need minimal contacts (*Intl Shoe*) among the defendant, forum, and subject matter of litigation. Faith conducts TV & newspaper ads in Philly and sells the vehicles at storage facility in Pennsylvania. Good case of purposefully availing & foreseeability of being in court (*McGee*). The fact that the ads are electronic isn't consequential. Faith targeted the forum (*Griffin*) — they rented parking facility in Philly. Fairness/justice? PA = good location for witnesses/evidence, accident occurred here, good for plaintiff's convenience as a PA resident, PA has an interest in protecting plaintiffs from harm

<!-- page: 2 -->

  occurring in-state and in people purposely availing themselves via doing business in-state, could maybe use Pennsylvania COL. Probably IPJ.

- **XH** — No GIPJ (same as above reason). SIPJ (have LAS and federal courts can piggy back off state courts via 4k1a) governed by minimal contacts. XH conducts no sales activity to consumers/business and no ads in PA; sells exclusively in VA. Arguably hasn't purposely availed to PA. Maybe it's sufficient that XH could expect its products to end up in Penn (stream of commerce) since it sells to various distributors who then sell to customers. But it's probably not — likely wouldn't foresee being hailed to court (*J. McIntyre*, *WW Volkswagen*) because of no ads/sales. Fairness/justice? — Same as above but inconvenient for XH given the probable insufficient contacts. Probably no IPJ.

**VA** — No GIPJ (not domiciled or essentially at home) for either, but, likely specific IPJ (LAS, FCs piggy back off state courts — 4k1a) for both. Both purposely availed themselves to the forum (*McGee*) — Faith advertises and sells here and purchases trucks from manufacturers at the Spike in VA. XH sells products exclusively in an "enormous" facility in VA. Fairness/justice? Both likely have minimal contacts here, failure to inspect (basis of suit) happened in VA, resultantly some witnesses/evidence are here, and VA has an interest in the business and redressing issues that originated within their borders.

### Where she SHOULD sue

**COL:**

- The issue of whether the § 5X5 violation can form the basis of the negligence claim is a substantive not procedural issue.
- Since failure to inspect = negligence only under NC/VA substantive law, Buffy should sue in a 2R state. Under a 1R state (PA, NC, SC), "blood was spilled" / *lex loci delicti* (LLD) is PA, which doesn't have this negligence law. Because XH has no MC in NC, VA is Buffy's best bet.
- 2R analysis — Start with presumption of LLD = Pennsylvania but consider other states' interests. The faulty car was manufactured in MD, it was sold and purchased in VA, inspection should've occurred in VA (i.e. where conduct-causing injury happened), see VA's other interests above. Ads targeted PA, aggrieved party domiciled in PA, harm occurred here, interest in addressing grievances that happened in state, see other PA interests above. But the conduct most closely tied to this particular issue, the failure to inspect, occurred in VA; the failure to inspect has nothing to do with Buffy, it was a failure between XH and Faith, whose entire relationship is centered in VA. Though PA's interests are likely stronger, Buffy's best shot at VA law is suing in VA.

## 2021-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-01-lin-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## #1

**AUJ:** Negligence is a state claim so there is no federal COA (*Mottley*), but there may be a substantial federal issue embedded within the state law claim. *Smith-Grable*. The alleged negligence stems from the failure to comply with 49 C.F.R. § 5X5. Congress expressed substantial federal interest in protecting pedestrians from the hazards of self-driving trucks. However, the defendants are unlikely to contest the law. The issues in this case are questions of fact. Additionally, exercising federal jurisdiction any time a plaintiff uses failure to comply with federal law to prove a state COA would sweep up too many cases into federal court and violate the division of labor between state and federal court systems. There is likely no *Smith/Grable*.

**DJ:** There is SMJ through DJ because there is complete diversity. *Strawbridge*. It is a close call whether Buffy is a PA or NC domiciliary. Buffy has not heard about admission to UPenn so it is unclear whether she will continue to reside in PA (*Mas*). However, because Buffy really likes PA, it is possible she intends to reside in PA whether or not she gets into UPenn. XH is a citizen of DE (incorporation) and MD (PPOB, *Hertz*). Faith is a citizen of NC (incorporation) and SC (PPOB, *Hertz*). The AIC is well above $75K.

## #2

SC and NC have no general IPJ over XH because XH is not a citizen and it does not do any business in those states, so it cannot be essentially at home (*Daimler*) nor have the minimum contacts required for specific IPJ.

MD and DE have no general or specific IPJ over Faith because Faith is not a citizen and does no business in either state.

Faith is not a citizen of PA, but PA has specific IPJ. PA has a long-arm going to the limits of the constitution and FRCP 4(k)(1)(A) piggybacks on state IPJ. There are likely minimum contacts. *International Shoe*. Faith keeps the trucks for sale, including the one delivered to Buffy, in a rented parking facility in Philly until delivery. Although the sale was over the internet, the negligence occurred in Philly, where Chase delivered the truck without conducting a test of the front proximity detector. Faith advertises in PA through newspaper ads and television ads, purposefully availing itself of doing business in the state and rendering it foreseeable that it could be called to litigate in PA. It is fair and reasonable because the witnesses and documents relating to the accident are likely to be in PA, it is convenient for the plaintiff, the harm occurred in PA, PA has an interest in protecting its' citizens and regulating business conduct occurring in its' borders.

XH is not a citizen of PA so there is no general IPJ. It is a closer call whether there is specific IPJ over XH, but it is unlikely. Like *Nicastro*, XH is an out of state manufacturer that sends their products through an intermediate purchaser-reseller. There are no contacts with PA since they

<!-- page: 2 -->

sell their products in VA. XH did not conduct any sales activity in PA, it does not advertise in PA and it does not deliver to PA. While XH does require that its' purchases must resell directly to a consumer, it's likely not enough that XH put its' goods into the stream of commerce, making it possible for their products to end up in PA. They must have targeted the forum. *Nicastro*.

XH and Faith are not citizens in nor essentially at home in VA, but both have minimum contacts with VA. XH operates an enormous purchase and sale clearinghouse market facility in VA. Faith advertises, sells and purchases self-driving vehicles in VA. They both purposefully avail themselves of doing business in VA by entering business transactions in VA. These contacts are related to this lawsuit: the failure to inspect forming the basis of the claim occurred in VA.

Since "failure to inspect" will only likely constitute negligence under NC and VA substantive law, Buffy has a better chance of prevailing under a second restatement state because the HCOL analysis will consider factors other than where the harm occurred. In a first restatement state, we apply PA law because it's the place where the injury occurred (Buffy got hit in PA). VA and DE are both 2nd restatement states, but DE likely does not have PJ over Faith so Buffy is more likely to prevail by filing in VA.

Second Restatement analysis presumes we apply the law of the place where the injury occurred unless the center of gravity test demonstrates otherwise. The accident occurred in PA, but the conduct causing injury occurred in Alexandria because both XH and Faith were supposed to conduct a test of the front proximity detector. VA has an interest in regulating business activity in the state and all XH's sales were conducted in VA. PA also has an interest in regulating businesses who sell to PA residents. The relationship between Faith and XH was formed in VA while the relationship between Faith and Buffy formed in PA. Because key events occurred in both VA and PA, the court is unlikely to depart from *lex loci delicti*, but it's more possible using VA HCOL analysis.

# ASSIGNMENT 2021-assignment-02 — Assignment 2 (graded) — Civil Procedure 2 & 3, 2021

Source: content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-02.md

## 2021-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2 (graded)
Civil Procedure 2 & 3, 2021
Professor Jim Greiner

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** October 17, 2021
**Due date/time:** 5pm on Thursday, Oct 21, 2021
**Word limit:** 900 words
**Time limit:** 2.5 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment. Students are on their honor to comply with this time limit.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response into the drop box created on the course website. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is graded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

### Suggestions for your answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

Begin by reviewing Fed. R. Civ. P. 8(a)(2).

Under North Dakota common law, in a lawsuit filed by a non-United-States plaintiff concerning events that occur in a location other than North Dakota, the court shall dismiss under the doctrine of forum non conveniens. A North Dakota statute provides that in any lawsuit alleging personal injury in which the plaintiff seeks to recover damages from medical expenses, the court shall dismiss the lawsuit unless the plaintiff attaches to the complaint complete copies of all corresponding medical bills. Finally, under a separate North Dakota statute, a plaintiff must file a personal injury lawsuit within two years of the action by the defendant alleged to have caused the injury. In 1990, Congress passed a statute providing that in any lawsuit filed in federal district court, any applicable limitations period begins to run when the plaintiff discovers the harm or injury giving rise to the claim.

A Canadian plaintiff living just across the North Dakota border in Manitoba, Canada filed a complaint against a company incorporated in North Dakota, with its principal place of business in Bismarck, ND, in the United States District Court for the District of North Dakota. Nothing was attached to the complaint. The plaintiff alleged that the company negligently shipped expired medicines from the company's storage facility in Pierre, South Dakota to them in their Manitoba home. The plaintiff alleged that they imbibed the medicines in their home four months before leaving for a scenic vacation in Montana. They became ill on vacation and spent several weeks in a hospital there before recovering. The plaintiff filed their lawsuit 22 months after they first became ill, alleging jurisdiction based on diversity of citizenship and seeking $300,000 in pain and suffering and hospital bills.

1. What arguments could the defendant use in support of a motion to dismiss the lawsuit? Analyze whether each argument is likely to succeed.
2. In what way do these arguments for the motion to dismiss differ vis-à-vis the application of the Erie framework?
3. Is there a concern for any of the Erie issues that using federal law for some aspects of it and state law for others may result in an incoherent system of law (like the "Frankenlaw" we discussed in our horizontal choice of law class)? If so, discuss how, and propose a solution.
4. Pick any one of the arguments for a motion to dismiss that you identified in your response to question 1. Discuss how your analysis and your recommended result further, are neutral with respect to, or undermine the justifications for the Erie doctrine that we discussed in class.

## 2021-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-02-cheang-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

*(848 words)*

## Q1

### SMJ

Complete diversity exists between plaintiff (Canada domiciliary) and defendant (ND citizen — incorporated, PPoB). AIC ($300,000) meets § 1332 requirements.

### IPJ

Federal court may exercise IPJ only if state in which it sits can (FRCP 4(k)(1)(A)). A ND court has general IPJ because Defendant is really at home in ND (incorporation, PPoB).

### Venue

Venue proper under § 1391(b)(1).

### Erie Arguments

State and federal law conflicts on whether lawsuit should be dismissed for: (a) FNC, (b) failure to attach medical bills (FRCP 12(b)(4)), (c) statute of limitations (FRCP 12(b)(4)). If using state law, dismiss. If using federal law, do not dismiss. Since issues that seem procedural (attaching medical bills, limitations period) can be substantive for VCOL, do Erie analysis.

**York:** If ND common law used, court would dismiss case for FNC (ND law focuses on a non-US-plaintiff and events in Canada). If federal law used, primacy of plaintiff's forum choice, private interest of ND defendant to litigate close to home in ND (where alleged misconduct occurred), public interest in deciding controversy implicating ND company may counsel against FNC dismissal. Outcome determinative. Use state rule — dismiss case.

Given plaintiff's failure to attach medical bills, court would dismiss case under ND statute. If federal law used, case might not be dismissed because no medical bill requirement under FRCP 8(a). Outcome determinative. Use state rule — dismiss case.

If state law used, court would dismiss case because statute of limitations has expired (plaintiff filed suit 26 months after defendant shipped medicine; limitations is 24 months). If federal law used, court would not dismiss case because 24-month statute of limitations still running (plaintiff filed suit 22 months after becoming ill). Outcome determinative. Use state rule — dismiss case.

**Harlan Hanna:** ND FNC common law affects primary conduct. Law may incentivize companies to take greater risks in transactions involving non-US-plaintiffs in non-ND locations, because of FNC protection. Substantive issue, use state law — dismiss case.

ND statute requiring medical bill attachment does not affect primary conduct, since it furthers procedural purpose of ensuring complaint to recover damages is supported by evidence of medical bills. Use federal law — do not dismiss.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Differing limitations period unlikely to affect parties' primary conduct, e.g. business decisions. Limitations period serves procedural purpose of preventing courts from being overwhelmed by outdated cases. Use federal law — do not dismiss, because limitations not expired.

**Scalia Hanna:** No valid and applicable federal provision; FNC is FCL (*Piper*). RDA controls. Since state law requires dismissal but federal law may not require dismissal, failure to apply state law may encourage plaintiffs to forum shop to avoid dismissal. Use state law, dismiss. (No equal protection problem, plaintiff is non-US (*Piper* — American plaintiffs are masters of complaint)).

FRCP 8(a), which does not require attachment of medical bills, is valid under REA since it "really regulate(s) procedure" (*Sibbach*) and applicable since it involves pleading requirements. Use federal law (Supremacy Clause) — because FRCP 8(a) does not mention medical bills, do not dismiss.

Source of law is congressional statute. Valid exercise of congressional authority under Congress's power to create the lower federal courts (and thus prescribe some rules for them), as augmented by the NPC, if needed. Applicable since statute's text reflects congressional intent to prescribe limitations period for personal injury lawsuits. Use federal law (Supremacy Clause) — do not dismiss.

**Ginsberg SG:** Same validity and applicability as Scalia. Use state law — dismiss.

Construing FRCP 8(a) narrowly, FRCP 8(a) is compatible with ND statute. FRCP 8(a) provides the basic requirements for complaint, ND statute adds medical bills requirement. Same validity and applicability as Scalia. No unavoidable conflict, use state law — dismiss.

Even construing congressional statute of limitations period narrowly, unavoidable conflict with state law, since both laws provide different markers for when limitations starts to run. Same validity and applicability as Scalia. Since unavoidable conflict, use federal law — do not dismiss.

## Q2

Different source of federal law in each argument. ND FNC common law conflicts with federal common law FNC doctrine (*Piper*). Medical bill pleading requirement conflicts with rule of civil procedure. ND statutory limitations period conflicts with congressional statute.

## Q3

Ginsberg argument for pleading requirement may result in incoherence. FRCP 8(a) stipulates certain pleading requirements, which strike balance between filtering out frivolous complaints while ensuring it is not overtly onerous for pro-se litigants to file complaint. Stitching FRCP 8(a) together with ND statutory requirement of attaching all corresponding medical bills defeats the latter purpose. Also confusing to litigants since pleading requirements are found in two places. One solution is for ND to incorporate FRCP pleading requirements into its statute, along with medical bill requirement — so litigants can refer to pleading requirements in one place.

Mixing state law for length of limitations period, which legislatures may have calculated based on one marker for when it starts to run, with federal law that has different marker for when period starts to run may result in incoherence. Solution would be to apply Ginsberg SG Erie analysis above, and use federal law.

<!-- page: 3 -->

## Q4

The York argument to dismiss case for FNC furthers Erie purpose of federalism. By using state law, courts are respecting how federal government should not usurp state powers beyond its limited enumerated powers. The York test is neutral regarding separation of powers since the court is applying court-made common law. York promotes equal protection among US plaintiffs since the FNC common law rule only mandates dismissal of complaints by non-US-plaintiffs. By applying state law, it addresses prudential concern that federal courts were crowding out state law.

## 2021-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-02-habib-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## 1

Cannot dismiss based on lack of SMJ. Canadian plaintiff and ND defendant mean complete diversity, and $300,000 AIC satisfy diversity jurisdiction requirements. IPJ exists because defendant corporation is a citizen of ND. Venue is proper because IPJ exists (§ 1391(c)(2)).

### FNC

Defendant can move to dismiss under FNC.

Under *York*, court will use ND FNC rule and dismiss because the choice is outcome determinative. Under ND FNC, because events of the lawsuit occurred outside ND (shipment from SD, imbibing in Canada, harm in MT), the case will be dismissed. Under Federal Common Law FNC analysis, court may or may not dismiss, depending on its analysis of the private and public interests at stake, but there is no obviously superior forum.

Under *Hanna* majority (Scalia or Ginsburg), because law at issue is FCL, Court will apply twin aims of *Erie*. Difference in law may encourage forum shopping, as foreign plaintiffs looking to sue in ND will bring claims in federal court if they can meet the AIC requirement to avoid dismissal. Difference in law would cause inequitable administration of laws, as foreign plaintiffs who can't meet AIC cannot avail themselves of a forum in ND. Court likely to use ND FNC law, dismiss case.

Under Harlan/*Hanna*, court assesses whether applying federal law would alter primary conduct of the parties. Applying Federal FNC to ND corporations opens them up to suits from foreign plaintiffs, which could dissuade corporations who ship overseas from incorporating in ND, altering primary conduct. Court likely to dismiss under ND FNC.

### Pleading

Defendant can move to dismiss under ND statute because plaintiff failed to attach medical bills. ND statute clashes with FRCP 8(a)(2), which only requires short plain statement of the claim.

Under *York*, Court will use ND statute because choice is outcome determinative. ND statute mandates dismissal, while FRCP allows the case to proceed.

Under *Hanna*/Scalia, Court will only use FRCP if it is both valid and applicable. FRCP is valid if it doesn't abridge, enlarge, or modify a state substantive right, which it never does. The FRCP is valid because it "really regulates procedure" (*Sibbach*). Though narrow construction is possible (*Walker*), Scalia likely to find FRCP applicable, and use FRCP. Case proceeds.

Under *Shady Grove*/Ginsburg, Court likely to interpret FRCP narrowly to as not to apply and thus not clash with state law, arguing FRCP only sets a floor for pleading requirements, states can require more. State law can apply, case dismissed.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Under Harlan/*Hanna*, Court is likely to find pleading rules would not alter primary conduct of litigants, because filing procedure would concern lawyers. Court would use federal law, and case would proceed.

### SoL

Defendant can move to dismiss under ND 2-year SoL, arguing that harm occurred 26 months before lawsuit was filed, with imbibing of medicine constituting the action by the defendant alleged to have caused injury. Conflicts with Federal law, which would start limitations period with plaintiff's onset of illness, occurring 22 months before filing.

Under *York*, choice of law is outcome determinative. Court would use ND law and dismiss.

Under *Hanna*/Scalia, court will use Federal statute if valid and applicable. Valid given constitutional grant of power to Congress to create lower courts and thus to administer their procedure. Applicable because the statute speaks directly to the SoL issue, and court is unlikely to construe Congressional statute narrowly. Federal law prevails, case proceeds.

Under *Shady Grove*/Ginsburg, it is hard to see how a court could construe this Congressional statute narrowly, and it would be reluctant to do so with a statute (unlike it would a FRCP). Federal law will govern under supremacy clause, case proceeds.

Under Harlan/*Hanna*, when SoL begins running won't alter primary conduct of parties, as it's largely a procedural concern for lawyers. Federal law applies, case proceeds.

## 2

Each basis relies on a different level of law. The FNC issue is FCL, the pleading issue concerns FRCP, and the SoL issue concerns a statute, each warranting a different level of deference and scrutiny under the "valid and applicable" assessment from *Hanna*.

## 3

Varying combinations of the state statute on the length of a SoL and the federal statute on when the SoL period begins to run can produce an incoherent system of law. For example, given facts analogous to those present, a plaintiff could bring a personal injury claim for injuries sustained after consuming expired medicine 10 years after the purchase and/or production of the medicine, despite having a 2-year SoL. This could undermine the legislative intent behind the state 2-year SoL. Solution is to use only state initiation doctrine with the state SoLs.

## 4

Under Harlan/*Hanna* and Harlan/*Scalia* analyses of the pleading rules, court would allow case to proceed despite failure of plaintiff to attach medical bills to complaint. This undermines some justifications of *Erie*: it overrides the ND state legislature's decision to require medical bills at the filing stage of a personal injury suit (federalism), and also puts in-state plaintiffs at a disadvantage, because they cannot avail themselves of more lenient Federal pleading rules (equal protection). Diverse plaintiffs' ability to do so may in turn encourage forum shopping. Conversely, this outcome is in line with *Erie* doctrine's interest in preventing the creation of FCL

<!-- page: 3 -->

(as law at issue is procedural) and ensuring that procedural rules across all federal district courts are consistent.

## 2021-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-02-sridhar-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Q1

**SMJ** exists under § 1332 — Canadian plaintiff vs. ND defendant, $300K > $75K. **There is PJ** — D is at home in ND (principal place of business, incorporated there, 4(k)(1)(A) empowers the federal court). **Proper venue** under § 1391(b)(1) (company is subject to PJ there). We are left with three arguments, all with *Erie* implications.

### Forum non conveniens

*York*

Definitely outcome-determinative. If we use state FNC law, dismissal (events occurred in SD and Canada); if we use federal law, have to continue analysis, with likely no better forum than ND (where the wrongdoing that the complaint focuses on occurred). **State law prevails.**

*Hanna* majority

Federal FNC law is common law. Ask if using state law would prevent forum shopping/inequitable administration of justice. Here, it would — a lawyer representing a foreign national wanting to litigate in ND, if AIC >$75K (thus diversity jurisdiction), would have to file in federal court, but couldn't in state court. The analysis is the same under Scalia and Ginsburg versions. **State law prevails.**

*Harlan concurrence*

No reason to think that FNC affects primary conduct. Drug manufacturers likely to behave the same way with or without FNC; even if they can't be sued in ND by foreign nationals, they can be sued by domestic plaintiffs, and they likely make the same drugs for all. **Federal law prevails.**

### Attaching bills

*York*

Outcome-determinative. Under state rule we dismiss, under federal rule (FRCP 8(a)) (which does not mention medical bills) we proceed. **State law prevails.**

*Hanna* majority (Scalia)

FRCP, so ask if valid and applicable. Under Rules Enabling Act, FRCP 8(a) doesn't "abridge, enlarge, or modify substantive right." It truly does govern procedure (*Sibbach*). FRCP 8(a) is applicable here — states all that is necessary for claim of relief. **Federal law prevails.**

*Ginsburg (Shady)*

Ginsburg tells us construe FRCP with an eye to protect state interests. Here, could argue that there isn't actually a conflict between FRCP 8(a) and the ND statute — FRCP 8(a) is inapplicable because it sets a floor for pleadings and ND adds onto it. Ginsburg tells us that **state law prevails** here.

<!-- page: 2 -->

*Harlan concurrence*

Doesn't affect primary conduct at all — this is a question of what gets attached to a complaint; no effect on incentives to behave a certain way. **Federal law prevails.**

### Statute of limitations

*York*

Outcome-determinative. Under state rule we dismiss (has been at least 26 months since action that caused injury), under federal law we continue (22 months since discovered injury). **Use state law.**

*Hanna* majority (Scalia)

Statute is valid under Congress' Article III power to create — and thus regulate — the lower federal courts. It is directly applicable here — defines statute of limitations tolling for all suits filed in federal district court. **Use federal law.**

*Ginsburg (Shady)*

Ginsburg would have us construe federal law narrowly to try and avoid a conflict with state law — but unlike with the FRCP conflict, basically impossible to avoid conflict here. **Use federal law.**

*Harlan concurrence*

Again, probably little effect on primary conduct. Perhaps companies will hide defects to make injury take longer? But unlikely that SOL affects either companies or consumers. **Use federal law.**

## Q2

The three issues deal with different types of federal law: common law (FNC), a FRCP (attachment) and a federal statute (commencement of the action). Under *Hanna* majority, we treat these differently (don't ask if common law is valid & applicable); also, Ginsburg's test only applies to FRCPs and statutes. Moreover, as described above, there's actually an argument (if not that persuasive) for primary conduct influence for the SOL issue, and none for the other two issues.

## Q3

Yes — in particular, with the statute of limitations issue, we run the risk of a confusing 'hybrid' if we apply the federal SOL running rule. (We'd have a SOL determined by state law but when it begins to run is determined by federal law.) Could end up in an undesired outcome — maybe ND legislature would've drafted a shorter SOL if they'd known SOL would run in federal district court cases from the time of discovering injury. Maybe the best recourse is having the legislature revise its SOL statute to be cognizant of this possibility (e.g. '2 year SOL in state court and 1 year SOL in federal court') — put state and fed court litigants effectively on par.

<!-- page: 3 -->

## Q4

*Hanna* majority is the dominant test, so if that prevails on the statute of limitations issue, we use federal law and end up with an inequitable administration of justice, which *Erie* seeks to prevent. The fortuity of diversity ends up mattering, since out-of-state plaintiffs have longer to decide whether to sue than in-state plaintiffs. Plaintiffs under these statutes will be individuals, but nonetheless, we worry about a *Black and White Taxicab* type situation in which plaintiff is incentivized to artificially change domicile to take advantage of a better ruleset — real forum-shopping concern. There's a separation-of-powers concern with judicially brokered compromise — judge, rather than either state legislature or Congress, decides to mix the federal SOL running rule with state SOL period. Also a potential federalism issue — federal government usurps state power by implementing the federal running rule. No real Rules of Decision Act issue — statute of limitation is definitely procedural (though it may signal the state's view on the substantive merits of such suits.) Finally, from a general prudential standpoint, it is unwise to create 'Frankenlaw' — the federal running rules and state statute of limitations were not designed to coexist.

## 2021-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-02-waldman-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Q1

No SMJ/IPJ/Venue/Process arguments. FRCP 12(b)(1–5)). Defendant has three arguments that plaintiff failed to state claim. FRCP 12(b)(6). FDC is sitting in diversity — it'll conduct VCOL analysis to determine whether to apply federal or ND law for each issue.

Application of ND law on any issue will lead to dismissal (out-of-state plaintiffs, no attached medical bills, and a lawsuit filed in month 26, not within 24 months, of the misconduct),[^1] while application of federal law means suit will proceed. Under *York*, FDC would dismiss under any of these arguments. This is true even for FNC because there are public interest factors favoring proceedings, and because the alleged misconduct, drug ingestion, and sickness all occurred in different states, leaving no obviously superior forum. *Piper*.

### Argument 1: FNC

**Hanna/Scalia:** Likely substantive/state law applies. Federal FNC is FCL. *Piper*. So the RDA controls. *Erie*'s twin aims favor dismissal here. Applying federal law would mean different results in state court (dismissal) vs. FDC (no dismissal), resulting in an incentive to forum shop if a lawyer wants to keep the litigation in ND.

**Hanna/Ginsberg:** Same as *Hanna* (Ginsberg/SG = *Hanna* Majority for FCL).

**Harlan:** Likely substantive/state law applies. Assuming the company ships outside the US often, ND's unwillingness to hear cases emerging from inevitable harm occurring from the company's products would inform the company's primary conduct. (If ND FDC hears these cases, then company might cease selling to non-US customers, or take more safety precautions as it does so.) However, if the company is small/negligence is rare, then applying FCL would *not* alter primary conduct.

### Argument 2: Pleading-Req

ND statute requires dismissal because plaintiff didn't attach their medical bills to their complaint. The ND requirement is absent in FRCP 8(a)(2), which doesn't require supporting evidence with a complaint (or speak to the issue).

**Scalia/Hanna:** Likely procedural/federal — no dismissal. FRCP 8 is valid (FRCP derived from REA, it "really regulates procedure," *Sibbach*, because what's required in a complaint concerns the manner and means of litigation). FRCP 8 is likely applicable, since it gives uniform pleading standards — it governs what's required in a complaint, and there's no requirement of medical bills (or any other attachment).

**Ginsburg/SG:** Substantive/state, favors dismissal. FRCP still valid (see Scalia argument), but not applicable to the question of whether medical bills are required — FDC will likely construe FRCP narrowly

[^1]: Under federal statute, the SOL wouldn't have lapsed because SOL doesn't begin to run until plaintiff discovers harm — plaintiff's at less than 22 months by federal standard.

<!-- page: 2 -->

so that it doesn't clash with state law. One could argue that FRCP 8 doesn't clash — ND's rule just imposes additional requirements. (FRCP 8 is a minimum requirement.)

**Harlan:** Procedural/Federal. Change in pleading wouldn't alter primary conduct — initial shipments are primary, wouldn't change on basis of pleading standard.

### Argument 3: SOL

**Hanna/Scalia:** Likely Procedural/Federal law. Federal statute valid under Congress' ability to create the lower federal courts, and prescribe housekeeping rules for them, just as the applicable SoL rule. Supremacy clause. The federal statute is applicable — Congress intended to address these particular circumstances, since it seems the law addresses late-arriving harm. This is a statute, so FDC is unlikely to construe federal law narrowly.

**SG/Ginsburg:** Likely substantive/state law. FDC might try and narrowly construe the federal statute to accommodate state interests (ND might have interest in ensuring domiciles don't get hauled into court years after trial), even if this renders federal SOL law unrecognizable. So FDC might conclude that "injury" occurred when plaintiff imbibed drugs, not when illness onset — there would be no state/federal clash.

**Harlan:** Likely procedural/federal. Tolling likely doesn't alter pre-litigation primary conduct. *Walker*.

## Q2

Each argument hinged on a different source of federal law: Argument 1/FNC was based on a state conflict with FCL; Argument 2/Pleading Requirements was based on conflict with FRCP; Argument 3/SOL was based on federal statute. These bring about different Erie tests, particularly under the two Hanna majority frameworks.

## Q3

Pleading issue presents incoherence concerns. If the FDC applies SG/Ginsberg, plaintiffs would have to ensure their claim complied with state *and* federal pleading requirements. This would be burdensome on pro-se/low-resource litigants. FRCP can be modified via SCOTUS-overseen process (REA). FRCP 8 could *clearly state* that state pleading requirements are the *exclusive* requirements in diversity cases. While this might create forum-shopping (litigants looking for states with favorable pleading requirements), it'll help litigants know what they have to file *before* filing. Confusing pleading requirements don't advance *Erie*'s policy goals, federalism, or federal supremacy — they instead create unnecessary confusion.

SOL issue portends incoherence. A FDC could apply *length* of SOL from state law while applying the federal standard for *when* SOL begins to run (possibly different from state's). This threatens incoherence — legislatures determine SOL *length* based on what causes them to run.

## Q4

<!-- page: 3 -->

FDC unlikely to dismiss on pleading basis — this doesn't further *Erie*'s goals. First, this solution resembles the "general law" *Erie* disparaged — it's a judge-made compromise (judge-made raises separation of powers concerns) that contorts federal law (it's not clear that there's a better solution, since Ginsberg's approach would constitute judicial contortion to minimize federal law). Second, it usurps state power — ND clearly desires to limit the volume of litigation in its courts, and this upsets that. Usurpation of state power is a federalism concern.

But allowing claim without medical paperwork does advance equal protection principles. There's equal protection in requiring non-ND domiciles to meet abnormal pleading requirements beyond that which exist in other states. *Cf. Swift*.
