For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

<!-- page: 2 -->

**Note:** The facts in this assignment bear some resemblance to those used in Assignment 1. Please treat this assignment according to the facts described here. Do not assume that anything from Assignment 1 is true here.

## Virginia Law

In 2018, responding to a well-funded lobbying campaign from the Virginia Chamber of Commerce, the Virginia General Assembly passed the Rupert Giles Tort Reform Act (the "Giles" Act). The Act includes the following provision.

> **Section 6.7: Heightened Summary Judgment Standard**
> (a) The General Assembly finds that lawsuits may deter investors from starting or investing in industries in the Commonwealth of Virginia.
> (b) Virginia Rule of Civil Procedure 56 is hereby amended to add a new subsection (i), as follows: "In any action alleging negligence against any manufacturer or distributor of a commercial or consumer product, if the plaintiff seeks damages for pain and suffering, the plaintiff shall before the close of discovery provide an affidavit from a licensed psychiatrist detailing the nature and extent of the plaintiff's mental distress. Upon a motion for summary judgment by a defendant alleging a plaintiff's failure to comply with this subsection, the court shall forthwith dismiss the action with prejudice. Any motion for summary judgment on the issue of liability or damages by a plaintiff who fails to comply with this subsection shall be denied."

In all other ways relevant to this exam, the Virginia Rules of Civil Procedure are identical to the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure.

## Law of Negligence

All states relevant to this assignment have a cause of action called "negligence." The elements of the negligence cause of action are that (i) the defendant owed a duty of care to the plaintiff, (ii) the defendant breached that duty by failing to act with due care, (iii) the defendant's breach caused (iv) harm to the plaintiff.

- For the purposes of this assignment, you may assume that all persons (including incorporeal entities) owe a duty to act with due care to all other persons (or corporations or incorporeal entities) with whom they interact or anyone affected by their actions. In other words, you can assume that a plaintiff need not prove element (i) of negligence, regardless of whether the case is brought in state or federal court.
- With respect to element (ii), a plaintiff ordinarily proves this element by alleging or providing evidence that the defendant failed to exercise the ordinary care that a reasonable person would exercise under the circumstances.

<!-- page: 3 -->

- With respect to element (iii), for the purposes of this assignment, a plaintiff proves causation by providing evidence that absent the action constituting the breach of duty, the plaintiff's injury would not have occurred.[^1]
- Injuries are proved through any relevant proof.

## Stipulated Facts

The parties to this lawsuit stipulated to the following facts (meaning that the parties agreed that they were true without the need for proof).

Xander Harris, Inc. ("XH") is a Delaware corporation with its principal place of business in Baltimore, Maryland. It manufactures self-driving trucks of all sizes, from light pickups all the way up to 18 wheelers. All of its manufacturing occurs in a massive plant outside of Baltimore, MD. Each vehicle that XH produces bears a distinctive script "XH" symbol on its front fender. XH conducts no sales activity to consumers or business, nor does it advertise. Instead, it sells its products exclusively in an enormous purchase and sale clearinghouse market facility located in Alexandria, VA called "The Spike." At The Spike, sellers and buyers of self-driving vehicles meet, conduct inspections, negotiate sales terms, transfer money, and take delivery of vehicles. XH sells to various distributors at The Spike. XH insists that any company to whom it sells one of its vehicles must subsequently sell the vehicle directly to a consumer.

Faith, Inc. is a North Carolina corporation with its principal place of business in Columbia, South Carolina. Faith is a large distributor (to consumers) of self-driving vehicles. It advertises and sells self-driving vehicles in a great many states in the eastern half of the United States, including Pennsylvania, Delaware, Maryland, Virginia, North Carolina, and South Carolina. Faith purchases self-driving trucks from, among other sources, manufacturers who patronize the clearinghouse market at The Spike in Alexandria, VA. In early 2020, Faith began advertising a set of XH self-driving trucks throughout its multi-state service area, with television ads running on local TV stations in different cities in Pennsylvania as well as all of the states mentioned immediately above. Faith also ran ads in local newspapers in Pennsylvania cities, including Philadelphia; similar ads ran in local newspapers of cities in other states. The ads all directed those interested in self-driving pickups to Faith's website, where potential buyers could see complete information, including photographs, of the trucks for sale. People interested were instructed to contact a Faith employee named Cordelia Chase. The ads that ran in Philadelphia specified that Faith had 10 XH self-driving trucks waiting in a rented parking facility in the area, ready for immediate inspection and delivery.

In early February of 2020, Chase received an Internet message from Buffy Summers, a Pennsylvania domiciliary living in Philadelphia. Buffy inquired how soon she could inspect one of the XH light vehicles she'd seen advertised on her local TV station, and how much it would cost. A series of email exchanges followed, in which Buffy indicated an interest in purchasing one of the trucks. Buffy was delighted to learn that she could buy the truck as soon as she liked after inspecting it, and that she could inspect it right outside her house, because Chase could instruct the truck to drive itself to her house and park there. Buffy was also excited that her sister, Dawn Summers, visiting from her home in Charleston, SC,

<!-- page: 4 -->

could also take a look at the truck to help her decide whether to buy it. On February 12, 2020, Chase used the Internet to deploy the codes for one of the trucks to instruct it to drive itself to park in front of Buffy's Philadelphia house at 11 am that morning.

At 10:45am, Buffy and Dawn bundled up and went to the sidewalk in front of Buffy's house, located at 1630 Sunnydale Drive, in Philadelphia. As they waited, the two faced each other on the sidewalk next to Sunnydale Drive and occupied themselves by practicing their singing, a pursuit at which both were as enthusiastic as they were unskillful. At 11:00am, the XH pickup approached the house, but neither noticed it. Instead of stopping a couple of feet short of the curb, the pickup pulled its tires right up against the curb. In doing so, the pickup's front right fender and front right side headlight struck Buffy and Dawn, throwing both to the ground.

Before being placed in the ambulance, Dawn photographed the front of the truck, and later noticed from the photograph the "XH" logo. She also noticed from the photograph that the front left fender and headlight (parts of the truck that did not strike the two sisters) bore numerous scratches, as well as substantial dents in three separate locations. The court subsequently accepted that Dawn's photographs, which were admitted into the record, were an accurate representation of the state of the truck that hit Dawn and Buffy shortly after the accident.

Buffy suffered a concussion, a broken leg, and several cuts; she required care in a nearby Philadelphia hospital for 25 days and underwent extensive physical and mental therapy before eventually making her recovery.

## The Litigation

In mid-May of 2020, Buffy filed a pro se complaint in the United States Federal District Court for the Eastern District of Virginia, the geographic jurisdiction of which includes Alexandria, VA. The complaint named XH and Faith as defendants. The complaint alleged the facts recited above and stated a single claim, negligence, against both defendants. Buffy demanded $300,000 to compensate her medical and rehabilitative expenses, as well as her pain and suffering.

No party took discovery, and the parties began to prepare for summary judgment motions.

**Question 1:** Buffy, too poor to afford counsel, is thinking of filing a motion requesting that the court appoint a lawyer to represent her. If she files that motion, will the court grant it? Please limit your answer to no more than three sentences.

**Question 2:** Buffy moves for a preliminary injunction, asking that the District Court order XH and Faith to provide her with a new self-driving truck free of charges so that she can ride in the truck to and from the physical therapy she needs to recover fully. Will the court grant the motion?

Buffy filed a motion for summary judgment on the issue of liability (meaning elements (ii) and (iii) only of the negligence tort defined above, recalling that element (i) need not be proved); she acknowledged in her motion that further litigation would be necessary on damages (element (iv)). In addition to the stipulated-to facts (again, the court will take these stipulated facts as true without a need for further proof), she attached to her motion two affidavits (no others), as follows:

- **Affidavit of Tara Maclay:** In her affidavit, Maclay averred that she had been an employee with XH at the time of the events in question. In mid-December of 2019, XH completed manufacture

<!-- page: 5 -->

  of a set of 50 self-driving light pickup trucks. In the middle of the night of December 16–17, 2019, Maclay, driving an XH car, accompanied the fleet of 50 light pickups in an overnight trip to The Spike. Maclay's instructions were to sell the trucks for a price of $25,000 each, more if she could get it. As her fleet pulled into one of the Spike's parking facilities, Maclay noticed that three of the trucks, while traveling at about five miles per hour, struck the lighting polls illuminating the facility that they were supposed to park behind, causing scratches and minor dents in their front left fenders and around their front left headlights. Maclay said to herself, "That's probably just a one-time malfunction," and proceeded about her duties. On the morning of December 18, 2019, Maclay met with Chase at The Spike. Chase was excited about the new XH trucks, so excited that she immediately began negotiations. During the negotiations, Chase inspected the trucks. She said to Maclay, "I see that a few of these have some dents and some scratches in the front. I thought that XH products were programmed to prevent that sort of thing. Y'all must have had an exciting trip!" Maclay did not respond. Maclay and Chase spent no further time with the trucks, nor did they run any tests. They quickly concluded negotiations for the purchase and sale of the 50 light pickups, and by lunchtime had agreed on a price of $28,000 per truck. Maclay was so delighted that, after signing the purchase and sale agreement on behalf of XH, verifying with XH's business manager that the latter had received the corresponding funds from Faith, and providing Chase with the required access codes needed to operate the vehicles, she took the day off. Maclay averred that when she found out what happened to Buffy, she quit working for XH and went back to graduate school to pursue life as a witch.

- **Affidavit of V. Angel:** Mr. Angel averred that he was a private investigator. Doing a favor for a friend who was a fan of Buffy's, he obtained the VIN numbers from Maclay (who had been willing to speak to him) of the 50 trucks she sold to Chase/Faith at The Spike and compared them to the truck that hit Buffy. One of the numbers matched. Next, he used various Internet sources to locate eight of the other trucks sold in the lot of 50 that Maclay transferred to Chase. During June 2020, he photographed fronts of the vehicles, and he attached the photographs to his affidavit. All eight showed significant scratching and denting in the front fender and around the front headlights.

XH and Faith filed a joint memorandum in opposition to the motion for summary judgment. They attached to their memorandum a single affidavit, from Cordelia Chase. In her affidavit, Chase agreed that she had met Maclay at the Spike, and had purchased a lot of trucks from her. But, she stated, she and Maclay had run standard tests on the trucks, including testing whether they stopped when approaching solid objects, at The Spike before agreeing to a purchase and sale contract.

**Question 3:** How should the court rule on the motion for summary judgment? Explain your answer.

**Question 4:** Suppose the court grants Buffy's motion for summary judgment. XH and Faith file an immediate notice of appeal. How should the circuit court of appeals rule on the appeal? Please limit your answer to no more than two sentences.

[^1]: In your first year torts class, you may (depending on who is teaching it) encounter a doctrine called "proximate cause." Ignore that doctrine in this assignment. You may assume that proving "but-for" cause is sufficient for this assignment.

## 2021-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-03-aarons-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

The *Matthews v. Eldridge* analysis to determine PDP demands for Buffy's access to counsel weighs against right to counsel. Buffy's private interest — damages — does not outweigh government interest in reducing costs, and *Lassiter* suggests a presumption against counsel unless physical liberty is at stake.

## Question 2

The balance of the four F.R.C.P. 65 PI factors weighs against granting the motion, mostly based on the lack of irreparable harm:

(1) Buffy is likely to prevail on the merits. The duty element of her negligence claim is assumed satisfied. Breach is also likely satisfied: based on the visible dents in the car that did not result from its collision with Buffy and the fact that the car went over the curb to hit Buffy and Dawn, Chase probably did not take ordinary care to inspect the car before sending it Buffy's way. Same for XH, but might be more tenuous because they gave cars to Faith. But-for cause is satisfied. Without the car going over the curb, Buffy would not be injured. Injury is satisfied.

(2) There is no irreparable harm absent relief because Buffy's injury already occurred. The court can compensate with damages. Money (that can be awarded/reimbursed later) can pay for her transportation.

(3) Balance of the equities does not tip in Buffy's favor. The harm resulting if PI issued to XH & Faith is monetary; the harm resulting if PI not issued to Buffy is also monetary.

(4) Public interest would not be served by granting PI. If there is a regulatory scheme governing self-driving cars, would damage that scheme (*Amgen*). If no regulatory scheme, PI could chill innovation of self-driving cars.

## Question 3

Applying F.R.C.P. 56, Buffy's motion for summary judgment would be granted. Buffy must show no genuine issue of material fact and that she is entitled to judgment as a matter of law. Using Brennan's framework in *Celotex*, all facts are reasonably viewed in the light most favorable to Faith and XH, the nonmoving party. Buffy, the moving party, bears the burden of persuasion for liability. She must provide evidence that if unrebutted would require a rational trier of fact to find in her favor. Buffy did do so through submitting the Maclay and Angel affidavits, which, together, show that there was a lack of due care in inspecting the trucks after obvious evidence that they were malfunctioning, and that this breach was causally connected with Buffy's injury by the specific truck.

The burden of production shifts to XH and Faith. They might have produced evidence of a genuine issue of material fact based on the Chase affidavit, which by averring adequate testing conflicts with a finding of a lack of due care based on Buffy's attached affidavits. However, an analogy to *Scott v. Harris* should lead to a court to grant Buffy's motion. Based on the photos of

<!-- page: 2 -->

the 8 damaged vehicles, which XH/Faith do not attempt to explain, the court would find no genuine issue of material fact on the question of due care / inspection, and therefore, liability.

If VA's heightened summary judgment standard applies (Section 6.7, including amended V.R.C.P. 56), Buffy's motion for summary judgment will be denied. She did not provide the required affidavit from a psychiatrist. *Erie*:

Under *York*, the difference between state and federal law is outcome determinative of summary judgment. Use state law and deny Buffy's motion for summary judgment.

**Harlan:** difference in V.R.C.P. 56 and federal law likely would not impact Buffy's pre-litigation conduct, only the acquisition of affidavits in preparation for litigation. However, the heightened standard's impact on investors might compel companies to move to VA and alter their conduct before litigation. Probably use federal law and grant Buffy's motion for summary judgment.

**Scalia:** F.R.C.P. 56 validity measured by REA (doesn't abridge, enlarge or modify a substantive right, as it "really regulates" procedure (*Sibbach*)). Applicability: governs the exact issue of what is required for summary judgment motion. Apply F.R.C.P. and grant summary judgment.

**Ginsburg:** Agrees with Scalia on validity. For applicability, construe federal rule with sensitivity to important state interests. Express state interest articulated in "Giles" Act against deterring investors from self-driving vehicle businesses in VA. Might say that F.R.C.P. and V.R.C.P. don't conflict because F.R.C.P. is merely silent on the issue of affidavit requirement. Apply V.R.C.P. and deny Buffy's summary judgment motion.

## Question 4

The circuit court of appeals will dismiss for lack of jurisdiction. Rerun of *Liberty Mutual*. Offensive summary judgment by a plaintiff with no ruling on liability.

## 2021-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-03-canzius-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Q1

*Matthews* governs this PDP issue. The courts will consider (i) private interests of the party; (ii) risk of erroneous deprivation; and (iii) the government's interest (fiscal/administrative burdens). In this case, Buffy's private interests in receiving damages (rather than say, her personal liberty being at stake) is low compared to the parental rights issue in *Lassiter*, which was still not enough for appointed counsel. In addition, there is nothing super-complex about this case, so the risk of erroneous determination is average. These factors thus do not outweigh the government's interest in reducing fiscal/administrative burdens associated with providing counsel.

## Q2

*Rule 65* governs this Preliminary Injunction (PI) issue. There is a circuit split as to whether the four factors are required, or merely balanced. Either way, for Buffy, court is likely to deny the motion for PI (lack of irreparable harm).

(i) Buffy's likely to succeed on the merits:
   - a. Duty: Assumed on the manufacturer/distributor's part;
   - b. Breach: A reasonable manufacturer/distributor of a self-driving car would take ordinary care in inspecting and ensuring such car would navigate accurately and stop appropriately prior to selling/sending it to customer's home;
   - c. Causation: If manufacturer/distributor inspected the car prior to sending it to Buffy's house, it would know its navigation/stopping was defective and would not have sent it to Buffy's home;
   - d. Damages: Buffy incurred physical harm and her pickup was damaged due to the self-driving car coming onto the curb (evidenced by dent to the pickup and her medical records).

(ii) Immediate/Irreparable harm: Because the injury already occurred, there is not much the court can do other than provide damages to Buffy, which is not irreparable harm. This factor would likely be determinative in denying the PI.

(iii) Balance of equities: Both parties have a risk of incurring only monetary harm whether or not PI is granted.

(iv) Public interest: This is a purely private dispute, with little affect on public interest either way. There is, for example, no regulatory schemes at issue.

## Q3

*Fed. R. Civ. P. 56 (FRCP 56)* governs Motion for Summary Judgment (MSJ). MSJ shall be granted if movant shows there is no genuine dispute of material fact and movant is entitled to judgment as matter of law. *Celotex* governs the MSJ analysis. Court would likely grant Buffy's MSJ on the two elements:

a. **Plaintiff bears burden of persuasion at trial as to elements ((ii) breach of duty of care and (iii) causation) of negligence claim:** Buffy would need to prove that the

<!-- page: 2 -->

manufacturer/distributor breached their duty of care and that such breach caused her and her sister's injuries.

b. **Since moving party in this case is plaintiff, J. Brennan's dissent in *Celotex* tells us that she also bears the burden of production:** Buffy's able to offer two affidavits suggesting there was a lack of due care by the manufacturer/distributor in inspecting the trucks that were sold. The affidavits further demonstrate such lack of due care was the reason Buffy was hit by the self-driving car, and suffered injuries. **This evidence, if unrebutted by the non-moving party, would cause a trier of fact to only find in favor of the Plaintiff (*Celotex*).**

c. **Since Buffy as the moving party and plaintiff met her burden of production, the burden shifts to XH/Faith (non-moving parties).** XH and Faith can submit Chase's affidavit, which on its face suggests that there may be a genuine issue of material fact. However, the court is likely to base its judgment on the photos and evidence submitted with the MSJ (*Scott*), which, even when viewed in the light most favorable to the non-moving party, show that it is more likely than not that there was in fact a lack of due care by the manufacturer/distributor which caused Buffy's injuries.

However, if VA law § 6.7 (VRCP 56) applies, Buffy's MSJ will be denied because she failed to submit the required psychiatrist analysis regarding mental distress, causing a vertical choice of law issue (*Erie*):

**York:** Outcome Determinative —
- Applying FRCP 56 for this MSJ would be outcome determinative. As such, we'd use the VA law (MSJ denied).

**Hanna Majority (for FRCPs): Valid and Applicable —**
- **Validity:** Measured by the REA (28 U.S.C. §§ 2071–2072(b)), and does not abridge, enlarge or modify a substantive right (*Sibbach*).
- **Applicability:**
  - Scalia: Construe FRCP 56 according to its text. Since it regulates the procedure of filing a MSJ, must use federal law (MSJ granted).
  - Ginsburg: Narrowly Construe FRCP 56; may claim VRCP 56 does not exactly "conflict" with FRCP 56, and instead requires additional documentation, so use state law (MSJ denied).

**Harlan:** Primary conduct —
- In Buffy's case, the VA General Assembly found that lawsuits may deter investors from investing in industries, thus causing some pre-litigation conduct of some parties. If the court agrees with the VA General Assembly that applying FRCP 56 would cause primary conduct of the parties to change, they will determine that FRCP 56 has wandered into a substantive sphere and therefore will apply the state law (MSJ denied.)

<!-- page: 3 -->

## Q4

*Liberty Mutual* governs this issue. In order to be able to file an appeal, there must be a final order (judgment) from the trial court. Because the finding on liability did not finally dispose of Buffy's prayers for relief, the issue is not appealable to the court of appeals. As in *Liberty Mutual*, none of the exceptions apply.

## 2021-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-03-wagner-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Q1

Probably not — there is a presumption of no entitlement to counsel where liberty not at stake (*Lassiter*). Applying *Matthews*, private interest of compensation is slight as compared to parental rights, and the increased risk of erroneous determination (attorney can probably more effectively prove negligence elements) is not enough to outweigh public/government interest in avoiding cost of providing civil right of counsel.

## Q2

(1) Likelihood of success of negligence claim:
**(i) Assumed (ii) Buffy probably satisfies, but hardest element to prove:** XH and Faith probably both breached their duty — scratches/substantial dents in multiple locations suggest that car was not inspected, and it's not reasonable that it would have hit/scraped multiple (unknown) objects without a malfunction. Also fact that it pulled right up to curb (if unreasonable — which seems likely) either is an unreasonable programming or further evidence of no inspection. **(iii) Buffy probably satisfies:** without it pulling up to curb, the accident wouldn't have occurred. They were standing on sidewalk, not in street. **(iv) Buffy satisfies:** injuries clear — she's in hospital.

(2) Irreparable harm: no irreparable harm because $ could cover cost of transportation, so reparable. And her injury already occurred — she can be compensated with damages.

(3) Balance of the equities: monetary harm for Buffy if injunction not granted; monetary harm for Faith/XH if it is. Doesn't tip either way.

(4) Public interest: possible analogy to *Amgen* cuts against Buffy — concern that forcing companies to give out merchandise for free is overstepping court's bound (though not frustrating regulatory scheme as obviously as *Amgen*, so weaker argument than *Amgen*).

Because she clearly lost on irreparable harm, court very unlikely to grant preliminary injunction motion.

## Q3

### Brennan *Celotex* framework (FRCP 56)

Court will grant if Buffy shows no genuine dispute as to any material fact and that Buffy is entitled to judgment as a matter of law. Buffy has the burden to produce evidence that, if unrebutted, would compel a rational trier of fact to rule in her favor (drawing all reasonable inferences for the nonmoving party).

Buffy meets burden of production via Maclay/Angel affidavits because they show that XH/Faith didn't inspect (breach) when presented with scratches and dents in the front suggesting malfunctions in bumping into things, and in fact, the trucks did bump into things at the Spike, followed by the lack of inspection, caused cars to hit Buffy (causation).

<!-- page: 2 -->

The burden of production shifts to XH/Faith, whose Chase affidavit (alleging testing demonstrating that trucks stopped when approaching solid objects) seems to show a genuine issue for trial.

However, because Buffy has Angel's photos of the fronts of vehicles showing dents and scratches (XH/Faith do not attempt to contradict), this is analogous to *Scott v. Harris*: no rational trier of fact can disregard this evidence, which shows that the fleet of trucks purchased do *not* stop when approaching solid objects. So judge cannot draw the reasonable inference in favor of XH/Faith w/r/t whether or not XH/Faith inspected the vehicles (they clearly didn't, or if they did, they did not react appropriately).

All in all, court would likely grant Buffy's motion for SJ under FRCP 56, but this depends on VCOL analysis (below).

### VCOL

Buffy sought damages for pain and suffering in a negligence action, but did not provide affidavit from licensed psychiatrist detailing nature/extent of plaintiff's mental distress, implicating VRCP 56 § 6.7(b)(i). If this section applies, they will deny Buffy's motion. Erie analysis needed.

**York:** outcome determinative because state/federal dictates whether or not motion for SJ will be granted. Use state law.

**Harlan:** wouldn't change Buffy's pre-litigation conduct. This is about attaching an affidavit to a complaint — which would be *during* litigation. However, perhaps (as the General Assembly found in the Giles Act § 6.7(a)) companies like XH/Faith who are typically defendants would become more aggressive in their conduct, perceiving less danger of litigation where plaintiffs have a hard time complying with VRCP 56 and procuring an affidavit from a licensed psychiatrist detailing nature/extent of mental distress (perhaps due to lack of merit in the case). Overall, probably use state law, but it's close.

**Hanna/Scalia:** Apply FRCP framework. Valid because under *Sibbach*/2072, really regulates procedure. Applicable because federal/state law both address what a SJ motion, as interpreted by *Celotex*/Brennan, must contain. Use federal law.

**Hanna/Ginsburg:** Same validity test as Scalia. Applicability = similar approach to *Cohen* — FRCP silence on needing psychiatrist affidavit means no particular preference on the issue, allowing states like VA to impose additional requirements. No conflict. VA expressed its interest in § 6.7: VA does not want litigation to deter investors from starting or investing in industries in VA. Use state law.

All in all, state law likely applies, so motion for SJ likely dismissed.

## Q4

<!-- page: 3 -->

Fact pattern mirrors *Liberty Mutual* — court should not grant the appeal because it was not a final order, and no exception, so no appellate jurisdiction. As in *Liberty Mutual*, lower court hasn't granted final judgment.

## 2021-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2021-assignment-03-walker-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

No. There is a presumption of no right to counsel unless physical liberty is at issue, and if termination of parental rights was not a weighty enough interest in *Lassiter*, a negligence claim seems unlikely.

## Question 2

The court will likely reject the injunction request. Elements in interpretation of FRCP 65 (in some circuits (e.g., 9th Cir.) these are factors, in others, they are requirements) (*Thanecoat*):

(1) Likelihood of success on the merits: The facts make it likely that Buffy will win on the merits. (i) We presume everyone owes a duty of care (given); (ii) Buffy likely will prove that XH and/or Faith breached the duty by not inspecting the trucks for malfunctions given how many bumps and scratches they had before hitting the sisters; (iii) Buffy likely will prove that had XH and/or Faith investigated the truck, she wouldn't have been hit (but-for causation), and that (iv) the truck's hitting her caused her physical and emotional harm.

(2) Irreparable harm: Buffy probably cannot show that without the injunction she would suffer irreparable harm. Buffy might argue that without the truck, her physical therapy would necessarily be delayed, causing a loss to bodily health (which might count as irreparable because body parts are irreparable for the purposes of preliminary injunctions), but this is a stretch because Buffy already "required care in a nearby Philadelphia hospital…before making her recovery." This indicates that the costs are mostly already borne and might therefore be compensable through money. Further, physical therapy might be possibly done satisfactorily later (after trial) if she has already recovered.

(3) Balance of the Equities: This injunction seems disproportionately harm the defendants for the benefit it gives. Even if we presumed there would be irreparable harm if Buffy didn't make it to therapy immediately, providing a self-driving truck for the sole purpose of driving her to and from therapy, which could be done with taxi or Uber money or, health permitting, public transportation is disproportionate.

(4) Public Interest: If the cars really are malfunctioning, putting more on the street (which is what Buffy is requesting) would endanger the public.

## Question 3

If state law, Giles Act applies, as Buffy gave no psychiatrist's affidavit, deny motion.

If federal law, apply *Celotex* (Brennan, J., dissenting). Buffy has burden of persuasion at trial and therefore must affirmatively produce evidence that if uncontradicted would compel a reasonable trier of fact to rule in her favor even though the judge reasonably construed the facts in the light most favorable to XH/Faith. Even in the light most favorable to XH/Faith, Buffy produced strong evidence of (ii) and (iii). For (ii) Buffy has showed with Maclay's affidavit that Maclay told

<!-- page: 2 -->

herself that three of the trucks hit polls and wrote it off to herself as a "one-time malfunction;" a reasonable person might think that a bigger cause for concern. Chase didn't investigate the scratches further like a reasonable person might have given she knew the vehicles were supposed to be programmed to avoid that and Maclay didn't give an explanation. For (iii), Angel's affidavit showing he was able to match numbers of the trucks is strong evidence that it was their failure to investigate the malfunctions which led to Buffy to be hit. The burden of production shifts to XH/Faith. XH/Faith with Chase's affidavit raised a genuine issue of material fact: if, as Chase avers, Chase and Maclay ran and relied upon the standard tests, they likely did act with due care, rebutting (ii). Likely, the motion for Summary judgment fails. There is a chance, however, the judge could grant it by analogizing it to the uncontradicted, object of the physical damage to the trucks, for which XH/Faith offer no explanation, to the video in *Scott*.

*Erie* implicated:

*York*: Dismissal of case would change outcome (no way Buffy can win if Giles Act applies on these facts, whereas she might under FRCP 56). Therefore, substantive, so use state law.

Harlan: One might argue that summary judgment rules are unlikely to affect how people act prior to any litigation, but as Giles Act § 6.7(a) states, maybe the heightened standard changes investors' conduct and attracts them to the state before litigation. If so, substantive and use state law.

Scalia: FRCP 56 is likely valid because it "really regulates procedure" (*Sibbach*, 2072(b), never found a FRCP invalid; courts approve them after all). Applicable to the situation because this is about summary judgment.

Ginsberg: Validity analysis is the same as Scalia. More willing than Scalia to render FRCP 56 inapplicable given the expressed state interest. FRCP 56 says the movant must show "no genuine dispute as to any material fact and that the movant is entitled to judgment as a matter of law." Maybe here there is no issue of material fact, but the lack of the affidavit (on which the rule is silent) is another way not listed in the rule, that the movant is not entitled to a judgment as a matter of law. Therefore, probably apply state law.

In sum, the tests point towards using state law, and therefore the denial of the motion per the Giles Act.

## Question 4

Only final orders are appealable (28 U.S.C. § 1291). Summary judgment here, a finding of liability *without* relief, is not a final order; none of the exceptions apply, as this case is procedurally identical to *Liberty Mutual*.

# ASSIGNMENT 2022-assignment-01 — Assignment 1 (Ungraded) — Civil Procedure 1 & 2, 2022

Source: content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-01.md

## 2022-assignment-01 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 1 (Ungraded)

Civil Procedure 1 & 2, 2022
Professor Jim Greiner

## Logistics and Rules

Posting date: Thursday, September 22, 5pm
Due date/time: 9am on Monday, September 26, 9:00am
Word limit: 850 words
Time limit: 3 hours and 15 minutes

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from Exam 4.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response via the Exam 4 software. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is ungraded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. The Registrar's Office has sent you information about what to use for an anonymous ID. Please follow those instructions.

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

<!-- page: 2 -->

---

All states relevant to this assignment have a standard products liability cause of action. Products liability requires a plaintiff to prove that the defendant sold the plaintiff a product, that the product was defectively designed or manufactured, and that the defect caused the plaintiff's injuries. Products liability is a tort. The Florida limitations period for a products liability cause of action is four years. Vermont's is also four years. Michigan, Oklahoma, and Texas have two-year limitations periods for products liability lawsuits. Michigan and Florida use the First Restatement for horizontal choice of law. Vermont and Texas use the Second Restatement for horizontal choice of law. All states relevant to this assignment have long-arm statutes that go to the limits of the U.S. Constitution.

Inigo Montoya was born in Detroit, MI, and grew up without family, living largely in foster homes. He loved vintage computers and computer games, particularly games with revenge themes. After graduating from high school in Detroit, and wanting eventually to pursue a career in the revenge business, he began to support himself by obtaining computers from the 1970s and early 1980s, restoring them so that they could play games from that era, and selling them to customers with whom he connected on the Internet. His business was a modest success, and having always wanted to travel, he bought himself a full-service recreational vehicle ("RV"), which he christened "Roberts," to use as his home. Hating the cold, in 2015 at the age of 23, he drove Roberts to Pensacola, FL, and rented an RV plot for one year, registering to vote there, obtaining a FL driver's license, and receiving mail at a local post office box. Financially, he opened a business checking account with the Bank of Florin at a Pensacola Branch, giving the bank his RV plot address and his post office box. The Bank of Florin was Florida corporation with its principal place of business in Pensacola, FL, but it had branches around the country. Although Montoya did almost all of his banking online (he received all of his statements electronically), occasionally he visited Bank of Florin branches wherever he happened to be, sometimes to deposit old-style personal checks that his customers sent him as payment. Perhaps illegally, Montoya never paid taxes.

In 2016, when the lease on his Pensacola RV plot expired, Montoya closed his Pensacola post office box (telling no one) and began to drive around the country in Roberts, parking for as long as he felt like staying, then moving on. He never stayed in one location for more than four months. He received any necessary mail at a FedEx facility near wherever he happened to be (he always told people with mail he wanted to receive which FedEx facility to use and did not worry about any other mail). As he began to travel, he bought an old Polaroid instant camera and a scanner, and he began to document his travels on his personal website, youkilledmyfather.net. He also ran his business from this website. He listed his (now-closed) Pensacola P.O. Box on the website, but he never used it. His business model was as follows. Montoya perused various traditional and non-traditional outlets, mostly online, across the world for no-longer-functioning vintage computer hardware as well as vintage software packages, particularly pieces that no longer worked. He bought these items cheaply and had them shipped to a FedEx wherever he happened to be at the time. He refurbished and repaired the vintage items, and marketed them on youkilledmyfather.net, which could be viewed anywhere in the world. He sold the products to customers from whom he received orders online, shipping them to addresses and receiving payments via Paypal.

<!-- page: 3 -->

In early 2017, Montoya finished work on a multi-month project, the restoration of a rare first edition of a Xerox Soprano personal computer, capable of running Guilder, one of the first multi-game personal computer software packages. Montoya listed the device on youkilledmyfather.net, with accompanying software, at $110,000.

Westley, a 30-year-old fencing instructor, contacted Montoya about the device via youkilledmyfather.net's chat function. Westley grew up and lived all his life Ann Arbor, Michigan. Westley conducted all of the negotiations via the youkilledmyfather.net chat function from his Ann Arbor apartment. He began all of his posts to youkilledmyfather.net's chat function, "Hello from not-so-sunny Michigan! Almost as bleak as a fire swamp, but twice as cold!". During the time he was negotiating with Westley through the website's chat function, Montoya happened to be in Oklahoma, parked outside Oklahoma City. Montoya enjoyed posting photos of the Oklahoma City Zoo, Museum of Science, and Museum of Art.

The negotiations between Montoya and Westley resulted in the following arrangement: Westley agreed to pay Montoya $110,000 via a personal check drawn on a Michigan bank, mailing the check to a Fed Ex facility in Oklahoma City. When he received the check, Montoya would ship the computer and accompanying floppy disks to a FedEx facility in Ann Arbor, Michigan, with instructions to allow Westley to pick it up. Westley told Montoya that he was looking forward to using the new machine for the first time on a vacation he was planning just outside Burlington, Vermont, where he intended to stay in his favorite bed and breakfast (in a historic building, constructed in the 1800s) and visit nearby lakes.

The transaction went off without a hitch. Westley mailed the check to the FedEx location that Montoya had specified in Oklahoma City. Montoya received the check, but did not immediately cash it. After mailing the computer from Oklahoma City to the specified FedEx location in Ann Arbor, MI, he hopped into Roberts and started driving south, intending to spend some time in Dallas, Texas. When he arrived, he rented an RV plot for Roberts in Dallas. The "lease" on the plot was for four months. When he arrived in the Dallas area, he visited a local Bank of Florin branch in person, deposited several customer checks including that of Westley, and did some other banking there related to his ongoing business.

On April 14, 2017, Westley retrieved the vintage computer from the FedEx in Ann Arbor, Michigan. He then drove his car through the night to Burlington, Vermont, arriving on April 17, 2017. Too excited to go to bed, he checked into his bed and breakfast, plugged in the computer, and started to play the vintage games. The computer caught on fire and exploded. Westley suffered moderate burns and had to be hospitalized in a Burlington hospital. His medical bills totaled about $150,000. When he recovered, Westley took the remains of the computer back to his apartment in Ann Arbor, Michigan.

Back in Burlington, the fire had been contained in the Westley's bed and breakfast room before spreading very far. The fire chief on the scene determined that the fire started at or near the outlet into which Westley had plugged the new computer, but did not attempt to determine further whether the wiring on the outlet was old or faulty, or whether there was something wrong with the computer's power cable.

Near the end of May, 2017, Westley received his bank statement and saw that Montoya had cashed his check at the Bank of Florin branch in Dallas, TX. He wrote to Montoya using the chat function of youkilledmyfather.net. He told Montoya about the fire and his injuries, and informed Montoya that he thought that faulty wiring on the computer's power cable caused the fire. He demanded that Montoya

<!-- page: 4 -->

pay him $150,000 in medical bills plus $50,000 in pain and suffering. Alternatively, he demanded that Montoya refund exactly what he had paid for the computer. Montoya refused, writing, "There was nothing wrong with the computer's power system. I wired that part of the computer myself, and I know what I'm doing. If you want to sue someone, sue the bed and breakfast where you stayed. Its wiring caused the accident."

Not having enough money to hire a lawyer, Westley did nothing until May of 2020. At that point, he consulted you; he'd heard great things about your legal skills. He asked two questions:

1. Could he sue Montoya in personam for products liability in a state court in some state? If so, in which state should he sue to give him the best chance to win? Explain.
2. Could he sue Montoya in personam in a federal court? If so, a federal court in what state? Explain.

Note: You need not discuss venue. In answering #2, you need not discuss in which district within a state a federal lawsuit could be filed. Also, please consider all Civil Procedure issues the fact pattern raises, even if you think one of them would be dispositive.

## 2022-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-01-dubbs-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## (1)

### In Personam Jurisdiction (IPJ)

Florida (FL), Vermont (VT), Michigan (MI), Texas (TX) have long-arm statutes to Constitutional limits.

**FL:** If Montoya is a FL citizen (see below, probably not), FL would have general IPJ: really at home. Otherwise, there are likely insufficient minimal contacts (*International Shoe*): The Pensacola P.O. Box was listed on Montoya's website but never used in Westley/Montoya's transactions; Montoya used a FL bank, but the bank is not relevantly involved in the facts leading to the lawsuit. Regarding fair/reasonable: Montoya has only small ties to FL (voting, license); Montoya can drive/live anywhere for litigation; inconvenient for Westley to come from MI; FL has no interest in noncitizens injured elsewhere; no evidence in FL; HCOL is likely VT. There is likely neither general nor specific IPJ in FL.

**VT:** There are likely not sufficient minimal contacts (*International Shoe*): Although the explosion burned Westley in VT, that's like the accidents in *World-Wide Volkswagen* and *Nicastro*; the accident alone is not enough for minimal contacts, even when Montoya knew Westley was going to VT to use the computer (*WWV*). Regarding fair/reasonable: Montoya has no contacts with VT; inconvenient for both parties; VT has interest in dangerous products brought into its borders and in fire management/prevention; the fire chief to-be-interviewed is in VT, hotel-room's wire-socket to-be-examined in VT; hospital records of Westley's injuries in VT; VT law likely applies for substantive HCOL (place of accident). There is likely no IPJ.

**MI:** If Montoya is a MI citizen (see below, likely), then MI will have general IPJ: really at home. Otherwise, there are minimal contacts (*International Shoe*): damaged computer/Westley in MI, computer shipped to/picked-up in MI, Montoya sold to a MI citizen (*McGee*). Regarding fair/reasonable: Montoya's client (Westley) in MI; long drive for Montoya but can litigate anywhere; MI interested in protecting safety of its citizen, regulating business with citizens; computer/Westley's burns in MI (although see VT above); MI law will likely not apply. There is likely general/specific IPJ.

**TX:** There are likely insufficient minimal contacts (*International Shoe*): Montoya cashed Westley's check in TX, but that's the only contact. Regarding fair/reasonable, Montoya likely has no more contact with TX (unless new clients); inconvenient for both parties; TX has little interest in culmination of business transaction (one cashed check); only physical check in TX (see evidence in VT/MI); and TX substantive law likely does not apply. There is likely no IPJ.

### Horizontal Choice of Law (HCOL)

Statute of limitations (SOL) procedural by state-court precedent: use forum law.

Clock starts 4/14/17 when Westley suffered injuries, it's now 3 years later (it's 5/2020). Westley cannot sue in MI or TX (2-year SOL), but can sue in FL and VT (4-year SOL).

Westley's best choice is FL and hope that it has general IPJ (still within SOL).

<!-- page: 2 -->

## (2)

### Subject-Matter Jurisdiction (SMJ)

**Arising Under Jurisdiction (AUJ)**

Products-liability = State-law cause of action (COA). There is no federal COA (*Mottley*) or federal issue embedded (*Smith-Grable*).

**Diversity Jurisdiction (DJ)**

The amount-in-controversy (AIC) is sufficient under § 1332 ($200k > $75k).

Montoya's citizenship: domicile pegged at MI (birth), but he moved to FL, showed some intent to reside there, which may be enough to change domicile (*Mas*). Montoya leased FL RV plot for only 1-year, but longer than his subsequent leases, and many leases are only one year. He registered to vote in FL, obtained FL driver's license, opened bank account in FL although he did so in a nation-wide bank accessible by him anywhere. Although he closed his FL P.O. box, he left address on his website. But his home/RV moves! Montoya's domicile likely MI but maybe FL. Subsequent 4-month moves had insufficient intent to change domicile afterwards.

If Montoya is a MI domiciliary, there is no DJ (Westley domiciled in MI too) because we lack complete diversity (*Strawbridge*). If Montoya is a FL domiciliary, § 1332 is satisfied.

There is likely no SMJ, meaning he likely cannot sue in federal court.

### IPJ

Under F.R.C.P. 4(k)(1)(A), federal court in FL, VT, MI, TX may exercise IPJ if FL, VT, MI, TX can. See above.

### HCOL

If DJ, federal court uses HCOL rules of state in which court sits (*Klaxon*). See above.

Ultimately, if Montoya FL domicile, have DJ: can sue in federal court in FL (general IPJ, within SOL). Otherwise, no SMJ for federal court.

## 2022-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-01-jee-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## (1)

Westley (W) *can* sue Montoya (M) in FL and MI; he *should* sue in FL.

### IPJ

State courts in FL and MI can likely exercise IPJ over M.

**FL:**

- M might be considered domiciled in FL; then FL can exercise general IPJ over him. M was registered to vote in FL and got a driver's license and opened a FL bank account with his RV address. On the other hand, he rented the RV plot for only one year and closed his post office box when he left.
- FL has specific IPJ over M if minimal contacts between M, FL, and the subject matter, and fair/reasonable factors met. M used a FL corporation, Bank of Florin, to cash the check that paid for the product at issue. This could be a minimal contact, but the bank account has little to do with the exploding computer. *International Shoe*. It seems unlikely that FL would be a fair/reasonable forum. M lived there before, but FL law will not apply, FL has little interest in the case, and FL is not convenient for W.

**MI:**

- M might be domiciled in MI. M was born and raised in MI. "Wanted to travel" — no indication he left MI with intention of having permanent residence in FL. Only rented RV plot in FL for one year. If shown M did not intend to stay in FL, M is still a domiciliary of MI. *Mas*. MI then has general IPJ over him.
- No *Daimler*-type IPJ because while MI has long-arm, no substantial and continuous contacts with MI.
- Specific IPJ likely. Minimal contacts met because M knew he was selling to a MI resident, shipped the product at issue to a MI FedEx, and was paid with a MI bank-issued check. Fair/reasonable factors: M is familiar with MI having been born and raised there, convenient for W, MI has interest in protecting citizens from defective products, the evidence (i.e., computer remains) is in MI.

**TX:** Little reason TX can exercise IPJ over M. Not domiciled: M was "intending to spend some time in Dallas" and the lease on the plot was only for four months — a definite leaving point. *Mas*. Also, no substantial, systematic contacts to justify *Daimler*-type IPJ. Finally, specific IPJ seems unlikely because few contacts linking M, subject matter (the "defective" Xerox Soprano allegedly causing injury), and TX. M cashed a check in TX (with a FL bank), did not manufacture or sell the product in TX; there is little purposeful availment otherwise. Also, not a convenient place for W (no sign he ever went or goes to TX), no evidence/witnesses there.

<!-- page: 2 -->

**VT:** No general or specific IPJ. While M might have spent some time in VT (during nationwide travels), M has not purposefully availed himself of the privilege of conducting any activities in VT. While M knew the computer would be used in VT because W told him, W just happened to be in VT for own reasons (favorite B&B) — not for any reason related to M or the subject matter. W's unilateral actions alone cannot force IPJ upon M. If there were minimal contacts, there is some evidence VT (the hotel wiring) and witnesses (hospitalization, fire inspector) in VT, and VT (place of the injury) law will likely be used, but this is likely not enough. *WWV*.

**OK:** No general IPJ; likely no specific IPJ. Not domiciled: M "happened to be" in OK. M received the check from W at a FedEx in OK and mailed the computer to W from OK, so there might be a minimal contact here with the subject matter (exploding computer). However, fair/reasonable factors reason against OK: not a convenient place for W, no evidence/witnesses there, no other contacts between M and OK, and OK law will not be used.

### Limitations Period

Limitations period is procedural, so use forum's own SOL.

W cannot sue M in MI or TX or OK because two-year SOL. W could sue under FL and VT's four-year SOL, but note: as stated, VT does not have IPJ over M. Best forum is FL.

<!-- page: 3 -->

## (2)

Westley can sue M in federal court based on diversity jurisdiction; Wesley can sue in federal court in FL.

### SMJ

*AU*: No AU SMJ because COA derives from state law. § 1331; *Mottley*. No *Smith-Grable* exception; no federal issue embedded in state COA.

*Diversity*: Since W is domiciled in MI, there is possibly diversity SMJ if M is domiciled in FL. § 1332. Mirrors analysis in Q1 about FL's IPJ over M; see above for why M can be considered domicile of FL. AIC > $75k also met.

### IPJ

All states have long-arm going to length of Constitution; federal IPJ piggybacks on state's exercise of IPJ under 4k1a. As stated, states that can exercise IPJ over M are FL and MI. Thus, federal courts in FL and MI can exercise IPJ over M.

## 2022-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-01-stapleford-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## (1) Yes, and he should sue in FL.

### PJ

#### General

*Likely FL:* There will be general IPJ wherever Montoya is domiciled, but Montoya's domicile is unclear. He perhaps left MI with intent to stay in FL (*Mas*). No other state clearly evinces intent to stay besides MI/FL; he has been transient since leaving FL. He registered to vote in FL, obtained FL driver's license, established place of residence/mailing, and opened bank account there. The one-year plot rental perhaps suggests no sufficient intent to stay, but then again, most residential leases are one year. Also, he closed PO Box without telling anyone, though he does still list it as his address. There is either general IPJ in MI (if no change of domicile) or FL (if domicile changed).

Note that FL's only other contact to this litigation is that Montoya opened his Florin account there, and that Florin is an FL bank, which is likely not enough for minimal contacts for specific IPJ.

#### Specific

For specific jurisdiction, we need (1) a long-arm statute going to the limits of the Constitution (LAS); (2a) minimal contacts linking Montoya, subject matter of litigation, and forum (*International Shoe*); and (2b) that the exercise of IPJ comport with traditional notions of fair play and justice.

*Likely MI:* If MI does not have general IPJ, MI has necessary LAS going to the limits of the Constitution (same with all states; assume applied below). Regarding minimal contacts, Montoya did "purposefully avail" himself of state in connection to the subject of litigation (*International Shoe*) by arranging for the computer to be sent there and forming a contract with a person who identified as being there during the negotiation, which was integral to the business transaction. One sale giving rise to this particular lawsuit is likely sufficient (*McGee*). Litigation in MI is likely fair and reasonable. MI has strong interest in regulating products shipped into the state that could harm its citizens; key evidence (computer remains) and witness (Westley) are there; it's convenient forum for Westley; it's not entirely inconvenient for Montoya as he grew up there and frequently travels cross-country; though MI law will likely not apply.

*Unlikely OK:* Minimal contacts is close, maybe probable. Montoya did "purposefully avail" himself of state/its resources in connection to the subject of litigation by negotiating the sale, receiving the check, and mailing the computer all from OK. But litigation in OK is perhaps not fair and reasonable. Though Montoya's contacts were not fleeting (i.e. staying there for a bit, visiting/posting about typical tourist spots, and doing transaction there), no witnesses/evidence of accident there; Westley's contacts are insignificant; location is not convenient for either; and OK law will likely not apply.

<!-- page: 2 -->

*Possibly TX:* Minimal contacts are unclear, but likely. Montoya "purposefully availed" himself of TX by doing bank business there. But this forum has little other connection with the litigation (e.g., RV plot not connected to litigation). On the other hand, TX bank access is how Montoya benefited from the sale of the computer, which might be just enough. But litigation in the forum would probably not be fair and reasonable. Montoya likely had only fleeting connection with state, and we don't know where he is now, so convenience of forum is unclear. TX is inconvenient for Westley. TX may have an interest in ensuring businessmen who avail themselves of state act legally, but unclear if some banking related to business activities is significant enough. Furthermore, TX law will likely not apply.

*No VT:* There are no minimal contacts. Montoya did not "purposefully avail" himself of state/its resources; did not target VT in particular, nor ship a substantial number of products to VT (*J. McIntyre*); and computer was only located in VT due to unilateral actions of Westley (*Hanson*). If minimum contacts *did* exist, VT is probably still not fair and reasonable. True, VT (place of the injury) law will likely apply, and evidence of wiring/fire chief witness is in VT. But Westley's contacts are relatively insignificant; Montoya has no contacts; and the location of forum is probably relatively inconvenient for both.

### HCOL

Statute of limitations is procedural, so law of the forum governs. SoL clock starts ticking when harm was suffered, aka 4/17/2017. MI, OK, and TX courts will dismiss under their two-year SOLs. FL and VT have four-year SOLs, and so will not dismiss, but there is likely no IPJ in VT.

Florida is only state where a court will likely have PJ and won't dismiss on SoL grounds.

## (2) Yes, and he should sue in FL.

### SMJ

*No AUJ:* Tort is state law cause of action, so no federally created cause of action (*Mottley*). No federal issue embedded in state claim, so no *Smith-Grable* exception.

*Most likely diversity jurisdiction:* AIC is sufficient; even smaller claim of 110k is > 75k. There is no complete diversity if Montoya is a MI domiciliary, since Westley's domicile is MI (*Strawbridge*). Assuming Montoya is FL domiciliary (see Q1), a federal court can hear the case.

### PJ

The same IPJ analysis from Q1 applies here, 4(k)(1)(A), so a FL federal court will likely have IPJ.

## 2022-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-01-stebbins-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

1. State courts in which to sue Montoya — options are FL, VT, TX and MI. (OK Westley would concede has insufficient relationship to the subject matter of the litigation therefore no specific IPJ under *Int'l Shoe*).

State courts are courts of general jurisdiction, so any would hear the case under SMJ.

**PJ:** The issue is where Westley can argue for PJ.

**FL** — FL likely has general IPJ. Westley can argue that Montoya's domicile shifts to FL when he registers to vote, gets a license, and opens post office box. Additionally, the fact that he never changes the address on his website indicates that he still considers FL "home." Montoya will argue a MI domicile, but it is likely that court would hold that, despite his not paying taxes (which he doesn't pay anywhere) he was in FL with an intention to stay in the sense of no natural preset leaving date.

If Montoya convinces the FL court that he is still domiciled in MI, specific IPJ will likely fail in FL. All states have long arm to the limits of the constitution, so the standard for specific IPJ is minimal contacts among the defendant, the subject matter of the litigation, and the forum, plus fairness and reasonableness. FL, apart from Montoya's bank account (which is not connected to the computer repair), lacks minimum contacts to the subject matter of the litigation.

**VT** — General IPJ not satisfied. Montoya appears to have never been in VT, much less make it home and domicile.

Specific IPJ will likely fail in VT. Specific IPJ — sufficient minimum contacts among defendant, forum, and subject matter of litigation are likely missing because, although the accident occurred there, it did so because of the plaintiff's "unilateral activity" (*Hanson*). True, VT law will likely apply for SOL, but absent minimal contacts and thus no IPJ, this likely does not matter.

**MI** — General IPJ may be possible. For domicile, you're from where you're from until you get where you are going. Westley can argue that Montoya is not settled in FL because he stayed so mobile while in FL.

Specific IPJ could pass in MI. Montoya has sufficient litigation-related contacts with MI and because the item was shipped to MI and Westley was in MI, as Montoya knew during the sale negotiations.

**TX** — General IPJ not satisfied. Montoya has not set up significant life ties to TX. No voting, license, taxes, home buying etc.

Specific IPJ will likely fail in TX. Specific IPJ will likely fail for lack of contacts between subject matter and forum. Check was cashed in TX, but the whole transaction took place in OK and MI, and the injury occurred in VT.

**HCOL:** SOL is always procedural for HCOL purposes. Use forum law for procedural issues. SOL in FL and VT — 4 years. Westley should sue Montoya in one of these states. He should choose FL because that's where PJ is. A court in MI and TX will dismiss because their 2-year SOL.

Overall, FL is the best place to sue, so long as we can persuade the FL court that Montoya is a FL domiciliary and obtain general jurisdiction. If so, then FL will use its 4-year SOL, and the case is not time-barred.

2. Yes, Westley can sue in fed court.

**SMJ:** No **AUJ** because no federal issue. Product liability is state law issue. No *Smith-Grable* exception because there is no federal issue, much less an embedded one.

**Diversity** — If Montoya is domiciled in FL, see above, then we have complete diversity (*Strawbridge*). Montoya will argue domicile still in MI which would destroy diversity if successful and get case out of federal SMJ. **AIC** $110k–$200k is greater than minimum $75k, so AIC satisfied.

**IPJ:** Court will piggyback on PJ of state court sits in under 4(k)(1)(a) so see IPJ discussions above, especially the PJ argument for FL.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Under Diversity SMJ, the case can be heard in fed court if they find that Montoya is a FL domiciliary. For HCOL and IPJ reasons discussed above, FL is the best place to sue in fed court. Procedural success of the claim will turn on Montoya's domicile.

# ASSIGNMENT 2022-assignment-02 — Assignment 2 (Graded) — Civil Procedure 1 & 2, 2022

## 2022-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2 (Graded)

Civil Procedure 1 & 2, 2022
Professor Jim Greiner

## Logistics and Rules

Posting date: Tuesday, October 4, 12pm
Due date/time: 9am on Tuesday, October 11
Word limit: 750 words
Time limit: 2 hours and 45 minutes

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from Exam 4.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response via the Exam 4 software. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until your assignment is returned to you. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is graded, as described in the syllabus.

<!-- page: 2 -->

11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. The Registrar's Office has sent you information about what to use for an anonymous ID. Please follow those instructions.

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

Assume that Massachusetts has the following law.

- **Negligence cause of action, two ways to prove breach:** The elements of the tort of negligence are (i) that the defendant breached the duty of due care that all people owe one another, (ii) that the breach caused the harm the plaintiff suffered, and (iii) that the plaintiff suffered quantifiable harm. The plaintiff may allege and/or prove element (i) in two ways:
    - By alleging/proving facts showing that the defendant failed to act with sufficient care for the plaintiff's interests under the circumstances (proceeding in this way is called "simple negligence"); or
    - By alleging/proving that the defendant's actions violated some state or federal law designed, at least in part, to protect people like the plaintiff (proceeding this way is called "negligence per se").
- **Massachusetts Rules of Civil Procedure 8 and 12** have texts identical to Federal Rules 8 and 12. In the 2012 case *Violet v. Dash*, however, the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court held, "Massachusetts rules of civil procedure implement the philosophy of notice pleading. In our courts, a short plain statement of the relevant facts giving rise to the claim for relief is sufficient to survive a motion to dismiss for failure to state a claim."

Massachusetts has only one federal district court, the United States District Court for the District of Massachusetts. Massachusetts has a long-arm statute going to constitutional limits.

<!-- page: 3 -->

In early 2021, Congress passed Drunk Drone Driver Act (the "DDDA"). The DDDA included the following provisions:

- **Section 11:** The Congress finds that as flying drones become cheaper, and as purchasers begin to use them more over populated areas, the number of individuals operating drones while drunk has increased, threatening health, safety, and interstate commerce.
- **Section 12:** The Consumer Products Safety Commission ("CPSC") is hereby directed to promulgate regulations prohibiting the operation of drones while the operator's blood-alcohol content is above .08.
- **Section 13:** The CPSC shall have the power to sue any individual who operates a drone while their blood alcohol content above .08 for a civil penalty of up to $10,000 per violation, regardless of whether any injury to person or property results from such violation.

The Consumer Products Safety Commission, acting pursuant to its authority under DDDA Section 12, promulgated a federal regulation with the force and effect of law prohibiting the operation of a drone while the operator has a blood-alcohol content above .08.

Bob Parr was born and grew up in Providence, Rhode Island. In 2018, he left his parents' home at the age of 18 to attend Boston College in Boston, Massachusetts. In his first year of college, he lived in a freshman dorm. Afterwards, he lived in a Boston condo he inherited in the summer of 2019 from an aunt who died; when he moved into the condo, he obtained a Massachusetts drivers' license and registered to vote in Massachusetts. In the late summer 2019, he met Helen Elast, a Boston College student who had lived her life in Boston. Helen and Bob each thought the other was incredible, and in the summer 2021, they married, with Helen moving into the Boston condo, and both staying there during of these events. Bob and Helen planned on moving to Florida in the winter of 2022, where they would compete in strength, cross-fit, and flexibility competitions.

In the early spring of 2022, Bob and Helen were walking in the Boston Common when a flying drone crash into Bob's neck, shoulders, and back. Bob was hospitalized, and suffered $100,000 in medical bills. At the time, Buddy Pine was operating the drone. Buddy, who had lived all his life in Newport, Rhode Island, was visiting a friend that day. The two had been drinking. Helen saw that Buddy was operating the drone (both the drone itself and the control module Buddy was holding had a distinctive "Syndrome" name and logo on them, and Helen recognized "Syndrome" as a high-end drone manufacturer). About 15 minutes later, when trying to drive away from the scene, Buddy was pulled over, and a breathalyzer measured his blood-alcohol level at .15. Helen later found the subsequent police report.

In the late summer of 2022, Bob sued Buddy in the United States District Court for the District of Massachusetts. The relevant parts of the complaint were as follows.

1. The plaintiff, Bob Parr, is a citizen of Massachusetts. The defendant, Buddy Pine, is a citizen of Rhode Island.
2. On April 11, 2022, in the Boston Common in Boston, Massachusetts, the defendant was operating a Syndrome Drone.

<!-- page: 4 -->

3. The defendant's Syndrome Drone struck the plaintiff, causing injuries and associated medical costs in the amount of $100,000.

**Causes of Action**

4. Count 1, Simple Negligence: The defendant's failure to exercise due care in the operation of the drone constitutes negligence, which negligence caused the plaintiff to suffer medical costs and pain and suffering.
5. Count 2, Negligence Per Se: When the defendant operated the drone, they were in violation of Drunk Drone Driver Act Section 12 and associated regulations, constituting negligence per se.

**Prayer for Relief**

6. Wherefore, the plaintiff demands $100,000 in medical costs, and $100,000 in pain and suffering.

---

Buddy visits your office a week after being personally served with the complaint while in Massachusetts and the corresponding summons. He asks whether you think he can move to dismiss the complaint, and on what grounds. Discuss fully.

## 2022-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-02-chung-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

We can dismiss the complaint possibly for lack of SMJ and more likely for failure to state a claim.

## I. SMJ

### 1. AUJ

Per well-pleaded complaint rule, cause of action must be created by federal law (*Mottley*). Simple negligence is a state law cause of action; no AUJ over Count I.

Possible *Smith-Grable* exception for Count II, but unlikely. Negligence per se requires showing violation of the CPSC regulation, a federal law. If proven, the violation proves negligence; Buddy would certainly dispute it. Therefore, a substantial and disputed federal issue is embedded within state law cause of action. However, allowing S-G exception sweeps too many state-law cases into federal courts, especially given Congress's finding about increased drone use. Moreover, proving violation turns on factual issues such as alcohol level and drone identification (*Empire*). District court will probably not exercise discretion to extend AUJ.

### 2. DJ

DJ might exist over the case, depending on Bob's citizenship. AIC = $100k > $75K.

Buddy = RI, having lived there his whole life. For Bob's citizenship, start with his place of birth and see if domicile changed from RI, i.e. whether he established residence elsewhere without intention to leave at a particular time. While going to college usually does not change domicile, Bob moved into a condo in MA; driver's license, voting registration, marriage & cohabitation with MA citizen all signal intention to stay long-term. If so, then Bob = MA and DJ exists. However, the plan to move to Florida arguably disproves intention to stay, although its lack of specificity in time discounts its relevance.

## II. IPJ

IPJ exists. D.MA has no general jurisdiction over Buddy (= RI), but has specific jurisdiction. MA has long-arm statute going to limits of Constitution. Per DPC, Buddy operated the drone and caused the accident in MA, so minimum contacts link him to the forum (*International Shoe*).
