Visiting his friend supports defendant's convenience in going to forum; MA has interest in providing redress to victims of accidents in its territory, and its law will likely apply — so fair and reasonable. Per 4k1A, a federal court could exercise IPJ.

## III. Failure to State a Claim (FRCP 12(b)(6))

Under federal law, Bob's complaint will be dismissed. FRCP 8(a) represents plausibility pleading. Under *Iqbal*, ¶4 recites the elements of simple negligence that D breached duty of care and that his negligence caused harm. Likewise, ¶5 merely asserts D's violation of federal regulation. So we ignore those. The remaining allegations — D's operation of Syndrome drone and the same drone striking P — do not render it plausible that D was negligent, mostly due to their failure to mention D's drinking/alcohol level.

However, there is a VCOL issue here due to conflicting MA rule, which implements notice pleading, i.e., P need only put D on notice of basic events giving rise to litigation (see,

<!-- page: 2 -->

e.g., *Dioguardi*). ¶2–3 mention describes the accident, along with date & place. Complaint would go forward under MA rule.

Conducting the Erie tests shows that complaint will likely be dismissed.

### 1. York

As explained above, FRCP vs. MA RCP is outcome determinative. Thus, follow MA rule; complaint dismissed.

### 2. Hanna Majority

We follow FRCP if it's valid and applicable. Federal rule is valid if it does not abridge/modify substantive right (REA). Under *Sibbach*, a rule is procedural if it really regulates procedure. Since FRCP 8 regulates process of writing complaint, it's arguably procedural and therefore valid.

**A. Scalia**

For applicability, we read rule in its plain meaning. FRCP 8 plainly applies to Bob's complaint, so no need to look at state statute. Follow federal rule; complaint dismissed.

**B. Ginsburg**

Consider policy behind state rule and see if federal rule can be narrowly construed to accommodate state policy. MA rule reflects plaintiff-friendly policy of facilitating access to courts, especially for unsophisticated litigants. However, the plausibility pleading of FRCP 8 implements a stricter standard than the notice pleading of MA rule, so it's difficult to construe FRCP 8 to defer to MA rule (i.e. FRCP is already narrower than MA rule). Even under Ginsburg, we probably follow federal rule; complaint dismissed.

### 3. Harlan Concurrence

State rule is substantive if it drives parties' primary pre-litigation conduct. Perhaps a lax pleading standard leads drone operators to be more careful and would-be plaintiffs to be more risk-tolerant, but it seems implausible that ordinary people consider pleading standards to calibrate their risk tolerance. The issue of what a complaint must include is likely procedural. Follow federal rule; case dismissed.

## 2022-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-02-kagan-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

### Subject-Matter Jurisdiction

Buddy can move to dismiss for lack of subject-matter jurisdiction.

**AUJ:** There is no arising under jurisdiction. There is no federal COA in the well-pleaded complaint (*Mottley*). The *Smith-Grable* exception looks more plausible: there is likely an embedded, substantial, and disputed issue in the complaint (the issue of how to minimize harms from drunk drone operators, of federal interest according to the DDDA). However, the federal issue at stake is not very significant, sending every issue of drunk drone driving to federal court could sweep too many cases into the federal courts, and this is an issue of fact and not law.

**Diversity:** There is also likely no diversity jurisdiction (1332). The AIC is $100k (> $75k). However, Buddy lived all his life in RI, so he is domiciled there. Though Bob left RI at 18 to go to college in MA, he does not have plans to stay (he and his wife plan to move to FL). Temporarily relocating for school without intent to stay doesn't change domicile (*Mas*) so likely domiciled in RI. True, he has an MA drivers' license, votes in MA, and inherited a condo there, but still likely not sufficient to change domicile. So Buddy and Bob are likely both domiciled in RI, therefore there is no complete diversity.

### Personal Jurisdiction

Buddy is unlikely to prevail on a motion to dismiss for lack of personal jurisdiction. Though there is no general PJ (Bob is not at home, since he is domiciled in RI, and there is no consent), there is specific personal jurisdiction. MA long-arm goes to limits of the constitution, (4k1a, federal court use's state's long-arm statute). Next, there are minimal contacts between the defendant, the subject-matter of the litigation, and the forum because Buddy purposefully availed himself of MA laws by entering the state to visit a friend, and the accident occurred in MA. Fair and reasonableness factors support PJ: though it's unclear how often Buddy comes into MA, witnesses and hospital bills are in MA, the state has interest in protecting its students and minimizing its hospital's expenses, and MA law will likely apply.

**Venue:** Venue is proper under 1391b2 (substantial parts of events occurred in MA).

### Complaint

Buddy can move to dismiss under FRCP 12b6, failure to state a claim. There are three elements of negligence, and line 3 of the complaint shows ii and iii (breach caused harm and that it was quantifiable). However, under plausibility pleading (*Twiqbal*), lines 4 and 5 are conclusory and should be struck. Without them, Bob has not alleged the first element of negligence moves from possible to plausible, either under simple negligence or negligence per se.

To survive a 12b6 motion, the complaint should have mentioned that the CPSC, operating under authority from DDDA, prohibits operating a drone with blood-alcohol content above .08, specifically to decrease threats to health and safety from drunk drone operators. A police report found that Buddy had a blood-alcohol level of .15, which means both that he likely operated the drone erroneously and that he violated a law designed to protect people like Bob.

<!-- page: 2 -->

MARCP 8 and 12 are identical to FRCP, but don't follow *Iqbal*; this complaint would be dismissed under federal law but not state. VCOL / Erie question. Four tests:

1. Under *York* outcome-determinative test, this is outcome determinative (the case would be dismissed if using federal law), so use **state** law.
2. Under *Hanna* Majority Scalia, validity of a federal rule is enabled by Rules Enabling Act, which says Federal Rule cannot "abridge, enlarge, or modify any substantive right"; *Sibbach* says the test is if the FRCP "really regulates procedure." This rule regulates procedure because it has to do with filing the complaint, so it is *valid*. The rule is straightforwardly *applicable* in this case because they govern motions to dismiss for failure to state a claim, so use **federal**.
3. *Hanna* Majority Ginsburg: validity is the same as with Scalia; for applicability, state interest is apparent (deliberately construed the rules differently to allow for more cases to survive motion to dismiss); however difficult to construe narrowly (*Walker*, *Shady Grove*) because normally the FRCP is floor and state RCP is ceiling — here state and federal are incompatible. Likely **federal**.
4. Harlan concurrence: state likely was worried about pre-litigation conduct (would suits be brought at all if likely to be dismissed), however unclear how much this actually impacts pre-litigation conduct in reality. Unclear, might lean **state**.

## 2022-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-02-nielsen-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

Buddy can move to dismiss for lack of SMJ and for failure to state a claim under 12(b)(6), but only the latter is likely to be successful.

## (1) SMJ

Buddy can argue no AUJ under § 1331 because no federal CoA (*Mottley*). Bob will respond that there's an embedded issue of federal law — whether Buddy violated the DDDA regulation by operating the drone while drunk — which is necessary to evaluating Bob's negligence *per se* claim. (*Smith-Grable*). But the regulation's meaning isn't disputed, unlike in *Grable*; rather, the fact that Buddy was drunk (a factual issue) is. If whether ∆ violated a federal law was all that was needed to get AUJ, federal courts would be flooded with state law claims hinging on federal law violations (*Merrill Dow*), which would frustrate the federal/state balance. So no *Smith-Grable*, and no AUJ.

Buddy can also argue no DJ under § 1332 because Bob is RI resident, destroying complete diversity (*Strawbridge*). But Bob will respond that he's lived in MA for four years, owns a condo there, and has registered to vote/obtained a drivers' license there. True, merely moving to a new state (especially for a time-limited activity like college) isn't enough to confer domicile (*Mas*), and we have no info suggesting Bob has affirmatively declared an intent to remain in MA (in fact, he has declared that he intends to move to FL with his wife). But the voter registration, drivers' license, and property ownership all suggest Bob is MA citizen, so there's probably DJ.

## (2) IPJ

No GIPJ because Buddy isn't really at home in MA (he lives in RI), nor is he "at home" there (*Daimler*), having only spent a day in MA. But there's SIPJ because MA has a long-arm that goes to constitutional limits, Buddy allegedly injured Bob in MA (so there are minimal contacts linking ∆ and the forum state (*Int'l Shoe*)), and the exercise of jurisdiction is fair/reasonable in light of Buddy being close to MA, MA's interests in adjudicating disputes involving its citizens, MA's being the location of key evidence (the hospital bills, eyewitnesses), and the likely application of MA substantive law.

## (3) Rule 12(b)(6)

Under *Iqbal*, Bob's complaint likely fails plausibility pleading. Paragraphs 4–6 are legally conclusory and constitute threadbare recitals of the elements of the two negligence causes of action. Paragraph 3 is also conclusory, because it simply states that the drone "caus[ed] injuries." That's enough to allege causation and injury, because getting hit by a drone will hurt, but it doesn't specify how/why those injuries were a product of Bob's negligence (element 2 of the tort). The key fact missing is Buddy's alleged drunkenness; paragraph 3 needs to include a statement that Buddy was drunk and this drunkenness caused him to carelessly operate the drone, which in turn led to Bob's getting hit.

Yet the plausibility pleading standard of FRCP 8(a)(2) and 12(b)(6) may not apply; if MA Rules 8/12 apply (as interpreted in *Violet v. Dash*), Bob's complaint is probably fine under the old notice-pleading standard (*Dioguardi*, suggesting the complaint need only present a discernable ground for relief). Here, since Bob has alleged that Buddy violated DDDA § 12, and has specified the date/location/nature of the key event (putting Buddy on notice), the court could discern that he is alleging Buddy did something wrong; it doesn't need to know exactly what at the complaint stage.

To decide whether FRCP or state rules apply, we need *Erie*.

<!-- page: 2 -->

- *York*: if FRCP applies, dismiss; if MA rules apply, don't dismiss. Apply MA rule/don't dismiss.
- Scalia *Shady Grove*: FRCP is valid b/c Congress delegated authority to promulgate FRCP in Rules Enabling Act and the rule doesn't abridge, enlarge, or modify a substantive right. Using *Sibbach*, the rule "really regulates procedure." FRCP is applicable b/c it directly speaks to the issue at hand (pleading standards). Apply FRCP/don't dismiss.
- Ginsburg *Shady Grove*: FRCP is valid for the same reason as Scalia test. It's hard to see how FRCP 12 isn't applicable; it directly speaks to pleading standards. Unlike in other cases, the federal rule doesn't set a floor with the state adding additional requirements; the state rule is actually a floor here, and the two are thus in direct conflict, which means apply FRCP/don't dismiss.
- Harlan: Pleading requirements don't change litigants' conduct and only affect what lawyers do. But could argue that pleading requirements change primary conduct by making corporations/institutional actors behave more boldly in light of fewer frivolous suits. If true, apply FRCP/don't dismiss.

## 2022-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-02-rodriguez-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

**You likely cannot move to dismiss under 12(b)(1)-SMJ:** Bob likely doesn't have AU-SMJ but likely has D-SMJ. He'll argue he satisfies *Smith/Grable*, but will fail. His state claim contains a federal issue (must show DDDA violation to prove negligence per se), but each *Grable* factor goes against him: the federal issue isn't significant (states regularly adjudicate BAC-levels); granting AU-SMJ to negligence per se claims implicating federal laws will flood federal courts; and the DDDA violation concerns a factual issue (BAC-level).

Bob likely has D-SMJ because he's likely domiciled in MA, you're domiciled in RI, and the $200K AIC exceeds $75K. He'll argue his voter registration, driver's license, MA property ownership, and MA wife all show intent to stay in MA, meaning his domicile changed from RI to MA. You'll argue going to school isn't enough to change domicile (*Mas*) and that he inherited (didn't buy) MA property and intends to move to FL soon, showing he doesn't intend to stay in MA. Bob's case likely prevails granting him diversity given voter registrations, drivers licenses, and property ownership are compelling indications of domicile.

**You cannot move to dismiss under 12(b)(2)-PJ:** You're subject to Specific-PJ in MA. D-MA will follow MA's long-arm statute going to the limits of the Constitution (4(k)(1)(a)). You have minimal contacts with the forum and litigation because you flew your drone in MA and it hit/injured Bob in MA. It's fair/reasonable for MA to exercise jurisdiction because you have sufficient MA contacts (see above); MA is convenient for you (you've shown you can go to MA for the day) and Bob (MA resident); MA is interested in protecting Massachussettans from drunk drone operators; MA has evidence and witnesses; and as place of injury, MA law will likely apply to many substantive issues.

**You cannot move to dismiss under 12(b)(3)-Venue:** § 1391(b)(2) grants venue because substantial events leading to Bob's claim occurred in MA (see above).

**Your ability to dismiss under 12(b)(6) depends on *Erie*:** MA's notice pleading standard differs from FRCPs 12(b)(6) and 8(a)(2) as interpreted in *Iqbal* creating an *Erie* issue. **Under MA's standard, Bob's complaint survives 12(b)(6)** because it includes sufficient information to put you on notice about what you're charged with. It describes the day you allegedly operated a drone that struck Bob and the DDDA section you allegedly violated. **Under FRCPs as interpreted in *Iqbal*, Bob's complaint doesn't survive** because it doesn't plausibly allege an MA negligence cause of action. The court will ignore the second half of ¶3 as conclusory about

<!-- page: 2 -->

injury causation and ¶¶4–5 as mere recitations of MA's negligence cause of action's elements. The remaining information doesn't nudge the complaint from possibly to plausibly giving rise to negligence. ¶¶1–2, 6, and the first half of 3 don't describe facts suggesting you plausibly failed to act with care or that your BAC-level was high enough to plausibly establish a DDDA violation and negligence per se.

If MA law governs, your motion fails; if the FRCPs govern, your motion succeeds. Two *Erie* tests suggest state law governs and two suggest the FRCPs govern.

**Under *York*, MA law governs.** The choice between MA's pleading standard and the federal *Iqbal* standard determines the outcome of the motion (see above) thus state (MA) law governs.

**Under *Hanna*-Scalia, the FRCPs govern.** FRCPs 12(b)(6) and 8(a)(2), as interpreted in *Iqbal*, are valid under § 2072 because they "really regulate procedure" (*Sibbach*) since they concern complaint-writing. Scalia holds they're applicable because they purport to govern what a complaint must factually allege to state a claim upon which relief can be granted, which is what this conflict concerns. Because the FRCPs are valid and applicable, they govern.

**Under *Hanna*-Ginsburg, the FRCPs likely govern.** FRCP 12(b)(6) and 8(a)(2), as interpreted in *Iqbal*, are valid for same reasons as *Hanna*-Scalia. Though *Hanna*-Ginsburg tries to interpret FRCPs to render them inapplicable and respect states' policies/interests, doing so here is unlikely possible because the FRCPs are more restrictive than MA's standard. Without a plausible interpretation that deconflicts the FRCPs, they're likely applicable and thus govern.

**Under Harlan, MA law likely governs.** *Iqbal*'s reasoning partially depended on idea that pleading standards impacts primary conduct by burdening parties with discovery, distracting them from jobs and other day-to-day activities. If an issue impacts primary conduct, it's substantive and thus state law governs, so here MA law governs. You'll argue pleading standards don't impact primary conduct, are procedural, and thus the FRCPs govern since pleading concerns complaint writing.

# ASSIGNMENT 2022-assignment-03 — Assignment 3 (Graded) — Civil Procedure 1 & 2, 2022

Source: content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-03.md

## 2022-assignment-03 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3 (Graded)

Civil Procedure 1 & 2, 2022
Professor Jim Greiner

## Logistics and Rules

Posting date: Thursday, November 3, 2022
Due date/time: 9am on Monday, November 7, 2022
Word limit: 900 words
Time limit: 3 hours, 15 minutes

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.
2. Download the substantive assignment from the course website.
3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.
4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment.
5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response via the Exam 4 software. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.
6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.
7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.
8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.
9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.
10. This assignment is graded.
11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. Please put ONLY your 8-digit Harvard ID number on your paper.

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.

<!-- page: 2 -->

- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

Jean-Luc Picard, a farm-hand, was born in western Massachusetts and lived his life there. He worked on Kahn Farm in Worf County, Massachusetts, toiling daily caring for cows, chickens, and tribbles.

Borg Bank, Inc. was incorporated in and had its principal place of business in Boston, Massachusetts. Massachusetts has only one federal district court, the United States District Court for the District of Massachusetts. Massachusetts has a long-arm statute providing that its courts may exercise any type of jurisdiction so long as doing so is consistent with the limits of the U.S. Constitution.

Picard liked the countryside. In January of 2022, Picard signed a mortgage contract with Borg Bank so as to buy a one-bedroom house on a five-acre plot of land in the woods of Worf County, Massachusetts. The purchase price for the property was $100,000. Picard paid $20,000 (his life savings) in cash as down payment to the seller, and he borrowed $80,000 from Borg to pay the remainder. The contract between Picard and Borg specified that the Bank would retain legal title to the property, but that the Worf County Property Registry would show both the Bank's and Picard's ownership interests in it. The contract obligated Picard to pay $2000 per month in principal and interest until the loan was repaid. Picard provided Borg with his cell phone number and his only email address, picard@kahnfarm.com, an email that the Kahn Farm provided for him.

Picard made the February–May payments. In the middle of May, however, Kahn Farm went bankrupt, shut down all of its systems, fired all of its employees, and shuttered its doors. Picard was devastated. Lacking a paycheck, he missed the payment due to Borg Bank on June 1, 2022. On June 10, 2022, Borg Bank Officer Uhura called Picard on his cell phone to ask him about the missed payment. Picard told Uhura that he had lost his job, and that he wasn't sure what he would do next as there were very few openings for uneducated farm-hands in the area. Uhura wished Picard good luck but told him that the Borg Bank's policy was to move quickly to foreclose on delinquent loans.

<!-- page: 3 -->

On June 14, 2022, a dear friend named Spock told Picard some good news. First, a federal program provided assistance to farm-hands hit by hard times: cash payments of $3000/month were available for as long as 12 months. Second, another friend of Spock's named Scottie was going to leave his job at the nearby Enterprise Farm at the end of September. Spock knew the captain of Scotie's farm well. According to Spock, that captain would likely hire Picard for the job.

Spock took Picard to a local federal government office and helped him apply for the federal farm-hand program. The federal officer said that the paperwork typically took about six weeks to process, but that it was highly likely that Picard would qualify. Next, Spock took Picard to meet Captain Kirk of the Enterprise Farm. Kirk liked Picard, and said that he could have Scottie's job at the end of September.

Picard was jubilant. He thoroughly thanked Spock (who responded only by raising his eyebrow), and thought for a moment how to celebrate his good fortune. He decided to go on a four-week camping trip in a national park adjacent to his five acres. About ten miles away from his property was a cave where Picard liked to spend time during the summer studying the bats who lived there. He departed for the cave on June 27, 2022. He intended to keep his cell phone with him at all times, but found on the 27th while hiking to the cave that it lost reception about two miles away from his home, a fact for which he was grateful given that he hoped to spend the four weeks exclusively focusing on bats. He decided to turn it off until he returned. Every week, Picard hiked back to his house to pick up his mail.

Meanwhile, Borg Bank, always anxious to consume, had been busy. It had heard nothing from Picard about the mortgage, and after July 1 came and went with no news of either the June or the July payment, it sprang into action. Its loan officer, Q, ordered Uhura to initiate foreclosure proceedings under the Massachusetts Bank Protection Act (the "MBPA"), adopted in 2021. Relevant portions of the MBPA read as follows:

**Section 1: Statement of Purpose**

The Legislature finds that banks in this state and across the nation face an unprecedented liquidity crisis due to bad mortgage loans, and that this liquidity crisis poses a grave threat to the public interest due to the citizenry's great reliance on banks. Among the causes of this crisis are:

-- an inability of banks holding mortgages to foreclose and to evict delinquent homeowners promptly, which prevents banks from quickly seizing and selling the property and removing bad loans from their books . . . .

**Section 5: In rem and Summary Eviction Lawsuit, Notice**

Any bank holding a mortgage on a property located in Massachusetts, when the mortgagor misses a payment of the loan, may institute an in rem lawsuit seeking summary foreclosure of the property and eviction of the homeowner from that property. The bank shall file the complaint in a Housing Court of the relevant county. Upon filing of such a complaint, the sheriff of the county in which the property lies shall immediately

<!-- page: 4 -->

give notice of the action to the mortgagor/homeowner. Notice of the action may be given to the mortgagor in any of the following ways:

- (a) via hand delivery/personal service on the mortgagor;
- (b) via posting of notice on the relevant property;
- (c) via regular mail sent to the mortgagor; OR
- (d) via electronic notice, if the mortgagor has provided a cell phone number and email address to the bank on the mortgage application. Electronic notice under this subsection shall consist of both of the following:
    - (i) a telephone call to a cell phone number previously provided by the mortgagor to the bank; AND
    - (ii) an email attaching the complaint and summons in .pdf form to an email address previously provided by the mortgagor to the bank.

The sheriff of the county Housing Court is deemed to have complied with subsection (d)(i) of this section if they leave a message on a voicemail system associated with the cell number notifying the mortgagor of the in rem and Summary Eviction action, the Housing Court Case number, and the address of the Housing Court.

**Section 6: Temporary Eviction: Seizure During Pendency of Proceedings**

When a bank files a complaint in a Housing Court, it may also request from the clerk of the court an order entitling it to take immediate possession of the property so as to make repairs needed to prepare the property for sale and to prevent the homeowner from damaging the property. The bank shall file an affidavit describing the facts underlying the delinquency of the loan and verifying its intent to put the property up for sale forthwith. Upon filing of a suitable affidavit, the clerk of the court shall issue an order of possession to the bank, which may then execute such an order by evicting the mortgagor (with the assistance of the Housing Court Sheriff, if necessary).

**Section 8: Summary Eviction: Proceedings**

- (a) If, in ten days after the Housing Court sheriff has given notice to the mortgagor of proceedings under Section 5, the mortgagor fails to file a response to the in rem and Summary Eviction lawsuit, the mortgagor is deemed to have consented to foreclosure and eviction, and the bank may proceed forthwith to claim title to the property and sell it to satisfy the amount of the outstanding loan, returning any excess in the purchase price to the mortgagor. A Housing Court judgment to this effect shall be issued immediately and is final. No motions to reconsider or for relief from judgment shall be entertained, and no appeal will lie.
- (b) . . . [this section, omitted, provides procedures to be followed when the mortgagor does respond to the lawsuit]

At 9 am sharp on July 12, 2022, at Q's orders, Borg filed an in rem lawsuit under Section 5 of the MBPA. That same morning, Borg provided the Sheriff of Worf County with electronic copies of the Summons and the Complaint, along with Picard's email address and cell phone number. At 10:30 am, the Sheriff called Picard's cell phone number, only to be sent to voicemail. The Sheriff left a message telling Picard that an in rem and Summary Eviction action had been filed against him for non-payment of his mortgage, providing the number of the lawsuit

<!-- page: 5 -->

and the address of the Worf County Housing Court. The Sheriff also emailed a copy of the Summons and Complaint in .pdf form to picard@kahnfarm.com, but received a bounce-back email notice that no such email existed. The Sheriff responded by forwarding a copy of that bounce-back email notice to Q at Borg. Q deleted the Sheriff's forwarded email.

Also on the morning of July 12, 2022, Borg filed a request under Section 5 for an order requesting immediate possession of the five-acre property. Accompanying that filing was an affidavit from Q, sworn under penalty of perjury, (i) providing a copy of the mortgage documents along with receipts showing Picard's February–May payments, (ii) swearing under penalty of perjury that no payments had been made in June or July, and (iii) affirming Borg's intention to sell the property immediately to recover the cost of the loan. The Housing Court Clerk, finding Borg's papers proper, issued an Order of Temporary Eviction awarding immediate possession and the right to make repairs to Borg. On the afternoon of July 12, 2022, a Borg-hired locksmith, accompanied by Uhura and an officer from the Worf County Sheriff's Office, visited the property and changed the lock on the front (and only) door. They noticed that a tree branch had fallen, inflicting minor damage to the roof. On July 13, a Borg-hired contractor removed the fallen branch and fixed the roof.

On July 18, Picard visited the property. He checked the external mailbox, and finding nothing of interest there, he did not try to enter the house. He walked around the exterior to satisfy himself that nothing was amiss. Finding nothing wrong, he began his long hike back to the cave.

On the morning of July 25, 2022, the Worf County Housing Court declared Borg to be the owner of the five-acre property and ordered the Worf County Property Registry to so record in its records ("the judgment"). Q, having lined up a purchaser the previous week, sold the property the next day to Ms. Diana Troi for $110,000. Troi moved in on July 27, 2022.

Subsequently, Q decided that, after accounting for interest and fees, the $110,000 purchase price was exactly the amount that Picard still owed Borg on the loan. Borg therefore returned nothing to Picard, and Picard lost his $20,000 down payment.

When Picard returned home on the morning of July 29, 2022, he found that he had no place to live. He immediately hiked to your office. You are a lawyer in Worf County, and a friend of Picard's. Picard asked you the following questions, and requested a brief explanation for each of your answers:

1. Did the July 12, 2022, pre-judgment seizure of the property under MBPA Sections 5–6 violate
    a. Picard's rights related to jurisdictional doctrines?
    b. any other of Picard's constitutional rights?
2. Did the July 25, 2022 Worf County court judgment and subsequent sale of the property violate Picard's constitutional rights?
3. Suppose the answer to question #2 was "yes," that is, that the judgment transferring ownership of the property to Borg Bank, with the subsequent sale, was unlawful. Suppose further that Picard then decided he wanted to sue Worf County and Borg Bank simultaneously in Massachusetts federal district court. His theory against Worf County

<!-- page: 6 -->

would be violation of his constitutional rights under 42 U.S.C. § 1983, and he would seek a declaration that its procedures violated his constitutional rights and an injunction requiring the Worf County Property Registry to record him as the proper owner of the five-acre property. Against Borg, he would seek $20,000 in damages, under a state-law tort cause of action of theft, corresponding to his lost down payment. He had the following questions:
    a. If he tried to sue the two defendants in a single federal court lawsuit, could the federal court hear the case? (Note: You do not need to discuss whether either the 1983 action nor the theft action would succeed on the merits, just whether the federal court can hear them together in a single lawsuit.)[^1]
    b. Suppose that, due to a prior criminal record causing him to fear that a jury would be biased against him, Picard wanted to avoid a jury trial at all costs in his federal court lawsuit. Would he have to give up one of his causes of action to assure that neither defendant could demand a jury trial?

[^1]: In case you'd like further information: Because Worf County is a County, all of its actions are taken "under color of state law" for section 1983 purposes. Also, assume that there is no 11th Amendment or sovereign immunity defense to this suit. Finally, some of you may have heard or read that at one time, a plaintiff could not use section 1983 to sue a county (because a county was not a "person" within the meaning of that statute). That was the law at one time, but it is no longer the law. You aren't supposed to know any of this, and you don't need the information in this footnote. Just focus on whether a federal district court can hear the two causes of action together.

## 2022-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-03-bharmal-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## 1a.

No jurisdiction issues. This is a straightforward in rem case where ownership of seized property is central to the lawsuit's subject (unlike quasi in-rem, where D's property is seized to secure satisfaction of an unrelated personal claim, like seizing a corporation's shares in DE for a harm committed in OR, *Schaffer*).

Establishing PJ in in-rem suit, is *International Shoe*'s minimum contacts required? *Schaffer* says yes, but doesn't provide clear answer if holding is restricted to its facts or if *International Shoe* applies to in-rem and quasi in-rem. Regardless, still no jurisdiction issues. If minimum contacts required, no problem, because Picard is a MA domiciliary and there is a clear relationship between Picard's house, MA, and the lawsuits subject. If not, then in-rem suits only require that the property be within the court's geographic jurisdiction; this is also true.

## 1b.

Yes, can likely bring a suit claiming the County violated his right to a hearing. To determine, *Doehr* allows for *Mathews v. Eldridge* test to evaluate an existing procedural scheme against a new one (i.e. new scheme = Picard having a hearing before his house was seized). Here Picard's private interests will likely outweigh the average risk of erroneous determination and competing public interest.

**Private interests:** Picard's personal interests are massive. He literally no longer has a place to live; he couldn't even pack up his things. Property ownership is vaunted and needed for survival (*Doehr*) and he put his life savings into the down payment. A basic need is more urgent than the bank's private interest of getting cash; it can look at other liquidable assets while Picard has only his "cows, chickens, and tribbles." (*Doehr*).

**Risk of erroneous determination:** Low risk, at least not as high as *Doehr* where there was a question to the extent of the plaintiff's injuries resulting from assault and battery. Here there isn't any uncertainty on if Picard missed his payments or not; the evidence is straightforward and it's not a huge risk that a clerk issued the seizure (*Sniadach*, *Doehr*). That said, Borg isn't required to file a bond, so there isn't a market filter preventing banks from light-speeding shaky foreclosures to unexpecting and impoverished debtors (*Fuentes*).

**Public interest:** High interest because of the MA statute that explicitly passed law to allow banks to quickly foreclose. That said, the envisioned liquidity crisis is probably not a farmer's $80k loan, but rather big biz that abuse speculation with multimillion/billion dollar transactions. Also, a hearing before the foreclosure could be expedited to days or weeks; which on balance would not push the crisis further.

## 2.

Yes, county likely violated Picard's right to notice. *Mullane* requires reasonably calculated attempt at notice. That said, if the county learns it failed its first attempt at notice, it has a continuing obligation to try again. (*Flowers*) Here, the county sheriff knew the email bounced back and forwarded it to Q, who deleted it. This is particularly damning given that MBPA § 5(d)(ii) specifies that email must be sent. Picard's address is easily known to the sheriff/county so it would not be a "heroic effort" (*Dusenberry*) to mail the notice. While sheriff did leave a voicemail, *Greene* suggests even tacking a notice on the door is insufficient for eviction cases, so this is still insufficient (statutorily via MBPA § 5(d)(ii) and through case law, *Greene*).

<!-- page: 2 -->

## 3a.

Yes, Picard can likely bring both claims together.

**PJ:** All parties are Massachusetts residents so Massachusetts can exercise general PJ (G-IPJ) over parties. Federal court can piggyback off of state G-IPJ via 4(k)(1)(a).

**Venue:** § 1391(b)(1) venue is proper in district where all D's reside. Here, all D's reside in MA and there is only one federal court. Also property in question is in Massachusetts so venue also proper under § 1391(b)(2).

**SMJ:** For claim against Worf County, there is AU jurisdiction because COA based on violation of federal statute 42 U.S.C. § 1983. This is the anchoring claim that can allow state theft COA to be added under supplemental jurisdiction § 1367(a) because the two claims share the "same case or controversy" (that the seizure of Picard's property was unlawful). 1367(b) is not relevant because the anchoring claim is AU. State won't use its discretion under § 1367(c) to decline jurisdiction because the theft COA is not a novel issue of state law, doesn't dominate the anchor claim, and the anchor claim has not been dismissed. Additionally Picard can use a permissive joinder to sue both Borg and the County under Rule 20(a)(2) because both claims arise out of the same transaction/occurrence — the legality of the property seizure.

## 3b.

Picard likely needs to drop the theft charge against Borg. *Terry* counsels a 2-prong test to determine if there is a right to a jury trial. The first prong considers the historical context and analogizing as necessary. We don't have any historical analogs, so we move to step 2, which examines the remedies sought. Against Worf County, Picard is seeking an injunction which is an equitable remedy determined by a bench trial/judge; he's okay there. But the suit against Borg seeks compensatory damages which is remedy of law determined by a jury. Drop the theft charge to avoid a jury trial.

## 2022-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-03-gladstone-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## 1

### A

It is unclear whether *Shaffer* is limited to its facts or whether all in rem now follows *Shoe*. If the former, MA doesn't have an egregious scheme that holds property hostage to compel a GIPJ waiver. Instead, this scheme operates like garden variety in rem. If *Shaffer* means in rem now follows *Shoe*, we need a basis for IPJ over Picard or, possibly, minimum contacts among house, MA, and lawsuit's subject. Either way, we have them. MA has GIPJ over Picard. The house is in MA, and the suit is about who owns it.

### B

No. The alternative is a seizure after judgment for the bank. Use *Matthews* (*Matthews* applies to old property, *Doehr*).

#### Private Interest

Somewhat strong. Picard has life savings in home, as do many (but not all) homeowners. No litigation bond or extra damages will compensate Picard if he prevails. Becoming unhoused while litigating to get one's home back is serious hardship. And because this isn't loss of income, homeowners probably can't get new benefits to finance housing in meantime.

But this isn't welfare (*Goldberg*), people won't literally die. Borg owns most of equity in house (unlike *Doehr*, where accuser had no preexisting interest in house), so it has interest too. And Borg's interest is strong: there's legislative finding that inability to foreclose immediately is creating liquidity crisis. Borg arguing it has title to house, so its seizure proportionate (unlike *Doehr*), and it can use § 6 to argue it needs to seize now to repair and sell (thus assure liquidity of the market). Without the statutory finding, this might seem suspect, but courts should probably defer to that legislative judgment about empirical facts.

Viewed as a whole, these factors don't point decisively in one direction.

#### Risk of Erroneous Determination

Low. For another kind of case, it'd be huge. A bank just needs to bring right papers and file an affidavit, then it can seize. We have a clerk instead of a judge. And the clerk has no discretion and doesn't even get to assess merits: if paperwork proper, clerk *must* issue seizure order even if they have doubts. No litigation bond, bank doesn't owe *any* damages if it loses — there little incentive against filing borderline claims.

But this kind of eviction seems straightforward. If a mortgagor hasn't paid, they're delinquent. The bank must come with a mortgage contract showing it's entitled to foreclose if payments missed and then swear under penalty of perjury that the mortgagor missed payment. (True, it's

<!-- page: 2 -->

just allegation and not evidence (*Doehr*), but payment that *doesn't* get made doesn't create evidence.) This isn't like *Doehr*, where we'd benefit from hearing other side. All the evidence is documentary (*Matthews*), what can Picard even say to rebut it? This system bad in general, but in the unique context of document-driven mortgage, the risk of error seems low.

#### Public Interest

High. We have legislative finding that the nation facing dire threat because banks can't seize and sell quickly enough. To be fair, making Borg wait for judgment before it can seize wouldn't increase cost to government — pre-judgment seizure doesn't eliminate hearing, just changes order of steps.

Overall, factors mixed.

## 2

No. When email bounced back, the sheriff was obligated to use reasonable alternative (*Flowers*). He did by leaving voicemail. Picard didn't get voicemail, but he had answered this number before when Uhura called, suggesting calling this number is reasonably calculated to succeed (*Mullane*). Picard would argue sheriff knew house's address and could have posted notice or sent letter, either of which Picard would have seen when he visited house. But making sheriff exhaust every conceivable way to get Picard's attention is requiring more than ordinary efforts (*Dusenberry*). Sheriff picked one method that was reasonably calculated to succeed, that's enough (*Mullane*).

## 3

### A

Yes. Can sue both in DistMass.

**SMJ**

Borg suit is state-law COA against non-diverse defendant. No DivJur, needs SSMJ. Worf suit is anchoring claim. Federal COA, AUJ. The core of both suits is the allegation that the foreclosure, seizure, and eviction were improper. Borg and Worf were two parties acting together to produce all three. Suits share CNOF and are convenient triable unit, so § 1367(a) applies. Because the anchoring claim is AUJ, § 1367(b) is irrelevant. Under § 1367(c), there is no reason not to exercise SSMJ — no novel/complex issue of state law, state-law claim and federal claim are roughly equal parts of suit, no exceptional circumstances.

**IPJ**

Borg MA citizen, Worf in MA. Mass courts can exercise GIPJ over both, so DistMass can too (4(k)(1)(A)).

<!-- page: 3 -->

**Venue**

Proper in DistMass. Both Ds subject to IPJ in MA, so both citizens of MA, so venue proper in DistMass under § 1391(b)(1). All events giving rise to claim took place in DistMass so also proper under § 1391(b)(2).

### B

Picard would need to drop theft COA. We have no information on historical analogs, but we know that an injunction is an equity lawsuit, and damages mean an action at law with a jury trial right. Since Picard would seek compensatory damages for theft, he'd be seeking a legal remedy with > $20 AIC, Borg would have right to jury trial.

## 2022-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-03-ramoutar-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## #1(a)

**IPJ:** Probably not. This is an *in rem* proceeding, the property is the subject of the dispute, and it is located within Massachusetts. In addition, the property owner (Picard) is a MA domiciliary, so there is GIPJ. So even if a literal reading *Schaeffer* applies to *in rem* proceedings, this is likely sufficient contacts to allow jurisdiction. If *Schaeffer* is not that broad, then this *in rem* proceeding is standard.

## #1(b)

There's probably a PDP violation.

Picard could argue that he received insufficient notice. *Mullane*.

a. Email/phone could be insufficient because messages are frequently missed.
b. The email bounced back to the sender. He could argue that more steps should've been taken under *Jones*. The Sheriff did email the Bank, but that is probably insufficient even applying *Dusenbery v. United States* because the Bank is the adverse party.
c. Picard could argue that all *in rem* proceedings on real property should require a notice delivered to the property. The address is readily available. *Mennonite / Tulsa*.

Additionally, he could argue that he had insufficient opportunity to be heard. *Sniadach*.

Factors in Picard's favor include:

a. Picard likely has a property interest in his home.
b. Adverse action (seizure of possession) was taken immediately. *Roller v. Holly*.
c. It likely was not urgent for the property to be seized because homeowners don't usually seek to destroy the property in which they live.
d. A clerk, not a judge approves the pre-judgment seizure.
e. There is no bond requirement and no opportunity to post a counter-bond.
f. Burden of proof: just an affidavit from one party.

Factors in Borg's/County's Favor:

a. The bank owns the title to the home, so it has its own property interest.
b. They needed to repair the home.
c. State has expressed interest in assuring real estate liquidity.
d. Reasonable to believe someone might trash property if they know they're getting evicted.
e. Burden of proof: Bank can easily demonstrate on paper that money hasn't been paid.

## #2

Picard could argue that there was another PDP violation.

**Notice:** Almost all analysis from Q1 can be applied here.

**Opportunity to be heard (*Mathews v. Eldridge*):**

1. Private interests involved:

<!-- page: 2 -->

a. Picard has an interest in having a home, and not being deprived on the money he's invested in it. (Strong)
b. Bank has an interest in returning their investment in a profitable manner, and ensuring that the home is not destroyed. (Weaker)

2. The risk of erroneous deprivation of these interests:
    a. There is no opportunity for a counter-bond/other method for Picard to stop the sale of the home.
    b. Not a high burden of proof, however, the evidence is quite straightforward (paperwork, not subjective witness testimony).
    c. However, perhaps at a more extensive hearing, Picard could introduce evidence regarding his future earnings, and therefore, wouldn't have lost his home if another solution was available.
    d. The apportionment of the sale by the bank has a high risk of erroneous deprivation because its conducted by an self-interested party.

3. Government/Public's interest:
    a. Government has expressed an interest in protecting banks.
    b. Government likely would prefer quick turnaround in litigation.

## #3a

**IPJ:** Probably not an issue. FRCP 4(k)(1)(A) says use state IPJ law. Massachusetts has general jurisdiction over all parties because all parties are citizens of the state. *International Shoe*. Picard was born and raised in Massachusetts. Borg Bank has its PPoB in Boston, MA. *Hertz*. Worf County is located in Massachusetts.

**SMJ:** There is no DJ because all three parties are citizens of Massachusetts. *Strawbridge v. Curtiss*.

Because 1983 is a federal COA, Picard can invoke § 1331 for SMJ against Worf County. *Mottley*. But theft, against Borg, is a state COA.

So, for Borg, he will likely need to rely on § 1367 and FRCP 20. For FRCP 20, we need the same T or O and a common issue. The T&O is the house seizure and sale, and the common issue is whether the procedures the bank and county used are constitutional. Because we have the same T&O, we have the same case or controversy under § 1367(a). § 1367(b) does not apply because the anchoring 1983 claim is AUJ.

A district court likely would not decline to exercise jurisdiction under § 1367(c)/*Gibbs*, because there probably is not a complex issue of state law (just tort claim), and damages against Borg does not substantially dominate over the constitutional questions towards the county.

**Venue:** Likely not an issue because all parties are citizens of Massachusetts, and Massachusetts only has one district court. Additionally, all of the facts described occurred in Massachusetts.

## #3b

<!-- page: 3 -->

Picard would potentially need to give up his claim against the Bank to avoid a jury trial. The theft claim seeks damages, which almost always triggers a jury trial right. That would leave the injunction request, which is always equity, and thus no jury trial right.

## 2022-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2022-assignment-03-washington-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## 1a.

No. This in-rem suit fine under any jurisdictional interpretation. A literal reading of *Schaffer* requires minimum contacts between defendant, property, and subject matter of the litigation even when suit in-rem. Have that here — Picard domiciled + owns property in MA and the litigation is about failure to make mortgage payments on that property. *Shoe*. Under a narrower reading of *Schaffer* — that the court was offended by DE trying to subject objecting, out-of-state defendant to a limitless judgment even if it exceeded property value in state — leaves traditional IR jurisdiction undisturbed, and MA can exercise jurisdiction because the property in MA.

## 1b.

Likely yes. Picard can challenge prejudgment seizure for lack of opportunity to be heard. To evaluate scheme, consider the *Matthews* factors, which apply to old property. *Doehr*.

### Private Interest

Picard's interest is enormous — this is Picard's only place to live, has life savings in house, and has no income to sure housing until end of September. This may be worst case scenario, and others being foreclosed may have other options (family, friends, savings, steady income to get cheaper housing). It's not exactly like welfare (*Matthews*), but housing still core to survival. Moreover, when the property is seized, Picard can't sell, so the effective market value goes to zero. *Doehr*. But unlike *Doehr*, the bank has pre-existing property interest. They retain legal title and Picard only has $28k in equity ($20k down + 4 x $2k monthly payment), leaving bank with $72k.

### Risk of Erroneous Decision

The alternative is to wait for judgment to seize and evict (which may or may not include an in-person hearing, MBPA § 8(b)). The bank filed a sworn affidavit describing delinquency and intent to sell property, and this is not the kind of case that requires experts and testimony (it's really just, did you pay or not?). *Doehr*. But if the affidavit is defective or not persuasive, there are few safeguards to prevent erroneous transfer of possession because a clerk makes the determination, not a judge. The bank also doesn't have to put up a bond, so there is nothing deterring banks from quickly seeking unwarranted foreclosures against vulnerable, unsophisticated mortgagors.

### Public Interest

Legislature found that inability to quickly foreclose jams up housing market liquidity, which poses a "grave threat to the public interest." A court should maybe defer to that, but not clear why waiting a small amount of time for the judgment will significantly jam up liquidity more. All bank cannot do in the interim is make repairs and prepare the home for sale. The legislature also wanted to prevent homeowner from damaging home, but that is dubious. Why would Picard (or any homeowner) damage the home? The only interest here is impairing ability to recover principal in case of foreclosure, so seems unlikely that such swift eviction necessary for that reason. On balance, OtbH challenge likely succeeds.

## 2.

Likely yes. Picard can challenge the judgment and subsequent sale for lack of notice. Notice must be reasonably calculated and the agent must act as if they truly desire to deliver notice, though actual notice is not the standard. *Mullane*. Sheriff complied with MBPA § 5(d) by calling and leaving voicemail as well

<!-- page: 2 -->

as emailing summons and complaint in PDF form to Picard's email address. While extraordinary efforts to provide notice are not required, you cannot simply stop after the first step. *Flowers*. When the Sheriff got the bounceback email and forwarded it to Q, he deleted it. The sheriff did not request alternative contact information nor did Q provide it. This is mitigated by successfully delivered voicemail (one would expect a normal person to check their voicemail regularly, and Uhura had already successfully communicated using that number). But, Q and the Sheriff had Picard's address, so they easily could have delivered notice by mail or by posting on a door/tree/post on the property. In total, their actions evince a desire *not* to deliver actual notice, so challenge likely succeeds.

## 3a.

Yes. PJ fine in both actions — all defendants MA citizens. 4k1a. Venue fine because MA has only one district and all defendants MA citizens, and all conduct took place in MA. § 1391(b)(1)/(2). 1983 action federal cause of action, so AUJ. Theft action state tort claim with no embedded federal issue (no AUJ) and all parties MA citizens (so no DJ). We need SSMJ. Both suits arise under the same "case or controversy," § 1367(a), because both are related to Picard's failure to pay mortgage and subsequent loss of his property. § 1367(b) does not apply because the anchoring claim is AUJ. The § 1367(c) factors don't support discretionary denial of supplemental jurisdiction. No novel theory of state law implicated, the federal claim dominant, the federal claim has not been dismissed, and nothing else extraordinary is going on. Actions survive.

## 3b.

Yes. The 7A preserves right to jury trial in suits at law. 2 part test: is there a founding-era analog cause of action at law? Is the remedy requested one traditionally granted at law or equity? The second prong is more important. *Chauffeurs*. Not sure about founding-era analogs, so evaluate requested remedy. The remedy requested in 1983 suit is declaratory and injunctive relief, equitable remedies. Theft COA asking for damages, legal remedy. Picard should drop theft COA to ensure no right to jury trial.

# ASSIGNMENT 2023-assignment-01 — Assignment 1 (ungraded) — Civil Procedure 2 & 6, 2023

Source: content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-01.md

## 2023-assignment-01 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 1 (ungraded)

**Civil Procedure 2 & 6, 2023**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** Thursday, September 21, 12pm
**Due date/time:** 8am on Monday, September 25
**Word limit:** 900 words
**Time limit:** 3 hours and 15 minutes

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.

2. Download the substantive assignment from Exam 4.

3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.

4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment.

5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response via the Exam 4 software. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.

6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.

7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.

8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.

9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

10. This assignment is ungraded.

11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. The Registrar's Office has sent you information about what to use for an anonymous ID. Please follow those instructions.

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.

<!-- page: 2 -->

- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

***********************

<!-- page: 3 -->

## Substantive Assignment

All states relevant to this assignment have long-arm statutes providing that their state courts may exercise jurisdiction to the extent permissible under the U.S. Constitution.

All states relevant to this assignment have a tort cause of action for "conversion," also known as theft. A defendant commits the tort of conversion if they exercise wrongful dominion or control of the plaintiff's property, assets, or money to the detriment of the rights of the actual owner. "Property" in this context encompasses digital assets. The last event necessary for the completion of this tort is the removal of the item converted/stolen from the control of the plaintiff, so the tort occurs where the item was located at the time of the theft.

All states relevant to this assignment have a tort cause of action for negligence. The elements of this cause of action are that the defendant owes a duty of care to the plaintiff (everyone owes each other a duty to exercise ordinary care), that the defendant breached this duty by failing to act with ordinary care under the circumstances, and that the breach caused quantifiable harm to the plaintiff. In all states relevant to this assignment, a plaintiff can sue a defendant for negligence if the defendant failed to exercise ordinary care in guarding an item (including a digital item) that the plaintiff entrusted to the defendant's keeping, where such failure caused the loss (due to theft or otherwise) of that item. In such cases, the last event necessary for the completion of the tort is the theft/conversion of the item from the possession/control from the defendant such that the plaintiff no longer has access to the item. In other words, in this context, the tort occurs where the item was located at the time of the theft.

In South Carolina, South Dakota, and Texas, a defendant may assert the defense of contributory negligence. All other states relevant to this assignment use a comparative negligence system.[^1]

Texas, Oklahoma, and South Dakota follow the Second Restatement for horizontal choice of law. Massachusetts, South Carolina, and Delaware follow the First Restatement for horizontal choice of law.

Sylvania, Inc. was incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Oklahoma City, OK. Its programmers all work in a massive facility in Oklahoma City, OK. The company specialized in selling cheap and un-hackable data storage. Sylvania was the first company to use quantum computing to encrypt its information on its servers, so anyone hacking into its systems except by way of a user account would obtain only gobbledygook. Users of Sylvania's data storage could access their data from anywhere in the world. They did so through a username and a password. Unlike most data storage companies, Sylvania did not require users to choose passwords of any particular length, nor did passwords have to include a particular mix of characters (e.g., numbers, letters, and special characters).

<!-- page: 4 -->

Also unlike most data storage companies, Sylvania allowed, but did not require, users to opt to enable two-factor authentication ("2FA") to log into their data storage accounts. In 2FA, a user must provide a username and a password to initiate the login process; the system then sends a code to the user's cell phone, which the user enters into the system to complete the login process. Thus, with 2FA, a hacker attempting to gain access to a user's account would require both knowledge of the user's username and password as well as possession of the user's cell phone (or some other method of acquiring and entering the system-generated code).

Sylvania's storage methods were as follows. When a user uploaded data to one of its accounts, Sylvania used patented technology to split the information into two pieces, neither of which could be used without the other. It stored the two pieces separately on its servers located in Boston, MA and in Columbia, SC.

Sylvania did no advertising. It depended entirely on word of mouth and recommendations from its users. Users created accounts on its website, sylvanianotfreedonia.com. Users paid in advance via credit card or cryptocurrency through an interface on sylvanianotfreedonia.com. Sylvania was especially popular among artists who created NFTs and other forms of digital crafts, and its customer base spanned the globe with no particular concentration in one country, but was overwhelmingly European and Asian. It had few if any US-based customers at the time period relevant to this assignment.

Groucho, who grew up and lived all his life in San Antonio, TX, was an art collector. Although he dabbled in artworks of all kinds, his specialty was depictions of waterfowl. He owned statues, photographs, paintings, and lithographs of egrets, herons, geese, and swans. In late 2019, while visiting a friend in Pierre, South Dakota, Groucho experienced a virtual reality work of art entitled "Duck in Soup." When run on an advanced virtual reality headset, "Duck in Soup" depicted in an astonishingly realistic way the point of view of a duck while the animal was swimming in a large pot of water filled with vegetables. The temperature of the water slowly increased during the 90-minute simulation until it reached boiling, at which point the experience ended. The author of the work, an artistic coder named Harposy, was one of the world's only users of a programming language called "Malbolge." Programming in Malbolge is so difficult that anything written in it is almost impossible to hack. In addition, the code for "Duck in Soup" was programmed to destroy itself when copied, leaving only the copy in existence. Thus, absent an unimaginably advanced hacking program, which had to be written in Malbolge, only one copy of "Duck in Soup" could exist at a time.

Entranced, Groucho bought "Duck in Soup" from Harposy for $300,000.00. Harposy provided the computer code to Groucho on an external hard drive. After making the purchase, Groucho returned to his hotel in Pierre. Groucho had heard of Sylvania through a friend while traveling in Europe. Using his laptop computer from his hotel room, he opened a Sylvania account (using a credit card to pay) on sylvanianotfreedonia.com and uploaded the code for "Duck in Soup" onto his Sylvania account (at that moment, the copy of the "Duck in Soup" on the hard drive self-destructed). He chose as his username "Groucho", and his password was also "Groucho". He then returned home to San Antonio. After purchasing his first-ever advanced virtual reality headset, he experienced "Duck in Soup" several times, and continued to be more enthusiastic.

<!-- page: 5 -->

In early 2020, Groucho visited a friend named Chico at the latter's house in San Antonio, TX. Also visiting Chico was a recent acquaintance named Zeppo, a Delaware domiciliary. Zeppo was on a driving trip around the country, and in particular he was headed to San Diego, CA by car, where he planned on spending a couple of weeks with a friend named Firefly who lived on the coastline there. Zeppo had stopped at Chico's house to spend the night. All Groucho could talk about that evening was his recent acquisition of "Duck in Soup." To mollify his friend, Chico got out his VR headset. Using Chico's computer, Groucho logged into his Sylvania account and accessed "Duck in Soup." Chico did not prefer the production, but after finishing his first experience, Zeppo asked for a second and a third viewing, and Groucho obliged. The next morning, Zeppo continued on his way to San Diego, and Groucho returned home to San Antonio.

Two weeks later, Groucho logged into his Sylvania account, ready to enjoy "Duck in Soup" again. He found, however, that the work of art no longer resided in his account. He went immediately to sylvanianotfreedonia.com and, using the online chat function, demanded to know what had happened. The Sylvania technician informed Groucho that four days earlier, his account had been accessed by a computer in the coastal area of San Diego (Sylvania could not further localize the account) via Groucho's username and password, and that the data for "Duck in Soup" had been transferred out of Sylvania's systems onto a hard drive in that location. The Sylvania technician regretted any inconvenience.

You are a litigator practicing in San Antonio. In the early spring of 2020, an enraged Groucho walked into your office. He wanted to sue both Sylvania and Zeppo. He was convinced that Zeppo stole "Duck in Soup" and that Sylvania's incompetent security protocols, particularly its allowing users to use short passwords and its not requiring users to enable 2FA, enabled the theft. He asked you to advise him on the following scenario.

---

**Question:** Groucho asked you to suppose that he sued Zeppo for theft and Sylvania for negligence in a Texas federal court, serving process promptly and legally. Against Zeppo, he demanded $250,000. Against Sylvania, he demanded $150,000. You inform Groucho that if he did so, the defendants were likely to move to dismiss. Groucho asked you:

**A)** on what grounds might the defendants (either together or separately) move to dismiss/remand, and how would the federal court rule?

**B)** whether Sylvania will be able to assert the defense of contributory negligence, or will the court instead use a comparative fault analysis. Assume (as we will later in class learn is true) that the Texas federal court will use Texas choice of law rules.

**Explain your answer. (Note: do not discuss venue or transfer in your response. Also, do not discuss the merits of the negligence or theft causes of action.)**

---

[^1]: As a reminder of our discussion in class:
- **Contributory negligence:** If the defendant persuades the trier of fact (the judge, or the jury) that the plaintiff was in any way at fault for the plaintiff's injury, no matter how small the plaintiff's contribution to the loss, the defendant is relieved of all liability. So, for example, if the plaintiff was 10% responsible for a $1000 loss, and the defendant 90% responsible, then the defendant owes the plaintiff $0.00.
- **Comparative negligence:** If the defendant persuades the trier of fact (the judge, or the jury) that the plaintiff was in any way at fault for the plaintiff's injury, the trier of fact apportions responsibility for the loss according to the fraction of the parties' fault. So, for example, if the plaintiff was 10% responsible for a $1000 loss, and the defendant 90% responsible, then the defendant owes the plaintiff $900.00.

## 2023-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-01-chavier-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 1 Model Answer

## 1. SMJ

There is SMJ through § 1332: Groucho is a citizen of TX; Zeppo is a citizen of DE, and Sylvania is a citizen of DE and OK, so there is complete diversity. *Strawbridge*. Groucho's claim against Zeppo is AIC sufficient (250k > 75k). So is Groucho's claim against Sylvania (150k > 75k).

## 2. PJ

The defendants might move to dismiss for lack of IPJ. Neither is from TX, and neither does more than 50% of its "business" in TX, so no G-IPJ. Given 4(k)(1)(A) and the Texas long-arm to constitutional limits, we look for minimal contacts to see if we can establish S-IPJ.

**Zeppo:** There are minimal contacts linking defendant, subject matter of litigation, and forum. *Shoe*. Zeppo met Groucho, experienced "DIS," and likely stole the password from TX. It would be fair and reasonable because Zeppo has already come to TX once so can come again, Groucho's convenience is high in TX, TX has an interest in protecting its citizens, and some evidence is there (Chico and his computer).

For **Sylvania**, it has few TX customers other than Groucho, it never "purposefully availed" itself to TX (no ads, word-of-mouth business, and Groucho even signed up in SD) and "stream of commerce" is insufficient. *Hanson*. Regarding fair & reasonable, TX has an interest in regulating allegedly wrongful conduct connected to its borders, but negligence may not have happened there (see below); TX is convenient for Groucho but inconvenient for Sylvania; evidence (Chico's testimony) is in TX.

## 3. HCOL

Whether contributory negligence is available requires an HCOL analysis. The question states that the TX federal court will apply TX choice of law rules. Whether the defense of contributory negligence applies here is a substantive issue for HCOL. Texas is a 2R state, so there is a presumption in favor of *lex loci deliciti*, but § 6 factors can overcome that presumption. *Lex loci deliciti* is complicated. Per the prompt, the negligence tort was complete at the location of "Duck" when it was stolen. Where was that? The artwork is incorporeal, and it does not consist solely of its code; the code is worthless without an interface to play it, and as this case demonstrates, code can exist in a number of places. To locate incorporeal things, we consider policy arguments.

- **South Dakota:** this is where the account-holder opened the Sylvania account (and here, the place where the account-holder relinquished total control of the item), but as this fact pattern demonstrates, this location could be gratuitous, depending on the vagaries of travel plans, and thus is both indefinite and accidental.
- Perhaps an analogy to *Harris v. Balk* suggests that an account follows the account-holder's current location (**Texas/California**). This is definite and ascertainable and might

<!-- page: 2 -->

comply with the holder's expectations. But it might not comply with company (Sylvania) expectations and means the location will be transient/uncertain.

- **South Carolina or Massachusetts:** "DIS" cannot usefully be in two places at once (or the judicial system would lack the certainty it wants regarding where the item is) and cannot be fractioned. Therefore, Boston, MA, and Columbia, SC (where Sylvania keeps records) makes little sense here.
- **Delaware or Oklahoma** are ascertainable and definite, and they have connections to the events. OK is likely stronger because Sylvania's principal place of business is a reasonable proxy for its system of records. Either choice might be fair to Groucho because he can be deemed to have had knowledge of these locations when he chose Sylvania as his storage. An analogy to *Harris v. Balk* may suggest the storage is a sort of debt owed to the account holder.

§ 6 factors likely won't persuade a court to apply TX law, which would allow for the contributory negligence defense. One can argue the center of gravity is TX, but Sylvania's negligence likely did not occur there (more likely that it occurred in OK, where the board of directors made decisions, or CA, where Zeppo opened Groucho's account). TX does not have a huge interest over conduct, or lack of (negligence), that happened elsewhere. Moreover, this rule is loss-allocating rather than conduct-regulating, so they may not have a strong interest in using their law. It also likely does not comply with the parties' expectations, since TX location was fortuitous to Sylvania.

If *lex loci deliciti* does not yield OK, § 6 factors might persuade a TX court to use OK law (no contributory negligence defense). The alleged negligence likely occurred there, where Sylvania's board of directors likely took the decision not to require 2FA. OK likely has an interest in how businesses conduct themselves within its borders, evidence about the relevant decisions is there so it may be in the interest of the judicial system, and it complies with the parties' expectations (see above).

**Word Count:** 791.

## 2023-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-01-kaur-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 1 Model Answer

## A) Grounds to Dismiss/Remand

The defendants could attempt to dismiss under 12(b)(1) for lack of subject matter jurisdiction. However, there is 1332 diversity jurisdiction. Groucho is domiciled in Texas (*Mas*). Sylvania is domiciled in Oklahoma (PPOB, *Hertz*) and Delaware (incorporated). Zeppo is domiciled in Delaware. There is therefore complete diversity under *Strawbridge*. The AIC requirements are met both in the claim against Zeppo (250k > 75k) and also against Sylvania (150k > 75k) so a 12(b)(1) motion to dismiss will not work.

The defendants could also attempt to dismiss under 12(b)(2) for lack of IPJ. 4(k)(1)(a) means that district court can piggyback off IPJ of state court, and Texas has a long-arm statute that goes to constitutional limits.

**Sylvania:** The minimal contacts for Sylvania (*Int'l Shoe*) are likely insufficient. While Sylvania clearly accepts TX domiciliaries like Groucho as customers, this might not rise to the level of purposeful availment since Sylvania does not target TX customers specifically, and may not even have known that Groucho was a TX domiciliary (making this more similar to *Hanson* than *McGee*). Groucho showed Zeppo DIS on Sylvania's website in Texas, but this seems to be too minimal. F&R? Contacts are very minimal, but there is some important evidence there, e.g. Chico witnessing Zeppo's enthralment with DIS. Unlikely that TX law applies to issues as between Groucho and Sylvania, so likely not F&R (R2 states allow for separation of issues). Court could dismiss the claim against Sylvania for lack of IPJ.

**Zeppo:** Minimal contacts for Zeppo are likely sufficient. TX is the center of gravity for the relationship between Groucho and Zeppo: this was where they interacted and the unfortunate chain started, where Zeppo first encountered DIS, and if Zeppo stole the password, he likely did so in TX. Engaging in this type of illegal conduct should have put Zeppo on notice for being brought to court in TX. F&R: TX might be convenient for Zeppo as mobile and been there before, but only stayed there overnight and now in CA; Chico's testimony is in TX and TX might have an interest in regulating illegal activity (e.g. theft of passwords) connected to its borders. Court will likely not dismiss claims against Zeppo.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## B) Contributory Negligence / Comparative Fault
