If a federal court sitting in diversity, apply the forum state's HCoL rules (*Klaxon*). TX = 2R state, TX applies *lex loci delicti* unless § 6 factors strongly suggest something different.

Q becomes: Where was DIS located at the time of the theft?

It is unclear where a digital item is "located," if it existed in servers split between MA and SC. However, since basing location of digital theft on server location could implicate as many states as there are servers, this is probably an unwise policy choice.

If the item follows the owner as in *Harris v. Balk*, then it was located in Texas at the time of theft. And if it follows the location of theft, then it is located in CA. Either one of these categorizations will enable ascertainability, but it would also mean that the property is always moving.

If the item were located in South Dakota, where Groucho made his account and where the item was first taken out of his exclusive control, this would raise similar problems for a court deciding on the HCOL question as the location could be random, unconnected either to the owner of the item or the storer of it.

It might be easiest to say it is located in Oklahoma or Delaware, if we think of DIS as information that Sylvania "owes" to Groucho as in *Balk* and which would therefore follow Sylvania's location. Oklahoma might make most sense as PPOB, given that policy decisions regarding the security policies of the company were likely made there. Either categorization will be ascertainable and efficient.

If the Court does apply Delaware or Oklahoma law, then Sylvania will not be able to make its contributory negligence claims. However, if Court decides that digital items follow their owners, then this would be Texas, which has a contributory negligence system.

If the place of injury is Oklahoma, § 6 factors might change the forum here to Texas. Texas is the center of gravity of the parties' relationship (although less so vis-a-vis Sylvania). Conduct linked to the injury happened in TX (Groucho showed Zeppo DIS, where Groucho and Zeppo met, and Zeppo likely stole the password here). Texas does have a strong interest in regulating conduct with strong links to its borders, and Texas courts will be good at applying Texas law. The other two parties have no connections with Oklahoma. If the Court agrees to use TX law, then Sylvania will be able to assert contributory negligence as a complete defense.

## 2023-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-01-macalpine-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 1 Model Answer

## A) Grounds to Dismiss/Remand

Defendants may move to dismiss/remand for lack of SMJ, which will fail.

There is diversity jurisdiction (*Strawbridge*) — Groucho is domiciled in TX, Zeppo is domiciled in DE, Sylvania has incorporation in DE and PPoB in OK. Both claims are AIC sufficient (the claim against Sylvania is $150,000; the claim against Zeppo is $250,000. Both claims exceed $75,000).

### IPJ

Defendants will move to dismiss for lack of PJ. That will fail against Zeppo, but might succeed against Sylvania.

We have a long arm to constitutional limits; under FRCP 4(k)(1)(a) the TX federal court can "piggy-back" off of it.

There is no general in personam jurisdiction (IPJ) since neither defendant is "at home." Neither is from TX, neither does >50% of business in TX.

*Shoe* factors/minimal contacts analysis:

**Zeppo:** There would likely be specific IPJ because Zeppo purposefully reached into TX (he drove into the state, first saw DiS there, visited a friend, and as a result later stole something from someone in TX). These key events relate Zeppo, Texas, and the subject matter of the litigation.

**Sylvania:** Sylvania does not solicit business in TX. Sylvania "reached into" the state to have Groucho as a customer, but didn't advertise to/target TX and has few US-based customers. *McGee* says that Sylvania purposefully availed itself of TX customers, but it wasn't all that much as there were few customers. Further, Groucho created the account while he was outside Texas and then later returned to TX (possibly like *Hanson*), and he paid with a credit card, so Sylvania may not have even known that he was a Texas resident.

Fair and reasonable factors: Texas has an interest in regulating conduct within its borders and in protecting Groucho from harm, Texas is convenient for Groucho, not too inconvenient for Zeppo (he went to the state before), less convenient for Sylvania (who has one customer there but no real contacts otherwise), witnesses/evidence may be in TX (Chico testimony, Groucho's computer), but the internet evidence is not clearly located anywhere, Texas law could possibly apply (see below) and TX best at applying TX law.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## B) HCOL

The question says that the Texas federal court will use Texas HCoL rules. Texas uses 2R — it will favor *lex loci delicti* but will also consider section 6 factors. The negligence tort occurred where DiS was stolen — the theft occurred on the internet. Where was the "item located at the time of the theft?" Policy should play into this discussion.

**MA/SC:** One could argue that Duck in Soup (DiS) was located on Sylvania's servers in MA/SC when it was stolen. But there is no way to determine which state held the most data and today, data gets stored all over the place, and often gets moved.

**CA:** We could also say that DiS was "stolen" when it allegedly entered Zeppo's possession/hard drive in CA, but even so, there's little argument that it would make sense that DiS was in California before or when it was stolen, which is what matters.

**DE/OK:** Sylvania considers itself to be in these states, and negligence causes of action could be directed at company PPoB or incorporation locations. The decisions that gave rise to the negligence were made in Oklahoma, and thus the evidence about those decisions is there. Groucho knew, or could have known, that he was doing business with a DE/OK company, so one could argue that either would meet the parties' expectations.

**TX:** This is the last place Groucho viewed and owned DiS, so a court may find that the "blood spilled" here with respect to the theft. This would comport with Groucho's conception, but perhaps not the conception of Sylvania, which may not have known where Groucho was. TX may also want to apply TX law for judicial efficiency and the case is largely grounded in TX.

If a court determines DiS was located in MA/SC/CA, it may choose one of them as the location of a substantial portion of the events for HCoL purposes. This seems unlikely, as Texas is a clearer location for the theft and Oklahoma for the negligence. If the court uses TX law, contributory negligence is available. However, for the negligence suit, the court may use OK law, because that is where the decisions on security were likely made. Thus, comparative negligence would be available.

## 2023-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-01-royce-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 1 Model Answer

## A) SMJ

Both defendants would move to dismiss from fed court for lack of SMJ. Groucho is domiciled in TX, Zeppo in DE, and Sylvania in DE (incorporation) or OK (PPoB); suits are for 150K or 250K, both >75K. TX federal court likely has SMJ due to diversity jurisdiction.

### IPJ

Both defendants would move to dismiss for lack of IPJ. TX has a long-arm statute to the limits of the Constitution, so TX fed court has same under 4(k)(1)(A).

Zeppo traveled to TX to visit Chico, setting off the events that culminated in the alleged theft; first experienced DiS in TX; and likely discovered Groucho's password there. The cause of action against Zeppo thus arose out of an act in TX, so TX jurisdiction likely to be fair/reasonable (*McGee*). Because these are key events in the subject matter of the litigation, TX likely has specific IPJ over Zeppo.

Like the *Luban* defendant, who didn't target AL but merely knew her rival lived there, Sylvania will argue that it didn't solicit ads in or otherwise target TX — Groucho wasn't even in TX when he signed up for the product (in SD) or heard about it from his European friend, so Sylvania probably didn't even know he lived there. Like the *Hanson* trust, which wasn't subject to Florida jurisdiction because it only continued a relationship when its beneficiary moved, Sylvania's only connection to TX is that a customer who bought the product somewhere else returned to the state. Even then, Sylvania has a stronger objection to TX jurisdiction, because it may not have received any money from Groucho while in the state (unclear if it's a subscription service or a one-time fee). TX asserting jurisdiction may offend fair play — Sylvania never solicited any business there and may not know any of its customers were there, because only Groucho's unilateral action brought its products to TX.

TX likely lacks personal jurisdiction over Sylvania.

## B) Possible Defenses

Applying TX HCoL rules, the location of the tort controls unless there are overriding factors (Second RS). So we have to locate the negligence, meaning locating DIS — difficult, as DiS is incorporeal.

- Sylvania is domiciled in DE/OK. DE has an interest in regulating its corporations, especially given its unique approach to corporate regulation, but unlikely that any decisions made there. OTOH, because programmers do work in OK, plausible claim that Sylvania's corporate decisions are there.
- Data storage centers in SC/MA, but nothing else links Sylvania to these states. Because Sylvania and other internet-based corporations could choose any warehouse to store its servers, little reason to locate it here.
- First Sylvania control over DiS in SD, where Groucho bought DiS and uploaded it to Sylvania's server. Might make sense to locate this one action in SD, but policy repercussions probably uncontrollable: Sylvania could simultaneously be located anywhere a customer accessed it, implicating the same concerns as for IPJ (no reason Sylvania would expect to be in SD), and a bigger company (Amazon) could conceivably be "located" in every state at once.

<!-- page: 2 -->

- CA: Last Sylvania control over DiS. Even fewer reasons to locate negligence here, as neither Sylvania nor Groucho had reason to believe DiS or Sylvania systems generally would ever be accessed in CA.
- TX: Groucho accessed DiS multiple times, discovered his loss, and got any benefit from DiS in TX. Sylvania could easily see DiS being downloaded in TX, so constructive notice that software accessed there.

After locating the tort, most likely in TX or OK, TX will consider the section 6 factors (Second RS) to determine which state's law applies.

*Location of evidence:* Unlikely in TX, as no Sylvania-directed activity there. Maybe some evidence in the states where the data was stored, but likelier that the programmers would be best-positioned to testify about their data practices, pointing towards OK.

*Convenience to parties:* Despite being right next to OK, Groucho will claim TX is the most convenient to him. Sylvania could argue OK/DE most convenient, but their own business structure — multistate data storage, international customers — indicate location isn't too much of a problem for them. Points towards TX.

*Protecting own citizens:* Like NY in *Babcock*, TX will want to protect its citizen Groucho from anachronistic interpretations of the law, and any interpretation of tort law that tries to assign physical location to internet "objects" is anachronistic, mitigating against CA or OK law. DE and OK have an interest in regulating their own corporations, but unlike in *Neumeier*, no clear evidence that the injury occurred there rather than in Texas. Points towards TX.

*Policy goals:* The policy concerns which led the *Neumeier* court to (perhaps regretfully) deviate from *lex loci delicti* aren't present here: in that case, an ON resident was trying to get around ON's allowed defenses and applying NY law would create liability for NY residents where none existed for similar ON residents. Here, rather than subjecting defendants to harsher penalties, the plaintiff stands the risk of more liability — TX's contributory negligence scheme stands to help Sylvania, the defendant, instead of the forum-shopping *Neumeier* plaintiff. Displacing the default rule and applying OK law won't advance any of TX's relevant substantive law purposes of incentivizing both plaintiffs and defendants to exercise care (such as original username/password combinations). Points towards TX.

The court is thus likely to apply TX law, allowing Sylvania to assert the defense of contributory negligence.

## 2023-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2 (graded)

**Civil Procedure 2 & 6, 2023**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** Wednesday, October 18, 12pm
**Due date/time:** 8am on Monday, October 23
**Word limit:** 900 words
**Time limit:** 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.

2. Download the substantive assignment from Exam 4.

3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.

4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment.

5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response via the Exam 4 software. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.

6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.

7. With Harvard policies (including the use of computer-based text-writing tools), you may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until your assignment is returned to you. This prohibition includes librarians.

8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.

9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

10. This assignment is graded, as described in the syllabus.

11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. The Registrar's Office has sent you information about what to use for an anonymous ID. Please follow those instructions.

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

***********************

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Substantive Assignment

Pennsylvania has three federal district courts: the Western, Middle, and Eastern districts. Philadelphia is in the Eastern District of Pennsylvania. New York has four districts: Northern, Eastern, Southern, and Western. Manhattan is located in the Southern District of New York; Albany is located in the Northern District of New York. Pennsylvania follows the First Restatement for horizontal choice of law. New York, as you know, deploys the Second Restatement approach.

All states relevant to this assignment have a cause of action for negligence. The elements of this cause of action are that the defendant owes a duty of care to the plaintiff (we all owe a duty to each other to act with ordinary care), that the defendant breached this duty by failing to act with ordinary care under the circumstances, and that the breach caused quantifiable harm to the plaintiff. In all states relevant to this exam, a plaintiff can sue a defendant for negligence if the defendant failed to exercise ordinary care in maintaining a mode of transportation, such as a railroad coupling. That said, regarding railroad safety, the law of some states requires more than simple negligence before a plaintiff may recover. In Pennsylvania, for example, a plaintiff injured by the actions or inactions of a railroad employee regarding railroad safety must prove that the employee was reckless, meaning that the employee disregarded a known risk. In New York, in contrast, a plaintiff need only prove simple negligence. This difference between negligence versus recklessness is substantive for both horizontal and vertical choice of law rules.

A federal statute called the Railroad Recovery Act ("RRA") creates a cause of action allowing a plaintiff injured by an action of an officer or employee of a railroad to sue for damages, if such action (i) was taken in the course of the officer/employee's company duties, and (ii) would constitute a tort in the law of the state in which the negligent act or omission occurred. Note that under the RRA, it is irrelevant in which state the injury occurred.

Many contracts include so-called "choice of law clauses." These clauses specify that the law of a particular state will govern any lawsuit arising among the parties that relates to their relationship. Such clauses can govern tort lawsuits relating in any way to the parties' relationship as well as breach of contract lawsuits. They can also govern lawsuits against employees of a company who made the relevant contracts. Federal common law provides that choice of law clauses are enforceable. Both Pennsylvania and New York law render such clauses invalid.

All states relevant to this exam have long-arm statutes that allow their courts to exercise personal jurisdiction to the extent permissible under the U.S. Constitution.

Leto Railroad Lines was a New York corporation with its principal place of business in Philadelphia, PA. Leto operated a small, high-speed railroad line between Philadelphia and Manhattan, New York. Leto trains had two cars each, with the cars connected by a standard railroad coupling. Because of the coupling's importance to the safety of the train, Leto policy required that the train's engineer, typically the only employee on each Leto train, inspect it at the station right before the train departs on each run.

On June 20, 2023, Chani, who had lived her entire life in Pittsburgh, PA, was in New York to see her mother, Jessica, who lived in Albany, New York. She decided to take a Leto train to visit her father, Stilgar, who lived in Philadelphia. After driving a friend's car to New York City, she purchased a train ticket at Leto's Manhattan ticket office. The engineer on the train that would make the run to Philadelphia that day, Duncan, sold Chani her ticket. Duncan had grown up with his family in Philadelphia and continued

<!-- page: 3 -->

to live there. To purchase the ticket, Chani signed a contract (Duncan provided it to her) that included the following clause:

> **Applicable law:** The parties to this contract agree that any lawsuit arising out of, or relating in any way, to the buyer's Leto train trip will be governed by the law of Pennsylvania. In particular, the standard of care owed by Leto or any of its employees to the buyer of this contract will be governed by Pennsylvania law.

After signing the contract and purchasing her ticket, Chani boarded the two-car train. Duncan, the train's engineer and driver, failed to inspect the coupling connecting the two cars before the train left the Manhattan station. About 15 minutes after the train crossed into the city limits of Philadelphia, the train's two cars decoupled, causing a crash. Subsequent examination demonstrated that the coupling was faulty in a way that a proper inspection would have uncovered. Chani suffered injuries in the crash.

In August of 2023, Chani sued both Leto and Duncan, demanding $100,000 from each, in the United States federal court for the Northern District of New York. Chani sued Leto under the RRA. Against Duncan, her cause of action was simple negligence. The parties have agreed that for the purposes of this lawsuit, Duncan's failure to inspect was negligent but not reckless. You are a partner in the law firm representing Duncan.

---

**Question 1:** What grounds do you have to move to dismiss, or in the alternative, to transfer the lawsuit?

**Question 2:** If the court did decide to transfer the case, to which districts might it transfer? Would one or another district be better for your client? Explain fully.

## 2023-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-02-kwende-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2 Model Answer

## Question 1

### 12(b) Motion to Dismiss

#### SMJ

There is SMJ under §§ 1331, 1367. Chani's claim against Leto arises under the federal RRA, and serves as an anchoring claim for the state negligence claim against Duncan, which arises out of the same case or controversy. The § 1331 anchoring claim makes § 1367(b) irrelevant. The court would likely apply supplemental jurisdiction because no novel issues of State law are raised, the state claim doesn't predominate over the anchoring claim, and there are no other compelling reasons for denying jurisdiction. *Gibbs*, § 1367(c).

#### IPJ

**Leto**

NY has GIPJ over Leto because Leto is domiciled, incorporated, in NY.

PA has GIPJ because its PPOB is in PA.

**Duncan**

NY likely has SIPJ over Duncan. NY has a long-arm statute that goes to constitutional limits and FRCP 4(k)(1)(a) allows federal courts piggyback off state courts IPJ. SIPJ is permissible under the DPC of 14A (*International Shoe*) because there are minimal contacts linking Duncan, the crash, and NY, and because the exercise of jurisdiction is fair and reasonable. Minimal contacts exist because Duncan conducted business — sale of the ticket — in NY, and served as an engineer in NY. Therefore, Duncan purposefully availed himself of NY such that he could reasonably anticipate being haled into court there (*McGee*). Duncan works in NY, he commutes to NY for work, and evidence of the alleged negligence may be in NY.

PA has GIPJ over Duncan because he is domiciled in PA.

#### Venue

Venue is improper in the NDNY. No substantial portion of events giving rise to the action took place in NDNY, § 1391(b)(2), and Duncan does not reside in NY, § 1391(b)(1), thus, we can move to dismiss or transfer the suit on this ground.

Since Duncan is a PA domiciliary and the crash occurred in Philadelphia, venue would be proper in EDPA. Also, the negligent act happened in Manhattan, thus under § 1391(b)(2), venue is proper in SDNY.

#### Failure to State a Claim

NY State and federal laws conflict on whether the suit should be dismissed based on the COL clause in the contract. If using NY law, the COL clause is invalid and only simple negligence is required, so the case will proceed. If using PA law, COL clause is invalid and recklessness is required, so the case will be dismissed. Under federal law, COL is valid, PA law applies, therefore the case will be

<!-- page: 2 -->

dismissed. The Erie issue is between NY and federal law, since a choice of federal law leads to the same result as PA law.

**York:** The difference between state law and federal law is outcome-determinative, thus NY state law would be used, and the case will proceed.

**Hanna majority (Scalia or Ginsburg):** Since the law at issue is FCL, the court will apply the twin aims of *Erie*. Federal law encourages forum shopping, because out-of-state plaintiffs seeking to enforce a COL clause will file in federal court, while those seeking to avoid an unfavorable COL clause will file in state court. This also results in inequitable administration of justice since out-of-state defendants sued in state court can remove to federal court to ensure the enforcement of a COL clause, while in-state defendants would not have the option. Therefore, NY state law should apply.

**Harlan concurrence:** If COL clauses represent agreements between sophisticated parties, they are likely to affect the primary conduct of plaintiffs because they may engage in more risky behavior knowing that favorable law protects them; therefore are substantive and NY law applies. There is an argument that COL clauses don't affect primary conduct of plaintiffs in adhesion/standardized contracts because most plaintiffs lack the bargaining power to negotiate COL clauses and are unlikely to be aware of these clauses within habitual documents, like train ticket receipts. Therefore, COL would be procedural, federal law applies and the case would be dismissed.

## Question 2

The Court might transfer the case either to EDPA or SDNY. Both are happy courts because there is SMJ, IPJ, and proper venue in both.

The EDPA is better for Duncan. PA is a First Restatement state, which means that PA courts will apply the substantive law of the state where the last act required to complete the tort occurred. Here, that would be the harm caused to Chani, which occurred when the train crashed in PA. Therefore, EDPA would apply PA law, where Chani must prove that Duncan was reckless to recover, and the case would be dismissed.

SDNY could be as favorable for Duncan. NY is a Second Restatement state, so presumption of *lex loci delicti*, then consider whether the center of gravity test privileges NY law under § 6 factors. The injury occurred in PA; the conduct causing the injury occurred in NY; Duncan is domiciled in PA; the relationship between Duncan and Chani is centered in NY — § 145. However, like in *Neumeier*, ignoring PA's policy requiring proof of recklessness in a case which involves a PA domiciliary does not further the substantive law of NY and would actually result in exposure of a NY domiciliary, Leto, to greater liability. Therefore, *lex loci delicti* should apply. However, this analysis could turn out differently because the issue is the standard to be applied to whether certain (in)actions constitute misconduct, which *Babcock* said should be governed by the law of the place where the (in)actions occurred, so EDPA is clearly a better option for Duncan.

## 2023-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-02-lee-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2 Model Answer

## Q1

### 12(b)(1) SMJ

1331 against Leto: The RRA is a federal CoA, so there is § 1331 jurisdiction for the Leto claim (*Mottley*).

There is no § 1331 AUJ against Duncan: Chani alleges a state tort, and there is no embedded federal issue (*Smith-Grable*). There is no § 1332 jurisdiction. Both Duncan and Chani have lived their entire lives in PA, so there is no complete diversity (*Strawbridge*). But using the Leto AUJ anchoring claim (making § 1367(b) N/A), Duncan's claim can be appended — it clearly arises out of CNOF and thus the same C&C. No § 1367(c) factors are present.

### 12(b)(2) PJ

Leto is incorporated in NY, so NDNY has GIPJ.

Duncan is domiciled in PA, so NDNY does not have GIPJ. Regarding SIPJ for Duncan (*Int'l Shoe*), 4k(1)(a) and NY's long-arm allows IPJ to constitutional limits.

There are minimal contacts between the state (NY), the defendant (Duncan), and the litigation subject matter. Duncan operates trains leaving from Manhattan, and he sold the ticket to Chani and omitted the inspection in Manhattan.

Regarding the fair and reasonable factors: Duncan has significant contacts with NY — he operates a train with a NY station. Chani can clearly reach SDNY — she's done so before. If there was video or testimonial evidence of failure to inspect, that would be in NY. However, the train and witnesses of actual decoupling are in Philadelphia. Duncan frequently travels to NY. For HCOL, see below but likely NY substantive law applies, which a NY court knows best. NY has an interest in providing means of redress for residents purchasing tickets (*McGee*).

It's likely that a NY federal court has SIPJ over Duncan.

### 12(b)(3) Venue

§ 1391(b)(1): EDPA. § 1391(c): Duncan (domicile) and Leto (PPOB) both "reside" in PA.
§ 1391(b)(2): Events occurred in EDPA (crash) and SDNY (omission), nowhere else.
§ 1391(b)(3): N/A because venue is proper in EDPA or SDNY.

Venue is improper in NDNY under § 1391(b)(1) — see above — and § 1391(b)(2) — no relevant events. The case must be dismissed or transferred.

### 12(b)(6)

If the substantive law chosen requires recklessness (PA law), there is a failure to state a claim (parties agree that Duncan was not reckless). If simple negligence suffices (NY law), claim likely survives (parties agree negligence).

### HCOL

Any NY federal court will employ HCOL of NY for state claims (*Klaxon*).

<!-- page: 2 -->

The difference between negligence and recklessness is substantive for HCOL/VCOL.

NY is R2nd — presume *lex loci delicti* (PA) but consider the center of gravity of the lawsuit (Section 6). Here the conduct causing injury occurred in NY, and Duncan and Chani's relationship was centered in NY (contract signed). Evidence of omission in NY, but witnesses to the decoupling in PA. PA and NY probably both have an interest in regulating railroad safety (see above).

HCOL analysis (without COL clause) says likely that NY negligence law will be chosen.

### Erie

This conflicts with COL clause, which would establish PA recklessness standard. FCL recognizes enforceability but PA/NY do not.

**York:** If COL clause survives, PA recklessness means claim is likely dismissed as opposed to claim surviving if NY negligence law applies — outcome determinative. Use state law.

**Harlan Concurrence:** COL clause's enforceability changes ex ante conduct — a higher standard for railroad negligence means that Leto/Duncan are less careful/trained. This affects more than ex post lawyer conduct. Use state law.

**Hanna Majority (either version):** FCL, so we consider twin aims. Allowing FCL (COL clause enforceable) promotes forum shopping and inequitable administration given outcome-determinative difference from state law. Use state law.

Ultimately, state law is likely chosen — all Erie tests favor state law. The COL law clause is thus unenforceable.

This means NY R2nd controls, and it's likely that Section 6 factors make NY substantive law govern (see above), and the claim survives 12(b)(6). If not, and *lex loci delicti* presumption controls, then we have dismissal based on PA substantive law.

## Q2

Venue is improper in NDNY, so we must transfer. We can only transfer to happy courts (*Hoffman*), which are SDNY and EDPA. SDNY is happy (see PJ and venue analysis above).

EDPA is happy because venue proper (see above), and we have GIPJ over both Duncan (domicile) and Leto (PPOB).

We want it transferred to EDPA, ultimately to get PA's recklessness standard on our side. When we transfer, given that NDNY was unhappy, the transferee court's HCOL controls (*Van Dusen*). If we get EDPA, then PA's R1st HCOL means we get PA substantive law of recklessness (blood spill was in Philly) and a likely dismissal under 12(b)(6). If we get SDNY, then NY's R2nd HCOL likely means we get NY substantive law of simple negligence (see above) and likely no dismissal.

## 2023-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-02-meron-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2 Model Answer

## 1)

### SMJ

Duncan can try to dismiss for a lack of subject matter jurisdiction but is unlikely to be successful. The RRA provides a federal cause of action, so there is arising under jurisdiction. Supplemental jurisdiction to add Duncan is proper even though the count against him is a state cause of action (simple negligence) because the negligence suit arises from the same case or controversy (§ 1367(a)): Duncan's failure to inspect. Duncan can't move to dismiss under § 1367(b) because jurisdiction isn't based on diversity. He can't dismiss under § 1367(c) because there is no novel issue of state law, the state law claim doesn't predominate over the RRA claim, and there are no exceptional circumstances.

### IPJ

Duncan can try to argue that IPJ is unavailable in New York. There is no general jurisdiction for Duncan since Duncan, a PA domiciliary, is not at home in NY, but there is general jurisdiction for Leto since Leto is a NY corporation. There is likely specific IPJ for Duncan. NY has a long-arm statute that goes to constitutional limits, and because of 4k(1)(a), the federal court will piggyback on this long-arm. To dismiss, Duncan would have to argue that there are no minimal contacts between Duncan, NY, and the subject matter of the litigation. However, Duncan failed to inspect the train's coupling in NY, which is likely enough for minimal contacts (*International Shoe*). The fair and reasonable factors are likely present for NY. Any witnesses to Duncan's failure to inspect would be in NY, NY has an interest in safety measures that are undertaken in their state, and it can't be too hard for Duncan to go to NY since he often works in NY. Duncan can argue that he lives in PA and it is inconvenient for him to be sued in NY. He can also argue that witnesses to the injury may be in PA, and it is more convenient for Chani to be in PA because she lives there. Nevertheless, IPJ in NY is likely proper.

### Venue

Duncan can move to dismiss or to transfer for lack of venue. There is no § 1391(b)(1) venue in the Northern District of New York because not all defendants are residents of New York (Duncan is a PA domiciliary). There is no § 1391(b)(2) venue because no substantial event or omission occurred in the NDNY. Chani visiting her mother in Albany is not sufficiently related to the lawsuit.

### 12(b)(6)

Duncan can attempt to move to dismiss for failure to state a claim upon which relief can be granted. Prong two of the RRA looks to the law of the state where the omission occurred. New York uses simple negligence so Chani can sue under the RRA. However, the choice of law clause is an issue. Under PA law, recklessness is the standard and Duncan wasn't reckless, so the suit would be dismissed. Under NY law, negligence applies. However, given the conflict between state and federal law on whether a choice of law clause is enforced, an Erie analysis is required. Under federal law, the suit must be dismissed. Under state law, the clause is not enforced, and negligence likely applies (see HCOL below).

**York**

<!-- page: 2 -->

Whether or not the choice of law clause is enforced is outcome determinative because the suit will be dismissed if federal, and thus PA, law is applied. Therefore, state law applies.

**Harlan**

The clause affects primary conduct because Chani (or passengers like her) may have decided not to sign the contract depending on which tort law applies. The clause also affects where a party decides to do business. State law applies.

**Scalia/Ginsburg**

The source of the federal law is federal common law, so both Scalia and Ginsburg will look to the twin aims of *Erie*. Following the Rules of Decision Act, state law applies when federal law would invite forum-shopping and yield disparate litigation outcomes. The choice of law provision encourages forum shopping because defendants benefiting from the clause will remove to federal court to enforce it. State law applies.

## 2)

The case must be dismissed or transferred, likely transferred. Possible districts are SDNY and EDPA. Duncan can only transfer to a happy court (*Hoffman*).

SDNY is a happy court. There is SMJ through arising under jurisdiction and supplemental (see above) and IPJ in New York (see above). Venue is proper in SDNY under § 1391(b)(2) because the omission occurred in Manhattan.

EDPA is a happy court. There is SMJ through AUJ and SSMJ. General IPJ exists in EDPA, and venue is proper under § 1391(b)(1), since all defendants reside in PA (Leto's PPoB), and Duncan resides in Philadelphia.

EDPA is better for Duncan. Duncan wants recklessness, not negligence. This is a substantive difference. PA follows the 1st Restatement. The injury occurred in PA, so PA law and recklessness applies. NY follows the 2nd Restatement. The injury occurred in PA, but Chani could argue the center of gravity is in NY because the omission was in NY, witnesses may be there, and NY has an interest in regulating safety in NY. If NY law applies, negligence is the standard, which is bad for Duncan.

## 2023-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-02-sharma-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 2 Model Answer

## Q1

### 12(b)(1): SMJ

§ 1331 against Leto: The RRA is a federal CoA, so there is § 1331 jurisdiction for the Leto claim. *Mottley*.

The claim against Duncan is state law with no embedded federal issue (no § 1331), and Duncan and Chani are both citizens of PA (no § 1332). But NDNY can exercise supplemental jurisdiction over the state-law negligence claim against Duncan. State and federal claims arise from the same CNOF. *Gibbs*; § 1367(a). § 1367(b) is inapplicable because the court is exercising AUJ over the RRA claim. None of the discretionary factors listed in § 1367(c) are present.

### 12(b)(2) PJ

**Leto:** NDNY can exercise general PJ over Leto, a NY corporation.

**Duncan:** NDNY cannot exercise general PJ over Duncan because Duncan is a PA domiciliary. However, NDNY can likely exercise specific PJ over Duncan. 4K(1)(A). The NY long-arm statute goes to the limits of the Constitution. There are minimal contacts linking Duncan, the subject matter of the litigation, and NY. The train ticket was sold in NY. The failure to inspect the train also happened in NY. Regarding fair and reasonable factors, Duncan has at least some contacts in NY, and it must be reasonably convenient for Duncan to get to NY, because he works there in part. NY has an interest in ensuring that trains leaving its stations are safe. Evidence regarding the negligence is likely to be in NY because the negligent omission occurred there. If any state law applies (see below), it may well be NY. A NY federal court will apply NY HCOL rules (*Klaxon*). NY is a R2 state, so we begin with the presumption that PA negligence law applies under *lex loci delicti*. Section 6 factors might be sufficient to dislodge that presumption and render NY law applicable. The center of gravity of the parties' relationship is NY because that is where the ticket was sold and the wrongful omission took place. *Babcock*. NY federal court is going to be better at applying NY law relative to PA law.

### 12(b)(3)

Venue is improper in NDNY because Leto is a PA & NY resident and Duncan is a PA resident (so no § 1391(b)(1)), and nothing substantial occurred in NDNY (so no § 1391(b)(2)). The train ticket sale and the negligent omission took place in SDNY, and the accident in EDPA. § 1391(b)(3) venue is inapplicable because there are at least two judicial districts in the U.S. where this dispute could be resolved (see Q2 transfer analysis below). Therefore, a motion to dismiss or transfer filed under 12(b)(3) is highly likely to succeed.

### 12(b)(6)

All parties agree that Duncan's actions were negligent, but not reckless. If PA law governs the dispute, then Chani's claim can be dismissed for failure to state a claim upon which relief can be granted. By contrast, if NY law governs the dispute, then the lawsuit can proceed. *Erie* determines whether choice-of-law clause is valid.

- *York*: Use state law because it is outcome determinative. Choice-of-law clause is invalid.
- *Hanna* Majority (Scalia + Ginsburg): Twin aims of *Erie*. Using the federal common law upholding COL clauses would promote forum shopping and inequitable administration of the laws. State law governs and the choice-of-law clause is invalid.
- *Hanna* Concurrence (Harlan): Choice-of-law clause may affect pre-litigation conduct because they change how aggressively businesses sell products. Leto sold train tickets under the assumption that PA law would govern any dispute. Perhaps employees would have been trained further if Leto thought NY's more pro-plaintiff law would control.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Choice-of-law clause is substantive; state law governs, so the choice-of-law clause is invalid.

In case *Erie* tests indicate the choice-of-law clause is invalid, we proceed with HCOL analysis. See NY HCOL analysis in PJ analysis in 12(b)(2) section above. If Section 6 factors overcome *lex loci delicti* presumption, lawsuit can proceed. If *lex loci delicti* governs, then the blood was spilled in PA, so PA law governs, and the lawsuit will be dismissed.

## Q2

Because venue is improper, NDNY is an unhappy court. Must transfer to happy court where case could have been originally filed. *Hoffman*. For venue, § 1391(b)(3) is unavailable, see above.

- **WDPA/MDPA/EDNY/WDNY:** Venue is improper because a substantial part of the events giving rise to the lawsuit did not occur in any of these districts, § 1391(b)(2). These are unhappy courts.
- **EDPA:** Venue is proper here § 1391(b)(2) because the train accident occurred in Philadelphia. It is also proper under § 1391(b)(1), and EDPA can exercise general PJ over Duncan and Leto, because Duncan is a Philadelphia domiciliary and Leto's PPOB is in Philadelphia.
- **SDNY:** Venue is proper under § 1391(b)(2) because the negligent omission that caused the accident occurred in Manhattan. SDNY can exercise PJ over both defendants for same reason NDNY can, see above.

Transferor court was unhappy, so we use transferee court HCOL rules. *Cf. Ferens*. If we transfer to EDPA, PA law governs because PA is a R1 state, and the blood was spilled in Philadelphia. If we transfer to SDNY, NY law might govern because New York is a R2 state, and the Section 6 factors might overcome the *lex loci delicti* presumption. Transfer to EDPA is better for Duncan's lawyer so recklessness standard is used, and case can be dismissed under 12(b)(6).

## 2023-assignment-03 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3 (graded)

**Civil Procedure 2 & 6, 2023**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

## Logistics and Rules

**Posting date:** November 8, 12pm
**Due date/time:** 8am on Monday, November 13
**Word limit:** 900 words
**Time limit:** 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.

2. Download the substantive assignment from Exam 4.

3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.

4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment.

5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response via the Exam 4 software. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.

6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.

7. With Harvard policies (including the use of computer-based text-writing tools), you may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until your assignment is returned to you. This prohibition includes librarians.

8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.

9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

10. This assignment is graded, as described in the syllabus.

11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. The Registrar's Office has sent you information about what to use for an anonymous ID. Please follow those instructions.

<!-- page: 2 -->

12. Any response uploaded after the due date/time in this assignment will receive two points off per fifteen minutes that it is late. For example, an assignment uploaded 16 minutes after the due date and time will receive four points off for lateness.

## Suggestions for Your Answer

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

**************

## Substantive Assignment

**Question 1:** Would you support a proposal to change United States federal court law and practice (including U.S. Const. amend. VII) to discontinue the use of the jury in civil cases? Explain why or why not. Your discussion of whether to continue juries should include a discussion of what our current system allows juries to decide, i.e., the allocation of power between judges and juries. It should also include some assessment of what the federal court system believes to be the strengths and weaknesses of judges and juries.

---

**Question 2:** Assume for the purposes of this assignment that the preclusion law of the states of New York and New Jersey is identical to federal law, and identical to the law that we have learned through (and including) class period 20. Assume also that both New Jersey and New York have declaratory judgment statutes identical in all ways to § 2201, and that their rules of civil procedure are also identical in all ways to the federal rules. New York has four federal district courts; the Southern District of New York includes Manhattan. New Jersey has only one federal district court, D.N.J. In this question, please consider all elements of a doctrine the question raises, even if you think one will be dispositive. Note also that because this assignment covers doctrines completed through the end of Class Day 20, you should not discuss issue preclusion in your response.

Splinter the Rat was born in the sewers of Manhattan. Infected by a radioactive slime at an early age, he became a national leader on the topic of civil procedure. He founded a law school for other creatures affected by the same radioactive slime. He taught classes in the sewers of his birth, deeming them, not unreasonably, the cleanest places in Manhattan. Splinter's teaching skills were not always the best, however, or at least, so his students thought. Splinter invited Professor Bebop, a domiciliary of Albany, NY, to help with a class on preclusion. The class did not go well. In particular, students April Mestre and Rafael Kleiner, both martial arts adepts and both domiciliaries of New Jersey, were enraged. They planned for several days, scouting out the sewer network and picking their perfect time. When the day arrived, April and Rafael lay in wait for Splinter in a dark sewer tunnel and, using ninja weapons, beat up

<!-- page: 3 -->

the hapless Rat. Two months later, April and Rafael were visiting Albany and unexpectedly encountered Bebop. The two seized the moment and also beat him up. The incidents caused $80,000 in medical bills and $20,000 in pain and suffering to each of Splinter and Bebop.

Both New York and New Jersey have a tort called "battery," which provides a cause of action to a plaintiff when a defendant intentionally causes bodily harm to the plaintiff via the use of fists, flippers, or weapons. The law of both states provides a defense to a battery claim called "justification," which states that if there was sufficient reason for the bodily harm at issue, the defendants are not liable.

Fearing that they were unpopular among the judges of their home state of New Jersey, Rafael and April decided to seize the forum. April said to Rafael, "I tell you what, I'll hire us a law firm, you just sign the papers and leave everything to me." Rafael said, "Thanks, just let me know what you need me to do, and I'll leave everything to you." April hired the law firm of Shredder & Donatello to represent them, with Shredder deciding to handle the case personally. Both April and Rafael signed retainer agreements with the firm, but then at the last minute, April decided to try to keep herself out of the litigation. April got Shredder to file a lawsuit naming only Rafael as plaintiff and naming Splinter as the only defendant in the Southern District of New York, seeking a declaratory judgment that any battery the two had committed against Splinter should result in no damages because the horrible civil procedure lecture Splinter and Bebop delivered on the fateful day justified the battery. Shredder worked exclusively with April on the decisions in the litigation through the jury trial. As soon as Splinter received notice of the lawsuit, he called his friend Bebop to tell him all about it. Bebop wished Splinter well.

The case proceeded to a jury, which rendered a verdict, and the Southern District judge rendered judgment in accordance with the jury's verdict.

**Question 2(a):** If, after the Manhattan case went to judgment, Splinter wanted to sue Rafael in a New Jersey federal court for damages, would Rafael have any Civ Pro defense? Why or why not?

**Question 2(b):** If, after the Manhattan case went to judgment, April wanted to sue Splinter in a New York federal court for a declaratory judgment that any battery Rafael and she committed was justified, would Splinter have any Civ Pro defense? Why or why not?

**Question 2(c):** If, after the Manhattan case went to judgment, Bebop wanted to sue Rafael in a New Jersey federal court for damages, would Rafael have any Civ Pro defense? Why or why not?

Note: You may answer subsections (a) through (c) jointly or separately as you prefer.

---

**Question 3:** There is a proposal to amend the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure to provide that the doctrine of claim preclusion will apply in a subsequent lawsuit anytime a person (or corporation or unincorporated association) could have intervened in a prior lawsuit, regardless of whether they received notice of the prior lawsuit, and regardless of whether they did in fact intervene. What would be the advantages and disadvantages of such a proposal? Would you be in favor or against? Explain.

## 2023-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-03-aida-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3 Model Answer

## Question 1

I would reject this proposal. While judges may be better at providing uniformity (stare decisis), construing complex written documents (like patents), or overseeing long-running disputes, the courts believe that juries may be better at resolving disputes of fact, providing community views (they can act as a cross-section of the community), and judging credibility. Additionally, plaintiffs may feel that justice has been denied to them if only a single judge decides their claim (rather than multiple "peers"). Discontinuing all civil jury trials would eliminate these potential benefits. Moreover, the current allocation of power between judges/juries supports the idea that we — as a community — see value in juries. Baked into the "law/fact" test is the idea that juries are better than judges at the tasks listed above. Additionally, the "law/equity" test weighs in favor of a jury when the plaintiff seeks remedies at law (e.g. damages), covering a significant number of cases. While juries may be far from perfect (ex: they may introduce discriminatory bias), eliminating jury trials would (1) threaten people's faith in the legal system (given our long-standing belief in juries) and (2) may lead to less just outcomes in some cases.

## Question 2(a)

For Q2 answers, L1 = original lawsuit against Splinter and L2 = proposed lawsuit.

**SMJ:** There is complete diversity (Splinter domiciled in NY, Rafael in NJ) and $100k AIC > $75k, so § 1332 provides diversity SMJ.

**IPJ:** NJDC has GIPJ over Rafael (since domiciled in NJ, 4(k)(1)(A)).

**Venue:** § 1391(b)(1) says NJDC can lay venue in a suit against Rafael (Rafael only defendant).

**Preclusion:** Applying Rule 13(a) shows that Splinter's L2 claim was a compulsory counterclaim in L1. First, the claim in L2 arises from the same T/O as the claim in L1. Both lawsuits focus on the beating of Splinter by Rafael/April in the sewer network (same actions, same time). While the "justification" claim in L1 would involve facts beyond the beating itself (e.g. the class), both lawsuits would involve evidence of the beating. Second, no additional parties would have been brought in if Splinter raised the counterclaim in L1. Third, the judgment was final and on the merits (judgment entered after a jury trial). No special exceptions apply (e.g. claim wasn't subject of separate pending action). Because Splinter's claim was not raised then, it may not be brought now.

## Question 2(b)

**SMJ:** There is complete diversity (Splinter domiciled in NY, April in NJ) and $100k AIC > $75k. § 1332.

**IPJ:** SDNY has GIPJ over Splinter (since domiciled in NY).

**Venue:** § 1391(b)(1) says SDNY may lay venue (Splinter only defendant). § 1391(b)(2) says SDNY can lay venue (beating occurred in Manhattan).

<!-- page: 2 -->

**Preclusion:** Splinter can successfully raise claim preclusion as a defense if (1) April and Rafael are in privity, (2) the claim in L1 is the "same" as the claim in L2 (T/O test), and (3) judgment in L1 was final/on the merits.

*Privity:* April likely had substantial control over Rafael's conduct in L1 given that she is the one who hired the law firm and Rafael let April "handle everything." Thus, she was in privity with Rafael.

*T/O:* Rafael and April both beat up Splinter on the same day using the same plan.

*Final/"On the Merits":* Judgment was final; and judgment was on the merits (jury trial).

Therefore, April is likely claim precluded.

## Question 2(c)

**SMJ/IPJ:** See 2A (Bebop domiciled in NY, like Splinter; same AIC).

**Venue:** § 1391(b)(1) says NJDC can lay venue (Rafael only defendant).

**Preclusion:** Claim preclusion will not apply. Bebop was not a party in L1 (and was not in privity with any party in L1), and it would be a denial of due process to apply preclusion to him. The T/O "same claim" analysis is closer. Both Bebop and Splinter were beat up by the same students, and the students were triggered to beat them up because of a single class taught by Bebop and Splinter. Thus, the "justification" legal issue and evidence is likely to have significant overlap in L1/L2. However, the assaults occurred in different states and months apart; evidence to prove assaults will likely be disparate (e.g. different CCTV, different witnesses).

## Question 3

I would reject this proposal. Potential advantages would be increased efficiency (fewer "redundant" cases for an already burdened court system) and uniformity (claims evaluated/determined at the same time). However, disadvantages would be severe. A party that never received notice of L1 but *could* have intervened (and thus would be precluded under the new rule), would have had no notice or opportunity to be heard (due process violation). To avoid this, people would have to constantly monitor the filing of new lawsuits to see if anyone was bringing a lawsuit where they could intervene. This goes far beyond the current claim preclusion, which only bars "same party" claims (brought by you or someone you were in privity with), not claims of unrelated 3rd parties. This could even lead to invidious "gaming" where defendant gets plaintiff P1 to *quietly* bring smaller claim to block future litigation from P2 (who might have a bigger claim or better resources).

## 2023-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-03-deschler-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3 Model Answer

## Question 1

I would not support a proposal to discontinue the use of the jury in civil cases. Such an amendment would offer little gain to the civil legal system, but would come with a high cost. Currently, the federal court system trusts juries to make certain findings of fact, and almost no findings of law. The judicial system trusts juries, and not judges, to judge the credibility of witnesses, both expert and lay, but only in non-equitable cases. The role of the jury is thus limited to only factual questions in legal cases (as opposed to legal issues, or equitable cases). The civil legal system already has safeguards in place to protect against incompetent or rogue juries. The system allows judges the power to preemptively remove the power of a jury via summary judgment or JAMOL, when a judge concludes that no reasonable jury could side with one of the parties. Judges can also overrule a jury after the fact, via Rule 59 and the motion for a new trial. Additionally, various doctrines, such as the public interest factors in a forum non conveniens analysis, allow judges to proactively make a jury's job easier. As such, I believe that little would be added to the system by removing juries, as the system already controls for potential problems. Much would be lost by such a move — trust in the civil legal system would likely drop as the right to have a case heard by a jury of one's peers disappeared in the civil sense. Decisions over whom to trust and what to believe would be made by individual judges, unelected, and more likely to be biased than a larger panel of one's peers. The loss of trust and introduction of potential bias are not worth any small gains made by removing juries from the civil system.

## Question 2

There are no SMJ issues (Splinter is NY, Rafael NJ, AIC met, so use § 1332 for all cases), no PJ issues (everyone being sued in home forum, so general IPJ), and no venue issues (via § 1391(b)(1)) with any of the lawsuits. Questions arise in each case regarding counterclaims or claim preclusion. For all questions, judgment in first lawsuit (Rafael suing Splinter in NY) is final and on the merits via jury verdict.

**(a)** Rafael can raise forfeiture of a compulsory counterclaim, because Splinter had to bring a compulsory counterclaim under Rule 13(a) in the original suit. Splinter's suing Rafael for damages would come from the same transaction or occurrence that Rafael's DJ in the Manhattan case came from. Counterclaim might require adding April, but court should have jurisdiction via diversity, as she is NJ resident, so no problems with FRCP 13(a)(1)(B). If Splinter did not raise the counterclaim in the Manhattan suit, it expires, since it was compulsory. If he did raise it, then he would be claim precluded from suing again in NJ because he is the same party, the judgment is final and on the merits (after the jury trial), and per above it came from the same transaction and thus is the same claim.

**(b)** Claim preclusion issue. Same transaction or occurrence is easy: beating of Splinter by April and Rafael at issue in both cases. Rafael's DJ went to judgment, so no issue there.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Only question is whether they are the same party. April is literally different from Splinter, but likely in privity via substantial control. Clear from facts that April controlled Rafael: Shredder "worked exclusively with April," April removed herself from suit after Rafael signed retainer, Rafael said "I'll leave everything to you." Splinter can successfully raise claim preclusion as a defense against April.

**(c)** Bebop was not a party in Rafael's initial lawsuit, so should not be claim precluded, as that would be a due process violation. Additionally, it is debatable whether beating of Bebop is same T/O as beating of Splinter (beatings were different, but cause of beatings the same, could be close). Claim preclusion is unavailable to Rafael.

## Question 3

The advantages of such a proposal would be to lower the number of cases on the federal courts' dockets and combine litigation on major issues (which is the goal of many complex litigation doctrines). A major negative would be claim precluding people who had no notice of a lawsuit, thus removing their right to relief without due process. This would create issues for notice/opportunity to be heard, as well as incentivize people to know about as many lawsuits happening as possible, to find out if they should/need to intervene. Large companies will be more able to monitor the dockets of all federal courts than individual people, leading to an imbalance of power as corporations will (1) intervene in earlier suits and (2) will be aware that individuals should have and raise the issue that they have not. Complex litigation is already balanced towards repeat players with large amounts of resources, and this change would further that trend. Additionally, this proposal would result in many, many more interventions, which would overcomplicate relatively simple cases, and likely remove power over the lawsuit from the parties who are actually litigating that suit. On the whole I would be against the proposal.

## 2023-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-03-engle-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3 Model Answer

## Question 1

No. I support the preserved right to a jury afforded by the Seventh Amendment. Traditionally, suits in law carried a right to a jury trial, while suits in equity were tried before a judge. There is also the law/fact divide. Our system supposes that judges are better when addressing issues of law and that juries are better when dealing with issues of fact. This is the traditional touchstone of allocating decision-making between judges and juries. The federal court system believes that the strengths of a judge are four-fold: interpreting law (statutes, administrative regulations, and case law); analyzing complex written documents (like patents); handling long-standing, complicated disputes; and ensuring that there is stare decisis and uniformity in the law. However, the system also finds strengths in juries, particularly the evaluation of the credibility of witness testimony. In addition, juries provide a common person perspective to issues such as negligence and provide demographic diversity and a better reflection of "community standards." Juries thus help democratize our judicial system. To cheat the system of that by eliminating the jury system in favor of efficiency would be a mistake.

## Question 2

**SMJ:** There is no AUJ because this is a state cause of action for battery (*First Federal*). There is DJ because the AIC is $100,000 and parties are diverse (Rafael = NJ, Splinter = NY) (*Strawbridge*).

**IPJ:** Although it probably doesn't matter here, there is general jurisdiction in the SDNY over Splinter (domicile, 4(k)(1)(A)). For IPJ issues related to Rafael and April, D.N.J. can exercise general IPJ (domiciles, 4(k)(1)(A)).

**Venue:** Although it probably doesn't matter here, venue in the SDNY is proper under § 1391(b)(1) (Splinter's domicile) and under § 1391(b)(2) (beating occurred there). Venue vis-à-vis Rafael and April would be proper in D.N.J. under § 1391(b)(1) (domiciles).

### 2(a)

Yes, Rafael could assert claim preclusion as an affirmative defense. Regardless of who won, Splinter is claim precluded (or precluded by FRCP 13) because Splinter should have brought a compulsory counterclaim against Rafael. Splinter's counterclaim here is compulsory because it arises from the same T/O. Remember that as here in the declaratory judgment realm, Splinter needed to counterclaim on the issue (Rafael's battery liability) that he would have sued on in the "imaginary case." Splinter is claim precluded because (1) the prior suit was final and on the merits (the court entered judgment on a jury verdict), (2) the present suit arises out of the same claim and surrounds the same T/O (because a suit about the justification defense and battery cover the same incident of Rafael and April beating up Splinter) and (3) the parties are the same in both suits (Rafael and Splinter).

<!-- page: 2 -->

### 2(b)

This is slightly less certain. For the reasons just stated, the SDNY case was final/on the merits and the present suit arises out of the same claim and T/O. For April to be claim precluded, she would also have to have been in privity with Rafael. She is not a successor in interest, but April exercised substantial (actually, complete) control over Rafael's litigation decisions. However, while she did not have a legal ability to compel Rafael, Rafael delegated control to April. That is likely enough for privity, so April will likely be claim precluded, and Splinter can use that defense.

### 2(c)

There is an argument to be made for either side here in relation to whether Bebop's claim arises out of the same T/O as Splinter's. Arguably, the two batteries were separate and occurred in separate cities, but one could also argue that the justification defense being used in both arises out of the same anger triggered by the same class. In terms of administrability and overlapping evidence, the cases are similar. Ultimately, however, Bebop *can* sue here because he was not a party to L1 and there is no privity here, as there was no substantial control or successor in interest. Bebop can assert diversity jurisdiction, see above re venue and IPJ.

## Question 3

No, it's a bad rule suggestion for several reasons. Proponents will point to a few advantages, namely efficiency in the form of lower litigation expense, fewer cases for overcrowded courts, and uniform outcomes across similar claims. However, there are distinct disadvantages to the interweaving of claim preclusion and intervention. First, significant access to justice issues will make the lives of ordinary civil plaintiffs seeking recourse much more difficult. While it will help limit claims, this rule would also bar many claims simply because a prospective plaintiff failed to intervene. Second, there are significant issues with not requiring notice. Notice and Procedural Due Process jurisprudence reflects a policy that would-be litigants shouldn't lose their rights to pursue claims and that they maintain a constitutional right to proper notice (Due Process under *Mullane*). This rule would violate that policy by forgoing a notice requirement like that statutorily prescribed by FRCP 4, which is meant to protect parties. While I am sympathetic to rule changes aimed at making our overcrowded and overcomplicated justice system more efficient, the negative implications of this rule are too great to warrant instituting it.

## 2023-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2023-assignment-03-estes-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

# Assignment 3 Model Answer

## Question 1

I would not support abolishing the right to a jury trial in civil cases, but would amend it to guarantee a jury only when there is an issue of material fact.

The law/equity divide doesn't draw efficiently on judges' and juries' skillsets. Fraud actions rely on determinations of credibility, and restitution reflects fairness to remedy harms in a community — both credibility and fairness determinations are thought to be jury strengths, but here are relegated to a judge. This suggests that the law/equity and law/fact frameworks are in tension. Rather than "preserving" the jury right along law/equity, the law/fact divide should control. Juries should resolve factual and credibility disputes, where community values and diverse perspectives are particularly salient. Judges should resolve issues of law, reflecting strength in interpreting complex and sophisticated statutes and promotion of legal uniformity.

This could impact the Public Rights Exception because the jury right would no longer turn on the COA. Administrability would marshal against juries in PR cases, and the interest in federal uniformity is particularly strong when Congress creates a federal COA to displace local law. The strength of these interests suggests that the PRE from *Atlas Roofing* should be retained under the amendment.

## Question 2

**SMJ:**

§ 1331: No. There's no AUJ because battery is a state claim. § 2201 doesn't provide jurisdiction because the would-be plaintiff (Splinter) could not have brought suit. *First Federal*.

§ 1332: Yes. There is complete diversity because Rafael/April (N.J.) are diverse from Splinter/Bebop (N.Y.). *Strawbridge*. AIC is greater than $75k ($80,000 + $20,000 = $100,000) for both Bebop and Splinter individually.

**IPJ:** New York has general IPJ over Splinter and Bebop because they are "really at home."

**Venue:** Venue is proper for Splinter in S.D.N.Y. under § 1391(b)(2) because substantial events occurred in Manhattan (the attack/lecture), and he is a resident under § 1391(b)(1), (c)(1).

### 2(a)

Yes. Rafael would have an affirmative claim preclusion defense because Splinter was obligated to bring a 13(a) counterclaim in LS1.

*13(a):* Splinter had an opposing claim against Rafael arising out of the same T/O (13(a)(1)(A)), with no jurisdiction issue (13(a)(1)(B)). Splinter might argue the battery was not the same T/O because the justification defense was based on the lecture and not the battery, which happened at a different time/place. But *Heyward* tells us T/O is broad, with high interest in efficiency. The court in LS1 had to have handled evidence regarding the battery to determine whether the lecture justified it. Thus, LS1 dealt with enough similar evidence to render same T/O, making Splinter's counterclaim compulsory.

*Claim preclusion:* Because same T/O, identical parties, judgment final and on the merits (judgment after a jury trial), Splinter is claim precluded.

### 2(b)

Yes, Splinter should argue that April is claim precluded. The T/O in LS2 is identical to LS1 because the lecture and the beating are the same. Per above, the judgment was

<!-- page: 2 -->

final and on the merits. And April was in privity with Rafael since she exercised substantial control over the decision to enter the litigation (Rafael would "leave everything" to her), chose the firm, and worked exclusively with Shredder on the litigation decisions.

### 2(c)

No; a claim preclusion defense would fail. While there's a colorable argument that T/O is the same (the justification defense involves overlapping evidence), the battery against Bebop happened 2 months later in a different city, with unique evidence and facts not previously litigated. Also, Bebop and Splinter are not the same party, and not in privity, so applying preclusion against him would violate due process. Bebop knew of the litigation, but did not exercise "substantial control" and is not a "successor in interest" to the litigation. As before, LS1 did result in a judgment final and on the merits.

**PJ/Venue in N.J.:** N.J. has GIPJ since April and Rafael are "really at home." Venue is proper under § 1391(b)(1) because all defendants reside in N.J., (c)(1).

## Question 3

I would not favor this proposal unless it incorporated PDP protections.

**Disadvantages:** Federal notice requirements are significant (at times requiring multiple notices by multiple means), reflecting a policy judgment that would-be plaintiffs are substantially burdened when they lose the opportunity to vindicate their rights. That value would be undermined if plaintiffs like Bebop lost the opportunity to claim $80k in medical bills without sufficient notice. And while there is an interest in quick resolution of lawsuits, that policy balance is already reflected in SOL periods that limit the timeframe for bringing suit. Considering *Mathews v. Eldridge* factors also suggests that insufficient notice would undermine private interests (lost claims) and raise the risk of erroneous determination, since adverse parties would not always be notified to represent their rights.

**Advantages:** There is a powerful gov't interest (*Eldridge*) in more affordable and efficient administration of the law, and a public interest in fewer lawsuits. This rule would support those interests by encouraging litigants to bring claims, leading to consolidation and reduced burden on the court system. That said, cases might become so unwieldy under this rule that courts would be forced to chop them up under Rule 42, and any administrative gain might be lost, all while risking parties' opportunities to vindicate their rights. But from a fairness perspective, the rule could lead to more uniform outcomes between individuals — for example, it might be more likely that Bebop & Splinter's claims would receive similar treatment if heard in a single case than if they were split.

### Suggestions for Your Answer
