For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise specific in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under traditional notes of fair play and justice."

---

## The Law

All states relevant to this exam had a common law cause of action for negligence. To prevail on a common law negligence cause of action the Plaintiff must show: (1) The Defendant had a duty of care as to the Plaintiff, (2) that duty was breached, (3) that breach caused injuries to the Plaintiff's person or property, and (4) damage resulted to the Plaintiff. The Plaintiff must prevail on all elements to win. With respect to duty, every person owed every other person a duty to exercise ordinary care in any interaction.

All states relevant to this exam had long-arm statutes going to constitutional limits.

**California** law provided an affirmative defense to a negligence cause of action called "contributory negligence." As discussed in class, under this affirmative defense, if a defendant in a negligence case proved that the plaintiff's conduct was in any way negligent, and that the plaintiff's negligence caused in any part (no matter how small the contribution) the plaintiff's injury, the plaintiff received no damages. Thus, if a jury found that a defendant 99% percent at fault and a plaintiff 1% at fault for an accident in which the plaintiff suffered a $1000 injury, the plaintiff received no damages.

**New York and Texas** law provided a different affirmative defense to a negligence cause of action called "comparative negligence." As discussed in class, under this affirmative defense, if a defendant in a negligence case proved that the plaintiff's conduct was in some way negligent, and that the plaintiff's negligence caused in part the plaintiff's injury, the trier of fact apportioned fault between the plaintiff and the defendant, and the court awarded damages to the plaintiff in accordance with the parties' respective fault. If a jury found that a defendant was 99% percent at fault and a plaintiff 1% at fault for an accident in which the plaintiff suffered a $1000 injury, then the court would order the defendant to pay the plaintiff $990, not $0 nor the full $1000.

California, Nevada, and Texas used the First Restatement for horizontal choice of law. Remember what you already know about New York law. Remember also, as discussed in class, that a federal district court uses the horizontal choice of law rules of the state in which it sits.

<!-- page: 3 -->

## The Characters

**Ishamael:** Ishamael was born, lived all his life, and operated a business out of his house in Lake Tahoe, Nevada. He never incorporated his business, preferring to own and operate everything personally. Ishamael's business involved constructing and training his buyers to deploy movable (with a sufficiently sized truck), some-assembly-required pop-up bumper boat facilities, called BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers. These constructs consisted of small pools holding a few inches or feet of water, as well as a number of propeller-driving boats that could reach surprisingly high speeds. Ishamael had been in this business for about twenty years until the time of the events described below. In a nutshell, potential buyers of one of Ishamael's BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers paid a flat fee of $300,000 for the full set of equipment and constructs as well as a three-week course in Ishamael's large back yard on how to set up and operate it all. Once customers underwent training in Lake Tahoe, they took possession of their equipment there (Ishamael often helped to load the equipment onto customers' trucks) and were free to go wherever they liked to start their own businesses. Ishamael marketed his product exclusively on social media sites, such as Facebook, Snapchat, X, and Instagram. His Internet content was viewable anywhere in the world, and he responded to questions about his BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers from anywhere using social media.

**Tuon:** Tuon grew up in Albany, NY. After graduating from high school, she spent five years amassing a small fortune playing online poker in her parents' home in Albany before looking for a different opportunity.

**Moiraine Damodred:** Damodred grew up in Austin, Texas. After graduating from high school there, she wandered from place to place for five years, never staying in one place for longer than three months, until at age 23, she began to think about going to college. At that time, it was the fall of 2020, and she was renting a studio apartment in Brooklyn, NY, a place she decided she liked. For work, she was bartending in Manhattan. She looked for schools nearby because she especially liked the 100-square-foot apartment she was renting at the time. It took her a while to identify the schools in which she was interested, but in the early fall of 2023, she applied to Columbia University (in Manhattan), NYU (in Manhattan), Hunter College (in Manhattan), and Barnard College (in Manhattan). She also inquired with her landlord about whether they were interested in selling her the studio apartment in which she was living (Damodred figured she had saved enough for the down payment and could earn enough for the monthly payments with a full-time job). The Manhattan bar where she was bartending at the time was due to shut down for repairs during the month of November of 2023, so in the middle of October of 2023, using a computer located at the Manhattan bar, she placed notices on job board websites with national scope (meaning they allowed job seekers and potential employers from across the world to post information) seeking temporary work for that month.

**Perrin Aybara:** Perrin was born and raised in Fort Worth, Texas. As an adult, he operated a special farm raising wolves just outside Austin for use in TV shows and movies. In early 2022, on an online dating site, he met a woman named Zarine Bashere, a native of Sacramento, California, who owned a grocery store near where she grew up. They started dating. Perrin flew into Sacramento one week every month, and similarly, Zarine flew into Fort Worth one week every month.

<!-- page: 4 -->

## The Events

In September of 2023, Tuon came across Ishamael's business online and decided to give it a try. She borrowed the $300,000-plus she needed to get started from her parents. She paid Ishamael his fee and underwent his three-week training course in Lake Tahoe in October of 2023. The course did not include any information on how to adjust the water levels in the BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers construct to accommodate the number of boats that were floating in it. The course did, however, include several hours on how to set up the boats in the construct properly, including two hours solely on the placement of the propellers that powered the boats. In late October, Tuon took possession of the pieces of her BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers construct in Ishamael's back yard in Lake Tahoe, Nevada. She hired a small flatbed truck and a driver. She loaded it all onto the truck, and the driver transported the whole thing to a vacant lot Tuon had rented in Sacramento, California. Tuon followed behind in her pride and joy, a fully restored red 1965 Mustang convertible that she had inherited two years earlier from an eccentric aunt. It had served as her primary means of transport ever since.

Upon arrival in Sacramento, Tuon constructed the BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers facility herself. She looked at some online job-seeking websites and found a notice there from Damodred saying that the latter was looking for temporary work. After a video interview, which Tuon conducted from Sacramento and Damodred conducted on her cell phone from the Manhattan bar where she was working, Tuon offered Damodred a job. Damodred accepted on the spot. The two set up a second, 30-minute video-call, which Damodred took from a computer at her Manhattan bar, during which Tuon (in Nevada at the time) provided Damodred with information on how to operate the BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers apparatus. This was the only training Damodred received on how to operate the equipment, and it included no information on how to attach the propellers to the boats. Tuon then bought a plane ticket for Damodred to fly out of La Guardia (in Queens), NY to Sacramento. Damodred landed on the morning of November 1, 2023, and reported immediately for work.

In the afternoon of November 1, 2023, Perrin was in Sacramento visiting Zarine. While out walking around, they encountered Tuon's BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers construct. As it happened, Damodred was working, and Tuon was there keeping an eye on things. Perrin decided to give it a try. Damodred sold him a ticket and set up his boat, including the propellor. But when it came time to get in the mini-pool to drive the boat around, there was no one else present against whom to bump his boat (Zarine did not like anything energetic or exciting). At Tuon's instruction, Damodred set up another boat and got in herself.

Perrin thought the boat was great. He immediately pressed the accelerator to its maximum speed and began spinning the steering wheel from right to left, and left to right, as fast as he could. What happened next was a matter of dispute. According to Perrin, as he was trying to steer around Damodred, he felt something in the bottom of his boat catch, with the result that his boat turned sharply at top speed, throwing him clear; Damodred believed that Perrin's dangerous driving threw him clear. What was undisputed was that Perrin flew out of his boat across a short stretch of water and smashed into Damodred, pitching them both out of her boat and into the side of the BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers construct. It was also undisputed that Perrin smashed his wrist and fingers on his right hand badly, suffering multiple severe breaks that put him in the hospital for weeks.

Immediately after the accident, while still in the hospital, Perrin contacted you, a litigator living in (wherever you want to have been living, you pick). You agreed to be his attorney. You are hoping to file a single suit on Perrin's behalf in a federal court somewhere in the country suing all three of Ishamael,

<!-- page: 5 -->

Tuon, and Damodred, seeking $100,000 from each of the three defendants. Your idea is to sue Ishamael for negligently training Tuon on how to set up the BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers construct; Tuon for negligently training Damodred on how to operate the BustYourYouKnowWhatBumpers construct; and Damodred for negligently operating the construct.

## Questions

1. Would there be subject matter jurisdiction over such a suit? Why or why not?

2. Assuming (regardless of your conclusion on Question 1) that there is SMJ, is there a single state in whose federal court(s) you could pursue such a lawsuit (against all three defendants)? If so, where, and why? If not, why not? Do not consider venue in your answer.

3. Assuming (regardless of your conclusions in Questions 1 and 2) that there is SMJ in a single federal court against all three defendants, all three defendants will likely assert that Perrin was at least partially at fault for the accident. Will they be able to assert the defense of contributory negligence, or will they have to be satisfied with only comparative negligence?

## 2024-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-01-ciambotti-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## QUESTION 1

There is no SMJ under § 1331 AUJ. This is a state law cause of action (*Mottley*) with no federal issue in sight, so no *Smith-Grable*. There may be § 1332 DJ if there is complete diversity, *Strawbridge*. Perrin satisfies the AIC by seeking over $75,000 from each defendant.

Perrin's domicile is TX. He was born and raised there and worked there as an adult. His monthly flights to CA show no intent to change domiciles.

Ishamael's domicile is NV. He was born and always lived there, and he operates his business from his home there.

Tuon's domicile is NY. She grew up, attended high school, and lives with her parents there. Renting property and operating a business in CA didn't change her domicile. Although she's "looking for a different opportunity," Tuon didn't change her voter registration, driver's license, etc., as would demonstrate an intent to change domiciles.

Damodred's domicile could still be TX. She hasn't changed her voter registration, driver's license, etc., to another jurisdiction. However, her domicile might have changed when she moved to NY, "decided she liked" and wanted to stay in Brooklyn, began working in NY, and only applied to schools in NY because she wanted to stay. Damodred's move to CA was only "temporary," suggesting an intent to remain in NY. If Damodred's domicile is TX, there is no SMJ over all three defendants. Perrin is also domiciled in TX, *Strawbridge*, and supplemental jurisdiction would fail (any anchoring claim would be based on § 1332, and § 1367(b) requires complete diversity).

**Word Count: 256**

---

## QUESTION 2

All states have LAS to constitutional limits. Under FRCP 4(k)(1)(A), we analyze federal district court IPJ by reference to the state in which it sits. Assume no defendant consents, and none was tagged. No defendant has anywhere near the kind of contacts required for "at home" IPJ, unless Damodred is domiciled in TX. But NY will not work even if so, see below.

TX potentially has no GPJ over anyone, and at least no contacts with Ishamael.
NY has GPJ over Tuon and Damodred but no contacts with Ishamael.
NV has GPJ over Ishamael but no contacts with Damodred.

Only CA might have sufficient contacts over the three defendants, but this is very tenuous for Ishamael. It is likely that no federal court can exercise PJ over all three.

Regarding SIPJ, Damodred operated the boats in CA, and Perrin was injured there. However, Tuon trained Damodred from NV, and Ishamael taught Tuon in NV. Although he marketed the boats in CA, consumers could take the boats anywhere (*Volkswagen*). This case concerns her negligent training (from NV), though Tuon also "instructed" and supervised Damodred in CA.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Damodred and Tuon's contacts in CA are significant. Damodred operated the boats in CA. Tuon rented space and supervised Damodred in CA. Ishamael has much weaker contacts, as he advertised the product online in CA. (*Volkswagen*). CA is convenient for Tuon (she rents property, drives, and resides there), and Damodred (she temporarily resides there). Ishamael is not far (a two-hour drive) from Sacramento. CA is a fairly convenient forum for Perrin since he flies there monthly.

CA has strong interests in deterring tortious conduct in CA that causes injury in CA, and in deterring the marketing of dangerous products for use in CA. The judicial system has a significant interest in CA as a forum. Evidence of Damodred's conduct and Perrin's injury are in CA (e.g., witnesses like Zarine, a CA resident; hospital records; etc.). Tuon's computer is in CA, with forensic evidence of her conversation with Damodred, including how short it was. Evidence of Ishamael's negligent training would be in NV, though. As a First Restatement jurisdiction, CA would apply its own law as the LLD.

Therefore, CA might be unable to exercise PJ over Ishamael, such that no federal court can exercise PJ over all three defendants.

**Word Count: 384**

---

## QUESTION 3

This issue is substantive, and a federal court sitting in diversity applies local (state) HCOL rules, *Klaxon*. The court will ultimately apply CA law, allowing contributory negligence.

CA, TX, and NV are First Restatement states. They apply the LLD (CA), which allows contributory negligence.

As a Second Restatement state, NY presumes the LLD (CA) applies, unless the § 6 factors weigh oppositely. They do not. The most relevant factors weigh in favor of CA:

(a) The only other interested state (NV) has weaker interests than CA's (injury was in CA from conduct in both CA and NV)

(d) As the victim, Perrin can expect LLD to apply. Damodred operated the boats in CA. However, Ishamael trained Tuon from NV, and Tuon trained Damodred from NV. They likely thought NV law would apply to their conduct.

(f) The LLD (CA) is more predictable/certain/uniform than locating vague elements of conduct.

(g) It's easiest for CA to apply its own law.

**Word Count: 158**

**TOTAL WORD COUNT: 798**

## 2024-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-01-li-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## QUESTION 1

There is likely diversity jurisdiction.

There is no AUJ. Negligence is a state law claim. There is no well-pled federal cause of action, *Mottley*, nor any intertwined federal issue (*Smith-Grable*).

There is likely diversity jurisdiction. Perrin is still a Texas domiciliary. Perrin only travels to California for one week every month, not enough to manifest an intention to remain there. *Mas*.

Tuon is either a New York (likely) or California domiciliary; either way, she's diverse from Perrin.

Damodred's domicile has likely changed to New York. Though originally a Texas domiciliary, she's moved to NYC and expressed interest in buying property. While her three-month-long stays in other places didn't change her domicile, and nor did simply going to school in NYC, looking to purchase property supports an intention to stay. Plus, Damodred doesn't have a time or plan to leave.

However, if the court finds otherwise, there's no complete diversity, meaning no diversity jurisdiction. *Strawbridge*. Perrin could argue for supplemental jurisdiction, but her claim against a non-diverse defendant falls within the § 1367(b) ouster; supplemental jurisdiction isn't possible here.

Ishamael is a Nevada domiciliary. Having never incorporated his business, the relevant citizenship is his own.

The amount-in-controversy is satisfied: for each defendant, the value of the claim against them, $100k, exceeds $75k.

---

## QUESTION 2

Maybe California has personal jurisdiction over all three defendants, but not Nevada, Texas, or New York. All relevant states have a long-arm statute going to the limits of the Constitution, so federal courts can piggy-back. 4(k)(1)(A).

### *California.*

No defendant is domiciled there, so there's no general personal jurisdiction over any of them. But there could be specific jurisdiction.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Minimum contacts are likely satisfied for both Tuon and Damodred. The accident occurred in California. Tuon traveled to California, rented space there, and trained Damodred from there (though since the call was virtual, she might dispute its location as gratuitous); Damodred did the same to operate the construct. Thus, both purposefully availed themselves of California laws. Fair and reasonable factors support specific personal jurisdiction over both: it's convenient to hale both into California court, it's convenient enough for Perrin (who flies there once a month), and California has an interest in product and consumer safety within its borders.

Specific jurisdiction over Ishamael is harder. He hasn't traveled to California and he trained Tuon in Nevada. He sold the construct in Nevada with no control over where it went. *Volkswagen*. Tuon acted unilaterally taking the construct there. *Hanson*. Fair and reasonable factors: it's inconvenient for Ishamael, though convenient for Perrin.

### *New York.*

There's general jurisdiction over Tuon and Damodred (assuming the court considers her a New York domiciliary). Asserting specific jurisdiction over Ishamael is harder.

Minimum contacts: Ishamael transacted with a New York domiciliary, Tuon, gaining economic benefit. *McGee*; *International Shoe*. But Ishamael didn't target New York in his marketing, and Tuon's not the plaintiff. He insisted that Tuon come to Nevada to receive training and complete the sale; in *McGee*, by contrast, the sale was completed while the plaintiff was in California.

Fair and reasonable factors disfavor specific jurisdiction over Ishamael. Maybe New York has an interest in monitoring its residents' unsafe online transactions, but that's a leap (and privacy violation). There aren't relevant witnesses or evidence regarding Ishamael's negligence in New York.

### *Nevada.*

There's general jurisdiction over Ishamael and likely specific jurisdiction over Tuon, who purposefully availed herself of Nevada laws by traveling there to take possession of the construct. But fair and reasonable factors might counsel against specific jurisdiction: though she can get there, evidence of her training of Damodred would be in California, not Nevada, and it could be inconvenient for Perrin. Nevada likely couldn't assert personal jurisdiction over Damodred, who's never been, so there are no minimum contacts.

### *Texas.*

A lawsuit isn't possible here. Two of the three defendants have never been, and Texas has no connections with the litigation subject matter.

---

## QUESTION 3

<!-- page: 3 -->

California law likely applies, enabling a contributory negligence defense.

The question of available defenses is substantive, not procedural, so we need an HCOL analysis. Federal courts apply the HCOL rules of the state in which they sit (*Klaxon*).

California, Texas, and Nevada: First Restatement states use the law of the state where the harm/injury occurred. Perrin was injured in California. Contributory negligence applies.

If the suit is heard in New York: Second Restatement states begin with California, where the injury occurred. The "center of gravity" test doesn't support overriding the 1R presumption. The injury happened in California, Perrin's relationship with Tuon/Damodred centers on California, Tuon/Damodred's guilty conduct occurred in California — California law will apply. Nevada's interest in safe user training is probably trumped by California's in regulating dangerous conduct and products within its borders.

## 2024-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-01-mestre-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

**Assignment 1 Draft Answer**

## Question 1

There likely would be SMJ.

There is no AUJ because there is no federal cause of action (*Mottley*) or embedded federal issue (*Smith*).

There may be DJ. Perrin is domiciled in Texas (once-a-month trips to California do not change one's domicile). Damodred's domicile may be Texas, where she was raised, instead of New York, since she only rented her apartment in NY and was hoping to attend a fixed-length school program. However, she did inquire into buying an apartment in NY and potentially getting a full-time job there. This may showcase an intent to stay in NY (*Mas*). Tuon is domiciled in NY, and Ishamael is domiciled in Nevada.

If Damodred is domiciled in Texas, then there is no DJ (*Strawbridge*), and the case would get dismissed or split. Perrin could try keeping Damodred in the suit by invoking SJ — since this is part of the same case or controversy, but this would not work per § 1367(b), because it would break complete diversity. If Damodred is domiciled in NY, then there is complete diversity.

Since Perrin seeks $100,000 from each defendant, which exceeds the amount-in-controversy requirement of $75,000, per § 1332, there is DJ.

---

## Question 2

All states below have a to-the-limits long-arm statute to constitutional limits, such that federal courts can piggyback off it (4(k)(1)(A)).

### NY

If Damodred is a NY domiciliary then there is GIPJ. If not, there is still sufficient minimum contacts. Damodred is likely "at-home" in NY since she spends greater than 50% of her time there (*Daimler*). Also, Damodred underwent training/her interview for the job in NY, and the issue at hand centers around her inadequate training (even though it was virtual, so the location is likely gratuitous).

The court likely lacks IPJ over Ishamael. Ishamael doesn't target New York through advertising nor have any offices there (*McIntyre*). A mere likelihood that a product will enter the forum state is insufficient (*Volkswagen*). He entered a contract with a NY domiciliary, *McGee*, but the F&R factors weigh against exercising IPJ, as NY is inconvenient for Ishmael and there likely are not

<!-- page: 2 -->

many witnesses or evidence in NY to corroborate Ishmael's negligence (though there may be witnesses in the NY bar that can attest to Damodred's lack of training). NY law will not apply.

### Nevada

A Nevada court would have GIPJ over Ishamael and potentially IPJ over Tuon, but there are likely insufficient contacts for Damodred. Tuon was in Nevada when she provided Damodred with training on operating the apparatus, but this meeting occurred virtually, making Tuon's location gratuitous. Damodred has no other connection to Nevada. The F&R factors weigh against exercising SIPJ because it is inconvenient for Damodred, who has never been, and Perrin, although NV does have an interest in regulating possible dangerous activity in its borders.

### Texas

A Texas court may have GIPJ over Damodred, but likely not sufficient minimal contacts for the others. No other defendant has any contacts at all to TX. Texas has no connection to the events of the case (harm/negligent act occurred elsewhere), other than its interest in protecting its domiciliaries (Perrin).

### California

A California court likely has IPJ over Damodred and Tuon, but not Ishamael.

Damodred worked there, purposefully availing herself of the privilege of conducting activities in California (*Hanson*). Also, California is F&R since a significant amount of evidence is there, the harm occurred there, and Tuon/Damodred/Perrin got there once so it isn't significantly inconvenient to return. CA law will apply per HCOL (see below). Additionally, California has an interest in managing negligent businesses within its forum.

However, IPJ is harder for Ishamael. He hasn't been to and doesn't advertise in California, and allegedly negligently trained Tuon in Nevada. The apparatus ending up in California after Tuon took possession (*Volkswagen*) resembles the unilateral action of a third party, which is insufficient to establish SIPJ (*Hanson*).

---

## Question 3

Regardless of what state hears the suit, California law will apply, allowing for contributory negligence.

The question of available defenses is substantive, not procedural, so we go through a HCOL analysis. The harm was felt in California (where the accident occurred). Any First Restatement state (California/Texas/Nevada) would apply California law and allow for contributory negligence.

<!-- page: 3 -->

NY, as a Second Restatement state, would likely also apply California law. We start with the presumption for California. Section 6 factors do not overcome this presumption. Significant evidence and witnesses are present in California, it is a convenient forum for the plaintiff and most defendants because they got there once, and CA has an interest in regulating potentially dangerous conduct occurring within its borders. Therefore, NY will apply CA law, allowing contributory negligence.

## 2024-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-01-reed-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## QUESTION ONE

### Arising-Under Jurisdiction

There is no arising-under jurisdiction. Breach of contract is a state law cause of action (*Mottley*), and there is no federal issue, so no *Smith-Grable*.

### Diversity Jurisdiction

Whether a court will have diversity jurisdiction depends on where the plaintiff and defendants are domiciled.

Clear cases:
- Perrin is domiciled in TX.
- Ishamael is domiciled in NV.

Less clear:

Tuon was originally a NY domiciliary. The question is whether her domicile has changed to CA now that she has rented a lot there and constructed her BYYKWB on it. *Mas* suggests not: We have no indication that Tuon intends to stay in CA indefinitely. BYYKWB is a pop-up-style bumper boats, and no other evidence suggests that Tuon plans to stay in CA.

Damodred was originally a TX domiciliary. The question is whether moving to NY, obtaining a job there, inquiring about buying an apartment, and looking at exclusively NY schools for her graduate education is enough to change her domicile to NY. If she has manifested an intent to stay in NY (*Mas*), then she is a NY domiciliary now. This is close.

Ishamael: There is complete diversity (*Strawbridge*).

AIC > 75k? Yes, 100k (§ 1332), for all defendants. So, there is DJ for Ishamael.

If Damodred is domiciled in TX, then there is no complete diversity. If Damodred is domiciled in NY, then the answer is the same as Ishamael and Tuon.

### Supplemental Jurisdiction

§ 1367(b) prevents the use of a DJ anchoring claim when the result will be a *Strawbridge* conflict (*Owen v. Kroger*), which again depends on Damodred's domicile, so § 1367(b) is not available here.

<!-- page: 2 -->

---

## QUESTION TWO

Assume no defendants consent to IPJ, nor a tag, in any jurisdiction. No defendant, except Damodred regarding NY, has anything close to systematic and continuous contacts (*Daimler*) with states other than their domiciles. For all analyses, federal courts piggy-back via 4k1a. All states have long-arms to constitutional limits.

### California?

**Specific IPJ (Tuon/Damodred)?**

Minimum contacts?
It is likely that Tuon and Damodred have sufficient contacts with CA for that court to exert PJ over them. Contacts relating subject matter, defendant, and the forum (*Kulko*) are as follows: the accident took place in CA, the state to which both of these defendants temporarily moved to (i.e., purposefully availed themselves of, *Hanson*) to make money from the BYYKWB business/job.

Fair/reasonable? (*McGee*)
Tuon is operating his business there, Damodred is working there and presumably living there for the job, so it cannot be too inconvenient a forum. It is convenient for plaintiff; though he's from TX, he does visit CA. Witnesses/evidence are in CA where the accident took place. As the situs of the accident, CA law will likely apply.

**Specific IPJ (Ishmael)?**

Minimum contacts?
This is almost certainly a "no." There is no evidence that Ishmael has ever been to CA or even knew that Tuon would take the BYYKWB to CA. However, maybe as in *McGee* Ishamel "reached in" to CA to sell his goods? But unlike that case, the money here was exchanged in NV, delivery was in NV, and it was Tuon who took the BYYKWB across state lines.

Fair/reasonable? (*McGee*)
Ishamael has no contacts with CA. That said, NV is not that far from CA. Also, it might be convenient for Perrin, for the reasons stated above. Witnesses/evidence are all in CA. And, the court will likely apply CA law.

### Nevada?

Damodred has no contacts at all there, and thus no minimal contacts, so no.

<!-- page: 3 -->

### Texas?

Tuon has no contacts at all there, so no.

### New York?

Analysis is the same as CA for Ishamael, but would be even harder because he never took actions that foreseeably resulted in a BYYKWB ending up there, so no.

---

## QUESTION THREE

A federal court sitting in diversity will apply the HCOL rules of state they are in (*Klaxon*). Whether the defendants can argue contributory negligence concerns the standard of care people owe each other in day-to-day life, so it is clearly substantive for HCOL purposes.

### California/Texas (First Restatement)

We apply *lex loci delicti*. Since the injury took place in CA, apply CA law (*Great Alabama Railroad*). Yes, contributory negligence defense is available.

### New York (Second Restatement)

We begin with a *lex* presumption, which means presuming that we will apply CA law (*Babcock*). The second step is to consider factors.

- The evidence is in CA.
- Convenience is a wash, since the court can easily apply either law.
- Contributory negligence (CA law) is more protective of NY resident (defendant) in this case than NY law, so there is really no policy reason for a NY district court to depart from applying CA law.

There is nothing here that overcomes the presumption, so apply CA law. Yes, contributory negligence defense is available.

## 2024-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

**Assignment 2 (graded)**
**Civil Procedure 2 & 7, 2024**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

**Logistics and Rules**

- Posting date: Wednesday, October 10, approximately 5pm
- Due date/time: 8am on Tuesday, October 15
- Word limit: 800 words
- Time limit: 3 hours

0. The Registrar will administer all your Assignments through the Exam4 software. You will also use Exam4 for your exams. This is a good thing; you will be the only sections who, at exam time, have previous experience with the software. Every year, some folks struggle with Exam4, just as anyone would with any new software. Please remember that I do not run Exam4 and have no idea how it works. If you have problems with Exam4, you can certainly email me with questions; doing so will be as productive as finding a nearby wall and hitting your head against it. Questions must go to the Registrar.

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.

2. Download the substantive assignment from Exam 4.

3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.

4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment.

5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response via the Exam 4 software. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.

6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.

7. With Harvard policies (including the use of computer-based text-writing tools), you may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding this assignment until your assignment is returned to you. This prohibition includes librarians.

8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.

9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

10. This assignment is graded, as described in the syllabus.

11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. The Registrar's Office has sent you information about what to use for an anonymous ID. Please follow those instructions.

**Suggestions for your answer:**

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.

<!-- page: 2 -->

- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

As of the time of the events described here, Texas had four federal districts. San Antonio was in the Western District. Ft. Worth was in the Northern District. Galveston was in the Southern District. Tyler was in the Eastern District.

California also had four federal districts. San Diego was in the Southern District. San Francisco was in the Northern District. Los Angeles was in the Central District. Sacramento was in the Eastern District.

California and Texas are both First Restatement States for purposes of horizontal choice of law.

All states relevant to this exam had the tort of products liability. As I explained in the class session on *World Wide Volkswagen*, a plaintiff whose injuries were caused by an improperly manufactured product could sue the retailer or the manufacturer for damages. In all states relevant to this exam, the plaintiff could request as damages their medical expenses as well as a sum for pain and suffering.

Two years ago, California amended its long-arm statute, which previously provided that its courts could exercise personal jurisdiction whenever permissible under the U.S. Constitution. It retained the authorization that California courts could go to the limits of the Constitution, but it now also included the following provision:

> **Products Liability:** The legislature finds that over the last several decades, California has been inundated with dangerous products that enter the state from out-of-state manufacturers. Therefore, any company that sells a defective product to a California consumer, via either direct sale or through a corporate intermediary, shall be subject to the jurisdiction of the California state courts.

Texas had a long-arm statute that goes to constitutional limits.

The Texas and California rules of Civil Procedure were identical to the federal rules in all ways with one exception. In Texas, but not in California, Rule 8 included a section 8(a)(4), which provided as follows:

> **Tex. R. Civ. P. 8(a)(4):** In addition to the foregoing, in a complaint alleging a products liability cause of action under the law of any state, the complaint shall state with particularity the alleged defect in the manufacture, design, or disclosure/warning. The complaint shall also identify, in a separate paragraph labeled "Testifying Expert," the name and contact information of at least one expert in the product's field who will testify that the product at issue was defective and that the defect caused the injury.

<!-- page: 3 -->

The Texas Legislature was responsible for the addition of Rule 8(a)(4) to the Texas Rules of Civil Procedure. The law that added this provision included the following language: "The legislature finds that the state has been inundated with meritless products liability lawsuits, and that as a result, companies in Texas are under-investing in production of consumer products, resulting in lost jobs for the Texas populace and reduced tax revenue for the state. Rule 8(a)(4) is one of the measures the legislature is adopting to address this problem."

Snow Industries, Inc. was incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Ft. Worth, Texas. Snow manufactured top-of-the-line bow and arrow sets (including quivers, sites, armguards, stabilizers, and targets), costing approximately $5000 per set, in its factory in San Antonio, Texas. It conducted no advertising and attended no trade shows. Its single-page website described the company as in the business of making archery equipment and listed its mailing address, email address, and snail mail address. The website included no way to order products online directly from Snow. Instead, Snow sold its archery sets exclusively through Capitol, Inc. which was incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Galveston, Texas. Capitol sold sports equipment of all kinds throughout all fifty states and the District of Columbia in its retail stores. It had 16 stores in California, four in each of the federal districts. It had 20 stores in Texas, five in each of the federal districts. Although it stocked items from various manufacturers for other sports, it stocked only Snow's line for archery. Since it began offering archery equipment three years ago, Capitol sold about ten Snow sets per year in each of California and Texas, with sales scattered throughout its various stores there.

Three months ago, Katniss and Peeta, who grew up and lived all their lives in San Diego, California, got engaged. To celebrate, Peeta visited the Capitol sports equipment store near their house to buy Katniss a new archery set. Katniss was overjoyed. The same day as the purchase, the two took the set out to some woods nearby to give it a try. Katniss asked Peeta to set up two targets about 50 meters apart from each other, both about 100 meters away from her. Peeta set up the first target, walked to the second, and began to set it up. Katniss, who had never in her life missed a shot by five meters, much less fifty, took careful aim at the first target and fired. The arrow spun wildly away from the target and hit Peeta in the arm. After rushing Peeta to the hospital, and while Peeta was in surgery, Katniss examined the archery set carefully. She found two problems. First, the archery set's site was badly misaligned. Second, the arrows were all warped in a way that would cause them to fly in unexpected ways. Peeta ended up needing three expensive reconstructive surgeries and a four-week hospital stay.

Peeta sued Snow in the United States District Court for the Southern District of California. His complaint stated a products liability cause of action. In paragraph one, he identified Snow (naming Snow's state of incorporation and state of principal place of business) as the manufacturer of the archery set and himself a California domiciliary. In paragraph two he described the facts listed in the previous two paragraphs with particularity. In paragraph three, he alleged that the defective site and the warped arrows caused his injuries. In paragraph four, he listed his medical expenses, which totaled $350,000. In paragraph five, he asked the court to award him $350,000 for his medical expenses and $50,000 of pain and suffering. There were no other paragraphs in the complaint.

You are an associate in the law firm that represented Snow. The partner told you that they did not relish the prospect of trying this case to a California jury. The partner asked whether there is some way for Snow to move the litigation to a location with a non-California jury pool, especially if doing so would allow Snow to take advantage of Tex. R. Civ. P. 8(a)(4) in its defense. Is there a way that might work to do

<!-- page: 4 -->

as the partner suggests? If so, what is that way, and what legal issues would you encounter? Discuss fully all issues fairly presented, even if you think that one of them would dispose of the matter.

## 2024-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-02-chu-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Assignment 2

Snow can't dismiss the case based on a lack of SMJ since there is DJ: Peta is domiciled in CA, while Snow was incorporated in DE with its PPoB in TX. The AIC is $400k — greater than $75k.

Snow can force transferring the case if S.D. Cal. is unhappy. Snow could argue for a lack of IPJ. CA has a long-arm statute that goes to constitutional limits (4(k)(1)(a)).

- **GIPJ**
  - S.D. Cal likely has no GIPJ over Snow. Snow is really at home in DE/TX. For *Daimler* at home, Snow has too limited a fraction of business in CA to make it unique — well below 50%.
- **SIPJ**
  - There is an argument for a lack of minimum contacts in CA. Snow conducted sales exclusively through an intermediary, which makes minimal contacts a tough call (*McIntyre*). Peta might argue that Snow has more than a single sale in CA per year (*McIntyre* Breyer), but it is unclear whether 10 sales are sufficient.
  - On fairness and reasonableness, CA is convenient to Peta, but TX is more convenient to Snow. Snow's PPoB and factory are in TX, and it has no factory or office in CA. Evidence is split — the injury happened in CA, but the manufacturing defects likely took place in Snow's factory in TX. CA has asserted an interest in protecting its residents from out-of-state dangerous products, while TX has asserted an interest in protecting its businesses from meritless product liability lawsuits (see the CA and TX Legislatures). Nevertheless, since both CA and TX are R1 states, CA substantive law will apply.

Venue is proper under § 1391(b)(2) since the product was bought and sold in S.D. Cal., and the injury happened in S.D. Cal.

If Snow can force a transfer, it would want to transfer to N.D. Tex. or W.D. Tex. Snow can also transfer to DE, where GIPJ exists, and § 1391(b)(1) venue is proper, but there is no benefit in doing so since none of the *Gilbert* factors point to DE. Snow is at home in N.D. Tex. (PPoB), and venue is proper under § 1391(b)(1). W.D. Tex. is likely happy too. For IPJ, Snow's products are all produced in W.D. Tex., so there's an argument for *Daimler* at home. If not, there are likely minimum contacts since TX's long arm goes to constitutional limits (4(k)(1)(a)), and Snow purposefully availed itself of the privilege of conducting activities within W.D. Tex. by manufacturing there. Venue is likely proper under § 1391(b)(1) (if Snow is *Daimler* at home in W.D. Tex.) or § 1391(b)(2).

Even if S.D. Cal is happy, Snow may still transfer if the *Gilbert* factors outweigh the presumption for Peta's choice of forum. The *Gilbert* factors are unlikely strong enough to defeat that presumption. For private interest, evidence and witnesses are likely split between CA and TX (see above, IPJ discussion). For public interest, jury duty burden is also split, as the case is related to both CA and TX: the plaintiff is a CA resident, whereas the defendant is a TX business. Both CA and TX have an asserted interest in this type of cases (see above, IPJ discussion).

If Snow can transfer the case to TX, it will want the court to apply TX pleading laws to take advantage of TRCP 8(a)(4). A federal court sitting in diversity uses HCOL rules of its state (*Klaxon*). If S.D. Cal. is happy, the court will use CA HCOL rules, which results in CA procedural requirements (*Ferens*). Because most things arguably procedural are usually procedural (e.g., SoL), CA HCOL rules would mean applying CA procedures and no TRCP 8(a)(4). If S.D. Cal. is unhappy, the court will use TX HCOL rules, resulting in TX procedures, including TRCP 8(a)(4). TRCP 8(a)(4) imposes a requirement absent in federal law, so we need an *Erie* analysis.

<!-- page: 2 -->

- **York:** Peta's complaint does not comply with TRCP 8(a)(4). If federal law applies, the case will survive. If TX law applies, the case will be dismissed. Use state law.
- **Hanna (Scalia):** FRCP is valid (§ 2072; *Sibbach*) because it "really regulates procedure." FRCP's plain text covers pleading requirements and requires only a "short, plain statement." Use federal law.
- **Hanna (Ginsburg):** FRCP is valid, see Scalia. However, Ginsburg is willing to creatively interpret the FRCP: federal law is silent on whether plaintiffs need to include the Testifying Expert section and merely sets the minimum, so there is no conflict between federal law and state law. Use state law.
- **Harlan:** While it could be argued that applying TRCP 8(a)(4) only affects lawyer conduct (what to put on paper), it likely affects investment decisions as well (as the TX Legislature suggests), which is primary conduct. Use state law.

## 2024-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-02-elshiekh-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## SMJ

- No SMJ objection since there's DJ: Snow (DE/TX); Peeta (CA); AIC: $400k > $75k.

## PJ

- **GPJ:**
  - There likely isn't GPJ over Snow since Snow is actually at home in DE/TX, not CA.
  - Snow is not "essentially at home" (*Daimler*) since less than 50% of Snow's business is done in CA.
- **SPJ:**
  - CA's long-arm statute with 4(k)(1)(a), shows it welcomes such suits, but there likely isn't minimum contacts (*International Shoe*).
    - Snow didn't specifically target CA/purposefully avail itself (e.g., did not advertise in CA). Snow used Capitol as an intermediary (*McIntyre*), which makes SPJ even harder.
      - Under Breyer's concurrence in *McIntyre*, it's clear that one sale is not enough. Here, there is more than one sale — there's 10, but it's unclear whether 10 sales are enough.
  - Fair/reasonable (*McGee*)
    - Interest of judicial system: This likely points to CA where any evidence from the accident is and likely where any witnesses are. However, there may also be evidence at the manufacturing site in TX.
    - Forum's interests: CA's long-arm statute specifically includes such suits, which indicates CA's strong interest, but TX has an interest in protecting its businesses from meritless product liability suits.
    - CA is more convenient for Peeta, but TX is likely more convenient for Snow since its PPOB and factory are there. Snow doesn't have an office in CA. However, Snow's products are sold in CA, but through an intermediary.
    - HCOL likely points to CA since CA is a first statement state and CA is where the blood is.

## Venue

- Venue is proper in district where substantial events giving rise to claim occurred (§ 1391(b)(2)). Since the accident happened in SD, venue is proper.

## Happy Court (venue and PJ)

- If no PJ (see above), then SDCA not happy court and must transfer.
  - Snow can transfer to NDTX because Snow's PPOB is in TX, so there's GPJ and venue is proper under § 1391(b)(1).
  - Snow can transfer to WDTX. Venue is proper under § 1391(b)(2) since potentially substantial events occurred there since manufacturing happened in San Antonio. There's SPJ because of CA's long-arm statute (4(k)(1)(a)). There's likely minimum contacts (*International Shoe*) since Snow purposefully availed self of San Antonio by having manufacturing site located there.

<!-- page: 2 -->

  - The court could transfer to DE since there's GPJ (Snow is really at-home in DE). Venue is proper under § 1391(b)(1) since Snow is incorporated in DE. However, the *Gulf Oil* factors don't point to DE, so a court wouldn't transfer to DE.
- If there's PJ, then SDCA is a happy court, but Snow **may** still transfer if in the interest of justice.
  - There's a presumption in favor of plaintiff's choice of forum (*Piper*).
  - *Gulf Oil* Factors
    - Private:
      - Sources of proof is likely in both CA, the site of the accident, and TX, the manufacturing site where the archery set likely was misaligned.
      - Any witnesses to the accident are likely in CA.
    - Public:
      - CA expressed local interest in such cases by adding products liability provision.
      - Relation of events to geographic location of court: The site of the accident is in CA, but manufacturing error happened in TX.
  - *Gulf Oil* factors seem to point slightly more to CA, so likely not enough to overcome presumption, making transfer unlikely.

## Transfer/TRCP 8(a)(4) defense

- If Snow can transfer to NDTX or WDTX, then can *maybe* use TRCP 8(a)(4) defense. Snow can get the defense if SDCA is an unhappy court and TRCP is procedural for HCOL.
- If transfer to NDTX/WDTX:
  - TRCP likely is procedural for HCOL because most things arguably procedural are procedural for HCOL. Therefore, apply the law of forum, so TX courts would apply defense.
  - If SDCA was a happy court, then SDCA HCOL rules follow to NDTX/WDTX, so no defense.
  - If SDCA was an unhappy court, then WDTX/NDTX HCOL rules govern.
- TRCP conflicts with federal law, leading to an *Erie* problem.
  - **York:** Peeta doesn't comply with TRCP. If TX law applies, case is dismissed. If federal law applies, case survives. Use **state** law.
  - **Hanna (Scalia):**
    - Valid? FRCP is valid because it really regulates procedure (*Sibbach*).
    - Applicable? Based on plain textual meaning, FRCP addresses pleading standards.
    - Use **federal** law.
  - **Hanna (Ginsburg):**
    - Valid? Same as Scalia.
    - Applicable?

<!-- page: 3 -->

      - Federal Rules are just minimum requirements and TX adds additional requirements.
      - Here, FRCP are silent on additional requirements for products liability, so seems like not applicable → use **state** law.
  - **Harlan:**
    - TX thinks companies are under-investing in production and thinks TRCP 8(a)(4) will change companies' behavior, so since affects primary conduct of parties, use **state** law.
- Erie tests seem to point toward applying state law, so Snow should try to transfer to NDTX/WDTX to get defense.

## 2024-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-02-oneil-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

If Snow can transfer to NDTX or WDTX, he may be able to use TRCP 8(a)(4).

Snow can't get SDCA to dismiss for lack of SMJ because there is DJ (Snow's incorporated in DE with its principal place of business in TX; Peeta is domiciled in CA; AIC = $400k > $75k).

Maybe Snow can force a transfer by objecting to IPJ. CA's long-arm goes to constitutional limits and explicitly welcomes these suits; per FRCP 4(k)(1)(A), federal jurisdiction piggybacks off CA's long-arm.

i. **General:** Snow is only actually at home in DE/TX. And Snow is not essentially at home (*Daimler*) in CA: Snow lacks 'substantial, continuous, and systemic contacts' with CA; it doesn't do anything close to >50% of its business there.

ii. **Specific:** Because Snow used an intermediary and didn't target any conduct at CA (e.g., via ads/website targeting), it's unlikely to qualify under the *McIntyre* plurality. Under Breyer's concurrence, a single sale is insufficient, but perhaps 10 would be sufficient. It'll be hard to find minimal contacts (*International Shoe*) due to lack of targeting and the use of a distributor. Fairness and reasonableness factors (*McGee*) are fairly pro-SDCA (the plaintiff is in SDCA, though the evidence is somewhat evenly split between the scene of the accident in SDCA and the manufacturing site in WDTX; CA substantive law will apply (CA is an R1 HCOL state (as is TX)). But ultimately, SIPJ is probably not satisfied due to lack of minimal contacts.

Snow probably can't force a transfer via a venue objection. Per § 1391(b)(2), venue is proper in the district where a substantial part of the events giving rise to the claim occurred — here, that's SDCA, where the accident happened (or maybe WDTX, where the set was manufactured).

So, it's not clear that SDCA is happy (venue + IPJ). If not, SDCA must transfer — likely to NDTX, where GIPJ exists and venue is proper under § 1391(b)(1) as Snow's PPOB. [Or perhaps WDTX, where venue is arguably proper under § 1391(b)(2), see above, and GIPJ is equally satisfied. It could theoretically transfer to DE, where venue is proper per § 1391(b)(1) as Snow's incorporation state and GIPJ exists for the same reason, but none of the *Gulf Oil* factors point there, so that's highly unlikely.]

Even if SDCA is happy, it may transfer anyway if doing so is in the interest of justice (*Gulf Oil/Piper*). So, presume plaintiff's choice (SDCA) and consider public/private interest factors, which here net out or lean slightly toward CA, so transfer is unlikely: For private interest, see *McGee* fair and reasonableness analysis above, pointing to SDCA; for public interest, CA and TX courts are similarly congested, though CA has demonstrated an interest in adjudicating these types of cases.

**If Snow effectuates a transfer, can he take advantage of TRCP 8(a)(4)?**

If SDCA was *happy*, its COL rules follow to NDTX or WDTX and the TX court will use CA procedural law (*Ferens*). If SDCA was *unhappy*, WDTX/NDTX would apply their own COL rules, and would apply TX procedural law.

<!-- page: 2 -->

TRCP 8(a)(4) is probably procedural, since most things arguably procedural are treated as procedural for HCOL. So, if SDCA was unhappy and TRCP 8(a)(4) is indeed procedural, TX courts will apply it. This sets up a conflict with federal law → *Erie*!

i. Under the *York* 'outcome determinative' test, Peeta's case will be dismissed if it doesn't comply with TRCP 8(a)(4). So, the outcome hinges on the application of TRCP 8(a)(4). Use **state** law.

ii. Under the *Hanna* majority test (Scalia version), FRCP 8 is valid under the Rules Enabling Act. (Per *Sibbach*: something is valid if it really regulates procedure, which Federal Rules do.) It's also applicable: The plain language of FRCP 8 speaks to pleading standards, which are at issue. Use **federal** law.

iii. Under *Hanna* majority test (Ginsburg version), FRCP 8 is valid (per the same analysis as Scalia). But it's arguably inapplicable. FRCP 8 sets a floor; TRCP 8(a)(4) adds more requirement atop the floor. FRCP 8 is silent as to whether products liability suits require heightened pleading standards, so it's arguably not applicable. Use **state** law.

iv. Under the Harlan *Hanna* concurrence 'primary conduct' test, applying TRCP 8(a)(4) might result in companies altering products manufacturing on the front end. Texas clearly thinks it does, since it's the stated purpose of the rule. Use **state** law.

Snow should request a transfer to NDTX or, in the alternative, WDTX.

## 2024-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-02-seabrooks-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Snow Can't Dismiss for Lack of SMJ

This suit has DJ. Snow is incorporated in Delaware, with its PPoB/nerve center (*Hertz*) in Texas. Peeta is a domiciliary of California. (*Strawbridge*). AIC is sufficient ($400,000 > $75,000).

## Snow May Object to IPJ

Three (maybe 4) ways to get GIPJ. None are applicable to Snow.

1. Snow is not at home in California (see analysis above).
2. Snow is not "essentially at home" (*Daimler*) because significantly less than 50% of Snow business is conducted in CA.
3. No evidence Snow consented to suit in CA.
4. There is no evidence Snow was tagged in CA.

### SIPJ is close.

California's extent-of-the-Constitution longarm, with FRCP 4(k)(1)(A), means that it welcomes these tort suits, but minimum contacts may be lacking under *International Shoe* (e.g., no advertising in CA, single-page website, no trade shows). Moreover, Snow should emphasize that Capitol sells goods into CA, not Snow directly. An intermediary makes it harder to find SIPJ (*McIntyre*). Especially true absent evidence that Snow directed its actions at California (*Calder*). The Breyer *McIntyre* concurrence leaves *some* room for an argument regarding SIPJ (i.e., how much business = a lot of business; 10 units/year cuts it close), but ultimately, the lack of contacts and purposeful availment probably overcomes.

The fair and reasonableness factors are also close. Though the defendant's contacts are not extensive, traveling to California is probably not too inconvenient and Snow products are sold there. Moreover, California is interested in providing justice to its plaintiffs and regulating tortious conduct within its jurisdiction. Evidence of the harm is in California, though evidence of the negligent manufacturing is probably in Texas.

## Venue is Proper

Venue is proper under § 1391(b)(2) because the sale and injury occurred there.

If Snow can win the IPJ argument, then SDCA is unhappy. Transfer is mandatory (*Goldlawr*). NDTX would be a happy venue because that is where Snow is PPoB, providing GIPJ and proper venue (§ 1391(b)(1)). Maybe WDTX (also GIPJ, because it's anywhere in TX) since that is where the bow is manufactured — therefore substantial portion of events (§ 1391(b)(2)). If the S.D. Cal. is happy, the court would consider if it *should* transfer. Snow must overcome a presumption for plaintiff's choice, but if transfer is in the interest of justice (*Gulf Oil Corp*), the court may allow it. Access to evidence split between SDCA, NDTX and WDTX. The premise of the accident is SDCA. One could argue that the people of Texas have stronger interest in regulating manufacturing in Texas. Conversely, a jury in CA may have strong interest in protecting plaintiffs. Congestion is equally problematic in either district. Access to witnesses and Peeta's hospitalization probably weigh against transferring to CA.

<!-- page: 2 -->

**Assuming Snow is able to transfer, Tex. R. Civ. P. 8(a)(4) would probably apply.**

Ultimately, TRCP is procedural, so the choice of law analysis does not matter much. But if happy court to happy court, California's choice of law rules would follow to Texas. If unhappy to happy, then Texas's choice of law rules would apply. Again, either way, TRCP would apply.

*Erie* analysis is necessary to resolve conflict between Texas pleading rules and federal pleading rules.

- ***York*:** The choice of whether to apply TRCP is outcome-determinative. If TRCP applies, Peeta's suit will likely get dismissed (see analysis below). Therefore, use **state** law.

- ***Harlan* Primary Conduct:** The stated purpose of Texas's law is to shield manufacturers from liability. This alters the conduct of both plaintiffs and manufacturers by deterring frivolous lawsuits and theoretically allowing Texas businesses to invest in the production of consumer products. Use **state** law.

- **Hanna Majority:** FRCP is valid because of the Rules Enabling Act. It does not abridge any substantive right (*Sibbach*). Therefore, it really does regulate procedure. Read with eye towards the FRCP's plain meaning, FRCP 8 addresses the issue regarding pleading procedure. Therefore, apply **federal** law.

- **Hanna (Ginsburg):** Validity analysis stays the same, but applicability can be read more narrowly. Here, the FRCP sets a minimum pleading standard. Texas's law imposes more limits, but does not directly conflict with the FRCP (*Walker*). Therefore, may use **state** law.

Snow should move to dismiss under 12(b)(6) (or state equivalent). Though Peeta does not have a "Testifying Expert." Therefore, the complaint can be dismissed under TRCP 8.

# ASSIGNMENT 2024-assignment-03 — Assignment 3 (graded) — Civil Procedure 2 & 7, 2024
