## 2024-assignment-03 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

**Assignment 3 (graded)**
**Civil Procedure 2 & 7, 2024**
**Professor Jim Greiner**

**Logistics and Rules**

- Posting date: November 7, between 3:30 and 5
- Due date/time: 8am on Tuesday, Oct 12
- Word limit: 900 words
- Time limit: 3 hours

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.

2. Download the substantive assignment from Exam 4.

3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.

4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment.

5. As suggested by #4, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response via the Exam 4 software. Please notify either the Dean of Students' Office or me BY NOVEMBER 6, AT 5PM if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response or if you think you may need an accommodation.

6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.

7. Within Harvard policies (including the use of computer-based text-writing tools), you may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer. You may not, however, communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding the substance of this assignment until your assignment is returned to you. This prohibition includes librarians. Keep logistical communications to only what is required.

8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.

9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper, other than an accommodation arranged through the Dean of Students office (so that the grading can remain anonymous). This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

10. This assignment is graded, as described in the syllabus.

11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. The Registrar's Office has sent you information about what to use for an anonymous ID. Please follow those instructions.

12. Any response uploaded after the due date/time of this assignment will receive two points off per fifteen minutes that it is late. For example, an assignment uploaded 16 minutes after the due date and time will receive four points off for lateness.

**Suggestions for your answer:**

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in

<!-- page: 2 -->

*International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under the circumstances."

---

Assume the following, which is totally fictional (including the law part).

The federal rules of civil procedure authorize the pre-judgment seizure of an item under the following circumstances:

- The court has subject matter jurisdiction over the plaintiff's lawsuit, and the plaintiff must file a lawsuit in a reasonable time after the seizure of the item;
- The object is within the geographic boundaries of the seizing district court; and
- The plaintiff asserts, and supports with competent evidence, an ownership interest in the item to be seized.
- In the event that the court orders a seizure, it shall hold a hearing on the topic of whether to continue to exercise dominion over the item within 60 days of the seizure.

All states relevant to this assignment have long-arm statutes going to constitutional limits. All states relevant to this assignment have the following cause of action:

- **Theft:** The elements of this tort are the wrongful removal of control over an object from those who should rightfully have it. The tort is complete where the object was when it was removed from the control of those who should have it (under either the laws of property or a duly executed contract assigning such rights).

The affidavit (a sworn witness statement as to the testimony they would give in court if called upon to do so) of Astrid in substance is as follows:

> I am a domiciliary of Nevada, where I have lived and worked all my life. I collaborated with Ruffnut, a domiciliary of Texas, and Fishlegs, a domiciliary of Maine, on the creation of digital artwork. I designed the lines of our animation; Ruffnut provided the coloring scheme; and Fishlegs worked the movement of the figures. To assure the uniqueness of our designs, Ruffnut and I created a unique programming language, a combination of C and Python, as well as a unique compiler. The language and the compiler depend on each other; neither can function without the other. The skeletal commands for the language were placed on my personal servers, located in Nevada. The skeletal commands for the compiler lived on unique servers in Oklahoma under Ruffnut's control. The code for our artwork was ordinarily saved on servers in Maine under the control of Fishlegs. To reiterate, the code for the artwork was useless without the skeletal commands of our unique programming language or without the compiler commands.
>
> In 2023, our tripartite partnership produced approximately 300 animations, each lasting on average 25 minutes, with length ranging from 10 minutes to 65 minutes. During the summer of 2023, with the permission of Ruffnut and Fishlegs, I began traveling twice a month to exhibit our work to movie producers in various locations. The work was well-received. I obtained four offers, each over $750,000, to purchase our existing portfolio, with a suggestion of future contracts to pay $1000 per minute of animation produced.
>
> In December of 2023, Ruffnut, Fishlegs, and I established the "Hiccup, Inc." in Delaware. Each of us individually held 1/3 of the voting stock. The three of us were the only members of the Board of Directors, and we served as co-Chief Executive Officers. The law firm of Snotlout &

<!-- page: 3 -->

> Gobber, LLC, from its only office in Boston, MA, produced all the legal documents. In addition to the documents needed for the establishment of Hiccup, Inc., Ruffnut, Fishlegs, and I each executed a contract that (i) assigned all rights and interests in any and all of the creative content in which we all three contributed to Hiccup, Inc.,[^1] and (ii) promised and warranted to each other that none of us would make any attempt to exploit the content assigned to Hiccup except through Hiccup, Inc. After the incorporation of Hiccup, the three of us continued to meet via Zoom videoconferencing software to administer Hiccup, Inc. and to create new content for the corporation.
>
> In March of 2024, Ruffnut, Fishlegs, and I unanimously voted during a meeting of the Hiccup Board to authorize me to pursue a sale of the existing Hiccup inventory for no less than $1.25 million. I began meeting with potential buyers.
>
> In early September of 2024, I logged onto my servers in Nevada to make final changes to the programming language. I discovered several things. First, I could no longer find the raw data that constituted the guts of my specialized programming language. It was gone from my servers. Second, I could not log into Ruffnut's Oklahoma servers to access the compiler, nor could I log into Fishlegs' Maine servers to access the artwork commands. Third, I could contact Ruffnut but not Fishlegs. I discovered the latter when I emailed, texted, and used other social media to contact Ruffnut and Fishlegs for an emergency meeting of the Hiccup Board that very day. Ruffnut responded immediately that she, similarly, had been unable to access her own Oklahoma servers, my Nevada servers, or Fishlegs' Maine servers. In other words, neither she nor I was able to access any of the three essential components of our animation (recalling that no two of them could produce anything without the third). Fishlegs did not show at this meeting, nor did she respond to further attempts to contact her for subsequent Board meetings two and three weeks after my initial discovery. I have tried all ways of contacting Fishlegs that I know, as has Ruffnut, to no avail. Neither Ruffnut nor I have a snail mail/physical address for Fishlegs, so we have only ever used and can now use only electronic means of communication. Thus far, none of our communication efforts has generated a notice that it did not get through, but there has been no response from Fishlegs.
>
> Ruffnut and I each hired independent forensic investigation firms to assess what happened to our servers and to our access to other protocols. Each of the investigators concluded that the server they examined had been hacked to remove all its contents and to scramble its access protocols. The forensic investigators were able to restore my access to Ruffnut's servers, and Ruffnut's to mine, but could do nothing to restore either of our access to Fishleg's Maine servers. In addition, each of the forensic investigation firms were able to determine that the attack that led to the hack, the removal of all data from our servers, and the scrambling of the access protocols came from somewhere in the New England states of New Hampshire, Massachusetts, or Maine. They could make no further determination.
>
> On the basis of these facts, Ruffnut and I believe that Fishlegs hacked our servers, stole the components needed to make our artwork complete, scrambled our access, and broke off communication with us.
>
> I swear under penalty of perjury that the foregoing is true and factual to the best of my knowledge, information, and belief. Dated Oct 1, 2024.

[^1]: You may assume that this contract validly and conclusively establishes ownership over the animations, including their component parts (the pieces previously held separately by Astrid, Ruffnut, and Fishlegs), in the corporation Hiccup, Inc.

<!-- page: 4 -->

Ruffnut's Affidavit reads in substance as follows:

> I have reviewed the Affidavit of Astrid dated October 1, 2024. Rather than repeat everything, I will say simply that all there is true as reported. I swear under penalty of perjury that the foregoing is true and factual to the best of my knowledge, information, and belief. Dated October 2, 2024.

On October 2, Ruffnut and Astrid gave notice of an emergency meeting to occur the next day of the Hiccup Board to assess litigation opportunities. At the October 3 meeting, Ruffnut and Astrid showed up at the meeting, Fishlegs did not attend. Ruffnut and Astrid, representing two-thirds of the voting power of Hiccup, Inc., voting that the corporation would pursue all litigation remedies to regain control of the animation and to pursue its sale.[^2] The law firm of Snotlout and Gobber produced a corresponding complaint. On October 7, Hiccup, Inc. filed a lawsuit against Fishlegs in the United States District Court for the District of Delaware. The lawsuit stated the cause of action as theft. The complaint stated the facts as outlined in Astrid's affidavit, above. Filed with the complaint were (i) the affidavits of Astrid and Ruffnut, (ii) copies of the forensic investigative reports, (iii) copies of social media, text, and email exchanges between either Astrid or Ruffnut on the one hand and Fishlegs on the other from January to August, 2024, in which Fishlegs participated, (iv) copies of the minutes of the Board meetings of Hiccup, Inc. detailing the votes to assign all property interests in artwork to Hiccup, as well as to pursue the lawsuit, and (v) a copy of the contract signed and notarized by Astrid, Ruffnut, and Fishlegs agreeing that sole ownership of any programming or code associated with the artwork would be owned by Hiccup, and that financial exploitation of such material would occur through Hiccup, Inc. only.

On the same day, Hiccup moved the court for two orders: (i) that the court immediately enter an order of seizure under the power/procedure outlined at the top of this assignment, and that the order issue the same day, before notice went to Fishlegs, seizing all material associated with the artwork, pending further litigation, and (ii) allowing service of process on Fishlegs via electronic means only.

**Question 1:** Will the court grant either the motion to seize the animation or the motion to allow electronic service, or both, or neither? Explain fully, assessing all issues presented even if you think one of them would be dispositive. If the court were to grant the first motion and Fishlegs, upon receiving notice of the seizure order and the lawsuit, were to oppose the seizure order, what arguments and/or defenses could Fishlegs assert? Explain (either as part of your original discussion or in a separate section) whether the court should vacate or alter its order in light of Fishleg's arguments or defenses.

**Question 2:** Hiccup wants to avoid the delay associated with a jury trial. What would you advise it to do to minimize the chances that the district judge sits a jury?

Suppose that, after a short time, Hiccup were to move for summary judgment. Fishlegs files an opposition to the motion agreeing that no further discovery is necessary but attaching the following affidavit.

> Affidavit of Fishlegs, dated October 21, 2024. I have reviewed the affidavits of Astrid and Ruffnut. I did not hack into their servers or otherwise steal their code. My access to all components of our software and artwork has continued uninterrupted. I stopped responding to Astrid and Ruffnut because they accused me falsely of theft. I oppose the seizure of our material by any court. The property should remain as is, under the control of Hiccup, not the court, and to be

[^2]: You may assume that this vote is valid, and that the Hiccup, Inc. does in fact under its corporate procedures and bylaws decide to sue Fishlegs.

<!-- page: 5 -->

> administered according to standard corporate rules. If Ruffnut and Astrid cannot take care of their own servers, that's their problem. I intend to continue according to our original agreement by allowing Astrid a short time to close one of the deals she negotiated for a lucrative sale of our material. If Astrid is unable in that time to complete the deal, I will declare it null and void, complete the deal myself, provide the buyer with the necessary coding and material in accordance with this agreement, obtain the proceeds, pay myself a reasonable fee for the unanticipated time I will have expended, and provide the rest to Hiccup in accordance with our agreement. I will not communicate with people who are accusing me of theft except in the context of Hiccup Board meetings in the presence of neutral third-party witnesses, such as representatives of the law firm of Snotlout & Gobber.

**Question 3:** How should the court analyze and rule on the motion for summary judgment? Explain your answer.

## 2024-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-03-bottinelli-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

### Seizure

Working through the FRCP requirements, the court has SMJ through DJ (Fishlegs domiciled in Maine and Hiccup incorporated in Delaware/no facts suggesting Hiccup's PPOB is Maine to break complete diversity (see *Hertz*), and AIC well above 75k, assuming sale offers are valid proxy). Hiccup's lawsuit was already filed, so first requirement is met. The copies of the minutes and contract should be sufficient to establish Hiccup's ownership interest, asserted in Astrid's affidavit, for the purpose of requirement three.

The more complicated requirements for the court are two and four. First, it is unclear if the artwork materials are located within the geographic boundaries of the District of Delaware. The court must have power over the artwork to seize it, but here, power can't follow from GIPJ over Fishlegs because GIPJ doesn't exist over her in Delaware (and unclear if artwork is properly considered her property). All *Shaffer* concerns are raised, leading to a QIR analysis, even though the pre-judgment seizure wouldn't be used to establish jurisdiction over Fishlegs. If *Shaffer* preserved old QIR rule, court will likely consider artwork to be within Delaware even though location is ambiguous (given that the servers for the code/commands are on servers in three different states). Like in *Balk*, where the court located debt with the debtor, perhaps the court will locate the artwork in the only place where all requisite components are authorized to be exploited (only in Delaware). If Fishlegs did steal the artwork, each requisite component could be used by her in Maine, but if the court considers Maine as a location, they may choose not to effectively reward theft by refusing to find QIR in Delaware. This also aligns with the tort itself, which could find the artwork was in Delaware when "removed" from Hiccup's control.

If *Shaffer* extended a *Shoe* analysis, QIR may still be found. The FRCP authorizes seizure almost like a long-arm, Fishlegs purposely availed herself of Delaware more than the stock owner in *Shaffer* by choosing to participate in an incorporation in DE, benefiting from its corp-friendly rules. The artwork is the subject of the litigation and therefore bears a strong relationship, and there is some link between Fishlegs' ownership interest, given that she assumedly will profit from Hiccup's work/pending sale, and the cause of action for theft, which (if true) let her profit far beyond her valid ownership of 1/3 the voting stock.

As such, the court could seize, but the FRCP demands a hearing within 60 days, where Fishlegs could raise PDP concerns about the pre-hearing seizure as compared to post-lawsuit seizure (*Eldridge*). She may be unsuccessful. First, Hiccup's private interests outweigh Fishlegs': all rights/interests in the artwork are vested only in Hiccup, by a contract to which Fishlegs was a party. If Fishlegs did not steal the artwork, her private

<!-- page: 2 -->

interests as CEO/voter weigh in favor of seizure to prevent further destruction while legal issues are sorted. If she did steal it, court will find seizure *more* compelling as any extra private interest there is illicit, and livelihood is not at stake for Fishlegs more than Astrid and Ruffnut. Second, the public interest weighs in favor of less litigation/resources and restoring rightful property interests; Fishlegs could mess with artwork further, so seizure is urgent. Third, though the risk of erroneous seizure is mitigated by the post-seizure hearing, the underlying tort suit requires tons of evidence. Seizure should turn on whether Fishlegs' stole the artwork, which will not be determined until the case is fully decided. Because of this, the court should **alter** the seizure motion to require a bond to protect Fishlegs and allow a counterbond so Fishlegs can repossess her interest (if that comports with how corporate ownerships function).

### Electronic Notice

Actual notice is not required to satisfy PDP so long as a reasonably calculated attempt was made (*Mullane*). Here, communication has only occurred electronically. Astrid and Ruffnut do not have a physical address for Fishlegs and don't have a duty to search for an unknown address (*Mullane*) or undergo another special heroic effort to ensure receipt (*Dusenberry*). If any notice-related communications had failed to send/bounced back, Fishlegs might have some defense (*Flowers*), but on these facts, electronic notice is sufficient.

---

## Question 2

Hiccup can control seating a jury by staying on the equity side of the law/equity divide. To do so, Hiccup must avoid seeking damages (remedies at law) and instead demand an injunction (such as forcing Fishlegs to return artwork to pre-theft state/continue duties or desist from theft) and/or perhaps restitution (disgorging any ill-gotten gains that may somehow have accumulated when Hiccup's possession was disrupted).

---

## Question 3

The court should not decide this case on SJ. Hiccup is the moving party and bears the burden of persuasion at trial as plaintiff. Hiccup might have produced evidence compelling a rational jury to find that Fishlegs stole the artwork (forensic reports, etc.), so the burden of production might shift to Fishlegs. Fishlegs' affidavit is part of the SJ record and rebuts key facts from Hiccup's filing, including whether a theft took place at all. The forensic reports are far from factually conclusive enough to preclude Fishlegs' version of events. Thus, drawing all reasonable inferences for Fishlegs (as the nonmoving party), there is a genuine issue of material fact that cannot be fairly settled based on the SJ record alone. As such, the SJ motion will be denied.

## 2024-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-03-danial-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

**There is D-J:** Fishlegs is ME domiciled; Hiccup is DE incorporated and has no physical PPoB, but there's no reason to anticipate a ME PPoB. AIC is alleged (albeit indirectly) to be over $1.25mil based on offers to purchase (> $75k) assigning value to stolen property.

If property is located in DE, might assert seizure on **QIRJ** basis. This presents *Shaffer* issue of seizing DE property and using it to drag defendant into court absent personal jurisdiction. If we do not take *Shaffer* seriously, then locus of property in DE would be enough. But where is the digital artwork located? Since artwork is incorporeal, this is a *Harris*-style problem. Here the code is spread across various states. Court should take functional approach and say digital artwork is located at the domicile of its owner (Hiccup), here DE. This brings certainty to parties and minimizes arbitrary location. Note that since Hiccup is incorporated in Delaware and has no physical PPoB, we do not have a concern of competing possible locations for the artwork based on owner domicile.

If we take *Shaffer* holding seriously, then QIRJ is dead and we need to establish IPJ with Fishlegs. Under 4K(1)(A), court looks to State of DE's jurisdiction. Fishlegs is ME-domiciliary, so absent consent, waiver or in-state tag (none of which are likely here), DE can't assert GPJ over Fishlegs. For SPJ, DE has long-arm. We likely have minimum contacts under *Int'l Shoe*, since arguably Fishlegs personally availed himself of DE law by incorporating a business there, serving as major owner and director of such business, and allegedly stealing a DE company's property. If property is located in DE then there is a strong connection between forum, Fishlegs and litigation since tort was completed in DE (where object was located at time of removal). Litigation in DE would also be F&R because DE has an interest in regulating (mis)conduct in its boundaries, DE law would likely apply (tort complete at location of stolen property at time of theft), and DE is not too inconvenient for Fishlegs. So likely have SPJ over Fishlegs.

**Electronic service:** fundamental requirement is notice reasonably calculated under the circumstances to apprise Fishlegs of the action. *Mullane*. Electronic service could be appropriate here considering can't find forwarding address for Fishlegs (after diligent effort), and she primarily conducts business online. No indication that the electronic messages are failing to reach Fishlegs, so no *Flowers* issue.

Does this FRCP seizure system meet **DPC guardrail** requirements of *Matthews*? Applying *Doehr* for private seizures: (i) Fishleg's private interest is low since seizure of the property should not prejudice Fishlegs, who allegedly has no direct ownership interest in the property to be seized. In fact, seizure may actually increase marketability of the asset to Fishleg's indirect benefit (and won't result in lost income); (ii) Risk of erroneous deprivation is low since (a) Fishlegs likely does not have direct ownership or possessory interest anyway given conclusive proffered assignment documents and (b) if Fishlegs did not steal the item (i.e. lawsuit ultimately fails), then the seizure won't deprive her of anything anyway. To reduce risk of erroneous determination, the court could require the movant (Hiccup) to post bond, allow Fishlegs to counter-bond to resist seizure, and/or hold a hearing *prior* to ordering the seizure; (iii) Plaintiff's (Hiccup's) interest is heightened by need to control and sell asset, but is attenuated by inconclusivity of whether it was actually stolen by Fishlegs, weighing against court interference. Gov't admin considerations are interesting since unclear how court will actually succeed in seizing this lost code. Seizure is judge (rather than clerk) administered, and hearing to be held in short order, but there is a higher risk of PDP issue if no bond is required. So seizure will likely be approved but not clear how it will be executed.

Fishlegs might counter that risk of erroneous seizure is high since the lawsuit does not plausibly allege that he stole the artwork, considering wide range of IP addresses who initiated the hack, a third party hacker or

<!-- page: 2 -->

simply computer malfunction could easily explain the assertions in the complaint. Additionally, Fishlegs may assert her private interest is high since she can sell the property if Astrid fails to, and seizure could block this.

---

## Question 2

To avoid jury trial, only ask for equitable relief (e.g., an injunction requiring Fishlegs to return the stolen artwork, and/or restitution), and no money damages.

---

## Question 3

Parties agree that Hiccup should rightfully have control over object. However there is a dispute over whether Fishlegs wrongfully removed such control, which is a necessary element for Theft tort. Quality documentary evidence does not relate to wrongful removal, so all we have is competing affidavits (so no *Scott* exception): Hiccup says Fishlegs wrongfully removed control, Fishlegs counters that he did not.

Hiccup is the movant and also bears burden of persuasion at trial. Based on its affidavits, Hiccup statements that the scrambling came from somewhere in New England, NH, MA, or ME, and "belief" that Fishlegs hacked the servers did not meet its burden to produce evidence that, if unrebutted and viewed in reasonably in favor of non-movant (Fishlegs), would result in no rational trier of fact concluding that Fishlegs did not wrongfully remove the object. Even if Hiccup had met this burden, Fishlegs rebutted it with his competing affidavit, resulting in a GIMF necessitating denial of the MSJ. *Celotex* (dissent).

## 2024-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-03-loughlin-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

**Civil Procedure Assignment 3 - Draft Model Answer**

## Question 1

### *Motion to seize the animations*

The court will likely grant the motion, because the requirements of the FRCP governing pre-judgment seizures can be met, and the court can exercise QIRJ.

#### *FRCP*

First, there is DJ over Hiccup's theft lawsuit against Fishlegs. Hiccup is incorporated in Delaware (there is no clear PPOB, but it isn't Maine), and Fishlegs is a domiciliary of Maine. (*Strawbridge*). The best AIC is the value of the inventory, $1.25M, above $75,000.

Second, policy considerations suggest that the artwork is best located within the "geographic boundaries" of Delaware. Animations, like debt, are incorporeal objects. (*Harris*). The servers for the different components of the animations are located in three separate states, and the components must converge digitally to create artwork, suggesting that locating the animations at the servers is not best. Because Hiccup owns and controls exploitation of the content, the components functionally combine within the company, incorporated in Delaware. Parties may reasonably expect to litigate content-related disputes in Delaware, and the relationship of the directors is most centered in the state, where they chose to incorporate. While there may be more evidence regarding the theft on Fishlegs' Maine servers, Delaware is the most ascertainable and definite state available to locate the artwork.

Third, Hiccup owns the animations, and attached supporting evidence to the complaint, including a copy of the contract assigning ownership of the artwork to the corporation.

#### *QIRJ*

Under the narrow view of *Shaffer*, the court can exercise QIRJ because, as discussed above, the artwork is probably located in Delaware, and there is no problematic statute at issue. Under the broad view of *Shaffer*, it is unclear if QIRJ still exists. Still, there are likely sufficient minimum contacts linking the defendant's interest in the property, the subject matter of the litigation, and the forum. (*International Shoe*). Per FRCP 4(k)(1)(A), the district court follows Delaware's long-arm statute, which goes to the limits of the constitution. Fishlegs does not have an ownership interest in the artwork. However, as co-CEO of Hiccup, Fishlegs purposefully availed herself of Delaware's law. Unlike in *Shaffer*, the property to be seized (the artwork) is directly

<!-- page: 2 -->

related to the subject matter of the litigation (the theft). Finally, theft is complete "where the object was removed from the control of those who should have it." If the artwork was located in Delaware when it was removed from Hiccup's control, the theft took place in Delaware.

The exercise of QIRJ is fair and reasonable. (*McGee*). It would not be inconvenient for Fishlegs to appear in a state where she already operates a business. Delaware has a substantial interest in ensuring its theft law is followed, particularly in the corporate context, as many companies rely on the state's laws to protect their assets. Assuming the artwork was located in Delaware, the theft was complete there, so Delaware law likely applies.

#### *Electronic service*

Plaintiffs must make "reasonable efforts" at notice. (*Mullane*). Electronic service is reasonable because Astrid and Ruffnut have been communicating with Fishlegs through electronic channels, and have not received any alert that their communications failed to get through. (*Contra Flowers*). Astrid and Ruffnut also have no physical address for Fishlegs, so electronic service is the most effective method of notice.

#### *Defenses*

Fishlegs could assert PDP defenses. Pre-judgment seizures ordinarily require notice and a hearing unless there are extraordinary circumstances, which are not present here (*Doehr*). Like the pre-judgment seizure in *Doehr*, this seizure likely violates the *Matthews* test. The private interests of Hiccup and Fishlegs are relatively balanced. Hiccup, in contrast to Fishlegs, has a property interest in the artwork, but the animations are not essential like a house or car (and Hiccup, a corporation, does not have human needs). Fishlegs' private interests are implicated because a seizure could prevent the sale of the artwork, depriving Fishlegs of income. There is a high risk of erroneous deprivation. The seizure order takes effect instantly, and requires no presentation of evidence prior to the deprivation. Like *Doehr*, the seizure would be based on a one-sided affidavit without any live testimony. Hiccup is also not required to post a bond.

---

## Question 2

Hiccup should seek a remedy that is traditionally equitable. Hiccup could seek an injunction barring Fishlegs from accessing or selling the content, or ordering Fishlegs to restore the access of Astrid and Ruffnut to the Maine servers.

---

## Question 3

<!-- page: 3 -->

The court should deny Hiccup's MSJ because the record suggests a genuine issue of material fact. (*Celotex*, Brennan J. dissenting). Hiccup, the moving party, bears the burden of persuasion on its theft claim. Although Hiccup produced evidence, including two affidavits and the forensic investigative reports, it has not met its burden of production. The affidavits and forensic reports demonstrate only that Astrid and Ruffnut's servers were hacked from somewhere in the New England states, and that Fishlegs ceased communicating with them. Drawing all reasonable inferences in favor of the non-moving party, a rational jury may find that Fishlegs was not behind the theft. Even if Hiccup had met its burden of production, Fishlegs has produced evidence showing there is a genuine issue of material fact. In her affidavit, Fishlegs stated she did not hack any servers, and provided an alternative reason for her silence. The court should thus deny the MSJ, regardless of whether the burden shift is triggered.

## 2024-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2024-assignment-03-yarborough-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

### Motion to Seize

For the seizure rule to apply, the court must have SMJ, the object must be within the bounds of the DC, and the P must have an ownership interest.

The court likely has SMJ under § 1332, as Fishlegs is ME domiciliary, and Hiccup is incorporated in Delaware. One could argue the principal place of business is elsewhere, but that argument would likely not stand, as there is no other location that acts as the nerve center. The amount in controversy, from the $1.25M property valuation, is over $75,000.

Next, the object must be in Delaware. The object in question, the animation, has no clear physical location, similar to a debt or a bond. Assuming the object has been stolen, the technology in question is being used somewhere in New England. However, prior to the theft, as each of the three individuals had some control of the object in three different states at the same time, the location of the person using the software is likely not an effective way to analyze location. As Hiccup owns the object, under *Harris*, the location of the object should be the location of the owner — Delaware. Further, a functionalist argument points towards Delaware. Hiccup owns and controls all rights and interests of the animation, making it reasonable for parties to expect to litigate in Delaware. Similarly, a desire for certainty and minimizing arbitrary locations points towards Delaware.

Finally, Hiccup rightfully asserts an ownership interest and supports this interest with competent evidence (such as the contract assigning ownership of the artwork to Hiccup), as required by the FRCP.

However, the court may dismiss for lack of QIRJ. The court could attach the property pending the litigation, in this case the theft, making this a lawsuit against the property. *Shaffer* leaves open the question of if QIRJ even exists. Under interpretation 1, QIRJ survives. Assuming the property is in the forum state (see above), there can be QIRJ. The court must also consider the type of property in question, and the policy implications. This legal matter is not as over broad as the DE sequestration statute at issue in *Shaffer*, as the D was a part of a three-person company that incorporated itself in Delaware. Under interpretation number 2 of *Shaffer* — *International Shoe* limits QIRJ. This would still probably survive, as there are minimum contacts. Owning a substantial (1/3) stake in a company is similar to owning property in Delaware, meaning Fishlegs may have purposefully availed herself to the forum state. However, it is a movable object, potentially more like a car than a house. Fair and reasonableness factors go either way. As the defendant has contacts through incorporating her company there, meaning it is somewhat convenient. The forum has an interest in property laws of companies in their state. However, the evidence is likely not in Delaware. Finally, if the location of the animation is in Delaware, HCOL points to Delaware. The tort occurred "where the object was removed from the control of those who should have it," meaning Delaware.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Fishlegs would likely succeed in raising PDP concerns on this pre-hearing seizure compared to a post-hearing seizure (*Matthews*). The private interest factors are high for both parties. Hiccup has a property interest in the animation, which evokes concerns of livelihood, although the property in question is not subsistence like *Goldberg*. Similarly, although Fishlegs does not have a property interest in the animation, she is a co-CEO of the company, and it is her means of income. Second, the *Snaidach* factors show a risk of erroneous decision, compared to a post-hearing seizure. It is unclear who the decision maker is in this instance. A pre-hearing seizure is almost never constitutional, and there are no exceptional circumstances present (*Doehr*). The seizure requires no live testimony, nor litigation bounds, both of which would both reduce the risk of erroneous decisions. Third, the government has an interest in accuracy and low adjudication costs.

### Notice by electronic mail

The court will likely allow notice by electronic mail. The three members of Hiccup have established modes of communication via electronic mail, and they have no mailing address for Fishlegs. It was the most likely way to give notice, due to this established method of communication. Also, nothing came back, so no reason to think this was not effective (contra, *Flowers*).

---

## Question 2

To increase the likelihood that a judge sits as a jury, Hiccup should ask for an injunction instead of monetary damages or seek unjust enrichment, if Fishlegs made money off the object.

---

## Question 3

To rule on summary judgment, the court will analyze the *Celotex* factors. Hiccup is the moving party and has the burden of proof at trial, so they must produce enough evidence on the issue that, if unrebutted, would show there is not a genuine issue of material fact. Hiccup produced two affidavits and a forensic report, showing that Ruffnut and Astrid were hacked, the hacker was in one of three states, and Fishlegs ceased communication with Ruffnut and Astrid. Drawing all reasonable inferences in favor of Fishlegs, a rational jury may be able find that she was not the culprit. Even if Hiccup met its burden of production, Fishlegs' affidavit shows a genuine issue of material fact, namely, who stole the technology. Fishlegs asserts that someone else must have stolen it, and her access has been uninterrupted.

## 2025-assignment-01 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

Assignment 1 (ungraded)
Civil Procedure 1 & 2, 2025
Professor Jim Greiner

**Logistics and Rules**

Posting date: Friday, September 26, 4pm
Due date/time: 8am on Tuesday, Sep 30
Word limit: 800 words
Time limit: 3 hours

0. The Registrar will administer all your Assignments through the Exam4 software. You will also use Exam4 for your exams. This is a good thing; you will be the only sections who, at exam time, have previous experience with the software. Every year, some folks struggle with Exam4, just as anyone would with any new software. Please remember a couple of things:

    a) I do not run Exam4 and have no idea how it works. If you have problems with Exam4, you can certainly email me with questions; doing so will be as productive as finding a nearby wall and hitting your head against it. Questions must go to the Registrar.

    b) This Assignment is ungraded. If you miss the time limit window, or have other problems with Exam4, there will be no effect on your grade, or on anything at all. Just email me a copy of your response (I suggest keeping one), and I'll send it through the usual process of obtaining TA feedback.

1. Prepare for the assignment however you'd like. I suggest reviewing and outlining the course materials through (and including) the posting date, nothing more.

2. Download the assignment from Exam 4.

3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.

4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment.

5. As suggested by the instructions above, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response via the Exam 4 software. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.

6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.

7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer, other than sources banned by HLS policy, such as AI programs. To clarify: the use of chatbots, large language models, and AI programs of any kind, including merely to summarize doctrine for you, while you are taking this assignment is cheating. Also, you may not communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding the substance of this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.

<!-- page: 2 -->

8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.

9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

10. This assignment is ungraded.

11. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. The Registrar's Office has sent you information about what to use for an anonymous ID. Please follow those instructions.

**Suggestions for your answer:**

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- Quote statutes and cases VERY rarely, if at all. We know what they say, and it takes up time and space.
- Cite cases rarely, and only when doing so might save you time. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise specific in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under traditional notes of fair play and justice."

---

All the facts and law in the following hypothetical are fictional.

## The Law

***Respondeat superior***: All states relevant to this assignment follow the doctrine of respondeat superior, meaning that an employer/company can be held liable for the acts or omissions of its employees performed during the discharge of their duties.

***Long-arms***: All states relevant to this assignment have long-arm statutes going to constitutional limits.

***Limitations***: In Colorado the statute of limitations for negligence lawsuits is one year from the events that gave rise to the lawsuit. In Arizona, the negligence limitations period is two years.

***HCOL Restatements***: Colorado follows the First Restatement for Horizontal Choice of Law, Arizona the Second Restatement.

***Negligence cause of action***: All states relevant to this assignment have a cause of action called

<!-- page: 3 -->

negligence. The elements of the negligence cause of action are

- the existence of a duty from the defendant to the plaintiff (all persons have a duty to exercise due care in their interactions with other persons),
- the breach of that duty through the defendant's failure to exercise ordinary care under the circumstances,
- the breach caused the plaintiff an injury, and
- the injury to the plaintiff resulted in quantifiable damages.

A plaintiff may prove a negligence cause of action in two ways, first by alleging and proving that the defendant's actions or inactions fell below the common understanding of ordinary care, and second that the defendant violated a statute or agency rule (federal or state) enacted to protect persons like the plaintiff (if you've taken torts, this latter theory is called "negligence 'per se.'")

***Congressional statute***: In early 2021, Congress passed the Intoxicated Pilots Act. Section T-800 of the act provided as follows:

> The Congress finds that intoxicated airplane pilots pose a danger to passengers, to people on the ground in airports and elsewhere, and to property. It shall be unlawful for any airline engaged in the business of transporting passengers to allow pilots to fly while intoxicated. The United States Department of Justice, but not any private individual, may sue any airline that fails to comply with this prohibition, and in such a suit, the court shall impose a civil penalty in an amount necessary to deter future violations.

## The Characters

***Connor***: Sarah Connor was born and raised in Phoenix, AZ, where her parents still lived. She hoped one day to be a pilot. When she graduated from high school, she began college at the University of Colorado in Boulder, CO, but kept her Arizona drivers' license. She was 21 years old at the time of the events described below, and in her junior year of college. After college, she hoped to attend a pilot's school somewhere, or perhaps join the Air Force to become a pilot.

***Reese***: Kyle Reese was a pilot. He grew up in North Dakota, joined the Air Force in his early 20s, and flew military transport planes until his retirement in 2019. Reese then moved to Tucson, Arizona, bought a small house there, and planned to live there for the rest of his life. He looked for a way to supplement his military pension, heard about an opening for a pilot at Skynet, Inc., applied, and got the job.

***Skynet***: Skynet, Inc., was a business incorporated in Mexico with its principal place of business in Nogales, Mexico, right across the U.S.-Mexico border from Tucson. Skynet offered scenic low-altitude flying tours of beautiful natural sites exclusively in Mexico, with one exception, namely, the Grand Canyon Tour, described below. Skynet operated a fleet of six airplanes, all based in Nogales: one that sat two passengers, four that sat eight passengers, and one that sat twelve. Skynet did no advertising and maintained only a single-page non-interactive website describing its business as recreational/touring flights (with no information about where it flew) and

<!-- page: 4 -->

inviting interested parties to call its 24-hour information and booking telephone line. It neither held assets nor maintained offices in the United States.

## The Events

***The Grand Canyon Tour***: In April of 2022, dialing from her college apartment in Boulder, CO, Sarah Connor called Skynet's booking number, hoping to find a trip she could take in June. She reserved a seat on Skynet's two-seater airplane for the "Grand Canyon Tour" in early June. The Grand Canyon tour's flight plan consisted of taking off from Nogales, Mexico; traveling northwest for a two-hour low-altitude flyover of several of the Grand Canyon's most beautiful locations;[^1] following the Colorado River north into Colorado for a 20-minute flight "so low that you'll feel like you're swimming" (in the words of the Skynet telephone salesperson); and then following a straight line back to Nogales. In part because of the amount of fuel required, the price of the "Grand Canyon Tour" was expensive, but Connor's relatives and friends chipped in to make the trip an early graduation present.

In late May of 2022, after taking her last exam, Connor took a bus from Boulder, CO to her childhood home in Phoenix. She spent a couple of weeks relaxing with her family. On the appointed morning, in early June of 2022, she drove her parents' car across the border to Nogales and checked in at Skynet's office.

Connor boarded the Skynet two-seater for her tour. The other passenger was a woman named Ginger Ventura, a student at the University of Denver, in Colorado. Connor and Ventura struck up a conversation, and before takeoff exchanged contact information so as to stay in touch after their adventure. Both passengers were in their seats when pilot Kyle Reese arrived. Connor noticed that he carried a 750 ml bottle of Woodford Reserve Bourbon Whiskey in his left hand as he entered the cockpit. The bottle was full. Connor also noticed that Reese did not lock the door to the closed cockpit.

At first, the flight went off as planned. The ride was smooth, and both Connor and Ventura enjoyed themselves. Toward the end of the time over the Grand Canyon, while the plane was still in Arizona, the plane's path became erratic. The plane's flight went from bad to worse. At that point, the plane's cockpit door banged open, and the Woodford bottle rolled out in the passenger compartment. It was empty.

While still over Arizona, the plane turned around and began climbing. It eventually landed back in Nogales without further incident. Ventura and Reese emerged from the flight with only a few bumps and bruises. Connor was less lucky. She fractured her shoulder and eye socket, requiring painful reconstruction of her face. Her medical bills were about $40,000.

***Connor's Threatened Lawsuit***:
In August of 2022, Skynet received a letter from the law firm of Silberman and Traxler. The

[^1]: All portions of the Grand Canyon relevant to this assignment are in Arizona.

<!-- page: 5 -->

letter stated that Silberman and Traxler represented Sarah Connor in the matter of her Grand Canyon Tour flight. Connor intended to sue Skynet for $60,000 ($40,000 in medical bills, $20,000 in pain and suffering) asserting common law negligence. With respect to negligence, Connor would prove breach of the duty of care by proving that Reese was drunk at key times during the flight in violation of section T-800 of the Intoxicated Pilots Act. Silberman and Traxler asked whether Skynet wanted to settle before litigation began.

***Skynet and Reese's Actual Lawsuit***

Nothing happened for a while. Then, in September of 2023, Skynet and Reese sued Connor in the United States District Court for the District of Colorado, seeking a declaratory judgment that any negligence lawsuit by Connor against Skynet and Reese in any Colorado court would be barred by limitations.

You are a law clerk for the district judge assigned to the lawsuit. The judge asks you for a memo addressing what defenses Connor might assert and how the court should rule on the declaratory judgment request. (You need not consider venue in your answer.)

## 2025-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-01-colón-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## SMJ

Connor could attempt to dismiss under 12(b)(1) for lack of SMJ. Courts have SMJ over a declaratory judgment if the court would have SMJ over a suit by Connor (imaginary lawsuit rule — § 2201). There is no AUJ under § 1331 because negligence is a state law claim (*Mottley*). Because there is only one claim, there is also no § 1367 S-SMJ.

There is likely no D-SMJ in a suit against Reese and Skynet. Connor is an AZ domiciliary given she was born and raised in AZ, has an AZ license, parents are still in AZ, and she only relocated temporarily for college (*Mas*). Reese is also likely an AZ domiciliary — he relocated to AZ, bought a house there, and planned on staying for the rest of his life, so there is no complete diversity (*Strawbridge*). Although Connor would have complete diversity against Skynet in a separate lawsuit (Skynet is incorporated/PPoB in Mexico — "52nd state"), AIC is insufficient ($60k < $75k) as to both defendants.

The court *may* have jurisdiction through *Smith-Grable* because Connor planned on using section T-800 of the IPA, a federal statute, to prove breach of the duty of care (embedded federal issue in state law claim). The IPA (T-800) is arguably a substantial federal issue (evidenced by Congress's findings on dangers to the public) and disputed by the parties (whether Reese violated the IPA/his drunkenness). However, the dispute here is purely factual (*Empire*) and allowing a T-800 violation serve as negligence per se to support AUJ would likely lead to too many cases being swept into federal court (*Merrill Dow*). The fact that Congress expressly barred individuals from suing under the IPA likely supports this point. *Smith-Grable* is thus likely unavailable.

## IPJ

Both CO and AZ have long-arm statutes extending to the limits of the Constitution — 4(k)(1)(a).

There is likely no GIPJ over Connor in CO. If *Daimler*'s "at home" rule applies to humans (open question), then it's possible Connor meets the substantial and continuous contacts threshold. Connor has been in CO for 3+ years and currently spends almost 100% of her time in CO. If not, then there maybe are minimal contacts for SIPJ. Connor contacted Skynet from CO, possibly entered into an oral contract from CO, and she bought a ticket for a flight that she thought would go over CO (because it regularly does go there), even though her particular flight never entered CO. For F&R factors, CO is arguably a convenient location for Connor because she lives there for most of the year (although not domiciled), the witness (Ventura) presumably lives in CO, it is not an inconvenient location for Skynet (it operates flights in CO) nor Reese (same), there's a high interest for CO to adjudicate tort-like conduct that occurs in the state (possibly future flights that do fly over CO). On the other hand, Reese/Skynet can argue that the flight never actually flew over CO and the evidence is in AZ, where the injury occurred. For HCoL, CO is a 1st restatement state so "lex loci delicti" applies (where was the blood spilled? — *Alabama*

<!-- page: 2 -->

*R.R.*) Because the injury occurred in Arizona, AZ's substantive law would apply. However, both CO and AZ have the same negligence statutes so applying either state's negligence law would lead to the same substantive outcome on the merits of the case.

On net, SIPJ is a toss-up given the tenuous minimal contacts between Connor, the forum, and the subject matter of the controversy (and F&R seem to net out).

## SoL

Connor's negligence claim is barred by SoL. The statute of limitations in Colorado is 1 year. For HCoL purposes, statute of limitations is procedural, and a Colorado court will apply the forum state's procedural law (*Klaxon*). Because the events giving rise to the suit occurred in June 2022, and this declaratory judgment suit was filed in September 2023, before any Connor lawsuit was filed, Colorado's SoL bars any negligence suit by Connor against Reese and/or Skynet.

## 2025-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-01-davydov-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

Declaratory judgment will likely fail.

## SMJ of the Declaratory Judgment

To examine SMJ of a declaratory judgment suit, look at the "imaginary suit" it is trying to anticipate (i.e., Connor's suit).

### AUJ

There is no AUJ. T-800 creates no cause of action available to private citizens (*Mottley*).

Is there *Smith-Grable*? Likely no. The issue is not substantial (the case does not necessarily turn on Reese having violated the IPA; Connor can prove negligence in another way), even though it is disputed. Even still, the dispute here is factual — whether or not Reese was actually drunk — more so than it is legal, pointing to this being an issue for state courts. Congress also likely intended for this kind of issue *not* to go to federal court, since it stated expressly that private citizens could not so. Similarly, there is no *Smith-Grable* because of a situation similar to *Merrill-Dow*. We do not want to cause a flood of cases into the federal system because of a violation of a federal duty/regulation (aviation rules would affect many people) (*Merrill*), nor do we want to interpret a state claim that looks at a duty imposed by federal statute to create a cognizable federal claim (*Moore*).

### DJ

There is no DJ (while Connor only wished to sue Skynet, the "imaginary" suit here envisions Connor suing both Skynet and Reese) because there is no complete diversity. To do this, we need to see where each one of these "characters" are "citizens."

Skynet: is incorporated and has PPOB in Mexico. For DJ purposes, this is a 51st state.

Connor: domiciled in Arizona. Born and raised there, has license and parents there, and only went to college in Colorado with no intent to stay indefinitely.

Reese: domiciled in Arizona. While born and raised in North Dakota, he moved to Arizona, bought house, and intends to live there "for the rest of his life."

Because Reese and Connor are both from Arizona, there is no complete diversity.

The suit also fails to meet the AIC requirement of DJ. $60K in damages is less than the $75K requirement for DJ.

## IPJ

Connor may also claim that the court has no personal jurisdiction over her.

### GIPJ

There is likely no general personal jurisdiction because Connor is domiciled in Arizona. However, while the *Daimler* test has never been applied to human beings, it could be argued that Connor, as a college student in the state who has spent well over half of each of the past three

<!-- page: 2 -->

years in CO, is essentially at home there. Similarly, the record does not indicate whether she was tagged, so we can't use *Burnham* reasoning.

### SIPJ

Colorado long-arm. 4K1A. The court likely does not have SIPJ over Connor, either. While Connor did call Skynet over the phone from her Colorado apartment (which might be a sufficient contact actually linking the defendant to *the subject matter of litigation*, unlike the fact that she just went to school there), and she later bought a ticket for a trip she thought would fly over CO, the plane never ended up flying in Colorado and she ultimately signed the contract in Mexico (*McGee*). Similarly, the action here is a tort suit that is unrelated to any claim that could be made on the contract.

Is it F+R? Connor's relevant contacts to Colorado appear limited; CO is convenient for her because she lives there (assuming she hasn't moved out yet after graduation); CO is convenient for the plaintiffs Skynet and Reese because they regularly operate/fly flights there; CO doesn't have any specific strong interests in this subject; location of witnesses/evidence is mixed (other passenger is likely in CO, but Reese in AZ and plane presumably in Mexico).

### Colorado Stay?

Connor cannot initiate a Colorado Stay because there is no active litigation occurring at the state level; it was only threatened.

## HCOL?

Putting all these to the side, and assuming, for the sake of argument, that the Court has both SMJ and PJ, then the declaratory judgment would likely come out in Skynet and Reese's favor. A federal court uses the HCOL of the state it sits. Statute of limitations is a procedural issue, so we use the law of the forum state as well. Colorado's SOL on a negligence claim is 1 year. Because the events that led to the suit occurred in June of 2022, and September 2023 is more than one year from there, the statute of limitations has expired.

## 2025-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-01-drescher-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## SMJ

It's a declaratory judgment action so we consider the imaginary lawsuit if Connor sued Skynet/Reese ("Connor's lawsuit") to assess SMJ in Skynet/Reese's declaratory judgment action against Connor ("declaratory judgment action").

AUJ is likely not met. There is no federal statute that Connor can sue Skynet/Reese under (*Mottley*). Violation of the Intoxicated Pilots Act (federal statute) however could serve as a basis of Connor's theory of negligence per se (that Reese's drunkenness violated T-800), so we look to *Smith Grable* factors. The federal issue at stake is relatively substantial (risk to public safety is important) but is a fact bound inquiry (whether pilot here was drunk in violation of the statute, compare with constitutionality of federal bonds or proper administration of IRS policies). Allowing this case in federal court is likely to increase number of negligence cases being brought in federal courts under state causes of action via trying to use violation of a federal statute for negligence per se (burdensome for federal courts). Plus Congress affirmatively indicated that private individuals could not bring suit under Intoxicated Pilots Act, suggesting that Congress wants to limit the cases involving this statute that are brought into federal court. *Smith Grable* exception likely does not apply.

DJ is also unlikely met in Connor's lawsuit. DJ requires complete diversity and AIC being at least $75k. Reese is domiciled in AZ because he resides there and intends to keep living there for the rest of his life (*Mas*). Connor is likely domiciled in AZ because parents still live there and she is in college elsewhere but with no intent to remain there. She could arguably be domiciled in CO because currently lives there and may intend to keep living there, but based on the available information, including that she maintains her AZ driver's license, she is likely domiciled in AZ. Skynet is domiciled in Mexico because incorporated and PPOB there (*Herz*). There is not complete diversity between Reese and Connor but there is complete diversity between Skynet and Reese.

Connor could also argue that the AIC is insufficient (based on the damages she intended to sue for) as to the claim between her and Skynet, so DJ altogether fails. Even if Skynet argued that the AIC is higher, Connor could assert that her medical bills were well below $75k and even the amount she intended to sue for pain and suffering was additionally below $75k.

## IPJ

Connor could contest that the federal district court in CO lacks PJ over her. Likely no GPJ because she is most likely an AZ domiciliary. If *Daimler* applies to people then it's possible there is GPJ because she's a student in Boulder and spends much of the year there, but

<!-- page: 2 -->

otherwise no GPJ. For SPJ, CO has a long arm statute and 4k1a applies. There are unlikely sufficient minimum contacts between Connor, CO, and the subject matter of the litigation. Connor called Skynet from CO to make the reservation, but all of the flight tour occurred over AZ. Plus, fair and reasonableness factors lean away from exercising SPJ. Reese and Skynet are principally located out of state, so limited convenience for them (plus Skynet is foreign company, no offices or resources in U.S., even less convenient than if were just geographically distant). Connor resides in CO, so it's convenient for her to litigate in CO. Evidence and witnesses likely in Mexico or AZ (plane, doctors from Connor's treatment), though also some in CO (Ventura). Court in CO might want to assert PJ and take the case because Connor is a student there and also might have some claim to air traffic safety for Skynet flights in general (although not for this flight). It's close but Connor could win by contesting PJ.

## HCOL

For purposes of HCOL, statute of limitations is procedural, so we look to law of the forum (CO). In CO, the statute of limitations for a negligence suit is 1 year. At the time of filing the declaratory judgment action, about 15 months has passed since Connor's Grand Canyon tour. Unlikely that Connor can challenge SOL if declaratory judgment action makes it this far.

## Colorado River

Colorado River is irrelevant since there is no simultaneous state court lawsuit (nothing for federal court to abstain from).

## 2025-assignment-01 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-01-murphy-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## SMJ

The court likely lacks SMJ. For a declaratory judgment request, we look at the imaginary lawsuit to determine SMJ. Here, the imaginary lawsuit would be a negligence lawsuit under Colorado state law, with Connor suing Skynet and/or Reese.

**AUJ:** The imaginary lawsuit does not involve a federal cause of action (T-800 does not create a cause of action), and likely does not raise an implied federal question per *Smith-Grable*. In particular, the issue is not embedded nor substantial, because negligence *per se* is only one manner of demonstrating Reese's negligence. The dispute is not over federal law, either, but instead over the actual fact of Reese's drunkenness.

The balancing factors point in the same direction: (1) If we allowed this case to be litigated in federal court, many other similar cases could follow, as there are federal rules and regulations controlling many aspects of pilot conduct and airplane safety, any of which might be implicated in a typical negligence claim. (2) If Congress wanted this to be litigated in federal court, it could have created a cause of action within T-800. (3) There is no unique federal interest at stake here beyond a generalized concern about safety.

**DJ:** We begin by examining the citizenship of each party to the lawsuit:

- Reese is domiciled in Arizona. When he relocated there from North Dakota, he purchased a house, and had no date at which he was planning on reconsidering his residence in the state.
- Skynet is a citizen of Mexico (i.e. "outside of America"), as it was incorporated there and has a PPOB there.
- Connor is likely domiciled in Arizona. She was born there, and though she went to college in Colorado, she had plans to relocate elsewhere (pilot's school) and had a definite date of reconsideration (graduation). *Mas*. Hence, she likely is *not* a citizen of Colorado.

Diversity jurisdiction requires complete diversity. *Strawbridge*. Therefore, because Reese and Connor are both citizens of Arizona, the court may not exercise diversity jurisdiction.

The lawsuit is also likely barred due to an insufficient amount-in-controversy ($60,000 is less than $75,000)—though, theoretically, Reese and Skynet could be exposed to more damages than Connor originally contemplated, if she revises her estimated damages for pain and suffering.

## IPJ

<!-- page: 2 -->

Connor may assert that the court cannot exercise personal jurisdiction over her.

**GPJ:** Connor is not a citizen of Colorado, as discussed above. If *Daimler* applies to natural persons, then she may be subject to general jurisdiction as she currently spends a substantial majority of her time in Colorado for college. Otherwise, the court may not exercise personal jurisdiction over her.

**SPJ:** Per FRCP 4(k)(1)(A), we look to the Colorado long-arm statute to determine the permissible jurisdiction for the court to exercise. Colorado's long-arm goes to the limits of the Constitution.

We look to see whether there are sufficient contacts between Colorado, Connor, and the subject matter of the lawsuit such that exercising jurisdiction would not offend traditional notions of fair play and justice. *International Shoe*, *Volkswagen*. Here, there likely are not: The incidents giving rise to the lawsuit occurred entirely within Arizona. Connor's typical residence in Colorado is unrelated to the subject matter of the litigation, and hence cannot qualify as a contact. However, Connor did reserve the trip from Colorado, and the plane's flight path originally would have taken it over Colorado. On balance, however, this likely is not enough: The alleged negligent behavior and the injury occurred entirely outside of Colorado airspace, and a single outbound phone call is less substantial than a single transaction (*McGee*).

In the alternative, it likely would not be fair + reasonable: Though Connor can travel easily to Colorado, plaintiff's convenience is not high as both Reese and Skynet are located elsewhere. Colorado does have an abstract interest in maintaining air safety standards, but since the allegedly unsafe conduct occurred outside of Colorado airspace, that interest is generic insofar as it would justify exercising jurisdiction over any drunken pilot who could have theoretically flown a plane over Colorado. There is (some) evidence located in Colorado: Ventura, the other passenger, has likely returned to her college residence and hence could conveniently testify. On balance, however, this does not cross the threshold for fair and reasonable.

## Statute of Limitations

Federal courts apply the HCOL rules of the state in which they sit. *Klaxon*. Since statutes of limitation are procedural for the purposes of horizontal choice of law, the district court will apply the forum state's law. Therefore, the statute of limitations for lawsuits in Colorado is one year, and if the court is able to establish jurisdiction, it should rule in favor of Reese and Skynet on declaratory judgment.

## 2025-assignment-02 · Assignment prompt

<!-- page: 1 -->

Assignment 2 (graded)
Civil Procedure 1 & 2, 2025
Professor Jim Greiner

**Logistics and Rules**

Due date/time: 8am on Tuesday, Oct 14
Word limit: 850 words
Time limit: 3 hours

    a) I do not run Exam4, and have no idea how it works. If you have problems with Exam4, you can certainly email me with questions; doing so will be as productive as finding a nearby wall and hitting your head against it. Questions must go to the Registrar.

    b) This Assignment is graded, see the syllabus.
