2. Download the assignment from Exam 4.

3. The word limit is listed above. Compose a response to the assignment in this number of words or less, including footnotes (if any), case citations (if any), etc. If your response is longer than the word limit, the first set of words up to the word limit will be reviewed and the remainder ignored.

4. The time limit for this assignment is listed above. You must complete AND UPLOAD your response within the time limit, with the clock starting to tick as of when you download the assignment.

5. As suggested by the instructions above, the only acceptable method of response for this assignment is to upload your typed response via the Exam 4 software. Please notify me before the assignment is due if you anticipate any difficulty complying with this method of response.

6. You will not need to do any additional research or read any source materials other than those assigned for class. You will not receive additional credit for citing or relying on materials other than those assigned for class.

7. You may refer to or use any non-human source to aid in your answer, other than sources banned by HLS policy, such as AI programs. To clarify: the use of chatbots, large language models, and AI programs of any kind, including merely to summarize doctrine for you, while you are taking this assignment is cheating. Also, you may not communicate with any person other than Professor Greiner regarding the substance of this assignment until its due date/time, listed above. This prohibition includes librarians.

8. Although I will read questions regarding the assignment submitted via email, it is unlikely that I will chose to answer them. If I do answer them, I will forward the question and answer to the entire class. The assignment is straightforward; don't make it more complicated than it is.

9. Your response is due on the due date/time above. Absolutely no excuse will be accepted for a late paper. This unavailability of excuses includes computer problems, travel (including the T) issues, alarm clocks, troubles with the HLS uploading system, etc. I suggest doing and handing in the assignment no later than the day before its due date to avoid any timeliness issue.

10. PLEASE DO NOT PUT YOUR NAME ON YOUR RESPONSE. The Registrar's Office has sent you information about what to use for an anonymous ID. Please follow those instructions.

<!-- page: 2 -->

**Suggestions for your answer:**

- DON'T use memo style format (i.e., "TO" and "FROM," etc.). Just write your answer.
- You are not required to cite cases or other law, but doing so might save you time and words. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise specific in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be reasonable under traditional notes of fair play and justice."

---

All states relevant to this assignment have a tort cause of action called "negligence." The elements of a negligence cause of action are that the defendant owes a duty of ordinary care to the plaintiff, that the defendant breached that duty by failing to act with ordinary care under the circumstances, and that the breach caused plaintiff to suffer quantifiable damages (such as pain and suffering). All persons (and corporations) owe all other persons (and corporations) a duty of ordinary care in regular day-to-day interactions; courts assume that this duty exists at all times, and parties do not need to articulate the existence of this duty in pleadings. All states relevant to this exam follow the doctrine of respondeat superior, meaning that a plaintiff may sue a company for damages caused by the acts of the company's employees within the scope of their employment.

All states relevant to this assignment have long-arms statutes that extend to constitutional limits.

Pennsylvania's state Rule of Civil Procedure 8 is identical in language to Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 8, except Pennsylvania's state rule adds an additional subsection (f). The Pennsylvania legislature added subsection (f) via statute in 2015. In the preamble to the statute that added the subsection, the legislature stated, "The Legislature finds that medical malpractice lawsuits are imposing undue insurance costs in this state. Some of those costs come from plaintiffs who file frivolous claims for damages due to pain and suffering. Some of those costs come from defendants who dispute obviously meritorious claims for damages due to pain and suffering. The high malpractice costs are making it more difficult for Pennsylvania to attract qualified physicians to the state. The legislature seeks to respond to this problem with Rule 8(f)."

**Pa. R. Civ. P. 8(f)**

> (1) In any suit alleging a tort of personal injury (such as negligence) and demanding compensation for pain and suffering, the plaintiff shall attach to the complaint an affidavit from a competent licensed physician detailing under penalty of perjury that the affiant has personally examined the plaintiff, and that the amount demanded is plausible given the plaintiff's injuries. The Court shall sua sponte promptly dismiss any personal injury tort lawsuit that does not comply with this requirement.
>
> (2) In any suit covered by subsection (1) of this section, upon plaintiff's compliance with subsection (1), a defendant shall attach to the first pleading or motion (including a motion to dismiss) an affidavit of a duly licensed physician. The affiant shall swear under penalty of perjury that they are available at an appropriate time to examine the plaintiff and to offer subsequently testimony on behalf of the defendant as to the veracity of the plaintiff's pain and suffering demand. If a defendant fails to comply at the time of the filing of the answer or motion to dismiss with this subsection, the court shall upon a plaintiff's motion issue an order prohibiting

<!-- page: 3 -->

> the defendant from disputing the amount of damages that the plaintiff demands for pain and suffering.

Pennsylvania state rules of procedure also allow a defendant 45 days from the later of filing or service to file an answer or a motion in response to a complaint.

In all other states relevant to this assignment, the rules of civil procedure are identical to the federal rules.

Pennsylvania has three federal districts, the Eastern, Middle, and Western. Harrisburg, PA, is in the Middle District. Philadelphia, PA, is in the Eastern District. South Carolina, Delaware, and Maryland each have a federal single district.

On April 1, 2025, the law firm of Baratheon & Martell, LLC filed the complaint below in Pennsylvania state court on behalf of plaintiff Podrick Payne. The complaint was served the same day by first class mail and hand delivery to Daario's corporate headquarters in Baltimore, MD. Baratheon & Martell has had extensive experience and success litigating cases in Pennsylvania state court and prefers to litigate all its cases there. It believes that it has its best chance of success in any case in Pennsylvania state court. If it cannot litigate cases in Pennsylvania state court, it would prefer Pennsylvania federal court to a court outside the state. The complaint read as follows.

1. Plaintiff Podrick Payne is a citizen of South Carolina.
2. Defendant Daario Naharis, Inc. is a Delaware corporation with its principal place of business in Baltimore, MD. Its business was the procurement and sale of exotic plants.
3. Plaintiff was a connoisseur of rare flowers. He has dedicated his life to the collection, study, purchase, and sale of exotic flowering plants. He maintains a small estate with a greenhouse in his home in Columbia, SC. He traveled widely in pursuit of his passion.
4. Plaintiff suffers from a rare and severe allergy to orchids. Encountering the odor of an orchid, or touching an orchid, is enough to cause Plaintiff the risk of severe allergic shock.
5. Defendant maintained offices in Baltimore and Harrisburg, PA. Defendant maintained a non-interactive website visible to anyone in the world in that included its catalog of currently available plants. The website advised anyone wishing to purchase a plant from Daario to call either its Baltimore or Harrisburg offices (phone numbers appeared on the website) to arrange for purchase. Daario offered various delivery options, depending on the type of plant at issue.
6. In January of 2025, Plaintiff was visiting Philadelphia, PA, to attend a series of lectures on rare flowers. During a lunch session, a friend recommended that he examine Daario's website.
7. Upon doing so, Plaintiff discovered that one of Daario's listings was a rare Frey flower. Plaintiff had always wanted such a plant.
8. Plaintiff called Daario's Harrisburg office from his hotel room in Philadelphia. He reached a person who identified himself as Eddard Stark, who claimed to be an employee of Defendant. Explaining that he was nearby in Philadelphia at the time, and thus available to drive to Harrisburg if necessary to pick up any purchase, Plaintiff inquired about the Frey flower.
9. Stark responded that he did in fact have a Frey flower available for purchase, but that there was no need for an irritating trip to Harrisburg to pick it up, so long as Plaintiff lived on the eastern seaboard of the United States. If so, Daario's network of delivery trucks could arrange for transportation without damage to the flower. Plaintiff arranged for purchase and for delivery of

<!-- page: 4 -->

the flower to his estate in Columbia, SC. Plaintiff provided the address. Stark agreed to arrange shipment.

10. As he was about to hang up, Plaintiff asked Stark whether the Frey flower was actually a type of orchid, explaining that if so, he wouldn't be able to buy it after all because of a severe allergy to orchids. Stark assured him that the Frey was not an orchid, and so that all was well.
11. Two weeks later, after Plaintiff had returned to South Carolina, he received via a Daario delivery truck a box containing his Daario order. But when he opened the box and inhaled, he went into shock. A friend was in the house at the time, and the friend called an ambulance.
12. Plaintiff was rushed to the hospital and spent three weeks there, including one in the ICU. His allergic reaction extended to the nerves of his face, causing him significant pain and suffering. Plaintiff required two months of sequestration at home to recover. Plaintiff eventually made a full recovery, but not until he had suffered pain during his hospital stay and during his home recovery.
13. At Plaintiff's request, a friend of plaintiff's took the flower in Defendant's delivery box to a botanist at the University of South Carolina. The botanist examined the flower and declared that it was not a Frey. Instead, it was a Coelogyne pandurate, a type of orchid.

**Count 1: Negligence**

14. Defendant and its employees owed a duty of ordinary care to Plaintiff. Stark or some other employee of Defendant failed to act with ordinary care when they placed, not the Frey plant that Plaintiff ordered, but an orchid, in the box for delivery to plaintiff's house. Defendant did so with knowledge of Plaintiff's severe allergy to orchids. This failure caused Plaintiff to be exposed to an orchid, which in turn caused Plaintiff to go into shock, to suffer a severe and painful reaction, and to be forced to pay extensive medical bills.

**Prayer for Relief**

15. Wherefore the Plaintiff prays that this Court order the Defendant to pay to him the sum of $200,000, representing $100,000 in medical bills and $100,000 in pain and suffering.

Attached to the complaint was an affidavit of Podrick's treating physician in South Carolina, who stated under penalty of perjury that he had examined Podrick personally and that the amount demanded was plausible given the plaintiff's injuries.

On May 7, Daario removed the suit to the United States District Court for the Eastern District of Pennsylvania, citing 28 U.S.C. § 1332. Immediately after the case was removed, Daario filed a motion to dismiss for failure to state a claim under Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)(6). The only item accompanying the motion to dismiss was an additional motion, this one to transfer the case to the United States District Court for the District of South Carolina.

You are an associate in the law firm of Baratheon & Martell. The date is May 10, 2025. The partner in charge of the case is about to go on a three-to-five-week vacation; she'll be backpacking with no access to the Internet or any other form of communication during this time, and thus no action on the case can be taken until she gets back. She asks you for a memo what action you recommend that the firm take to maximize the chances of success for your client, with an accompanying explanation. She asks you to include in your memo (i) whether it makes a difference whether her vacation is three or five weeks long, (ii) how you think the E.D. Pa. court will rule on the motions to transfer and to dismiss. How do you respond?

## 2025-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-02-dimeglio-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

Word count: 790

## File Motion to Remand

The partner should take a 3-week vacation so she can file a timely motion to remand (by June 6, 30 days after May 7 removal), which must be granted because Defendant's removal was procedurally defective (removed more than 30 days after the removable event: service on April 1). An extra incentive to remand is that a PA state court will apply PaRCP 8(f)(2) (*infra*).

## File PaRCP 8(f)(2) Motion (if partner does not file a timely motion to remand)

Because Defendant failed to attach the requisite affidavit to its motion to dismiss, the partner should file a motion to fix damages, which will succeed if PaRCP 8(f)(2) applies. The EDPA will apply PA HCoL rules, *Klaxon*, and anything arguably procedural is procedural for HCoL, so 8(f)(2) is procedural for HCoL. Whether that rule applies to Defendant's motion to dismiss in PA federal court, given FRCP 8's existence, is an *Erie* problem.

Under *York*, because guaranteeing significant damages without further investigation of Plaintiff's injuries may well "substantially affect" the "outcome" of the case, the state's law should apply here. Under *Hanna* (Ginsburg and Scalia), every FRCP is valid because it "really regulates procedure" (*Sibbach*). Under Scalia's interpretation, FRCP 8 is applicable because it concerns pleading requirements, so it — not PaRCP 8(f)(2) — applies here. Ginsburg, though, may have cast the FRCP as a "floor" that allows PaRCP 8(f)(2) to add a requirement that does not directly conflict with the FRCP — apply state law. Under Harlan, because the state legislature enacted PaRCP 8(f) to address "primary conduct" — willingness of doctors to practice in PA — apply state law.

## Predicted Ruling on Defendant's 12(b)(6) Motion to Dismiss

Likely denied. Applying *Iqbal*, the complaint is factually specific everywhere except parts of paragraph 14, which contains some conclusory statements. The non-conclusory alleged facts left over obviously may well constitute "damages" (paragraph 12), "breach" (paragraphs 10-11), and "causation" (paragraphs 11-12) if true. With "duty" imputed by law, the complaint therefore makes out a plausible (not merely possible) case for negligence under PA law.

Although Defendant's motion to dismiss was limited to 12(b)(6), the court has an independent duty to independently (*sua sponte*) assure itself of its own SMJ before ruling on any motion. Here, 1332 diversity jurisdiction exists, as Plaintiff (SC) and Defendant (Incorp.-DE, PPoB-MD) are completely diverse (*Mas*; *Strawbridge*), and the AIC ($200,000) exceeds $75,000.

## Predicted Ruling on Motion to Transfer

Apply JG's "Happy Court Rule."

### Must Court Transfer/Dismiss?

No, because the PA federal court is likely happy with proper venue and PJ over Defendant. Venue is automatically proper in removed cases. There is no GIPJ over Defendant (DE, MD, *see supra*), who is not a citizen of PA, and is not "really at home" under *Daimler*, as there is no evidence more than 50% of its business is in PA. But SIPJ likely exists. FRCP 4(k)(1)(A) and

<!-- page: 2 -->

PA's long-arm statute combine to extend SIPJ to constitutional limits. Minimal contacts consist of the facts that Defendant has an office in Harrisburg, PA and partially conducted the sale at issue in Philadelphia. It is likely also "fair and reasonable" to extend SIPJ over Defendant, because D has extensive contacts with PA (*supra*), its office in the state makes litigating there convenient, PA has an interest in adjudicating torts that occur (at least partially) within its borders, and the key witness (salesman who assured plaintiff that the plant was not an orchid) works at the Harrisburg, PA office.

### May the Court Transfer?

Maybe. Venue is proper in SC under 1391(b)(2) because the injury (allergic reaction) occurred in SC. PJ is less likely. There is no GIPJ over Defendant (*supra*). Regarding SIPJ, FRCP 4(k)(1)(A) applies SC's long-arm, which is to constitutional limits. There are minimal contacts, because Defendant shipped directly into South Carolina without an intermediary (distinguishing *McIntyre*), with a single such event sufficient (*McGee*). And as in *McGee*, South Carolina has a "manifest interest in providing effective means of redress when" businesses selling products into the state hurt their residents, but that the crucial witness is in PA (*see supra*), the Plaintiff can travel there as he has made a full recovery and has already been to PA counsel against extending SIPJ over Defendant in SC. That said, SC (place of the injury) law will likely govern substantive tort issues. This could go either way.

### Should the Court Transfer, if it may or may not?

With no forum-selection clause, apply *Gulf Oil*'s standard. The plaintiff's choice of forum is presumptively correct. And, because of the location of the key witness (PA), the office from where the sale was made (PA), and the local interests in adjudicating a tort in the jurisdiction where the misfeasance of the Defendant occurred (PA), that presumption is not unsettled.

Additionally, it is not "in the interest of justice" (*Hoffman*) to transfer a removed case in which a motion to remand would obviously succeed.

## 2025-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-02-guo-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

There is SMJ. Plaintiff is citizen of SC, defendant is corporation incorporated in DE with PPOB in MD, and AIC of 200K > 75K, meaning there is diversity jurisdiction. (*Strawbridge*). Court will not sua sponte remand to PA state court.

**(i)**

The partner needs to take a vacation <30 days to timely file a motion to remand arguing procedural defects — defendant's removal on May 7 was untimely because it was >30 days after service on April 1. This is the only way to return to PA state court.

**(ii)**

Defendant's motion to dismiss unlikely to succeed. Under *Iqbal/Twombly*, we eliminate paragraph 14 as legally conclusory. Existence of duty is always present. Paragraphs 10-11 describe breach through failure to exercise ordinary care despite knowledge of Podrick's severe allergy. Paragraph 11-12 describe causation. Paragraph 12 describes damages. The complaint is not just possible but plausible and contains sufficient specificity.

Motion to transfer is unlikely to succeed.

EDPA is a happy court, so the court does not have to transfer. EDPA does not have GPJ over Daario given it is a DE corporation with PPOB in MD, and there is unlikely an argument for "at-home" (*Daimler*) since Daario appears to ship and sell its plants across US East Coast (unlikely >50% of plants sold in US is sold in PA). Under FRCP 4(k)(1)(A), court will use PA long-arm to constitutional limits. Minimum contacts (*Shoe*) for SIPJ are likely available because Daario purposefully availed itself to doing business in PA by having an office there (*McIntyre*) and having consumers contact its PA office phone numbers to purchase. Both the purchase of the plant and the switching of the plant occurred in PA. It would be fair and reasonable (*McGee*) because Daario has an office in PA, PA has interest in negligent sales within its borders, and a key witness Stark works there. Venue is proper because case was removed.

DSC is likely happy court, so court may transfer. Similar arguments suggest no GPJ over Daario in SC. SIPJ similarly utilizes FRCP 4(k)(1)(A) and SC long-arm. There may be minimum contacts because Daario purposefully availed itself to doing business in SC by shipping products there directly without an intermediary (*McGee*, contra *McIntyre* and *Hanson*), but less contacts than between Daario and PA. It could be fair and reasonable given SC has interest in injuries from dangerous products shipped within its border, SC law likely applies in HCOL analysis (where the injury happened), and evidence of type of plant is in SC. Venue is proper under § 1391(b)(2) since allergic reaction occurred there.

<!-- page: 2 -->

Given EDPA is happy, apply *Gulf Oil* FNC test. Since Podrick is domestic, presume their choice of forum in PA is appropriate, then consider private and public interest factors. Evidence is spread across SC and PA. Witness Stark is in PA. The injury happened in SC, but the negligent act occurred in PA. Given *Gulf Oil* factors could go either way, they likely will not overweigh presumption of plaintiff's choice in PA so court will likely not transfer from EDPA to DSC.

Firm should file PRCP 8(f)(2) motion because Daario failed to attach to its motion to dismiss an affidavit of a licensed physician. The PA federal court will apply PA HCoL rules, *Klaxon*, and anything arguably procedural is procedural for HCoL, so 8(f)(2) is procedural for HCoL, and would apply. PRCP applies in PA state court but may not in EDPA or DSC, so if motion to remand is not timely filed, we apply *Erie* tests to determine if federal court follows FRCP 8 or PRCP 8.

Under *York*, it is likely outcome-determinative because it determines damages amount, though possibly an argument that it doesn't determine who wins, which is the real outcome, but this is a weak argument. Apply state law.

Under *Hanna* majority, FRCP is valid because Congress delegated authority to promulgate FRCP in the Rules Enabling Act. FRCP is a housekeeping rule which is procedural since it really regulates procedure (*Sibbach*) and does not abridge/modify substantive rights.

Scalia: The federal rule is applicable because its plain meaning suggests FRCP directly conflicts with PRCP. The FRCP does not require any physician affidavit. Given supremacy clause we follow FRCP and not state law.

Ginsburg: The federal rule is likely inapplicable because we consider the policy behind state rule and see that federal rule can be narrowly construed to avoid conflict. Here, the FRCP can be considered to set a minimum baseline for personal injury torts so states are free to set stricter standards, since they would still comply with FRCP. Follow state law for federalism reasons so state law applies.

Under *Harlan*, federal law may only cause alteration in conduct of lawyers in what motions they choose to file. However, given PA state enacted PRCP 8 to attract physicians into the state, that is likely substantive primary conduct that could be changed if we apply federal law, so follow state law.

## 2025-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-02-loewenson-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

Word count: 735

**We should file a petition of remand.** Defendant's removal petition was filed >30 days after the removable event (service). If we file a timely remand petition, EDPA will remand.

Because we'll be challenging removal, **I'd caution against a five-week vacation.** We have to file the remand petition within 30 days of removal — by June 6. So you have to be back at work roughly three weeks from now. Sorry!

**We should also file a PRCP 8(f)(2) motion.** Defendant didn't file the required affidavit. The EDPA will apply PA HCoL rules, *Klaxon*, and anything arguably procedural is procedural for HCoL, so 8(f)(2) is procedural for HCoL, Thus, if PRCP 8 applies as an *Erie* matter, the court will prohibit Defendant from disputing the damages we demand for pain and suffering. It definitely applies in PA state court, so all the more reason we want to end up there. Whether it applies in EDPA is a closer call. We apply the *Erie* tests. Under *York*, it's substantive because applying PRCP 8 could significantly alter the case's outcome by basically ensuring we get large pain-and-suffering damages if we win. Under Scalia/*Hanna* Majority, it's procedural, because FRCP 8 is both valid (it "really regulates procedure" under the broad *Sibbach* test) and applicable because it directly speaks to how defendants must respond to complaints. Under Ginsburg/*Hanna* Majority, PRCP 8 is substantive; FRCP 8 is valid (see above) but not necessarily applicable because you could narrowly interpret it as setting a minimum requirement that PRCP 8 merely adds to. Under Harlan, it's substantive because PA crafted PRCP 8 to shape primary conduct: to attract more qualified doctors to the state.

**Defendant's MTD will likely fail.** Applying Iqbal: The only conclusory statements to cross out are in ¶14. The underlying facts left over certainly nudge the allegations for each element from possible to plausible. They allege specific facts that could support findings of breach (¶¶10–11), causation (¶11), and injury (¶12). The complaint survives 12(b)(6).

**Defendant's motion to transfer will also likely fail.** Must the court transfer, i.e., is EDPA "unhappy"? No. EDPA has PJ over Daario. First, note 4(k)(1)(A) and the PA long-arm lay the groundwork for IPJ. There are minimal contacts (*International Shoe*): The key conversation kicking off the allegedly negligent sale took place while both Podrick and Daario's employee were in PA, and the allegedly negligent act — the switching of the plants — occurred in PA. And it would be fair and reasonable (*McGee*) for PA to exercise jurisdiction over Daario. It wouldn't be too inconvenient for the defendant, since Daario

<!-- page: 2 -->

has an office in PA. Plus, Stark, a key witness, works in PA — going toward both extent of the defendant's contacts and convenience for witnesses. Venue is automatically proper in EDPA because of the removal.

May the court transfer, i.e., is DSC "happy?" Maybe. It can lay venue under 1391(b)(2) because SC is where the blood was spilled (the allergic reaction). But PJ is a close call. Given 4(k)(1)(A) and the long-arm (and no sign of general PJ), we again look for minimal contacts. While Daario didn't specifically target SC, it did deliver there without intermediary, unlike in *Nicastro*; in fact, it advertised that it could deliver anywhere on the Eastern Seaboard (also unlike *Nicastro*). That's probably enough purposeful availment for minimal contacts. The *McGee* factors are iffier. On the one hand, it would be convenient to view the flower and the medical records in SC. And maybe SC would be convenient for the plaintiff (but the plaintiff selected another forum, so plaintiff convenience cuts both ways!). On the other hand, Daario's contacts in SC are hardly extensive. All it did in SC was send a delivery to a customer there. And while it is possible for Daario to send delivery trucks to SC, it might not be so convenient for it to litigate a lengthy lawsuit there.

Even if EDPA somehow found DSC "happy," should it transfer? Presume Podrick has the privilege of choosing the forum: Because he chose PA state court, maybe EDPA is closer to being Podrick's chosen forum than DSC. The *Gulf Oil* factors don't override that presumption. The private factors are mixed; true, it would be easier to access the flower and the medical records in SC, but it would be easier to get Stark's testimony and to view the relevant Daario office in PA. The public interest factors don't seem applicable. And it wouldn't be "in the interest of justice" (*Hoffman*) to transfer from EDPA, given that the initial removal from PA state court was improper and ought to be reversed. In sum, a transfer is unlikely.

## 2025-assignment-02 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-02-nunez-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

Word Count: 745

**(i) Actions**

**We should file a motion to remand to state court immediately.** Defendant's removal petition was filed >30 days after service and therefore is procedurally defective. As for your holiday, we have 30 days from the May 7th erroneous motion for removal to move for remand. After a 3-week trip we would still be able to file, but if a 5-week vacation is taken we will be procedurally ineligible to motion for remand and could be stuck in federal court!

**We should file a PRCP 8(f)(2) motion.** The Defendant failed to file the requisite affidavit such that under PRCP 8, they are barred from disputing the damages we demand for pain and suffering ($100,000). The EDPA will apply PA HCoL rules, *Klaxon*, and anything arguably procedural is procedural for HCoL, so 8(f)(2) is procedural for HCoL. We would be able to bind Daario in PA state court, but PRCP 8's applicability in federal court is less certain. An *Erie* inquiry is necessary. Under *York*'s "outcome determinative" test, the rule is determinative and thus substantive in that is fixes the amount of P&S damages, therefore it applies. Under both Scalia and Ginsberg in *Hanna* FRCP 8 is valid as it "really regulates procedure" (*Sibbach*). Under Scalia's test in the *Hanna* Majority, FRCP 8 is applicable and controls as it conflicts with PRCP 8, because both directly set response requirements. Under the Ginsberg test in *Hanna*, FRCP 8 is not necessarily applicable because looking to uphold the policy goals of PA, the court would interpret FRCP 8 narrowly, as setting a minimum requirement to which PRCP 8 merely adds. State law controls. Under the *Harlan* majority test, PRCP 8 is substantive because it affects pre-litigation "primary conduct" (attracting qualified physicians to PA), therefore the state law applies.

**(ii) Motion Analysis**

**The MTD will likely be denied.**

Under *Twombley/Iqbal* we remove ¶ 14 as legally conclusory and find the remaining factual allegations nudge the complaint from possible to plausible. Specific facts support all elements, duty (legally prescribed), breach ¶10-11, causation ¶11, and damages ¶12.

The Court will *sua sponte* review the complaint for SMJ, which is present under § 1332. Complete diversity exists (*Strawbridge*): Podrick is a citizen of S.C., Daario's POI is DE and PPOB is MD; and the AIC ($200,000) exceeds $75,000.

**The motion to transfer will also likely be denied.**

*Must the court transfer? No.*

EDPA has proper venue and PJ over Daario and is likely a "happy court". Venue is automatically proper in all removed cases. There is not GIPJ over D. Daario's POI is DE and PPOB is in MD and there is no evidence that 50+% of its revenues come from PA. (*Daimler*) However, SIPJ is likely present. There is a PA state long arm statute reaching constitutional limits and FRCP 4(k)(1)(A) authorizing jurisdiction over an out of state D. There are minimal contacts (*Int'l Shoe*) as Daario maintained an office in Harrisburg. Plaintiff was in Philadelphia when he learned of Daario and called the Harrisburg office to order his flower, which in turn led to the potential negligence claim. Extending SIPJ over D is also fair and

<!-- page: 2 -->

reasonable as D's brick and mortar office in PA makes litigating there convenient and likely expected; PA has an interest in torts that originate within its borders and a key witness, the salesman who assured plaintiff that the flower was not an orchid works, in Harrisburg.

*May the court transfer? Potentially.*

The transfer statutes allow transfer from any district to any "happy court". Venue is proper in DSC under § 1391(b)(2) because the injury occurred in DSC. There is likely no GIPJ over Daario in SC (see above). SIPJ would rely on FRCP 4(k)(1)(A) and long arm. Here, there are potentially minimum contacts, because Daario purposefully availed itself of SC by doing business there, shipping their floral products directly into the state without a distributor. (*McGee*, contra *McIntyre*). It could be fair and reasonable because HCOL analysis likely points to applying SC law, SC has an interest in torts "blooming" within its borders, and evidence from the injury and hospital stay, to the plant itself, is in S.C.

*Should the court transfer? Probably not.*

Since EDPA is a "happy court", we apply the *Gulf Oil* factors. Plaintiff's choice of venue was EDPA, which presumptively should be honored. While much evidence is in S.C. (see supra), a key witness is in PA (see supra). Juries in both districts would be concerned about negligence (PA) and injury (SC) in their jurisdictions. The factors coming out in both directions is likely not enough to overcome the presumption.

## Question 1 (suggested 400-450 words against limit of 850)

All states relevant to this exam had enacted the Uniform Seizure of Property Law (USPL). The USPL provides as follows:

- **Section 202:** A seller who claims ownership of an incorporeal piece of property deemed to be located within this state due to a buyer's failure to make one or more installment payments pursuant to a contract with an agreed-upon purchase price may, if they can do so without violence and if the contract so provides, repossess the item at issue, provided:
    - The seller gives reasonable notice to the buyer of the installment payments missed and the total amount due, as well as the intention to repossess if the arrearage is not paid in full; and
    - Within a reasonable time after such repossession, the seller initiates an in rem lawsuit in a court of this state over the piece of property seized seeking a declaration that due to the buyer's failure to make appropriate payments, the seller is now the legal owner of the property.

If the court, pursuant to such lawsuit, finds that the seller has proved that the seller's claim of ownership was proper under applicable law, the court shall declare the seller the owner of the property, and shall issue such orders as may be necessary to require the seller to refund the amount of payments the buyer did make against the purchase price, less the amount of seller's damages (or damage to the property) and expenses in the litigation not including attorneys' fees. If the court finds instead that the seller's claim of ownership was not proper under applicable law, the court shall declare the buyer the owner of the property, shall order the seller to return possession of the item to the buyer, and shall award the buyer appropriate damages caused by the improper seizure as well as the buyer's expenses in the litigation not including attorneys' fees.

All states relevant to this assignment have long-arm statutes allowing the exercise of personal jurisdiction to constitutional limits.

Pugsley Addams was a digital art collector. Pugsley did business in his own name, personally owning all NFTs that he purchased. He was born in Massachusetts and maintained his residence in Martha's Vineyard (in MA). He bought, enjoyed, and occasionally sold NFTs, specializing in artwork available via cryptographic service/environment called Fester, which administered a blockchain written in a programming language called Constryctor.[^1] Lurch Industries, Inc., a Delaware corporation with its

[^1]: If you didn't understand much about this sentence, fine. All you need to know is that NFTs are a form of digital artwork that use fancy computer stuff to assure that there is only one copy (unique, indivisible, and verifiable) of the piece of art at a time. NFTs can be bought and sold, and "possession" of them can be transferred, all while still maintaining their uniqueness, via exchanges of pieces of information/code from one person to another. In this way, NFTs are like cryptocurrency.

<!-- page: 3 -->

principal place of business in Portland, Maine, operated and maintained Fester. Lurch's operational hub was in Bismarck, North Dakota. Lurch maintained in Bismarck all its programmers and its servers (except those associated with its public-facing website). The Bismarck servers stored all code and data associated with the Fester environment, including the information associated with works of art. Information/codes needed to exercise ownership of Fester NFTs were transferred via the Internet. Owners of NFTs that existed within Fester were not visible to the public. Lurch administered its public-facing Internet website/presence from servers maintained near its Portland headquarters. Its website included a catalog of NFTs that owners made available for purchase on Fester, including some that Lurch itself owned. Interested buyers could click on website links to exchange encrypted messages with owners, usually to negotiate a purchase price. Lurch kept a 5% commission on all sales.

In early January, 2025, Pugsley noticed on the Lurch website an NFT available in the Fester environment called "Wednesday & Itt Are Friends," an incredibly detailed and intricate image of two beloved characters from a TV show Pugsley remembered watching as a boy. Using the Lurch website's encrypted messaging function, Pugsley discovered that Lurch itself owned "Wednesday & Itt." Pugsley negotiated a purchase price of $200,000, to be paid in $20,000 payments due on the first of each month in Bitcoin cryptocurrency, with the first payment due on March 1, 2025. The parties executed a contract via digital signatures on Lurch's website. The contract provided that if Pugsley failed to make a payment by the 15th of the month upon which the payment was due, then Lurch could take any action available under the law, including repossession of the artwork. After execution of the contract, Lurch transferred the codes and information necessary for ownership to Pugsley. As was the case with all artwork created within the Fester environment, Lurch continued to host "Wednesday & Itt Are Friends" within its network.

Pugsley made the March, April, May, and June payments. In July, no payment was forthcoming. On July 10, Lurch emailed Pugsley to demand payment and to warn him that if no payment was made, it would take any action available under the law to protect its rights. The next day, by return email, Pugsley stated that he would not be making further payments. Pugsley further demanded that Lurch agree that the $80,000 he had paid thus far was in full payment of the purchase price for "Wednesday & Itt Are Friends," and that he (Pugsley) was now the owner of the NFT with no further payments due. Pugsley acknowledged that proceeding in this way would constitute a reformation of the contract as though the purchase price had been $80,000 instead of $200,000. Pugsley explained that the reason for his demand was that he believed, as a result of chatter in online chatrooms that he monitored, that Lurch had built a vulnerability into the Fester environment that exposed NFT assets to theft. Although Pugsley could not himself pinpoint the vulnerability, he believed that others might be able to do so and thereby steal his artwork. Pugsley alleged that this vulnerability constituted a breach of contract and allowed him to rescind the purchase transaction.

In a sense, Pugsley was correct: Lurch had built a backdoor into the Fester environment, one that it believed that it alone could use, and that it had only ever used to repossess items that it sold when it believed that it had not been paid. And that is what Lurch did with Pugsley. Specifically, on July 16, 2025, Lurch used the backdoor it had built into Fester to transfer ownership of "Wednesday & Itt" to itself. On September 9, 2025, Lurch initiated a suit captioned "In re Wednesday & Itt" in a state court in Portland, ME, seeking a declaration that Lurch was the legitimate owner of the NFT and praying the court for an order setting the financial terms needed to unwind the sale of the artwork. Lurch notified Pugsley of the

<!-- page: 4 -->

suit the same day via email and, in accordance with the Maine Rules of Civil Procedure, served a copy of the summons and complaint via first class mail on Pugsley at his Martha's Vineyard address. The parties (Pugsley and Lurch) agree that this form of notice was sufficient to meet due process/Constitutional requirements.

Pugsley hires you to represent him. What civil procedure arguments does Pugsley have against either the repossession of the NFT or in response to the lawsuit? Explain.

## Question 2 (suggested 400-450 words against limit of 850)

In multiple places in the class thus far, we have seen the Constitution and some other source of law speak to the same concerns. In each of these instances, the Constitution provides a broad set of limits within which the government can operate, and some other source of law (such as a statute, a rule of civil procedure) narrows the range of what is permissible. In each of those instances, the parties or the court system must operate within both the broader set of Constitutional limits and the narrow range set by the other source of law. And in each case, it is not clear that the narrow range of the non-Constitutional source of law does a very good job of furthering the goals/policies of the area.

Write a short paragraph or two naming one area we've studied with this legal structure. In your mini-essay, begin by identifying the Constitutional provision and the non-Constitutional source of law, and briefly explain the contours/limits each imposes. Include a brief discussion of whether, in your view, the non-Constitutional source of law does a good job of furthering the goals or policies of the area. Suggest reforms or alternatives or changes to the non-Constitutional source of law and discuss briefly advantages and disadvantages of your proposal.

To guide your writing, an example of a strong response appears below in italics. You may **not** use the topic of this example (the arising under basis of subject matter jurisdiction) in your writing.

*Article III section 2 clause 1 and 28 USC 1331 both address whether a federal district court may exercise "arising under" jurisdiction. Both sources of law provide that a federal district court can adjudicate a lawsuit that "arises under" federal law. But while Art. III extends to cases that have any federal issue, possibly even any nascent federal issue (*Osborn*), *Mottley* interpreted 1331 as allowing AUJ only when the plaintiff asserts a federally created cause of action. The *Smith-Grable* doctrine provides an exception when a federal issue is embedded in a state cause of action, but such embedding rarely occurs, and even embedding is not enough on its own. Supposedly, the narrow interpretation of 1331 serves the value of federalism because it limits the number of cases in which the availability of AUJ provides a federal forum, meaning more cases end up in state courts. In my view, however, 1331's limit makes little sense. Historically speaking, the problem is not that the number or importance of cases for the state courts to adjudicate has decreased over time; as state statutes have become more common and more complex, state courts have more, and more important, cases to decide. The problem is that federal statutes have become more common and more complex even faster than their state counterparts. If the federal courts are to specialize in adjudicating federal issues, their SMJ should grow even more than the state courts. Any so-called "power grabs" are the result of Congress' passing too many statutes.*

*With this in mind, *Mottley*'s well-pleaded complaint rule does a poor job of furthering the goals of the arising under power. If the goal is to have some but not all cases with federal issues end up in federal*

<!-- page: 5 -->

*court, fine, but the filter should turn on the importance of the federal issue, not whether the issue happens to arise via a federally created cause of action. The Supreme Court should reconsider *Mottley* and instead interpret 1331 to turn on whether the federal issue in the case (regardless of whether it appears as part of the cause of action or a defense or a response to a defense) is sufficiently important as to merit federal district court adjudication. True, this will add uncertainty into each case, and require federal courts to adjudicate the importance of each issue. But the Supreme Court can clarify this vague "importance" standard by deciding several cases and providing examples, the way it did in specific IPJ with minimal contacts (*International Shoe*) and in notice for procedural due process (*Mullane*).*

## 2025-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 1

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-03-elmosleh-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1:

### In Rem Jurisdiction (IRJ)

Pugsley can argue that there is improper exercise of IRJ, though the ME statute authorizes it.

Under the narrow interpretation of *Shaffer*, the court can exercise IRJ if the property is located in Maine. NFTs are incorporeal objects like debt (*Harris*), and there are multiple states where it could be located. Under a functional approach, the court may locate it at its owner's domicile. But here, it is unclear if the owner is Lurch (DE, ME) or Pugsley (MA). There is also an argument to locate it in ND, where the code necessary for it to exist is stored. In the interest of stability, the court could choose to locate it in DE, where Lurch is incorporated, since Pugsley could have easily ascertained the state of Lurch's incorporation.

Under the broad interpretation of *Shaffer*, *International Shoe* requires IPJ with Pugsley. Pugsley is domiciled in MA, so ME cannot exercise GIPJ over him unless there is consent, waiver, or in-state tag (which didn't occur here). ME has a long-arm, and SIPJ requires minimal contacts between Pugsley, the subject of the litigation, and ME. There is no evidence that Pugsley knew Lurch operated the business from ME, and likely did not expect ME law to apply. But the facts are similar to *McGee* (contract with a ME citizen, suit about that contract), it likely controls, and the court may therefore find minimal contacts met. Nevertheless, IPJ over Pugsley is likely not fair and reasonable under *McGee* because he has no relationship with ME and did not expect to be hauled into court there, although ME does have an interest in protecting its citizens from unpaid debts.

### PDP

Pugsley is likely to succeed in raising PDP concerns on this pre-hearing seizure under USPL compared to a post-hearing seizure (*Mathews*).

The private interests for both parties are relatively high. Lurch has an ownership interest in the NFT, but subsistence is not at issue (like in *Goldberg*). Pugsley also has an ownership (equity) interest in the property since he has already completed four payments.

The risk of erroneous deprivation is high because this is a pre-hearing seizure, which requires special circumstances (*Doehr*), and there appears to be no reason for the rush to a seizure (Lurch could simply sue Pugsley in personam and use a seizure post-judgment). No judge (or any court) is required to authorize the seizure, so there is no live testimony and no litigation bonds.

Finally, the government has an interest in accuracy and low costs, both of which would be better served by requiring a pre-seizure hearing.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Question 2:

The Due Process Clauses of the Fifth and Fourteenth Amendments to the Constitution and FRCP 4 both address the issue of proper notice. The Due Process Clause, as interpreted in *Mullane*, mandates that notice must be "reasonably calculated" to inform the defendant of the action and give them an opportunity to respond (*Mullane*). FRCP 4 responds to this requirement of notice by outlining specific procedures for service of process to properly notify defendants of lawsuits. FRCP 4 goes above the constitutional requirement of notice because it requires the defendant be served with a copy of the complaint alongside the summons via certain methods, none of which include electronic communication.

To properly serve a defendant (individual or corporation), FRCP 4(e) and 4(h) requirements are more stringent than the broad constitutional requirement for reasonably calculated notice. For example, they do not allow electronic service of process via e-mail, even though e-mail has become a primary method of communication and may even be faster and more reliable than mail. The plaintiff might have to expend additional resources to discover the defendant's physical address even if their e-mail address is more readily available, creating tension with the constitutional principle that no "heroic efforts" are required by the plaintiff to serve proper notice.

Additionally, FRCP 4 can be read as not fully encompassing the constitutional requirements for notice, because it contains no language about the plaintiff's continuing duty as outlined in *Jones v. Flowers*. In that case, the Supreme Court held that the plaintiff owes a continuing duty to notify the defendant if they know that their first attempt at notice failed, but FRCP 4 does not address this issue.

FRCP 4 can be amended to better align with the constitutional requirement for notice, such as by including language about the plaintiff's continuing duty to notify the defendant if they know that their initial attempt failed. To better respond to evolving methods of communication, FRCP 4 can also be amended to allow electronic service of process, although this might be problematic for certain defendants (e.g., older). These changes require balancing the interest of not unduly burdening the plaintiff with the due process interests of the defendant to be properly informed and given an opportunity to respond to lawsuits.

## 2025-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 2

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-03-fisher-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

Pugsley should object to the lawsuit on the grounds that the Maine court lacks in rem jurisdiction (IRJ). Note that under either version of *Shaffer*, the Maine statutory scheme authorizes the seizure and Maine has a long-arm statute that extends to constitutional limits.

First, if IRJ survives post-*Shaffer* (via the *Burnham* Scalia opinion), IRJ is only proper if the NFT is deemed to be located in Maine. As with the debt in *Harris*, it is unclear where incorporeal objects like NFTs are "located." There are plausible arguments for North Dakota (location of Fester code/data), Massachusetts (domicile of Pugsley), Maine (PPOB of Lurch), or Delaware (Lurch's place of incorporation). Given that only one copy of the NFT exists and was always hosted on Fester's servers in North Dakota, Pugsley could argue that the NFT was located there. However, that could make customers susceptible to suits wherever a company maintains servers, which is both unpredictable and could include many states. Lurch might argue that the NFT was located in Maine because it owns Fester and has its PPOB there. But under that line of logic, Pugsley can argue the NFT was located in Massachusetts once he *purchased* it from Lurch. Delaware, therefore, might provide the best answer because a company's place of incorporation is both easy to ascertain and unlikely to change.

Second, if *Shaffer* implies that IRJ now requires an *International Shoe* analysis, Pugsley should argue that he did not purposefully avail himself to Maine law. Lurch will probably claim that Pugsley did so by transacting business with a Maine-based company (analogous to *McGee*). But Pugsley never stepped foot in Maine and perhaps had no idea that Lurch's PPOB was there. Pugsley should also argue that IRJ would not be fair/reasonable. The extent of his contacts to Maine is limited and Maine is inconvenient (commuting from Martha's Vineyard). However, Maine is convenient for the plaintiff, may be the location of witnesses (like sales representatives), and Maine has an interest in protecting corporations that operate there.

Pugsley should object to the seizure on the grounds that it violated his procedural due process rights (*Eldridge*). Both parties have substantial interest in retaining ownership of their property, and there is no indication Pugsley needs the NFT for subsistence. Compared to an alternative seizure process that provides for pre-seizure hearing and a litigation bond (*Doehr* concurrence), the USPL carries a high risk of error. No judge signs off on the seizure, no sworn affidavits are required, and the lack of bond requirement means that the market has not evaluated the claim's viability. Lurch will probably respond that there is no risk of wasting the NFT that continues to be hosted on Fester while the proceeding plays out. But there is no government/public urgency to warrant pre-hearing seizure (*Fuentes*) and the USPL does not require an immediate post-seizure hearing.

## Question 2

Article III, Section 2, Clause 1 of the U.S. Constitution and 28 U.S.C. § 1332 both address the exercise of diversity jurisdiction by federal courts. Both sources of law provide that a federal court can adjudicate a lawsuit between citizens of different states. But while Article III could extend to any dispute with minimal diversity, *Strawbridge* interpreted § 1332 as requiring complete diversity. § 1332 also imposes an AIC minimum ($75,000). Supposedly, the limits of

<!-- page: 2 -->

§ 1332 narrow the scope of DJ to cases in danger of "home cooking," where there is both much to lose and a substantial risk that state courts will be unfairly biased in favor of citizens of their own state. In my view, however, § 1332's limitations are arbitrary and no longer serve a valid purpose in twenty-first century litigation. Modern technology and transportation have facilitated movement between states and diminished the importance of state identity. At the same time, however, that interconnectedness has given rise to complex disputes that arise between citizens from many states and involve challenging choice-of-law questions. Given a state court's likely preference and expertise in applying their own law, federal courts *should* exercise DJ to specialize in adjudicating these increasingly complicated multistate disputes.

With this in mind, § 1332's AIC and complete diversity requirements prevent federal courts from exercising DJ in cases that they may be more qualified to adjudicate. If the goal is to have only exceedingly challenging choice-of-law cases appear in federal court, fine, but DJ should turn on the complexity of the dispute, not whether it exceeds an arbitrary AIC threshold or happened to meet the complete diversity requirement. The Supreme Court should reconsider *Strawbridge*. Congress should amend § 1332 to remove the AIC threshold and in its place, grant federal courts discretion to adjudicate complex disputes that meet the minimal diversity requirement. To be sure, this will cause some uncertainty among litigants and force federal courts to weigh their relative expertise in resolving each lawsuit. But federal courts can develop a balancing test of relevant factors to guide their discretion as was the case for abstention doctrine (*Colorado River*). The Supreme Court should weigh in if circuit splits develop (and therefore avoid a *Lacy*-like result).

## 2025-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 3

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-03-guzdial-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

### IRJ

Pugsley can argue that the NFT is not located in ME, so Lurch cannot repossess under USPL/sue IRJ. Because NFTs are incorporeal, this is a *Harris*-style problem. The court could locate the NFT at the owner's domicile (Pugsley in MA or Lurch's PPOB in ME), Lurch's place of incorporation (DE), where the code is (ND), or where the website servers are (ME). From a policy perspective, DE is the strongest choice. Using incorporation is easily administrable for courts and predictable for litigants, as it's easy to find out where a business is incorporated. ND makes less sense since only Lurch knows where it stores its data, and it would necessarily follow that an NFT could "exist" in many places if data is in a cloud that uses multiple places. ME is not ideal since Lurch could easily move its website operations (which have little connection to contents of NFT anyways).

Even if the court decides the NFT is in ME, we could argue that *Shaffer* did not leave IRJ undisturbed. Under a broad view of *Shaffer*, ME must have IPJ over Pugsley. Pugsley is domiciled in MA and there was no consent/waiver/tag so no GIPJ. ME's long-arm goes to constitution, so there must be minimum contacts linking Pugsley's interest in the NFT, the subject matter of the litigation, and the forum — which likely is not satisfied. Pugsley may not know that Lurch maintained their website from ME — since that was not advertised. Unless the court considers one sale with a ME business enough for minimum contacts, as it admittedly did in *McGee*, Pugsley likely prevails under a broad view of *Shaffer*. It likewise does not seem fair and reasonable to hail Pugsley into ME court, as he is potentially not aware he was doing business with a ME citizen, even though ME does have an interest in protecting its citizens from nonpayment.

### PDP

Pugsley should also argue that USPL did not afford him PDP and demand a pre-seizure hearing. Pre-judgment seizures typically require a hearing (barring extraordinary circumstances which are not present here) (*Doehr*). Like the pre-judgment seizure in *Doehr*, this seizure likely violates the *Mathews* test.

The private interests of Pugsley and Lurch seem to be balanced — the facts do not suggest that either are relying on this NFT or sale money for their livelihood, and both have a property interest. This NFT is not an essential item like a house or a car.

There is a high risk of erroneous deprivation. The seizure did not require any presentation of evidence or findings of fact before the deprivation (*Vitek*) or even a court's authorization. By allowing Pugsley the opportunity to be heard prior to the seizure with written findings of

<!-- page: 2 -->

fact, the court would dramatically safeguard his interests. There is also no bond requirement for Lurch or counterbond option for Pugsley.

The government always has an interest in accuracy, which would weigh towards allowing Pugsley a hearing before seizure.

## Question 2

Article III, Section 2 of the Constitution and 28 U.S.C. § 1332 both address the question of DJ in the federal courts. Article III, Section 2 authorizes federal judicial power to extend to "Controversies… between Citizens of different States." The Supreme Court has interpreted this broad grant to require only *minimal diversity*. This broad constitutional scope reflects the Framers' goal of providing a neutral federal forum to protect out-of-state litigants from local bias (home cooking). However, Congress has narrowed this power by requiring *complete diversity* (at least according to *Strawbridge*). The AIC requirement further restricts access to federal court.

The policy justification for § 1332's narrowing of Article III's scope is to respect federalism and prevent the federal courts from being overwhelmed by state-law disputes. By limiting DJ to completely diverse parties, Congress sought to reserve federal courts for cases truly at risk of local bias and to reduce their overall caseload. In practice, though, the complete diversity rule poorly furthers these goals. Local bias is unlikely to disappear simply because only one of many defendants happens to share a state with a plaintiff, and the rigid rule often forces multi-party disputes with largely out-of-state interests into state court, where bias can persist. Meanwhile, federal courts are already equipped to manage their caseload through other procedural tools (remand, Colorado River stay, etc.) without such an inflexible bar.

A more balanced approach would be to amend § 1332 to permit minimal diversity, aligning the statute more closely with the Constitution's broader grant. This change would preserve the availability of a neutral federal forum in genuinely interstate disputes, especially in an era where commerce and liability often cross state lines. The downside is that minimal diversity would increase the federal caseload and could blur the distinction between state and federal judicial responsibilities. But Congress could mitigate this by pairing minimal diversity with expanded discretionary abstention standards, ensuring that federal courts hear only cases with significant interstate dimensions and risk of state court bias. Overall, relaxing § 1332's complete diversity requirement would better reflect the original constitutional purpose of protecting against local bias in multistate controversies while maintaining manageable limits on federal jurisdiction.

## 2025-assignment-03 · Exemplary student answer 4

*Past student exemplar (content/course/assignments/2025-assignment-03-hayduk-model-answer.md): circulated by the instructor as among the best in the class. May inform writing and organization; never doctrinal authority, never cited to a student.*

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Question 1

### IRJ

Because the seizure took place in July and the suit wasn't filed until September, that was not a "reasonable time" after the repossession per the USPL. Though the parties agreed notice was reasonable, the USPL doesn't apply because the in rem lawsuit was faulty.

Under the narrow view of *Shaffer*, IRJ is lacking because the NFT isn't in the forum state, Maine. Because the NFT is incorporeal, it's unclear where the NFT is. Maine doesn't make sense from a policy perspective because as Lurch's PPoB, it is moveable and Pugsley may not know that's where Lurch hosts its public-facing website. Pugsley could argue it was in ND because the NFT was in the Fester environment there and everything necessary to host it was there (e.g., programmers, servers, Fester code / data), though only Lurch would know where it stores data and because data may be stored in multiple places, the NFT might exist in multiple places. The NFT could be in MA (*Balk*), because the NFT codes and information necessary for ownership transferred to Pugsley. Based on administration ease, it could make sense for the NFT to be in DE, since it's easy to find that Lurch is incorporated in DE and places of incorporation don't move.

Under the broad view of *Shaffer*, with no argument for GIPJ, we need SIPJ. We have statutory authorization (provided by the USPL) and the Maine long-arm statute that goes to the limits of the Constitution. However, *International Shoe* demands minimal contacts, which are lacking. Though this case is like *McGee* because the dispute is about a contract with a ME citizen without an intermediary, Pugsley did not purposefully avail himself of Maine's laws (*Hanson*) just because he bought the NFT from Lurch whose PPoB was in Maine and its public-facing internet website/presence was administered from servers in Maine. Pugsley purchased the NFT at his home in MA and he had no notice he might be hauled into court in Maine (*WW Volkswagon*). F&R factors (*McGee*) also disfavor ME because ME is inconvenient for Pugsley and the evidence of Lurch's backdoor in the Fester environment is in ND, though some evidence may exist in ME (e.g., public-facing servers).

### PDP

Like the pre-judgment seizure in *Doehr*, this seizure likely violates the *Mathews* test. 1. The private interests of Pugsley and Lurch are relatively balanced as they both assert a property interest in the NFT. The NFT implicates subsistence for neither Pugsley nor Lurch (*Mathews*), though trading this type of property was Pugsley's main source of income. 2. There is a high risk of erroneous deprivation compared to alternatives. Through Lurch's backdoor, the seizure takes effect instantly and requires no presentation of evidence prior to the deprivation. To reduce risk of erroneous determination, the court could require Lurch to post bond, allow Pugsley to counter-bond to resist seizure, and/or hold a hearing prior to ordering the seizure. 3. The government also has an interest in accuracy and low adjudication costs.

## Question 2

The 14th amendment and FRCP 4 address what is necessary to assert IPJ so as not to deprive a person of due process. Though the 14th amendment broadly requires due process, Rule 4(k)(1)(A) ensures that a federal court can have IPJ over a defendant only if the state in

<!-- page: 2 -->

which the court is located would have jurisdiction. For a suit filed in federal court where the court does not have GIPJ over a defendant, to establish SIPJ over a defendant, the court requires a long-arm statute, minimal contacts linking the defendant, the subject matter of the litigation, and the forum, and that the forum is fair and reasonable, per *McGee*.

Though the details in FRCP 4 ensures that a defendant is only subject to litigation where a plaintiff has followed every step determined by the Supreme Court via the Rules Enabling Act, the system can make it difficult for a plaintiff to sue a large foreign corporation in a court that's accessible to the plaintiff, such as in *McIntyre*. The *McIntyre* plurality justified the ruling that SIPJ did not exist based on state sovereignty and respecting the defendant's rights. However, if Congress passed a long-arm statute that deputized the federal courts to exercise SIPJ where the corporation maintains minimal contacts with the US, instead of just with NJ, the *McIntyre* case may have been ruled differently. Congress and the federal courts may worry this would overwhelm the US court system with additional litigation and discourage foreign companies from doing business in the US to avoid creating minimal contacts. However, I believe this would be an improvement in holding foreign companies who do harm accountable and allow recovery for aggrieved plaintiffs. As Justice Ginsburg noted in her dissent, for a large corporation like *McIntyre* it should have foreseen being haled into court in NJ, or at least somewhere in the US. What is fair and reasonable for a giant corporation that sells its products worldwide is different from what is fair and reasonable for an individual who can be subject to SIPJ by visiting a forum temporarily, such as in *Burnham*.
